# Ghost invariants

The package map and the load-bearing design invariants: what each package owns, and the rules a change must not break. Moved verbatim from `CLAUDE.md` so that file stays short; the full design narrative is `docs/architecture.md`. The PR reviewer workflow reads this file from the base ref as part of its conventions.

## Stack
- Go 1.26+ CLI application
- SQLite with FTS5 for memory persistence (modernc.org/sqlite — pure Go, no CGO)
- CLI harness callers (claude/opencode/codex/goose subprocesses) — used by reflection, resolve, and supersede. Each harness owns its authentication and billing; Ghost does not add a direct API client. The prompt is written to the child's stdin and no positional message is passed: the kernel caps one argv element at 32 pages (128 KiB on a 4 KiB-page x86, 512 KiB on a 16 KiB-page one) and a reflect prompt reaches megabytes, so an argv prompt failed the spawn with E2BIG instead of answering. `claude -p` and `opencode run` read the message from a pipe when none is given; codex gets `exec -` and goose `run -i -`. `claude --system-prompt` stays an argument — small, fixed, and never confusable with user content. `cli.model_reflect` / `model_resolve` / `model_supersede` pin the opencode harness model per phase (each phase is its own process; ignored by claude/codex/goose which have no model flag). Empty = any inherited `GHOST_OPENCODE_MODEL`, else Ghost's explicit `opencode/big-pickle` default; a configured pin overrides an inherited env pin for that phase.
- MCP server via modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk (stdio transport)

## Package map
- `cmd/ghost/main.go` — CLI entrypoint; subcommands: mcp, hook, reflect, resolve, supersede, prune, lifecycle, project, obsidian, opencode, bench, backup, export, import, history, maintenance, upgrade, context, version, help. `runCLI` answers `-h`/`--help` (`cmd/ghost/help.go`, one usage table) before the dispatch, so a help request has no side effect. A command line the dispatch cannot route — an unknown command, a missing or unknown subcommand, no command at all — is `usageError` on stderr (the diagnostic, then that level's own usage) and exit `2`, the conventional usage-error code, kept distinct from the `1` every command uses for a run that started and failed (#691; bare `ghost mcp` still starts the server, the one group with a default action). Only the groups in `commandGroups` dispatch on a subcommand word: a word after a command that takes an OPERAND (`ghost history <memory-id>`, `ghost reflect <project>`) is data and its parser's business, and an unknown FLAG is that parser's too — exit `1` where it rejects one (`ghost reflect` still ignores it, as it always has). An unknown flag VALUE is not ignored: `validateReflectSelection` refuses a `--tier` outside auto, cli, opencode, sqlite and a `--source` outside `ai.KnownSources()` with exit `1`, naming the allowed values, before `bootstrap` opens the store, because either one used to reach the tier switch's `auto` default and run the SQLite floor with exit `0`; the removed `haiku` passes through to its own message, and a known source whose binary is missing keeps the documented SQLite fallback. `ghost context`'s two modes are the parsers that got this wrong once (#1090): an argument `contextAsOf` or `parseContextAuditArgs` rejects is a run that started and failed, so `runContext` and `runContextAudit` RETURN `1` to the dispatch rather than calling `os.Exit(2)`, which stays for routing alone, and returning the code is what lets a test read it instead of taking the process down with it. A SCOPE FLAG'S EMPTY VALUE IS A REFUSAL, NEVER ITS DEFAULT, for every parser that takes a project. Every one of those parsers counts OCCURRENCES of a project with a seen bool, asks the duplicate before the empty, and refuses an empty value — but the set of SPELLINGS a given parser accepts differs, and the seen bool covers exactly the spellings that parser takes, no more: `parseResolveArgs`, `parseReflectArgs` and `parseSupersedeArgs` take all three (`--project X`, `--project=X`, and a bare positional), `parseExportArgs`, `parseHistoryCompactArgs` and `parseObsidianFlags` take the two flag spellings and no positional, `parseLifecycleArgs` takes `--project X` and the positional but has no `--project=` form, and `parseContextAuditArgs` (`ghost context --audit`) and `parsePruneArgs` (after #646) take the flag spellings. `runProjectDelete`'s project is positional alone and it counts it the same way, refusing an empty name. In short: an empty value is a refusal in every spelling the parser accepts, and the count is what makes the refusal order-independent (`cmd/ghost/scope_flag_parsers_test.go` asks each of these parsers the questions its own spellings admit). The rule has no exception, and it is one rule for parsers whose empty value answers DIFFERENT things, which is why none of them may take it as its default: for `prune`, `export` and `obsidian` an empty scope is answered with EVERY project in the store, and for `resolve`/`reflect` an empty one decides nothing and prints the usage block instead, so what the refusal stops is not one wrong corpus but a scope the command line never named (`cmd/ghost/obsidian.go`'s --out and --interval are DESTINATIONS and a CADENCE, not scopes, and are held by the two rules below rather than by this one). A DESTINATION IS NOT A SCOPE, but it is refused on both of the same two questions: `parseExportArgs`, `parseBackupArgs` and `parseObsidianFlags` refuse `--out=` and `--out ""` with `--out requires a value`, because a blank the command line named is the unset-variable spelling, and taking it as "no flag given" wrote the artifact to the timestamped default path inside the data directory under a success exit (#1094; for `ghost obsidian` it mirrored into the default vault, #1129). THE SAME RULE COVERS OBSIDIAN'S OTHER VALUE FLAG: `--interval=` and `--interval ""` are refused with `--interval requires a value` (#1276), because the empty value was read as "no cadence given" and the command silently polled at `obsidian.interval` (30s by default) and exited 0. AND A REPEATED DESTINATION OR CADENCE IS A REFUSAL, asked by occurrence count and BEFORE the empty check, in every parser that takes one — `parseBackupArgs` and `parseExportArgs` for `--out`, `parseObsidianFlags` for `--out` and `--interval` (#1227): `ghost backup --out intended.db --out actual.db` kept the last value, reported it and exited 0, so the artifact landed at a name the command line named twice, on the commands people chain or build from an inherited argument list. It is asked by COUNT and not by a value test because a value test cannot see `--out A --out=`: the second value is empty, so it reads the first as unset and answers a command line that named two destinations with one; the duplicate therefore precedes the empty check, so a named destination followed by an empty one is "given twice" naming both. The refusal names both values as `ghost context` does for `--cwd`, and both go through `memory.RefusalArg` (#839), because a destination path is caller text that reaches the terminal and the log. The default is the flag left off. Every stored value a report prints is rendered, not echoed: an id through `assemble.Token`, a name or path through `assemble.Label`, a stored stamp through `assemble.Label` too (`ghost history`'s `recorded_at` and `resolved_at`, which `ghost import` can carry verbatim: Label leaves a real stamp byte-identical where Token would quote its space, and escapes the C0/C1 controls `assemble.Data` passes through), and stored free text inside `«…»` data delimiters, so a newline inside a stored id or memory cannot start a line the reader takes for Ghost's own output — `cmd/ghost/print_sites_forge_test.go` holds the adversarial test for every print site, and `assemble.Data` is the one delimiter implementation the CLI reaches rather than a seventh copy. The post-upgrade integration-wiring check runs in the binary the run just installed (`ghost mcp wiring`), never in the upgrading process: the opencode-plugin and goose-package checks byte-compare installed wiring against the sources embedded in the image running them, so the process that began as the old release can only judge against the old release's sources — and a child that cannot be run is reported with the command to run by hand, because a check that silently does not run is the failure it exists to catch (#1215).
- `internal/adversarial/` — Test-only support: the shared hostile-input corpus (prompt injection, secret shapes, invisible-character tricks, path shapes) and the shared inert-content invariant the three untrusted parse surfaces assert against (#585). Nothing under `cmd/` may import it; it exists to be called from `_test.go` files. The invariant in one sentence: a value parsed from an untrusted surface is data — stored exactly as parsed, naming at most one path component inside the directory its surface owns, and never deciding the parse. `internal/mcpserver/adversarial_test.go` (#538) proves the same invariant one layer out, over the wire
- `internal/ai/` — CLI-harness providers (`CLIClient`/`OpenCodeClient`/`SourceProvider`) used by reflection + resolve + supersede — no Anthropic HTTP API client. Every child is spawned through the one `harnessCommand` funnel, which applies the `harnessEnv` allowlist and confines the working directory and temp variables to an invocation-owned scratch dir. Confinement is not conditional on that scratch dir: when it cannot be created the funnel confines the child in its own invocation-owned `MkdirTemp` tree instead, under the same rewrite, and when even that is impossible it returns an error and the call fails — no backend can run a child unconfined (#751). The backend is part of the policy, so each harness keeps only its own auth/config root (`CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, `CODEX_HOME`, the safe `GOOSE_*` set, `OPENCODE_API_KEY`). Two harnesses additionally get an isolated config tree because their plugin/MCP discovery would otherwise find Ghost's own registration: opencode gets a fresh home plus an `mcp: {}`/`plugin: []` config, and goose gets an empty invocation-owned `HOME` (its plugin root is home-relative by specification, so `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` alone cannot move it). The goose home carries the config rather than restating where it lives — the inherited `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` when set, otherwise reproducing each home-relative root goose may resolve (`.config/goose`, `Library/Application Support/goose`; Windows uses `%APPDATA%`, which the allowlist already passes through) — so goose's own per-platform resolution is untouched and authentication still works. `GOOSE_PATH_ROOT` is the exception: goose consults it before any home variable (plugins resolve to `$GOOSE_PATH_ROOT/.agents/plugins`), so it is repointed at a root inside the isolated tree with the real `config/` carried into it, and a relative value is left alone because goose ignores one. Where a symlink is refused (Windows without Developer Mode) the files are copied instead. If the home cannot be established, the call fails rather than running with the real home. Codex needs no tree — `--ignore-user-config` already covers it. OpenCode's tree also owns a DATA root (`XDG_DATA_HOME`), which is what keeps a lifecycle run out of the user's session list (#588): `opencode run` has no flag to skip saving a session, so the store's location is the only control, and OpenCode resolves its session database from that variable (verified on v2.0.15 with `opencode debug paths`). V2 needs `--standalone` for this to hold — without it `run` attaches to the user's shared background service, and the server holding the user's data dir is what creates the session. No `session delete` follows a run: the store sits in the invocation-owned directory whose deferred cleanup removes it, so a session cannot outlive its call, and a delete would cost a second OpenCode process per harness call (hundreds per lifecycle) to erase a row from a directory about to be deleted. The pre-isolation backlog is `ghost opencode cleanup-sessions`. A child gets a minimal environment AND NO TOOL SURFACE — see “No harness child gets a tool” below for the per-harness mechanism, the argv goldens, the codex probe, the residuals and the live tests. On an exit-0 opencode run, a top-level error event fails the call, and its message is rendered through the token guard. AN OPENCODE CALL CUT BY ITS OWN CONTEXT — a caller's deadline, `boundHarnessCall`'s per-call default, or a signal-driven cancellation — is a FAILURE CARRYING THAT CAUSE, never a declined-tool success: opencode writes the declined shape as it begins to shut down, so the salvage applies only to a run that exited on its own with a live context, and because `Wait` reports only the kill ("signal: killed") rather than the context error, the cause is attached to the failure explicitly (`TestOpenCodeClient_Run_DeadlineCutDeclinedShapeFails`, `TestOpenCodeClient_Run_DefaultBoundCutDeclinedShapeFails`).
- `internal/memory/` — SQLite CRUD, FTS5 search, vector search, time-decay scoring, and the retriever contract behind the assembler (`Store.Candidates`: one read snapshot, widened untrimmed rows with their scoring facts, leg/edge statuses). It is also the seam's second shape: a request with no query and a non-empty `Passive` list is a PASSIVE retrieval, one `[]SlicePolicy` per bucket, one policy per bucket BECAUSE the buckets genuinely disagree (order, over-fetch, two-pass selection, near-duplicate policy: the `_global` policy DROPS its losers, the project policy only reorders and leaves the drop to the cap), where the policy states the ORDER (`decay`, or `pinned_importance_updated` for `_global`), the over-fetch, the two-pass selection, the near-duplicate threshold and whether its losers are dropped — and where `SlicePolicy.Bucket` IS the project predicate, because `passiveKeyScanSQL` binds it as the `WHERE` clause and does not consult `CandidateRequest.Mode` at all — EXCEPT that `SlicePolicy.IncludeGlobal` widens that one predicate to the union `project_id = ? OR project_id = '_global'`, which is what a whole-project listing reads and what TWO buckets cannot express (capped at the caller's limit each, they admit twice the rows the caller asked for). The union is **PARENTHESISED** and that is load-bearing rather than decoration: `AND` binds tighter than `OR`, so an unbracketed form reads as `project_id = ? OR (… AND resolved_at IS NULL)` — every row of the requesting project escapes the resolved filter and the scope clause applies to the `_global` half alone (`TestCandidatesPassiveAMixedBucketFiltersResolvedAndScopeForBothProjects`). The flag defaults to FALSE, so the two session-start buckets stay disjoint and its golden does not move. Because a bucket name and a row's own project then stop being the same thing, `Candidate.FetchedBy` reports the policy that ADMITTED each row and the assembler's stage 9 keys its per-slice cap on that — a `_global` row inside a project bucket names no slice by its own project, so a cap keyed on the row leaves it unbounded and "at most N rows" becomes "at most N project rows, plus however many globals ranked nearby". A request that both mixes `_global` into one bucket and fetches it in another is refused at BOTH seams: the bucket names differ, so the repeated-bucket refusal cannot see it, and the rows would be admitted twice under two different caps (`TestCandidatesPassiveRejectsAPolicyThatBothMixesAndFetchesGlobal`, and the assembler's half, which resolves the flag across the WHOLE budget because a check that sets it as it walks refuses `[mixing, _global]` and serves `[_global, mixing]`). A caller that wants both runs two REQUESTS, which is what the project-context surface does. The decay order binds the caller's instant (`decayRankingSQLAt` derives the expression AND its bindings from one count of the constant's `julianday('now')` occurrences, round-tripped in a test) rather than `julianday('now')`, so the window, the Go-side decay score and the row ages the trace reports cannot straddle a second boundary. Passive rows carry the **-1 leg-rank sentinels**, not 0: rank 0 is a real first place, and a passive row reading 0 would clear the keyword floor arm on a rank nobody produced. Every bucket window is bounded, because this path runs at every session start and a passive window is read, ordered and demoted IN FULL — there is no leg to fuse and nothing to discard behind it — so a policy stating no `OverFetch` is refused (its window would be the whole store) and one above `maxPassiveOverFetch` (200) is refused rather than CLAMPED: a caller that states 5000 and is silently given 200 has been answered a different question. A policy naming no bucket, and two policies naming one bucket, are both refused — the first reads as an empty bucket and the second returns every row twice. A passive request cannot also be a HISTORICAL read: `AsOf` and `Passive` are mutually exclusive, checked in `Candidates` before the passive branch because the bench harness and the tests call it without going through the assembler. Routing it to the historical path would silently discard the policies and answer with `Fetch.Limit` while the caller believed its per-bucket over-fetches were in force; the two select over different things (live rows vs recorded versions) and there is no combination of the two here yet. A passive read is the first whole-`Memory` read this package makes through a NON-MIGRATING handle — every store Ghost opens itself migrates on the way in, and the session-start block it serves reads through `OpenReadDB`, which runs no DDL — so the fetch SELECTs `memoryColumnNames` with a column the store may lack replaced by a literal **of the shape `scanMemories` expects**: `retention` becomes `'' AS retention` and NOT NULL, because the scanner binds that one into a plain `string` and resolves `""` to `project` itself ("a row whose tier reads empty is a row a query did not select, not a fourth tier"), so a NULL there is a `Scan` error — "converting NULL to string is unsupported" — rather than a label; `scope` and `expires_at` take NULL precisely because they ARE bound as `sql.NullString` and NULL is the honest value for "no scope stated" and "no expiry claimed". The version is probed through the SNAPSHOT (`s.queryDB()`), never the pool: the read transaction already holds the only connection at `MaxOpenConns(1)`, so a PRAGMA on the pool is a deadlock rather than an error. An UNREADABLE version is treated as the FLOOR, not as "current" — a store whose version cannot be read is read without the columns that might not be there, which loses a label rather than failing the read, where returning an error would turn a transient PRAGMA failure into no session context at all. That leaves the evidence table (`memory_provenance`, v18) with a THREE-WAY decision rather than a boolean (`evidenceReadMode`), because substituting for a column that might exist is the safe direction while SKIPPING a read that might have succeeded is not: **read** (version read and at/above the floor), **report zero** (version read and below it, so the table was never created), and **read, tolerating ONLY a missing table** (version unreadable, so existence is unknown — skipping would report "no recorded evidence" as a fact about support, and a zero on a store that HAS the table would be a false claim that no memory is supported; anything but a missing table stays a real error). An unstated `DemotionThreshold` falls back to the store's configured one rather than to 0.0, which `nearDuplicatePenaltyRows` binds as `l.strength >= ?` — so 0.0 makes EVERY `related` edge a near-duplicate and a `DropDemotedLosers` bucket then DELETES a memory over a 0.1-similarity edge. `DemoteOnlyWhenOverCap` skips the near-duplicate demotion when the selected set fits entirely under the bucket's cap, because a demotion is a REORDER and on such a set it can only shuffle rows the answer shows in full — which is what both shipped readers do: the session block's project bucket states it, and the tool surface has no cap to be over. Near-duplicate protection is `Pinned || RetentionExempt(m)`, the same map every other caller passes: a pin-only map would penalise an unpinned `persistent` row on the losing end of a pair here and spare it everywhere else, and on a bucket that DROPS losers penalised means REMOVED from the block. `Store.CreateWithID` is the only writer that takes a primary key THE CALLER COMPUTED for a row Ghost is creating fresh: Create's write — same transaction, same history and evidence rows, same checks — plus a named key, and an empty one is refused rather than falling back to the column default. The other two writers that write a caller's id preserve an id Ghost already held rather than being handed a new one, and that is the whole difference: `ImportMemory` writes the id an artifact carries (so an unchanged store re-exports byte-identically, and id-only dedup means the destination's own copy is never overwritten), and `RestoreSnapshot` brings back rows under the id they had, so everything still pointing at them resolves again, and it does so **only within the project that took the snapshot**: the in-place UPDATE and the changedIDs read both match a snapshot row to a live memory only where `memories.project_id = memory_snapshots.project_id`, so a memory promoted to `_global` after the snapshot — a promotion moves the memory and its history but not its snapshot rows — is left to the scope that now owns it rather than rewritten back to its pre-promotion text. `CreateWithID` is the inverse — nothing Ghost holds names the row otherwise, which is exactly what a reproducible measurement needs, and the only caller is the benchmark: a store's ids are part of what a measurement of that store means (the ranking breaks tied fused scores by id, and re-sorts the window by a decay factor built from `created_at`), so `internal/bench` seeds a corpus under ids derived from its keys and one `created_at` per pass rather than from `hex(randomblob(16))` and `datetime('now')` (#708). The benchmark calls `CreateWithIDFromCorpus` rather than `CreateWithID`, which is the same write with the record bit OFF, and the distinction is the corpus rule and not tidiness: a dataset's `verified_at` is the DATASET's claim, so the column keeps it verbatim and no `verified` record is appended — a record's stamp is the store's clock by design, so one for a claim the dataset dated to a year earlier would assert a check that happened now. `CreateWithIDFromCorpus` keeps the credential guard, which `CreateFromCorpus` waives for downloaded public eval corpora; the benchmark's own fixtures are committed and a review has read them, so it gets the rule without giving up the guard. Nothing here is on `provider.MemoryStore` or in the MCP surface, and `Create` still mints every id a live save gets — production rows are unaffected. `migrateV20` REFUSES a database that already holds a `retrieval_record` that is not Ghost's — the same treatment `migrateV18` gives the evidence table, and the guard is the half of that step a copied DDL does not bring with it. `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` is a SILENT no-op against a same-named table, so without the refusal the step stamps v20 over a shape nothing here can write and the store opens and then fails every search's record with `no such column: verdicts` — a store that appears to work and is not, reached through a migration that reported success. It recognises the table by an IDENTITY subset (`project_id`, `source`, `verdicts`) rather than by its whole column set, per `evidenceTableIdentity`: the remedy it prints is `DROP TABLE retrieval_record`, so a check that demanded every column would issue a destructive instruction to a store whose table is merely a version ahead, and this one runs on EVERY open rather than only mid-migration. It runs at TWO sites, and the second is the interesting one: `migrateV20` calls it before its own create (the create's index statement is what fails first, with an error naming neither the table nor a remedy), and `OpenDB` calls it BEFORE `initSQL` and therefore before `backupBeforeMigrate`. `retrieval_record` (v20, #646) is one row per RETRIEVAL CALL — the grain is the call and not the (call, memory) pair, because the audit's denominator is calls and a table holding only the calls that admitted something has already dropped the ones worth auditing (the empty call above all). It holds ids, verdicts, a digest and the reason vocabulary, and NOTHING ELSE: there is no query column and no content column, and `RowVerdict` has no field either could be written into. `query_hash` is structural about it — `CHECK (query_hash = '' OR (length = 64 AND NOT GLOB '*[^0-9a-f]*'))`, so the column physically cannot hold a question however a future writer builds its statement. Growth is a `retrievalRecordRowsCap` (5000) window on rowid, oldest first, and the eviction DELETE rides in the SAME transaction as the append off the INSERT's own `RETURNING rowid` (nothing is probed in the common case) so the table cannot exceed its bound even transiently. The write is refused on a store a newer Ghost owns (#746) like every other write here, because a stale server's records are exactly the ones that lie — the store may since have gained a stage or a retention tier that build's verdict vocabulary cannot name. The parsed arm goes through `readableVerdicts`, because TWO DIFFERENT functions raise on a column they cannot read — `json_each` on a document that is not JSON, and the `->>` path accessor on the value `json_each` yields from one that is a JSON OBJECT rather than an array (measured on the SQLite 3.53.4 this build links: `json_each('{"id":"X"}')` walks the object happily; the `->>` on its value is what raises). But a substituted `'[]'` is an EMPTY array, so a row whose verdicts column is malformed matches nothing — and if that row names the memory, a parsed-only purge reports success over a name it left in the store. The second arm is **SCOPED to exactly those unreadable rows** (`COALESCE(verdictsUnreadable(...), 0)`), because an unscoped `OR` would also fuzzy-match every well-formed row: purging the id `A_1` would delete a record whose verdict names `A_1%done`, which the parsed arm — which knows the exact ids — correctly left alone. `verdictsUnreadable` is the single definition the substitution and the scope are both built from, and it is written to be TOTAL rather than to rely on `AND`/`OR` short-circuiting: `json_valid` never raises but `json_type` does, and an expression that read correctly inside a `CASE` raised as soon as it appeared in a `WHERE`, so the inner `json_type` is handed the column only once `json_valid` has accepted it. `verdicts` deliberately carries no `json_valid` CHECK: the readers must tolerate a document they cannot read, and a constraint refusing to store one would make that tolerance unreachable from Ghost's own writers. A reader returns such a row with NO verdicts rather than failing — a reader that errors on one bad row cannot report on the store at all, which is the state an operator is in precisely when they need the report. The `project_id` index is honest about being unread: no build queries it yet (`RetrievalRecords` takes a limit and no project, and the purge matches a memory id), and it is kept for the per-project read #646 asks for — one b-tree insert per search inside a write that already costs an INSERT, measured ~45us — because re-adding an index is itself a migration. `retrieval_record` (v20, #646) holds no query TEXT and no content: `query_hash` is an **HMAC-SHA256 under a per-install key**, because a bare digest of a short question is a fingerprint — the store's own memories are a dictionary of what a user would ask, and a hash confirms a guess as efficiently as it hides the original. The key is a sibling FILE (`retrieval-record.key`, 0600) in the data directory and **NOT in the database**, because `ghost backup` is a `VACUUM INTO` of `ghost.db` and a backup is the most ordinary thing to hand a colleague; `TestTheQueryKeyLivesOutsideTheDatabaseAndIsNotInABackup` asserts the key's bytes are absent from the database file. The key is **published atomically** (staged to a temp file, fsynced, then hard-linked into place, with an `O_EXCL` fallback where a filesystem has no links) so the real path is never a 0-byte file: creating with `O_EXCL` and writing afterwards leaves exactly that window, and a second process reading it would be refused over a file that is about to become valid. A key is resolved **at startup** (`Store.WarmQueryKey`, called from `mcpserver.New`) so the search path never pays a directory resolution, a read and a create — and a **failure is cached with a 30s backoff** rather than not cached at all — neither extreme is right: caching it forever makes one unlucky moment permanent, while never caching it re-runs the whole resolution (directory lookup, read, mkdir, rand, a temp file, an fsync, a link) on EVERY search while holding the cache mutex, for a store that simply cannot keep a key. The backoff makes that per-interval instead of per-search, and `WarmQueryKey` **bypasses** it so startup gets a real attempt rather than an inherited failure. That retry needs TWO distinguishable verdicts, which is why one `classifyQueryKey` reads every key file: a file too SHORT to be a key is what a half-finished write looks like, so it is retried, while a full-length file that is not hex is corruption or a human's edit and is refused on the first attempt. A key that cannot be read yields an EMPTY hash plus a log line and never an unsalted fallback — an empty `query_hash` means either "no query" (a session-start injection) or "no key", and the column never guesses. `RecordRetrieval` is **bounded three times** because it runs on EVERY retrieval path — the search tool and, since #850, the two passive surfaces — and each bound closes a wait the other two cannot. **One, the write itself**: a 250ms sub-deadline in `assemble` — the measured uncontended cost is ~45us and the fleet's contended p99 wait ~179ms, so it sits above what contention actually costs and far below the store's 5s busy_timeout. **Two, the store's own mutex**: `sync.RWMutex` is taken through a ctx-aware `TryLock` poll, because `Lock` ignores a context and would otherwise wait out another tool call's long transaction. **Three, another process's write lock**, which is the one no context can give: that wait is SQLite's busy handler, a sleep loop inside the driver's C call, so a 250ms deadline had no effect on it at all — a `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` spent 5s inside SQLite and, through `beginWrite`'s one bounded retry, **10s**, on a search that had already been answered. So the record write does not use `beginWrite`: `beginScopedWrite` pins one connection and asks it for a `busy_timeout` of 150ms as a **ceiling**, lowered to `remaining − 40ms` when that is positive and under it — the floor is kept when the remaining deadline is at or under the margin, or when there is none, so the timeout is a ceiling this path cannot always be talked below (deliberate: it loses a row rather than a caller's time) — and restores the value it READ before returning. The pin is load-bearing: `busy_timeout` is per-connection, and an unpinned `PRAGMA` could land on a connection the transaction does not use. The store-wide five seconds is untouched for every other write, and this path does not retry, because two attempts at 150ms is past the caller's budget and a record is the one write whose loss is recoverable. The second arm matches the id in the **VALUE position** — `instr(verdicts, '"id":' || json_quote(?))` — and not as a substring of the document, because the document is serialized JSON full of the schema's own words: a bare `instr(verdicts, ?)` against an id like `id`, `stage`, `true` or `validity` matched nearly every row, and `ImportMemory` accepts any non-empty id verbatim, so a short imported id turned `ghost history purge` into a delete of the whole audit trail. Every importer (`ImportProject`, `ImportMemory`, `ImportTask`, `ImportDecision`) takes `s.mu` above the presence check, then runs the id-shape check (`CheckImportedID`, `CheckImportedProjectID`, `CheckImportedProjectText`) BEFORE any field check or error message that names the id, and re-checks presence inside the `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction. `s.mu` is a per-Store lock and closes nothing across processes, so the in-transaction re-check is what makes the check-then-write atomic; `projectCollision` takes a `Queryer` and is called twice for the same reason. A record id is REFUSED rather than clamped, because a shortened key names a DIFFERENT ROW; a project id carries no length bound, because a project id is routinely a filesystem path that `ghost export` writes into the artifact. A failed project step cascades — its id is never recorded, so every memory, task and decision naming it is rejected as project-not-found. `TestThePresenceCheckIsUnderTheLockAndBeforeTheShapeCheck` and `TestNoImporterErrorAboveTheShapeCheckNamesTheID` pin the order textually, `TestAnIDAlreadyInTheStoreStaysASkip` pins idempotence across all four record types, and `TestTheShapeCheckStillPrecedesEveryMessageThatNamesTheID` pins the half that keeps a hostile id out of a refusal. **AN IMPORTED STAMP IS READ BY THE SAVE TOOL'S OWN RULE, WHICH LIVES HERE SO THERE IS ONE OF IT.** `NormalizeStampArg` (store.go) is the accepted forms, RFC 3339 or a bare date, the end-of-day reading of a bare `valid_until` and the refusal's sentence; `CheckConfidence` is the 0-to-1 rule with its NaN and ±Inf guard. `internal/mcpserver`'s `parseStampArg` and `parseConfidence` keep only the argument's JSON shape and delegate. `NormalizeImportedStamp` adds the stored layout and returns a value it reads as a copy of the raw text, never re-formatted, because that is what `ghost export` writes and because `time.Parse` accepts a fractional second the layout does not name: formatting would truncate `00:00:00.2`–`00:00:00.8`, a window `ParseStamp`, `CheckWindowOrder` and `readableStampSQL` all order, into a refused empty one quoting values the row does not hold (`TestImportMemoryKeepsAFractionalStoredStampRaw`). `ImportedValidity` normalizes the triple and runs `CheckWindowOrder` on the normalized instants: `CheckImportedMemory` calls it for the refusal, and `ImportMemory` calls it for the values it binds, so the two cannot read a stamp differently. An unreadable stamp, an inverted window and a confidence outside 0 to 1 are refusals, returned as `ValidityRefusalError` around the save tool's unchanged sentence so export can advise an in-place repair; a blank stamp is no claim and is stored as NULL. `TestImportMemoryRefusesWhatTheSaveToolRefuses` pins the refusals in both modes, `TestImportMemoryReadsABareDateValidUntilAsTheEndOfThatDay` and `TestImportMemoryNormalizesAnRFC3339Stamp` the readings, and `TestImportMemoryKeepsAStoredLayoutStampUnchanged` the byte-stable case. **AND AN EVIDENCE RECORD'S OWN CLAIMS ARE READ BY THE SAME RULE, BECAUSE THE SUPPORT SUMMARY COUNTS THEM.** `ImportedEvidence` (import_check.go) reads one evidence record's `observed_at` and `verified_at` through `NormalizeImportedStamp` and its `confidence` through `CheckConfidence`, so a hand-typed RFC 3339 moment is normalized into the stored layout and a stamp no accepted form reads — or a rating outside 0 to 1 — is refused by name, with the record's POSITION (`evidence[N].verified_at`) and never the value. `CheckImportedMemory` calls it for the refusal and `ImportMemory` for the values it binds, the same pairing as `ImportedValidity`, so the two cannot read a claim differently. The rule exists because `EvidenceCounts.Verified` counts any record carrying a verification stamp, whatever its kind: an unreadable stamp or a rating of 99 would report a memory as supported by verified observations on the strength of values the save path would never have written, and every reader treats an unreadable stamp as no claim at all. The refusal runs after the token guard, so a row that also holds a token-shaped field is reported as that rather than as a stamp to repair, and it is a `*EvidenceRefusalError` — a type apart from `*ValidityRefusalError` because the REPAIR differs: no tool edits an evidence row, so the export report cannot advise an in-place repair for it. `TestImportedEvidenceClaimsAreRefusedByName`, `TestImportedEvidenceReadsAnRFC3339StampAsTheSaveToolDoes`, `TestImportedEvidenceKeepsAReadableStampAndRating` and `TestSharedMalformedStampsAtTheEvidenceBoundary` pin it. `retrieval_audit` (v21, #646 part 2) is one row per (call, KEPT memory) saying what the agent did with what that call admitted, and it exists because a derived verdict has to be PERSISTED: the verdicts are a function of the transcript, and no part of Ghost can read a transcript again after the hook that wrote the sidecar has run (opencode and codex remove theirs on hook close). Nothing is backfilled, for the same reason `retrieval_record` is not, and it is sharper here — a verdict is a judgement ABOUT a transcript, so a synthesised row would report every pre-v21 call as `ignored`, a claim about sessions nobody watched, in the denominator of a report about precision. It holds ids, a fixed outcome vocabulary, a signal word and — since schema v22 — `content_hash`, the 64-character DIGEST of the text the verdict judged and never that text, and NOTHING ELSE: there is no transcript column, no query column and no content column, and the reader re-derives the memory's own tokens from the store rather than being handed them (`TestRecordRetrievalAuditsStoresNoText` rejects the table's own vocabulary in its bytes). Growth is a `retrievalAuditRowsCap` window on rowid, oldest first, and the eviction DELETE rides in the SAME transaction as the append off the INSERT's own `RETURNING rowid`, exactly as the record cap does and for the same reason: `recorded_at` is second-precision, so two rows written in one second have no defined order by it. `record_rowid` is `NOT NULL DEFAULT 0` and ZERO IS ACCEPTED, deliberately: a verdict about a session rather than about one call is a real thing to want to record, so it is filed unattributed rather than dropped. The replacement skips it — `DELETE ... WHERE record_rowid = ?` runs only for a named call, because "replace everything with rowid 0" would delete every unattributed verdict in the table — and so a re-audit of one call is idempotent rather than doubling its denominators. **THERE IS NO FOREIGN KEY, AND THE RULE THAT STANDS IN ITS PLACE**: `project_id` is deliberately not one, so the rule is that every path DELETING `retrieval_record` rows deletes the `retrieval_audit` rows whose `record_rowid` names those rowids, IN THE SAME TRANSACTION, keyed on the CALL that is going away and never on the memory that named it — a purge is asked to remove a NAME, but the row it removes is a call that judged many memories, and a delete by `memory_id` reaches one of that call's verdicts and not the rest. It has to be this exact rule because `retrieval_record` has no AUTOINCREMENT: a freed rowid is REUSED by the next call, so a verdict left behind is not merely dangling but silently re-attributed to a call that never admitted its memory, and `RetrievalAudits` counts it in a report about precision. **THE PAIRING IS TWO-SIDED, AND THE WRITE IS THE HALF THE DELETE CANNOT REACH**: a verdict is filed from a rowid its caller read BEFORE it began judging (`audit.Run` reads the recent calls, judges them, and only then writes), so a purge landing in that window deletes the call row, takes the verdicts filed so far, and frees a rowid the next `RecordRetrieval` takes — the sweep cannot help the write that follows it, because by then the rows it would have swept are the ones being written anew. So `RecordRetrievalAudits` files a verdict only against a call that KEPT its memory, and the guard checks the call's CONTENT rather than the rowid: `EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM retrieval_record WHERE rowid = ? AND <kept that memory>)`, because checking only that the rowid still EXISTS is not enough — the window is wide enough for the freed rowid to be RE-LET before the write lands (the purge removes the newest call, the next `RecordRetrieval` takes the table's maximum), and a verdict filed against the successor is the same wrong pair by another route. A verdict whose call did not keep its memory becomes a HOLE: one lost (call, memory) pair, against the alternative of a WRONG pair counted in the denominator and indistinguishable afterwards from a real one. What remains deliberately is a row whose pair is TRUE but whose call INSTANCE may be a successor that admitted the same memory — closing THAT needs `AUTOINCREMENT` on `retrieval_record`, i.e. a table rebuild and a migration, and it is not a claim about a memory a call never admitted. The guard is a `WHERE` on the INSERT rather than a branch above it, so the check runs in the same transaction as the write it guards — AND IT GATES THE REPLACE-DELETE TOO, which is the half that is easy to miss: the batch's `DELETE FROM retrieval_audit WHERE record_rowid = ?` used to run for every rowid up front and unconditionally, so a batch carrying a row the guard refuses still WIPED that rowid's existing verdicts on the way to refusing it. In the re-let window that destroys a legitimate call's evidence — a stale pass deletes the successor's stored verdict and is then refused its own row, so the table loses a pair nothing re-files and the successor's already-printed report claims a figure the table does not hold, which is the wrong number `Summary.Unfiled` exists to prevent reached through the DELETE instead of the INSERT. So a pass may replace a call's verdicts only once the guard has accepted a row for it: the claim is earned, not assumed, and the delete runs immediately after that insert with `AND rowid <> ?` so the row just written does not erase itself (which matters when a batch is PARTLY refused, the one shape where a refusal and an acceptance share a rowid). It cannot be narrowed to `(record_rowid, memory_id)` pairs instead: the call a batch may speak for is the one it read, and the rows already under that rowid may name memories this pass judged differently or never reached, and replacing them is the point. `? <= 0` keeps the accepted unattributed verdict, and `ErrNoRows` from a refused row is a drop rather than a failure — BUT IT IS RETURNED, as the refused ROWS rather than a count, because the caller has already counted every row it handed over into the figures it is about to print, so a refusal it cannot see leaves the report claiming a number the table does not hold and makes a branch that stored nothing indistinguishable from one that stored everything. The rows and not a count because the caller subtracts per source and per outcome as well as from the total: `audit.Run` reconciles them through `dropUnfiled` into `Summary.Unfiled`, takes each one out of `Verdicts`, `VerdictList` and its per-source bucket, and `Summary.String()` prints the loss BESIDE the figures ("N stored verdict(s) and not the M judged") rather than folding it into them — a report whose figures describe the table and state the difference. Absent when nothing was refused, because a line that always prints is a line an operator learns to skip. The predicate is `retrievalRecordKeepingMemory`, which is deliberately NOT the purge's `retrievalRecordNamingMemory`: the purge must MISS nothing and so keeps a fuzzy textual arm for rows whose verdicts JSON cannot be walked, while a report must claim nothing it cannot support, so an unreadable verdicts column reads as a call that kept nothing. `kept` is compared as `= 1` and not `= '1'`/`= 'true'`, because `->>` returns a JSON boolean as the INTEGER 1 (`typeof` 'integer') and the text forms compare an integer against text, match nothing, and silently reduce the guard to "never". What it costs per row is one primary-key seek on `retrieval_record` plus a walk of that ONE row's verdicts array — O(one call), not O(rows stored), so it needs no index on `record_rowid` and `TestRetrievalAuditsCarryOneIndex` is unaffected. `DeleteProject` deletes both tables by `project_id` and was correct from the start; `ghost history purge` collects the doomed rowids with `retrievalRecordNamingMemory()` — ONE function building the TWO statements' predicate, because two copies of that JSON could drift into removing the record rows while keeping their verdicts — and the cap's eviction takes them by the SAME rowid bound (`record_rowid > 0 AND record_rowid <= ?`, where `> 0` is load-bearing: 0 is the accepted "not attributable to a call" value and a bare `<= ?` would read it as inside the range and take every unattributed verdict in the table). The purge's sweep runs BEFORE the record delete because its predicate is a subquery over rows that are about to be deleted and can no longer be seen once they are, and it costs one scan of a table bounded at `retrievalAuditRowsCap` — the same scan `TestRetrievalAuditsCarryOneIndex` says the report's own read already costs, and the reason that test forbids an index on `record_rowid`. No trigger and no schema version: the pairing is a code invariant, and `TestAPurgeDoesNotLeaveAVerdictThatTheNextCallCanInherit`, `TestTheRecordCapTakesTheVerdictsWithIt`, `TestTheRecordCapPairsOnEveryEvictionNotOnlyTheFirst`, `TestEveryWriterThatDeletesRetrievalRecordsTakesTheirVerdicts`, `TestAVerdictForACallThatHasGoneAwayIsDroppedRatherThanInherited`, `TestAVerdictForACallThatHasBeenReplacedIsNotInheritedByItsSuccessor`, `TestAVerdictIsNotFiledAgainstACallThatConsideredItsMemoryAndDroppedIt`, `TestTheAuditCapStillBoundsTheTableWhenAPassDropsARow`, `TestTheAuditWriteReportsTheVerdictsItRefused`, `TestTheSummaryCountsOnlyTheVerdictsThatWereFiled`, `TestTheSummaryKeepsEveryFigureWhenNothingWasRefused`, `TestTheSummaryNamesTheRefusals` and `TestRunReconcilesWhatTheStoreRefused`, `TestALatePassCannotWipeTheSuccessorsVerdicts` and `TestAPartlyRefusedBatchStillKeepsTheRowsItFiled` hold each path and each way the write-side guard can be broken to it (#852). The export path excludes the table exactly as it excludes `retrieval_record`, since both are derived evidence about a machine's own sessions and neither survives an import onto another one. **THE AUDIT'S NEGATIVE VERDICTS REACH resolve AND reflect, AND `used`/`ignored` CANNOT (#648)**: `Store.UsefulnessByMemory(projectID)` returns `{contradicted, superseded_in_session}` counts per memory plus the latest of THOSE two verdicts' session and date, and it is the only evidence resolve and reflect are given. The two positive buckets are filtered in the reader's SQL and not at the call sites, because `used` means a call RETURNED the memory — true of everything in the result set — so in front of a classifier it is the #284 popularity loop: a memory that has merely been shown starts to look reliable because it is often enough in the context, and a maintainer told so keeps it. A reader returning four buckets and trusting two callers not to look at the other two is one careless caller away from a boost, so `TestUsefulnessByMemoryCountsTheNegativeBucketsAndNothingElse`, `TestAUsedOrIgnoredVerdictChangesNothingResolveSendsTheHarness`, `TestAUsedOrIgnoredVerdictChangesNothingThePromptSays` and the two `cmd/ghost` capture tests hold BYTE EQUALITY of what each pass sends, so a leak as a count, a ratio, a mention in the most-recent clause or a trailing byte all fail them. The most recent is therefore the latest NEGATIVE verdict and never the latest row — on any store this is for, the latest row is usually a `used` verdict, and ranking by it would report a popularity signal under an evidence label. The project scope is REQUIRED rather than defaulted, since another project's contradiction is not a claim about this one's corpus. **THE READER IS ALSO NARROWED TWICE MORE, AND BOTH NARROWINGS ERR TOWARD SILENCE**, because the failure this reader must not have is a WRONG annotation and not a missing one. (1) A verdict whose `degraded` column is set came off a PARTIAL transcript read, and the schema says outright that a reader which cannot see that caveat reads the row as a claim about the session — so `degraded <> ''` is filtered in SQL rather than qualified at render time, which keeps the byte-for-byte promise: a memory whose only negative verdicts are degraded renders NOTHING, not a weaker line that is still a line. `internal/audit` prints "N of those verdicts are degraded" for the same reason. (2) Every verdict is kept only while it still DESCRIBES the content the memory holds now, and since schema v22 that comparison is against `retrieval_audit.content_hash` — the hash of the text the auditor judged, stamped by `audit.Run` from `memory.ContentHash`. A verdict is a claim about the CONTENT a call admitted, and Ghost rewrites content IN PLACE under a stable id — `ReplaceNonManual`'s reuse branch and `ghost_memory_update` both UPDATE `content` on the existing row — while nothing deletes the audit rows for a rewritten memory (`retrieval_audit` has no foreign key to `memories`, and the only DELETE by `memory_id` is `PurgeMemoryHistory`'s). Without that rule the next pass annotates a claim written AFTER the contradiction it is quoting. The rule is per VERDICT, not per memory: a memory with one pre-rewrite and one post-rewrite verdict keeps the post-rewrite figure, and one whose text has since changed renders nothing at all — byte-for-byte the input the pass had before the audit existed. A row whose stored stamp is EMPTY — every verdict written before v22, and nothing is backfilled onto one — falls back to the NAMED LEGACY RULE, `recorded_at >= updated_at`, which is the pre-v22 rule verbatim: the old rows' judged text is not recorded anywhere, and a hash computed against TODAY's text would make a stale row look current. The hash arm, the withdrawal arm and the legacy arm are pinned separately by `TestAHashedVerdictSurvivesAMetadataOnlyEdit`, `TestAHashedVerdictIsWithdrawnWhenTheContentChanges` and `TestAVerdictWithNoHashFollowsTheLegacyStampRule`. **WHY THE RULE IS A HASH AND NOT A TIMESTAMP**: `updated_at` is not a content clock. `UpdateMemory` moves it on a METADATA-ONLY edit (store.go:5164, unconditionally, including the tags/importance/category case its own comment names at store.go:5145), and `ReplaceNonManual`'s `reusePreservesAge` branch (store.go:5451-5453, the UPDATE at store.go:6160-6172, reached through `ApplyReflection`) moves it after writing an IDENTICAL `content` back — `reusePreservesAge` compares content and category only, so any re-emission matching on those two bumps the stamp whether or not importance, tags or scope changed; only the narrower `reuseChangesNothing` case skips the write (#727) — unless the provenance stamp a consuming merge applies makes it one, which moves the stamp and files a version. So a retag, a re-weight or a `ghost_memory_update --verified` on a memory the audit contradicted used to WITHHOLD that memory's evidence from every later resolve and reflect pass — silently, with no error, no report line and no log. `resolve_kept_hash` was the near miss and cannot serve (`MarkResolveKept` stamps it only on a resolve KEEP, `SetResolved` and `MarkResolved` clear it, and no content edit writes it), so the stamp lives beside the verdict as its own column. The digest is `sha256(ContentHashVersion || NUL || content)` hex — of the TEXT alone, so a tag, importance, scope or verified edit leaves it standing while a rewrite moves it — and it is ONE implementation: `internal/resolve`'s KEEP-cache key delegates to `memory.ContentHash`, so the two can never drift apart. Bumping `ContentHashVersion` withdraws every stored verdict at once, which is deliberate and survivable for the same reason every other narrowing here is: it errs toward silence and toward re-asking, never toward a wrong annotation. Passive validity (#892): the passive fetch applies the validity window inside `passiveKeyScanSQL`, BEFORE the window closes, against the request's `Now` (`validityMatchesSQL`, one predicate shared by the fetch and the exclusion probe; held to `ValidityState` by `TestValiditySQLAgreesWithValidityState`, which includes fractional-second bounds and a fractional Now; the SQL compares at whole-second precision, so it is a SUPERSET of Go's verdict, may keep a row stage 2 then drops, and never drops one Go keeps), so a closed or not-yet-open row never spends the over-fetch. Assemble stage 2 stays the authority and must agree. The passive demotions run AFTER validity, never before it (#893): `selectPassive` splits the fetched window with `passiveEligible` (`ValidityState`, the rule stage 2 applies) before the two-pass selection and before `passiveDemote`, so a row outside its window fills no pool slot, outranks no live row, and is neither the winner nor the loser of a supersede or near-duplicate edge, which on the `_global` bucket would otherwise remove a live row while stage 2 dropped the expired one. Withheld rows trail the eligible ones undemoted so stage 2 still drops and reports them. `TestSelectPassiveDemotionsIgnoreRowsOutsideTheirWindow` calls `selectPassive` without the SQL predicate, so it holds the order independently of the fetch. Rows the predicate removed from an emptied bucket are counted into `CandidateSet.ValidityExcluded`, capped at `PassiveEligibleCountCap` the same way `PassiveEligibleCount` is (so a value equal to the cap is a lower bound on what stage 2 would withhold), which the assembler reports as `all_invalid` rather than `no_memories`. A passive `DropDemotedLosers` policy returns the rows it removed in `CandidateSet.DroppedLosers` (`DroppedLoser`: the candidate and its sorted winner ids), read from the same `nearDuplicatePenaltyRows` verdicts that decided the removal, never a second edge read. **A PIN IS A SLOT GUARANTEE ON EVERY PASSIVE SURFACE (#924):** `passiveRankKeyLess` leads the window order with `pinned DESC` (so a pinned row is inside the over-fetch window whatever it ranks) and `selectPassive` sets the pinned rows aside, runs the two-pass selection over the unpinned rows, puts the pinned rows at the head of the set the demotions run over, and arranges them back at the front afterwards. A pin is a SLOT and not a rank above the replacement: a pinned row with a live `supersedes` edge (`Candidate.SupersededBy`, read with `supersedePenaltyRows`' own edge rules) keeps its slot directly behind its superseder, and the superseder is reserved and fetched into the window so it holds a slot too; `assemble` renders `superseded_by=` on that line (`markSuperseded`, naming only superseders on the page). **AN EXPLICIT SUPERSEDE NEVER SHOWS LESS THAN THE LEXICAL GUESS IT SWITCHES OFF.** A live `supersedes` edge removes its pair from `lexicalReplacements`, and resolve never retires a `convention` or `preference` (the standing-category guardrail, unchanged), so for an unpinned row of those categories the edge was a rank demotion and nothing more and the lifecycle took the `[maybe replaced by a newer note]` marker away. `passiveSupersededBy` therefore names the superseders of EVERY eligible row, pinned or not, with `supersedePenaltyRows`' own edge rules (live edges only, so a withdrawn edge names nothing; a scope-conflicting edge names nothing) EXCEPT the persistent-retention exemption: a keep-forever target is never sunk, but the edge switches the guess off for it just the same and a marker withholds nothing, so it is NAMED, for the marker only (`TestAKeepForeverSupersededRowIsNamedButNotSunk`, `TestAKeepForeverSupersededConventionIsMarkedOnTheSessionStart`). `Candidate.SupersededBy` is set from that full map, while only its PINNED, non-exempt subset (`passivePinnedOnly`, exactly the map the pinned path read before) reaches `passiveReserve`/`passiveArrange`, and `passiveDemote` reads its own edges with the exemption, so naming an unpinned or a keep-forever row reserves no slot, sinks nothing and moves no row (`TestAnUnpinnedSupersededRowReservesNoSlot`, and the order half of the keep-forever test). `markSuperseded` keeps its on-page filter and has neither a pin gate nor a passive gate; a superseder that is not on the page is not named, like `markMaybeReplaced`, because a marker must point at a row the reader can see and the demotion has already sunk the row. A non-empty `Item.SupersededBy` renders `SupersededMarker` (`[superseded by a newer note]`) at the SAME prefix field as `MaybeReplacedMarker` (after `[resolved]`, ahead of it when a row carries both), worded as a recorded relation rather than a guess and held to the same field rule (eval/storyline's `carriesReplacedMarker` accepts either marker there and neither inside a tag) (`TestAnUnpinnedSupersededRowIsNamedByItsSuperseder`, `TestAWithdrawnOrScopeConflictingSupersedeNamesNothing`, `TestRunPassiveMarksAnUnpinnedRowWithItsSupersederOnThePage`, `TestRunPassiveDoesNotMarkARowWhoseSupersederIsOffThePage`, `TestAnUnpinnedSupersededConventionIsMarkedOnTheSessionStart`). A QUERY read names them too, for the marker only: the same live edge switches the guess off for its pair on a search, so `querySupersededBy` reads `supersedePenaltyRows` with no exemption over the edge scope (the ids the lexical guess is asked over) after the query demotion has run, and sets `Candidate.SupersededBy` without reordering, demoting or removing a row; a failed read is logged and names nothing (`TestAQuerySupersededRowIsNamedByItsSuperseder`, `TestAQueryReadWithNoLiveSupersedeNamesNothing`, `TestAQueryKeepForeverSupersededRowIsNamedButNotMoved`). **A LEXICAL REPLACEMENT IS NAMED, ORDERED AHEAD ON A PASSIVE READ, AND NEVER WITHHELD (#1405).** With no lifecycle between sessions a value-changing save is a second unlinked row (the save path refuses to fold it, `statesDifferentValue`), so `lexicalReplacements` (`lexical_replacement.go`) pairs the rows of one retrieval the save path's same-category bar would have folded but for the value, whatever the two rows' categories, AND that name the same thing: same project, EQUAL scope maps (stricter than `scopesConflict`, which lets a scoped and an unscoped row stand together; two environments' values are two settings), `mergeScore > 0` WHATEVER the two rows' categories (#1427: the save path's cross-category fold bar, Jaccard >= `upsertCrossCategoryThreshold`, was the read-side bar across categories, and a replacement that rewords around its values never clears it, so a decision replacing a fact was never paired; that bar exists to keep paraphrases of one rule from splitting across categories on a WRITE and is unchanged there, while here a category says how a note was filed, not what it is about, and the value tests below are what keep two different claims apart; the standing categories, `convention` and `preference`, are parties like any other, because resolve's exemption for them is a rule about a write and the guess writes and withholds nothing — `TestLexicalReplacementsPairsAReplacementSavedInAnotherCategory`, `TestLexicalReplacementsAcrossCategoriesKeepsEveryOtherRule`, `TestLexicalReplacementsPairsAStandingCategoryRow`, `TestRunPassiveOrdersARecategorisedReplacementFirstAndMarksTheOlderRow`, `TestRunQueryOrdersARecategorisedReplacementAheadOfTheRowItReplaced`; the one bar carries the same-category rule's known false pair across categories too: two live notes about one threshold family, one adding a second threshold beside the first — "demotion uses related links at strength >= 0.90 … configurable as linking.demotion_threshold" / "related-links are created at strength >= 0.70; demotion uses a higher 0.90 threshold" — share 0.90 and each holds a value the other lacks, so they pair, as they already did in one category, and the older one is marked, never withheld), `statesDifferentValue`, at least one value token IN COMMON (`sharesValue`, over the same `valueTokens`: the identifier the claim is about — the one test the save path does not make, because a refused fold costs nothing there while a false pair here reorders a block and labels a row; parallel template notes about different things, "auth-service runs on node-3" / "billing-service runs on node-7", clear the similarity bar and score HIGHER than a real reworded replacement, so no raised bar could separate them), and strictly ordered readable `created_at` stamps (a tie is no chronology, the supersede pass's `orient` rule; it also keeps the relation acyclic). A RETIREMENT is a pair too, in place of the value test: the NEWER row `retires` the older one (the save path's retirement rule above, over the same `valueFields` reading: a retirement phrase and a value token of the older row in one sentence, no plan word or negation there, and no retirement phrase in the older row), with every other condition here unchanged — project, equal scopes, the similarity bar, `subjectSwapped`, the stamps, resolved and validity, and the explicit-edge exclusion — so the retirement is ordered ahead and the retired row carries the SAME `[maybe replaced by a newer note]` marker, marked and never withheld, and the near-duplicate exemption keeps both rows of the pair on every reader. It is directional: an older retirement retires nothing newer (`TestLexicalReplacementsPairsARetirement`, `TestRunPassiveOrdersARetirementFirstAndMarksTheRetiredRow`, `TestRunPassiveDoesNotPairAPlannedRetirement`). A resolved row, a row outside its validity window, and a pair joined by a live `supersedes` or `contradicts` edge in either direction are never paired, because the explicit relation's handling wins; a cosine `related` edge does NOT exclude a pair, since the linker writes exactly those between such rows. The result rides `Candidate.MaybeReplacedBy`, newest first. `candidatesPassive` asks it per bucket run AFTER the edge read and skips it when that read failed (an explicit relation must be visible to overrule a guess). The retriever NAMES pairs and orders nothing; `passiveSelect` also reports the behavioural floor's picks as `Candidate.FloorReserved`, which the assembler's arrangement protects. A further rule beside the anchor: `subjectSwapped` (over `valueFields`, the ordered reading `valueTokens` is now built from) aligns the two texts on their longest common word subsequence and refuses a pair whose change is ONE plain content word on each side in the same place (not value-shaped, at least four letters, not a function word) — "auth-service"/"billing-service", "staging"/"reporting", and `make test`/`make lint`, decided as two steps rather than a changed one because a code-span word counts by its SHAPE and the text cannot say which; a replacement rewords around its values (an insertion, or a run of several words) and still pairs. The alignment is BOUNDED, because it runs on the session-start path over every pair of a bucket: the common word prefix and suffix are trimmed first, and a changed region over `maxAlignedWords` (64) words on either side refuses the pair unaligned — no marker, no move, the safe direction; the largest true region measured is 23 words, and 45 near-identical 8KB notes went from 10.2s to under 30ms (`TestLexicalReplacementsStaysCheapOnLongNearIdenticalNotes`, whose bar is the CHEAPEST CPU time over several rounds read through `getrusage` (`cpuClock`, on the model of `internal/secret`'s cost tests), not one wall-clock sample: a single wall sample of this millisecond-scale call flapped on a loaded shared runner (#1458) at 3% over a 250ms bar, while the quadratic alignment this bounds reads seconds of CPU whatever else the runner is doing). The query path names the pairs over the edge-read rows and does NOT reorder: a query's relevance order is what its verdict is decided on, and the display move is the assembler's, after that verdict. **A NEAR-DUPLICATE REMOVAL NEVER TAKES ONE ROW OF A LEXICAL REPLACEMENT PAIR OUT.** The linker writes a cosine `related` edge between exactly such rows, and one at or above the demotion threshold made the lower-ranked row the loser, so a query in the old wording removed the replacement before the pair could be named and answered with the stale claim alone. `dropNearDuplicates` (both passes, window and window-plus-tail) and `passiveDemote`'s `DropDemotedLosers` branch therefore read the pairs over the PRE-removal rows (`replacementPairsWithin`, or on the query path `replacementsWithin` through `replacementRemovalVetoes`, both of which call `lexicalReplacements` itself over the rows and the live `supersedes`/`contradicts` edges among them, so the removal and the marker cannot disagree about what a pair is and an explicit relation still wins) and drop every verdict whose two rows are a pair, WHICHEVER lost (`withoutReplacementPairs`, shaped like the `contradicts` veto; a read that fails stops the removal, as that one's does). Both rows stay, rather than the older one going, because a replacement is not a duplicate and the guess never withholds a row: a wrong guess that removed the older row would hide a live claim with nothing saying so. A restatement pair (no value changed) is still removed. The demotion-only readers are exempted the same way, so a cosine edge sinks neither row of a pair there either: `demoteNearDuplicates` (via `demoteResults`, the `fuseAndRank` paths behind `ghost_search_all` and plain `ghost bench`, which render `memory.Memory` with no assembler, so no marker and no move), `GetTopMemories` (which demotes before its cut, so a sunk replacement left the list), and `passiveDemote`'s REORDER branch on a project bucket over its cap (which pushed the newer row behind the cap, where `arrangeReplacements` brings it back only into an unreserved slot). Every reader judges a pair by ONE rule, `lexicalReplacements`: the query path's removal through `replacementRemovalVetoes` over its whole window (see the next rule), every other reader through ONE helper, `exemptReplacementPairs`, over `replacementPairsWithin`. `exemptReplacementPairs` hands the rule only the endpoints of the window's SAME-PROJECT verdicts (each condition of `lexicalReplacements` is a condition on the two rows of a pair, so the subset gives every verdict the answer the whole window would, and a pair across projects is never a replacement), which bounds `ghost_search_all`'s cross-project window: no verdict, no read; otherwise at most two rows per same-project verdict and one indexed edge read. A failed read leaves a REORDER as it was (no exemption: the reorder keeps both rows, so the safe direction is the ranking the store gave before) and still stops a REMOVAL. `DemotionPenalties` stays the raw edge verdict over ids, with no exemption, because an id list carries no text or stamps; no reader in the tree ranks by it alone (`TestDemoteNearDuplicatesDoesNotSinkALexicalReplacement`, `TestSearchHybridAllDoesNotSinkALexicalReplacement`, `TestGetTopMemoriesKeepsALexicalReplacementInTheCut`, `TestPassiveReorderDoesNotPushALexicalReplacementBehindTheCap`, with the restatement, explicit-edge, scope and cross-project negatives beside them in `replacement_demotion_test.go`; the removal readers' tests are `TestRunQueryKeepsAReplacementACosineEdgeCallsANearDuplicate`, `TestRunQueryKeepsTheOlderRowOfAPairACosineEdgeLinks`, `TestRunPassiveGlobalBucketKeepsBothRowsOfAReplacementPair`, `TestRunQueryStillDropsANearDuplicateThatIsNotAReplacement`). **THE QUERY PATH'S NEAR-DUPLICATE REMOVAL NEVER REMOVES THE LATER ROW IN FAVOUR OF THE EARLIER, NOR A ROW THAT REPLACES ANOTHER ROW OF THE WINDOW.** `nearDuplicatePenaltyRows` picks a loser by rank and protection, and a later restatement often ranks below the row it restates, which carries every fold's strengthening: a revert note linked as the original's `duplicate` (the restatement fold) was removed in favour of the original, which took the one row saying the change was undone out of the answer and left the reverted claim first and unmarked. `dropNearDuplicates` (both passes) therefore turns a verdict round when its loser's `created_at` (the stamp a fold never moves) is strictly later than its winner's (`earlierRowLoses`, run before the `contradicts` and replacement vetoes so they judge the row that would actually leave); a tie or an unreadable stamp keeps the rank verdict, and when turning it round would remove a protected row in favour of an unprotected one, or (on the window-plus-tail pass, `dropNearDuplicatesOver`) a window row in favour of a tail row, the RANK verdict stands, because the pair is still a near-duplicate and keeping both is a duplicate answer, and because the tail sits behind the window where the item budget can cut it, so that trade could leave neither row of the pair (`TestDropNearDuplicatesKeepsTheRankVerdictWhenTheEarlierRowIsProtected`, `TestQueryModeCollapseNeverTradesAWindowRowForACutTailRow`). A pair joined by a live `supersedes` or `contradicts` edge, in either direction, is never turned round: the explicit relation decides it, as it does for `lexicalReplacements`, because the rank the flip would override is the order AFTER the supersede demotion, and a superseded row can carry the later `created_at` (`orient` orders by `updated_at` first), so a flip there would remove the superseder and keep the retired row (`TestDropNearDuplicatesNeverFlipsASupersedesPair`). A veto of a turned-round verdict's loser (`contradicts`, or either replacement veto) falls back to the rank verdict unless its loser is vetoed too (`vetoLosers`; `TestDropNearDuplicatesFallsBackToTheRankVerdictWhenTheFlippedLoserIsContradicted`, `TestDropNearDuplicatesDropsAPairWhenBothVerdictsAreVetoed`). The survivor of a turned-round verdict STANDS IN for the row it displaced, which by rank would have been kept and is often the stronger match (both legs, higher Base): the retriever files it on `DroppedLoser.StandIns`, and leg consensus and the relevance cutoff (`internal/assemble`'s `standInEvidence`) admit a stand-in whenever they would admit a row it stands in for, which is why the retriever files each loser with the admission facts a kept row carries (`KeywordReserved` from the window's reservation, `Unembedded` from the same `unembeddedAmong` read as the rows; `TestCandidatesFilesALoserWithItsReservationAndEmbeddingState`, `TestQueryModeStandInForAnUnembeddedRowSurvivesLegConsensus`), followed through a chain of such removals, so a single-leg or low-Base stand-in cannot take the subject out of the answer with no loss recorded against it; each row's own evidence is reported unchanged (`TestQueryModeStandInSurvivesLegConsensus`, `TestLegConsensusJudgesAStandInByTheRowItDisplaced`, `TestCutoffJudgesAStandInByTheRowItDisplaced`). In a removal chain the id a loser is recorded as lost to can itself be a removed row (a lost to b, b to c); `near_duplicate_of` names the verdict's partner, not the final survivor. It also keeps a loser that is the lexical replacement of ANY row of the window, not only of its own winner (`withoutReplacingLosers`, through `replacementRemovalVetoes`, which reads `lexicalReplacements` over the whole pre-removal window with the direction kept), because removing it leaves the row it replaced in the answer unmarked. The relevance rank is unchanged: only which row leaves, and the id it is recorded as lost to, change. The reorder-only readers keep the rank verdict. **The passive `_global` drop (`passiveDemote`'s `DropDemotedLosers` branch) applies NEITHER rule:** it removes the rank loser and exempts only a verdict's two endpoints (`exemptReplacementPairs`), and `TestCandidatesPassiveANearDuplicateLoserIsDecidedOnThePostSupersedeOrder` and `TestSessionStartBlockGolden` pin that rank-decided removal, so the two removal surfaces differ here (`TestDropNearDuplicatesKeepsTheRevertNoteThatReplacesTheRevertedClaim`, `TestDropNearDuplicatesRemovesTheEarlierRowOfARestatement`, `TestDropNearDuplicatesKeepsALoserThatReplacesAThirdRow`, with the cluster, tied-stamp, protected-row and later-row-first controls beside them in `near_duplicate_chronology_test.go`). **THE PASSIVE `_global` DROP NEVER REMOVES A PROTECTED ROW.** `nearDuplicatePenaltyRows` flips a verdict only when exactly one side is protected (pinned or `persistent`), so a protected loser means BOTH rows are protected — two pinned copies, the pair a pinned fold writes. `passiveDemote`'s drop branch takes such a pair out of the verdicts before the contradicts and replacement reads: both rows stay, nothing reaches `DroppedLosers`, so no `near_duplicate` decision, `lostTo` or `near_duplicate_of` names a removal that did not happen, and the header counts no withheld row. A pair with one protected row is unchanged (the unprotected row is removed and attributed to the protected one). The filter is the drop branch's alone: the reorder-only readers (`passiveDemote`'s project branch, `demoteNearDuplicates`, `GetTopMemories`) keep the pair and still sink the lower-ranked pin, and the query path's removal (`dropNearDuplicatesOver`) is not a passive surface and keeps its rule (`TestTwoPinnedNearDuplicatesBothStayOnTheGlobalDropBucket`, `TestSessionStartShowsBothRowsOfAPinnedNearDuplicateGlobalPair`). Search sinks a pinned superseded row, so both agree. Validity, scope and resolved filters still apply to a pinned row. `CandidateSet.PinnedBeyond` reports pinned rows past a window that is all pinned. The `pinned DESC` half and the Go half each have a test that fails without it; the 30-row case fits every window and proves only the Go half on some surfaces. **A decision's companion memory is linked by id and retired with it.** `RecordDecision` writes `memories.source_ref = 'decision:<decision id>'` in the companion's INSERT; `SupersedeDecisionReport` finds the old decision's live companion by that link across the decision's project AND `_global` (never by text, so two decisions with identical text and an edited memory are safe), and stamps it resolved under the resolve guard with **no project bound** — promotion moves a memory to `_global` and leaves the link alone, so binding the decision's project would filter a promoted companion straight back out (the selection already scoped these ids by the link, the same reason `SetResolved` binds none). That widening is the link arm's alone: the composed-text fallback below stays project-scoped, because its match carries no decision identity and reaching `_global` there could retire another project's promoted memory. It appends exactly one history row per stamped memory in the same transaction ('supersede' naming the replacement's companion with a `manual` `supersedes` edge, or 'resolve' when no edge is written). It never stamps or links the replacement's own companion — and the replacement's companion is found the same way, so a promoted replacement still names the reason. A companion written before the link existed is matched by composed text only when exactly one decision and one unlinked `decision_log` memory carry it. The link is trusted for a memory with `category = 'decision'` OR source `decision_log` OR source `reflection`, whatever its category now (#1176, #1205): `ghost_memory_update` (which `ghost_decision_record` points the caller at) can recategorise a companion but cannot change its source, no writer tool sets `decision_log` or `reflection`, and a default import downgrades it — so a recategorised companion is still found, and the resolve guard then retires it or reports it in `Declined`, rather than the lookup skipping it and the supersede answering clean while the reversed decision stays live.  A reflect successor (a merge that names the companion among its sources, or the SQLite tier rewriting the companion in place) inherits the link and keeps or becomes `source='reflection'` with a category that may be the cluster head's rather than `decision`, so it must be found by the link arm; the composed-text fallback never sees it because it remains linked. A row carrying the link with neither the decision category nor that source (a forged or downgraded one) is not retired, but a LIVE one is returned in `DecisionRetirement.Declined` rather than filtered out of the lookup (#1283): the trust arms are applied in Go, the composed-text fallback still runs when no trusted row claims the link, and the read arm therefore holds even when the write did not — pinned by `TestSupersedeDecisionReportsALinkedRowTheTrustArmsReject`. The write arm makes the ordinary case a trusted row instead: `markReusedSuccessorSourceTx` stamps `source='reflection'` on a reused row whenever the emission absorbed a source this pass actually CONSUMES (#1283) — a row in the pass's delete set, so an id no row holds, a manual, builtin, pinned or retention-exempt row, and a concurrently edited row the pass keeps never relabel it — which is the shape a merge takes when the emission's text byte-matches an ordinary agent save and the deleted companion's `decision:<id>` link is carried onto it. The stamp is its own statement and runs for all three reuse branches, because the verbatim branch writes nothing at all (#727); it writes `source` and moves `updated_at` in the same statement — every writer of the `reflect` phase must move the live row's stamp in the statement that files its version, or the phase stops being a reliable witness for the `--fix-updated-at` anchor (`stampMovingPhases`, `history_compact.go`) — and since source is a column `memory_history` records, when it changes the row the pass adds the id to its reflected set and files the version through the ordinary history append like any other write; pinned by `TestSupersedeDecisionRetiresACompanionAbsorbedIntoAnAgentSave`, `TestAReusedRowAbsorbingAConsumedSourceRecordsAProvenanceVersion` and `TestAReuseIsNotRelabelledByANamedSourceItDoesNotConsume`. The `decision:` source_ref namespace is RESERVED: `UpsertWithOptions` and `UpdateMemoryWithOptions` refuse it (`checkReservedSourceRef`) so a caller cannot forge a link — refused wherever the lookup's TOKEN reader would take one, not only as a leading prefix: the guard calls `decisionRefsInSourceRef` itself, because a prefix-only guard beside a token reader let `runbook.md,decision:<id>` through as a claimed companion. `ImportMemory` deliberately does not (a restore keeps the link) and writes source_ref verbatim, so more than one memory can claim a decision: if exactly one claimant has source `decision_log` OR source `reflection` (an import downgrades the source) it is the companion and only it is retired; otherwise, and when two decisions share composed text, nothing is retired and the live claimants are returned in `DecisionRetirement.Ambiguous`, which `ghost_decision_record` reports. A companion the resolve guard refuses (pinned, retention-exempt, convention/preference) is left live and reported in `DecisionRetirement.Declined`. **A WRITER THAT STORES A `source_ref` NEVER DROPS A LINK THE ROW ALREADY CARRIES (#1732).** The reserved namespace stops a caller from FORGING a link; it did not stop a caller from DESTROYING one. `checkReservedSourceRef` reads the value the caller SUPPLIES, so an edit that wrote an ordinary reference REPLACED the `decision:<id>` the companion carried — and the decision was then left with no companion at all: the lookup's link arm stopped matching, the composed-text fallback needs an EMPTY `source_ref`, so `SupersedeDecisionReport` found nothing, the supersede answered a plain success with an empty `Retirement`, and the reversed decision's row kept a NULL `resolved_at` and went on ranking.
