Then it must be BOUND, and the two arms bind by DIFFERENT rules because the thing bound differs: an id is bound by COUNTING the words between it and the cue (`boundToCue`), and that count is bounded twice over: up to `cueGap = 1` word however it is spelled, or up to `cueFillerGap = 3` words **if every one of them is in the CLOSED SET `cueFillers`** (#860) — the determiners an agent uses for a memory (the, this, that, a, an), the nouns Ghost's own vocabulary is written in (memory, note, entry, advice), the one preposition that binds a cue to its object (in), and the two adverbs that interrupt a denial (now, also). One word was narrower than English: "Memory <id> is now obsolete", "disregard the memory <id>" and "ignore the advice in <id>" are all denials and each puts a determiner, a noun or a preposition between the cue and the id, so each came back `used`. A set rather than a distance, because the DISTANCE is not what was wrong: widening `cueGap` for arbitrary words would trade those misses for the false contradiction #858 exists to prevent, since the sentence that rule is FOR — "Per <id>, I'll ignore the formatting" — also reaches an id across two ordinary words. It is MEMBERSHIP and not proximity: every word between them must be in the set, so a gap of the allowance's width holding ONE word outside it is not an allowance at all ("ignore the formatter, <id>"), and a run of four is beyond the width ("ignore the memory note in <id>") — both pinned by `TestTheGapWidensOnlyAcrossAClosedSetOfWords`. What the set must not contain is a SUBJECT or a VERB, since those are what a sentence's own clause is made of ("per", "my", "formatter", "covers"), and the guard above survives only because no filler is one of them — note that "I'll" was never safe by being a subject but by tokenising to two words, so "Per <id>, I now ignore the formatting" is the same sentence with the contraction expanded and is pinned in the same place. The same set is transparent INSIDE a cue's own run of words, bounded at `cueRunFillers = 1` (`cueRun`), so "is now obsolete" is the cue "is obsolete" with an adverb in it and is a denial rather than no cue at all; a separate constant from `cueFillerGap` because it is a different question — what separates two words of ONE cue, not a cue and what it denies — and since a cue run is the whole of what makes a sentence a negation SEGMENT, the wider bound would be widening the set of sentences this package reads as denials. Every one of those is a membership test rather than a distance, so a cue still cannot match inside "xis wrongy" and "is very obsolete" is not a cue at all. **AND NEVER ACROSS A CLAUSE BOUNDARY** (#860), which is a SEPARATE rule and not a wider condition on the count, because the words either side of a boundary can be zero and the binding still has to die: `splitClauses` splits a sentence into its words AND the clause each word falls in (`clauseBoundary` — comma, semicolon, colon, and the ASCII, em and en dashes), and `boundToCue` requires the cue's first word and the id to be in the SAME clause, so the rule holds in BOTH directions — "Memory <id> is wrong" binds and "Memory <id>, that is wrong" does not. It cannot be fixed by one fewer filler, and the reason is the whole of it: the words in `cueFillers` are precisely the words an agent uses to CITE a memory, so an allowance that spans them also spans the punctuation of ordinary agreeing prose. "The build is stale, the memory <id> applies" denies the BUILD and cites a memory, and every word between the cue and the id is in the set — the citation and the filler run are the same words. Nor is it a consequence of the widening: "The CI is deprecated, also <id> covers lockfiles" and "Disregard this, <id> is accurate" each reach the id across a SINGLE arbitrary word, so the comma was already the only thing stopping them on a gap of one. The cost is stated rather than discovered: a denial phrased across a boundary is a contradiction that goes UNFILED, which is the direction `cueGap` already chose, and a dash boundary splits a hyphenated compound so "ignore the memory-entry <id>" no longer binds. Parentheses, quotes and an ellipsis are deliberately NOT boundaries — they bracket a clause rather than ending one, and the marking they do is the cue's own. The rule is the ID arm's alone and the asymmetry is the point rather than an omission: the fingerprints are bound by `boundPositions`' skip, which crosses every boundary, because "that is wrong: <the memory's wording>" is a denial followed by its RESTATEMENT and the words on the far side of the colon ARE the memory, whereas an id is only a NAME for the memory, which the words around it are free to introduce and walk away from (`TestACueIsNotBoundAcrossAClauseBoundary`, which asserts the verdicts, the clause ids themselves, and the two-sidedness at the mechanism; `cueRun` applies the same clause test to the cue's own MATCHED word as well as its fillers, or a run straddles a boundary and the span has no single clause to compare an id against). **AND THE TWO HOLES THIS DOES NOT COVER ARE PINNED, NOT ASSUMED CLOSED** (`TestWhatTheClauseBoundaryDoesNotCover`): the FINGERPRINT arm's skip still crosses every boundary, so `boundPositions` lands on the closed-set noun of a citation and a memory whose own wording contains that noun is still filed contradicted — "The build is stale, the memory <id> says <the memory's wording>" — which is pre-existing (verified at d0f53686 and at main), not introduced here, and closing it needs the skip to tell a COLON from a COMMA, which an int clause id cannot express and which the colon-restatement positives forbid: the boundary would have to become a boundary KIND rather than a boundary COUNT. And `;` is in `clauseBoundary` AND in `sentenceSplit`, and that overlap is a property of the CALLER rather than of the function: a `;` DOES reach `clauseBoundary` on every prose scan, because `splitWords` is `splitClauses` with the clause ids dropped and `addWords` calls it through `memoryIDs` over the same text — so the counter splits at the `;` and the split is then never read. What is unreachable is a `;` on the only path that READS the ids and can therefore change a verdict, because inside `AddProse` `segments` split at the `;` first; the exported `HasNegationCue` is the other reader and has no such guard, so it can be handed a multi-sentence string by a careless caller and is only as disciplined as that caller. It is kept so both boundary lists can be audited against each other. **AND NEVER BEFORE A CUE'S FIRST WORD**, which is the one place that tolerance could become a second way to widen `cueGap`: a filler AHEAD of a cue is part of the distance its object is measured over, so consuming it would move the span's leading edge onto the filler, and both span consumers then read from the wrong position — `boundToCue` would measure a one-word-shorter gap (filing "Per <id>, I now ignore the formatting" contradicted) and `insideCue` would report the filler as cue text, so `boundPositions` steps over it and a memory whose own distinctive word IS that filler ("the entry that records weekly releases", denied by "that entry is obsolete, we discussed weekly releases earlier") stops being contradicted at all. Those are opposite errors from one mechanism, and the second is the price of naming `memory`/`note`/`entry`/`advice` at all — the alternative lets the skip stop ON the word, a false contradiction on any memory sharing a cue-adjacent closed-set word — so the miss is the one taken, being the same honest direction `cueGap` already chose (`TestANAdjacentFillerIsStillApartOfTheDistance`, which asserts the SPANS as well as both verdicts because a verdict can be reached by the other arm), while the fingerprints are bound by SKIPPING to the nearest word on each side that could BE a token (`boundPositions` + `isDistinctive`), a skip that crosses punctuation without looking at it because a clause boundary is not a word, which is what makes "that is wrong: <the memory's wording>" a denial rather than a missed one; the id arm cannot use that skip because an id is 32 characters and would always survive the filter, reaching the cue across a subject clause — and the id arm carries the clause rule below instead, which is strictly tighter than the skip it refuses: the skip crosses EVERY boundary and the id arm crosses none. `boundToCue` counts a SLICE of the words STRICTLY between them, which is what makes both constants readable and is what stopped `cueGap` from meaning adjacency by accident: it was once a pair of position bounds (`c.start-cueGap-1`/`c.end+cueGap+1`) where the ±1 is the cue's own edge, and dropping that ±1 reads as "one word may separate them" while admitting none — silently dropping "ignore memory <id>", the construction the constant exists for, while the comment still claimed it (#858). A slice has no such edge to get wrong (`TestTheGapIsAWordGapAndNotAdjacency` holds the sentence rather than the formula, and its fixtures carry NO memory wording so the fingerprint arm cannot carry them by accident). What a cue binds to is also NEVER THE CUE'S OWN WORDS (`insideCue`): they are ordinary tokens — "ignore", "stale", "wrong", "superseded" all clear `minTokenLen` and none is a stopword — so recording them lets a memory whose wording happens to contain one satisfy the binding while the cue is about the changelog (`TestTheCuesOwnWordsAreNotWhatACueIsBoundTo`), and a sideward skip that stops on a SECOND cue in the same sentence binds the first one to it (`TestASidewardSkipStepsOverASecondCue`). The fingerprint arm therefore needs BOTH `clearsTokenBar` and one of the words clearing it to be bound to the cue (`deniesByWording`), so a sentence that denies a formatter and then quotes the memory verbatim is not a denial of the memory — the 3-token floor is retained on top of the binding, never replaced by it. Because the binding is what the comparison asks about, it is what the sidecar carries: the `neg` line gained a third field kind, a cue-bound fingerprint marked with `~` (`cueMark`), so the header is `v2` and `ReadSidecar` REFUSES a v1 file rather than reading it back with nothing bound — a v1 line parses cleanly and would silently unfind every contradiction in that turn. A token is `HMAC-SHA256(key, lower(word))[:8]` as sixteen lower-case hex, not a hash: an UNKEYED digest of a word is a pure function of the word, so a reader holding a word list reproduces the file exactly, and the word list in question is this project's own database — the comment that claimed a hash "cannot be read back as that word" was false. The key is the per-install key that already guards `query_hash`, read through `memory.ReadRetrievalKey` and NEVER created by either side, so a store that has never recorded a retrieval writes no sidecar and loses one turn's audit rather than putting a reversible file in the shared temp directory (`TestTheTokensAreKeyedRatherThanHashed`, `TestAnUnkeyedSignalsProducesNothingRatherThanUnkeyedTokens`, `TestTheHookAndTheChildMustSignWithTheSameKey`). There is no unkeyed fallback and no way to build one: `Hasher.distinctTokens` returns nothing for a keyless hasher (HMAC will happily hash under an empty key, so the zero value would otherwise produce a file of perfectly reversible tokens while looking keyed), a keyless `Signals` is `Empty` so the hook never even attempts a write, and `WriteSidecar` REFUSES one — the one way to get an unkeyed file on disk is to not be able to build one. The hasher is a PARAMETER to `ReadSidecar` rather than something the file carries, because a key written into the sidecar would be a key beside the hashes it protects, and a child under a different key from the hook would find no common token and file every kept memory as `ignored` — a confident report of total silence from a store that was fine. The REPORT (part 3, `report.go`) reads those rows back and prints figures an operator can act on, and its whole shape exists because a wrong number here looks exactly like a right and unflattering one. PER SOURCE AND NEVER POOLED is structural, not documented: `Report` has no project-wide `Used`/`Kept` field and no method that computes one, `Report.Pooled() *int` returns nil ALWAYS and says why, and `BuildReport` refuses an empty `ProjectID` so the store-wide scope cannot arrive at the type at all — a caller wanting one number over everything has a method to call and that method cannot answer. The one permitted combining direction is `MergeProjects`, which pools PROJECTS within a source and hands back a `Report` with an empty `ProjectID` so it cannot be printed as a per-project one; the arithmetic is SUM-of-counts and never an average of percentages, because a project with one verdict would count as much as one with three hundred. `Precision()` is `UsedByID / SCORED` (the cited-by-id count over scored verdicts, #930; wording-only `used` is in no percentage) and returns `(percent, ok)` so a caller MUST ask: a 0% over a denominator of nothing is a claim, and `Scored` rather than `Kept` is the denominator because dividing by admitted-but-unjudged memories would make precision DROP as an audit gets more complete, which is backwards — a fresh install with searches and no lifecycle run reports `no verdict recorded yet` instead. There are THREE shapes and only the third is a measurement: no rows (`Calls == 0`), calls with no verdict (`Precision` not ok), and figures; a line of zeroes would make all three read as a precision the report does not have. Every source in `KnownSources` (from `assemble.Source`, a copy would drift) is reported even when empty, because before #850's passive injections write their records `session_start` and `project_context` have no rows on ANY store and a report built from the rows present would be silent about them — and unrecognised sources found in rows are reported TOO, after the known ones, because a closed list must not be able to hide rows. The undetectable half of "missed" prints as NOT MEASURED in those words and is never 0: no heuristic can tell a fact the agent re-derived in-session from one it worked out. `--since` filters BOTH tables in Go over `recorded_at` (`RetrievalAudits` has no time filter), an unreadable stamp is KEPT rather than dropped, and the two tables are never JOINED by `record_rowid` (#852's orphan rowid reuse after a history purge would attribute a verdict to the wrong call; both carry `source` themselves). The two columns are TWO INSTANTS, not one — `retrieval_record` is stamped when the call happened and `retrieval_audit` when the detached run judged it, so one window over both is two populations and a call at 23:50 judged at 00:05 splits across any boundary. The verdict half is therefore intersected with the rowids of the calls the report COUNTS (an existence check, not an attribution join), and a verdict naming any other rowid is counted and named as **detached** rather than dropped in silence — the call cap evicts at 5000 rows while the verdict cap holds 50000, so verdicts outlive their calls and `Scored` would otherwise exceed `Kept` permanently. A verdict with `record_rowid = 0` names no call at all and is counted and named as **unattributed**, which is the only other way the numerator can outrun the denominator. Output is ids and counts: contradicted memories by id through `assemble.Token`, grouped under their source, with no content and no query text reachable because the rows hold none. A sidecar is only ever opened as a regular file: the sidecar sweep and `ReadSidecar` both go through `openSidecar`, which opens with `sidecarOpenFlags` (no symlink followed, a FIFO cannot block the open), fstats the descriptor and refuses anything that is not a regular file before a byte is read, and `ReadSidecar` reads the body from that same descriptor. The sidecar lives in the OS temp dir, which any local process can write, and the coordinator reads it while holding the per-project lifecycle lock, so a FIFO swapped in at the path must read as "no usable sidecar" (the audit phase is skipped with the error) and never wedge maintenance.
The REPORT (part 3, `report.go`) reads those rows back and prints figures an operator can act on, and its whole shape exists because a wrong number here looks exactly like a right and unflattering one. PER SOURCE AND NEVER POOLED is structural, not documented: `Report` has no project-wide `Used`/`Kept` field and no method that computes one, `Report.Pooled() *int` returns nil ALWAYS and says why, and `BuildReport` refuses an empty `ProjectID` so the store-wide scope cannot arrive at the type at all — a caller wanting one number over everything has a method to call and that method cannot answer. The ONE combining direction is `BuildStoreReport` (see the STORE-WIDE reading below), which pools PROJECTS within a source and hands back a `Report` with an empty `ProjectID` so it cannot be printed as a per-project one; the arithmetic is SUM-of-counts and never an average of percentages, because a project with one verdict would count as much as one with three hundred. There is deliberately NO exported summing function over `[]Report`: the per-project sum the store-wide reader is tested against is spelled out in the test, because a shipped combiner with no caller is how the next reader concludes it is the sanctioned way to pool. That is now ENFORCED rather than promised — the guard is `TestSourceReportHasNoExportedCombiner`, which parses `report.go` and rejects any exported `SourceReport` method with a pointer receiver (every read on that type has a value receiver, so a pointer receiver exists to mutate a per-source figure) or a `SourceReport` parameter, and any exported function taking two `SourceReport`s or a `[]SourceReport` — because a claim about an API cannot fail a build. `SourceReport.AddInto` shipped for one review round under a docstring calling itself "the ONE permitted direction" while adding the two sources it had not checked were the same, and the guard's failure message prints the docstring's first line because THE CLAIM WAS THE FAILURE. `Precision()` is `UsedByID / SCORED` (the cited-by-id count over scored verdicts, #930; wording-only `used` is in no percentage) and returns `(percent, ok)` so a caller MUST ask: a 0% over a denominator of nothing is a claim, and `Scored` rather than `Kept` is the denominator because dividing by admitted-but-unjudged memories would make precision DROP as an audit gets more complete, which is backwards — a fresh install with searches and no lifecycle run reports `no verdict recorded yet` instead. There are THREE shapes and only the third is a measurement: no rows (`Calls == 0`), calls with no verdict (`Precision` not ok), and figures; a line of zeroes would make all three read as a precision the report does not have. Every source in `KnownSources` (from `assemble.Source`, a copy would drift) is reported even when empty, because a project that has only ever searched records no `session_start` or `project_context` call at all and a report built from the rows present would be silent about them — and unrecognised sources found in rows are reported TOO, after the known ones, because a closed list must not be able to hide rows. The undetectable half of "missed" prints as NOT MEASURED in those words and is never 0: no heuristic can tell a fact the agent re-derived in-session from one it worked out. The DETECTABLE half is the per-source empty-call figure, and it is NAMED after the source that ran it — a search's renders as "kept nothing" and every other source's as "admitted nothing" (`keptNothingName`, shared by `report.go`'s two renderers and `run.go`'s lifecycle summary) — because both renderers are followed by a sentence saying that a search's is the detectable half of "missed", so one name for both put an injection's silence under a count of failed lookups, and an injection admitting nothing is what a healthy session start looks like. The COUNT is unchanged; the name is what says which question it answers. The count is per source and never pooled for the same reason every other figure here is not pooled. `--since` filters BOTH tables in Go over `recorded_at` (`RetrievalAudits` has no time filter), an unreadable stamp is KEPT rather than dropped, and the two tables are never JOINED by `record_rowid` (#852's orphan rowid reuse after a history purge would attribute a verdict to the wrong call; both carry `source` themselves). The two columns are TWO INSTANTS, not one — `retrieval_record` is stamped when the call happened and `retrieval_audit` when the detached run judged it, so one window over both is two populations and a call at 23:50 judged at 00:05 splits across any boundary. The verdict half is therefore intersected with the rowids of the calls the report COUNTS (an existence check, not an attribution join), and a verdict naming any other rowid is counted and named as **detached** rather than dropped in silence — the call cap evicts at 5000 rows while the verdict cap holds 50000, so verdicts outlive their calls and `Scored` would otherwise exceed `Kept` permanently. A verdict with `record_rowid = 0` names no call at all and is counted and named as **unattributed** — counted and named but in NO figure, because precision is a ratio over (call, memory) pairs and a verdict with no call has no pair to belong to; a numerator and a denominator from two populations under one name is how this report could report a precision its own numerator was not part of. So the printed precision is never a ratio of two populations, and `Scored` never exceeds `Kept`. Output is ids and counts: contradicted memories by id through `assemble.Token`, grouped under their source, with no content and no query text reachable because the rows hold none. **A STORED ID IS CARRIED WHOLE AND NEVER ROUND-TRIPPED THROUGH A DELIMITER**, in this report and in the store-wide reading below: a memory id is arbitrary stored text, not a 32-hex shape — `retrieval_audit.memory_id` carries no shape CHECK on purpose, `memref.FullIDLen` is a boundary between two honest sentences rather than a test, and `CheckImportedID` refuses only length, control characters, whitespace, a backtick and «», so `ghost import` puts a comma inside one routinely. The contradicted ids are therefore SELECTED AS ROWS (`SELECT DISTINCT source, memory_id`) rather than pasted into a `GROUP_CONCAT` column and cut apart again on a separator somebody believed absent from the data: the old shape printed a CORRECT count beside a list of ids naming rows that do not exist, which is the wrong-but-plausible output this package exists to refuse, and a count that is right is what let it survive. The listing pastes its attribution and its window from the SAME helpers the aggregate uses, so a memory is named only under a figure that counts it and only for a period the figures describe, and it runs only when the aggregate found a contradiction to name (`TestRetrievalSourceTotalsKeepsAContradictedIDHoldingAComma`, `TestRetrievalSourceTotalsNamesOnlyTheContradictedIDsItCounted`, `TestRetrievalSourceTotalsWindowsTheIDsItNames`). The STORE-WIDE reading of the same figures (`memory.RetrievalSourceTotals`, assembled by `audit.BuildStoreReport`, rendered by `ghost_health`) is ONE GROUP BY per table rather than `BuildReport` per project summed in Go, and the reason is cost, not taste: `OpenDB` pins the pool at one connection, so the per-project loop made a health check cost a number of full passes over both tables proportional to the number of checkouts on the machine — the tool an agent calls exactly when something feels wrong. Its output is bounded by the CLOSED vocabularies (sources × outcomes × degradation reasons), not by the table sizes, and `TestBuildStoreReportIsTheSumOfThePerProjectReports` asserts it equals the per-project reports field for field and line for line, because two implementations of one figure is how the degraded note's denominator came to disagree with the health block's. The block says its scope is STORE-WIDE on its face and names `ghost context --audit --project` beside it, because every other number in `ghost_health` is store-wide and a per-source line under them otherwise reads as a statement about the project the agent is working in.
**A VERDICT IS A CLAIM ABOUT ONE SESSION'S TEXT, SO A RUN JUDGES ONLY THAT SESSION'S CALLS, AND A CALL WITH NO SESSION IS LEFT UNJUDGED, NEVER GUESSED (#648).** `audit.Run` used to read the project's newest `CallWindow` calls whatever session made them and compare every kept memory with the one session the stop hook had just scanned; on a real store that filed 615 verdicts, all `used`, over calls dated four different days, and the usefulness readers handed them to resolve and reflect. Now `Signals` carries the hook payload's own `session_id` (`SetSessionID`, set in `scanAuditSignals`; sidecar header v3 then, a `session` line, now v5, older files refused by name) and `Run` reads `Store.RetrievalRecordsForSession`. The predicate is in the SQL so `LIMIT` applies after it (`TestRunFindsThisSessionsCallsBehindNewerOnesFromOthers`: filtered in Go, 50 newer calls from other sessions evict this session's), and an EMPTY session id reads NOTHING and sets `Summary.NoSession`, because `session_id = ''` is exactly what every legacy row and every host that cannot name its session holds, so matching "" to "" would judge the calls nobody can attribute (`TestRunWithNoSessionJudgesNothing`). The mirror case is stated too: when a scan WITH a session id matches no call, one bounded project-wide read counts the recent calls that carry no session id (`Summary.UnscopedCalls`) and the summary says so, so "the audit can never judge these calls" is not printed as "the agent used nothing". It states only what was observed, not why: an unscoped call can also come from `ghost context` or opencode's plugin beside a host that names its session, and it is never judged (`TestARunThatFindsNoSessionOnTheRecordsSaysSo`). Rows already in a store that were judged across sessions are unreliable and are NEVER deleted: `UsefulnessByMemory`'s audit branch requires `session_id <> ''` (flags are explicit acts and are not subject to it), and the reports (`BuildReport`, `BuildStoreReport`/`RetrievalSourceTotals`, the `ghost_health` block) count a verdict with no session as `Unscoped`, named and in no figure, the same shape as `Unattributed` and `Detached`. A CALL IS JUDGED ONLY AGAINST THE TEXT WRITTEN AFTER IT, AND ANYTHING THAT CANNOT BE PLACED CAN NEVER MAKE A MEMORY `used` (#648): every scanner stamps what it reads with the line's own instant (`Signals.SetAt` before each line or part; Claude and codex line `timestamp`, opencode part or message `time`, parsed by `parseStamp`/`firstStamp` in `internal/hostevent/audit.go`), `Signals` keeps the LATEST instant per fingerprint and id (a call asks "does this text occur at or after me", which is exactly "its latest occurrence is"), and the sidecar is header v5 (`save|id <value> <unix ms>`, `turn <unix ms> <fp> …`, `neg <unix ms> <fields>`), with v4, v3, v2 and v1 refused by name — a v4 file's `prose` lines pool the whole session and cannot say which words arrived together, which is exactly the union this build refuses to judge — and a `turn` line with no instant refused rather than defaulted (a `prose` line in a v5 file is an unknown field and an error, not a guess). `Run` takes each record's `recorded_at` as the END of its second (`recordedInstant`: the store stamps to the second, a scanned line to the millisecond, so a same-second line may predate the call, the call's own query for one) and compares against `Signals.Since(cutoff)`, so all four arms (id, token, save restatement, negation) see only later text (`TestEveryArmJudgesOnlyTextAfterTheCall`, `TestRunDoesNotJudgeCallsBeforeTheSessionBegins`). Unplaced is never evidence: an entry with instant 0 is dropped by `Since`; a scan with no placed entry judges nothing (`Summary.NoOrder`); a call whose `recorded_at` is empty or unreadable is skipped (`Summary.UnorderedCalls`); a scanner that carried unplaced lines marks the scan degraded (`TestTextWithNoInstantIsNeverEvidence`, `TestACallWithNoInstantIsLeftUnjudged`, `TestEveryAuditScannerStampsWhenTheAgentWrote`). THE TOKEN ARM IS CLEARED BY THE WORDS OF ONE TURN, NEVER BY THE UNION OF THE SESSION (#932): `Signals` groups the agent's fingerprints by the instant `SetAt` stamped (one `turn` per instant, de-duplicated within it) and `matches` asks whether ANY placed turn clears the bar, so a restatement is judged where it happened. The union let domain vocabulary spread over forty turns clear a bar no single turn came near — the operator was told a memory had been used when no instant of the session was about it — and the negation arm has been local since it existed, for the same reason. `Since` keeps each turn whole and drops only turns written before the cutoff, so no turn ever straddles a call, and prose is still pooled for `Empty`, `Unplaced` and `Ordered`, which count entries rather than judge wording (`TestTheTokenArmNeedsTheWordsInOneTurn`, `TestTheSidecarCarriesEveryTurn`, `TestUnplacedTextIsNeverATurnThatCounts`, `TestEveryAuditScannerStampsWhenTheAgentWrote/*/turns`). `SweepSidecars` matches the header prefix of ANY version, so a stale file an older build wrote (unreadable by every later one) is removed instead of left behind (`TestSweepSidecarsDeletesOlderVersionSidecars`). `SweepSidecars` opens only a REGULAR file: the sweep runs over the OS temp dir, which every local process can write to, so a FIFO named like a sidecar blocked its open and held the lifecycle coordinator before its first phase, and a symlink was followed to whatever it named. The directory read's lstat skips anything that is not a regular file, a symlink included even when it names a real sidecar, and such an entry is never removed; `readSidecarHead` then opens with `sidecarOpenFlags` (`O_NOFOLLOW|O_NONBLOCK` on unix, plain read-only on Windows, which has neither) and refuses a descriptor whose fstat is not a regular file, which closes the swap between the lstat and the open, and it reads only the header (`TestSweepSidecarsLeavesAFIFOUnopened`, `TestSweepSidecarsLeavesASymlinkToAFIFOUnopened`, `TestSweepSidecarsLeavesASymlinkToASidecarInPlace`, `TestReadSidecarHeadRefusesWhatIsNotARegularFile`). KNOWN RESIDUAL, measured and not fixed: a single turn that happens to repeat half of an own-domain memory in words that are not common to the session still clears the bar for a call placed early, so own-domain memories no turn restates are still judged `used` in 3 of 11 cases for a call before turn 0 of 40 and 3 of 15 for a call before turn 20 (they were 10 of 11 and 10 of 15 before the half-bar, the outlier line and the generic-word rule below; scenario-start `used/token` precision 0.286 → 0.571, recall 1.000 unchanged), and the figure is the one `ghost bench --audit` pins in `internal/bench/testdata/audit_report.golden` (the recall side, every labelled cite and restatement, is scored there too — cited ids 3 of 3, restatements 4 of 4 — as is the line this change exists for: the four memories spread across turns went from judged `used` 4 of 4 to 0 of 4; `TestSameDomainResidualWithTheOrderInPlace` still logs the older fixture's counts, 7 of 20 against 10 of 20 at session start with the tool bodies excluded).

**THE INVISIBLE FORMAT CLASS IS ONE PREDICATE, AND IT LIVES IN THE TOKEN-GUARD PACKAGE (#1663, #1689, #1781).** `memory.IsFormatRune` answers "is this rune an invisible Unicode FORMAT character?" for every caller that asks it: the renderers that print stored text (`assemble.Data`, `assemble.Label`, `assemble.TagsLabel`, `assemble.PreviewLine`), the tag write boundary, the scope key/value guard and the blank-text check. It is the WHOLE of category Cf rather than a list of characters, because a list holds only what somebody thought of — the bidirectional controls were escaped first and the rest of the class (U+200B ZERO WIDTH SPACE, U+200C and U+200D, U+2060 WORD JOINER, U+FEFF ZERO WIDTH NO-BREAK SPACE among them) was not, so a tag of `gh` + U+200B + `ost` printed as `ghost` — and it is a CATEGORY test rather than a hand-written set, so a character a later Unicode version adds is covered without a change here. It now LIVES in `internal/secret` rather than here, and this package's function DELEGATES to it, because the dependency runs the other way: `internal/memory` imports the token guard to reach the detector, and the detector has to see THROUGH the class to recognise a token one of its characters hides (#1781) — so the rule cannot live above the package that needs it. A predicate that answers for the callers above it AND for the detector below it is one implementation and not two: the renderers that escape the class, the write boundaries that refuse it and the detector that sees through it share the one predicate rather than each spelling it. The set is pinned EXACTLY by `TestTheFormatRuneClassIsExactlyUnicodeCategoryCf`, exhaustively over every code point, because a table of hostile examples passes whenever the class is widened by a character nobody listed.

**A CLOSED VOCABULARY IS DECLARED ONCE, AT ITS COLUMN, AND EVERY READER OF THAT COLUMN MATCHES THE SPELLING ITS WRITER USES.** `retrieval_audit.outcome`'s four buckets are declared in `internal/memory` as `VerdictOutcomeUsed`/`Ignored`/`Superseded`/`Contradicted` — AT THE COLUMN, because `internal/audit` imports `internal/memory` (its report reads a `memory.Store`) and not the other way round, so that is the only direction two packages can share a declaration in. `internal/audit` derives its typed `Outcome` constants from them and lists them in `AllOutcomes`; `RetrievalSourceTotals` switches on the same constants, and `retrievalContradictedIDs` BOUNDS `contradicted` as a query argument rather than pasting it into the statement (a pasted spelling there disagrees silently: no rows come back, so the report names no contradicted memory while still counting the verdicts). The bug this rule exists for cost a release: the store-wide aggregate's switch matched `case "superseded"` while the comparison stored `superseded_in_session`, so every superseded verdict was counted in `Scored` and in NO bucket — `ghost_health` printed "0 superseded in session" beside a precision whose denominator included them, the four buckets stopped summing to `Scored`, and `BuildStoreReport` stopped equalling the sum of the per-project reports. A spelling is not tidiness here: NO test could have caught it while every fixture filed its superseded verdicts by hand around the reader, because a hand-typed bucket in a fixture is itself a second spelling. The fixtures now reach the bucket through `VerdictOutcomeSuperseded` (`TestRetrievalSourceTotalsCountsEveryShapeTheTablesHold` carries a verdict in EVERY bucket), and two guards hold the list itself. `TestTheStoreWideAggregateMapsEveryOutcomeTheComparerCanStore` drives the store-wide aggregate with each value in `AllOutcomes` and requires it in exactly one bucket, with the four buckets summing to `Scored` and the contradicted case naming its id — Go cannot enumerate constants, so a new bucket added without an arm otherwise fails in a store. `TestTheOutcomeVocabularyIsOneListSpelledOnce` parses `compare.go` and holds `AllOutcomes` and `memory.AllVerdictOutcomes` to it in BOTH directions and in ORDER (a set comparison passes a list that dropped a bucket the const block still declares), and it fails if a constant in `compare.go` carries a string literal instead of NAMING the `internal/memory` constant — so the derivation the comments claim is checkable, and re-spelling a bucket here is a test failure even when the re-spelling happens to be right. The tolerance is ONE-DIRECTIONAL and deliberate: an outcome this build does NOT know — a stranger store's row, or a bucket added by a later Ghost — is counted in `Scored` and in no bucket, because the row IS a verdict and dropping it would report that store's precision as better than it is; a gap over OUR OWN vocabulary is a bug, not a tolerance, and the two are told apart by which list the value came from.
**A FLAG IS APPEND-ONLY EVIDENCE, NOT AN ACTION, AND ITS REASON NEVER LEAVES THE STORE.** `memory_flags` (schema v23) holds one agent's objection to ONE memory: `kind` is a closed two-word vocabulary (`wrong`/`stale`, CHECKed at its column the way `retrieval_audit.outcome` is), `content_hash` stamps the text the objection was about so a rewrite withdraws it while a retag leaves it standing, and the write refuses an unknown id, a memory in another project, a third kind, an empty or over-long reason (500 RUNES — refused, never clamped, since a truncated reason is a claim nobody made) and anything `rejectSecret` refuses. Nothing here resolves, deletes, demotes or re-ranks: the flag reaches `resolve` and `reflect` ONLY as a `Flagged` count in `UsefulnessEvidence`'s `Line()`, the reason column is read by no prompt, no ranking and no evidence line, and `ghost_memory_flag`'s answer carries a count and an id rather than echoing the reason it was given. A flag's `session_id` is the TRANSPORT's only (`provenanceFor`), so under Claude Code — where the stdio transport reports none — a flag records no session while the `retrieval_record` beside it records the host's id from `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID`: a retrieval record needs the id the stop hook's payload also carries so the audit can match the call to the scan that judged it (#922), and provenance deliberately does not borrow it — a provenance row claims what the transport said and nothing inferred. The count joins `resolve.KeepStamp`, so a flag filed after a KEEP re-asks that cached KEEP exactly once. A memory's flags go with the memory — purge, retention eviction, consolidation and project delete, in the same transaction, the way `retrieval_audit` rows go — and the writer that MOVES a memory between projects reassigns the flag's `project_id` with it: `MergeProject` by `project_id` (it moves a whole corpus), `PromoteToGlobal` by `memory_id` (#1046), because the per-project evidence read asks by that column and a flag left naming the project the memory just left is one no reader reaches. The project delete follows the MEMORY rather than the flag's own `project_id`, so it cannot take the objection of a memory that has moved elsewhere.

**A RESTORE PICKS ITS SNAPSHOT INSIDE ITS WRITE TRANSACTION (#1190).** `RestoreSnapshot` reads the latest `snapshot_id` through the `restore-snapshot` transaction, after `beginWrite` took the lock, and every later statement is keyed on that id. Read on the pool before `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, a reflection another process committed while the restore waited for the lock was serialised before the restore yet skipped by it, so the older snapshot was applied and a memory the newer reflection consumed stayed deleted under a successful exit. `s.mu` cannot close this; it serialises one process only. Guarded by `TestRestorePicksTheSnapshotInsideItsWriteTransaction`, which holds the lock from a second handle while the restore waits.

**A RESTORE THAT REVERTS A ROW'S CONTENT INVALIDATES THAT ROW'S EMBEDDING AND LINK SCAN WITH IT (#1085).** `RestoreSnapshot` rewrites a reverted row's text in place and deletes the `memory_embeddings` and `link_scans` rows of every row whose CONTENT it changes, in the same transaction, the way `UpdateMemoryWithOptions` invalidates both on a content edit. The predicate is the CONTENT difference alone, not the four-column difference the UPDATE is decided on: a metadata-only revert (category, importance, source) restores no text, and a row whose content the restore leaves alone keeps its vector and its scan slot, on the same terms a tags-only edit does. The delete runs BEFORE the UPDATE and while the live row still differs from the snapshot, because that difference is the predicate — once the snapshot's content is written the row equals it and nothing matches. The rule it enforces is the store's byte-identity one: an embedding is valid only while the text is byte-identical to what it was computed from, and the scan slot records which neighbours were compared in that vector's space, so a vector kept across a revert scores the row for the text it reverted away and claims neighbours of text that is gone. `UnembeddedMemoryIDs` cannot repair such a row — it still HAS a vector under the configured identity, so it is not unembedded — so the delete is the only thing that re-queues the re-embed and the re-link, which is the whole of what a revert of content has to mean beyond an FTS refresh.

**A RESTORE PUTS BACK THE RETENTION TIER ITS SNAPSHOT RECORDED, AND REPORTS THE ROWS WHOSE SNAPSHOT COULD NOT (#1341).** `memory_snapshots.retention` and its `retention_captured` marker (schema v26, `migrateV26`) are `scope`/`scope_captured` (v14) one version later: `snapshotReplaceableTx` copies the tier and stamps the marker 1, and a snapshot written before the column (or a hand backfill) reads 0, so a restore never claims a tier it never read. Both restore paths put a recorded tier back even over a tier a later save or fold raised: the snapshot is the undo history and the raise is part of what it undoes, the same way a restore reverts content, scope and importance rather than merging with the state the row holds now. The in-place `UPDATE` takes it when the marker is 1 and leaves the live row's own tier otherwise, because a NULL there would read as "the memory was a project memory", which is a claim the snapshot did not make — the same rule `scope` already follows. The `INSERT … SELECT` (the row the replace deleted) has no live tier to leave alone, so a row an uncaptured snapshot cannot speak about comes back at the column DEFAULT (`project`, no expiry, kept longer and never pruned), which is the one honest answer for a row arriving from nowhere: inventing `session` would schedule a memory for deletion on a clock nobody set, the direction the portable importer refuses too. The expiry is RE-DERIVED through `sessionExpiry`, never copied — the snapshot stores no expiry, and that function is its only source everywhere else — with one exception, which is the one that keeps a restore idempotent: a row whose tier never moved (already session, with an expiry) keeps the expiry it holds, so a second restore changes nothing. A tier that actually comes back as session on a row carrying none gets a fresh `now+SessionTTL` and is a prune candidate like any other; a durable tier comes back with none. `RestoreSnapshot` returns a `RestoreResult` carrying `UncapturedRetention`, the count of the chosen snapshot's rows whose marker is 0, and the CLI prints a note when it is above zero: an undo the snapshot cannot complete is reported rather than presented as a full revert. The restore's history set still gates on the four recorded columns, so a revert that changes only the tier appends no `memory_history` row, for the reason the change log records no tier at all. `persistent` rows stay outside the statement (`retentionExemptSQL`) and are untouched, as before. Guarded by `TestARestorePutsTheTierAndItsExpiryBack` (the deleted-row re-insert, the in-place raise, and the raise `raiseReusedRetentionTx` makes inside the replace), `TestARestoreLeavesTheTierAloneForALegacySnapshot`, `TestARestoreReportsTheLegacySnapshotOnTheInsertPath`, `TestMigrateV26MatchesTheFreshDatabaseSchema` and `TestOpenDBUpgradesAV25DatabaseAndItsOldSnapshotReadsUncaptured`.

- `internal/mcpserver/` — MCP server: `optScope`'s refusal of a non-string scope value names the entry by its position in sorted key order and never quotes the key (a key is caller text, and quoting it echoed a token-shaped or 200 KB string back, the surface the scope token guard closed). 23 tools + 4 resources + 2 prompts (`recall_project`, `record_decision`). The project-context MEMORY ROWS are `assemble.Run`'s (`projectContextBudget` + `assembleProjectContext`, `project_context.go`): the tool, the `ghost://project/{id}/context` resource and the `recall_project` prompt each get one passive assembly at their own cap, and the resource and prompt now fit that assembled block to the same 16,000-byte response budget through `capResponseParts` (the tool already did), and the resource's `## Global` section is a SECOND REQUEST rather than a second slice, because a project and `_global` that overlap are one cap under one section and the `seen` filter between them is a SECTION BOUNDARY — it runs after the cap, and moving it into the store would admit 15 new rows where the shipped code admitted 15 rows of which some were repeats (`SlicePolicy.ExcludeSeen` stays unread for that reason). The empty block is gated on the VERDICT, and the gate is the interesting part: the census ("nothing has been saved for it") is true only of an EMPTY window (`ReasonNoMemories`), because a `LIMIT limit*2` read never counted the store, while any reason meaning rows were FOUND and withheld renders the assembler's own abstention sentence and points at `ghost_memories_list` — without that a project whose every memory has retired is reported as never having been saved for. AN UNRESOLVED project name is the same class of problem one step earlier: `ResolveProject` answers an unknown name with `""`, and the old loader was handed it and read `project_id = '' OR project_id = '_global'`, so the block MISCELLED the global rows under a `## Memories` heading for a project that does not exist and fell through to the census only when the store held no globals. The assembler refuses a project context with no project, so all three surfaces now SKIP the project-keyed reads and render the `## Global (applies to all projects)` section alone — it does not depend on a project, and the base ref delivered those rows (under the wrong heading, which is the mislabelling being removed, but delivered) — then APPEND `projectNotRegistered(asked)`. The APPEND rather than a replacement is load-bearing: returning the sentence alone drops the cross-project preferences on a project's first session, which is exactly when they matter and exactly what the server's own SessionStart instructions tell the agent to look for. The `as_of` branch is the ONE case that REFUSES instead, and the asymmetry is the point rather than an inconsistency: a caller who asked for an instant must not be handed the present, and there is no set to show, so the answer names the instant instead — and the store's `asOfCapableStore` assertion is made BEFORE that case, or an unregistered name would report success on a store that cannot read history at all. `asked` is the RAW name, never the resolved id, which is `""` here. One function produces that sentence for the three project-context surfaces (`projectNotRegistered`) and ONE writes the Global section (`projectContextGlobalSection`, which now writes into the caller's builder rather than returning a string, so the heading and the blank-line separation are `projectContextSection`'s job alone). That section is not only the second request: it is also where a RESOLVED project's window hands over its `_global` half, because `projectContextSplit` partitions the admitted rows by their own `ProjectID` before either surface renders them (#809). The split is a partition and not a selection — nothing is added, dropped or re-sorted by it, and on the TOOL `limit` still caps the whole block, because the tool's Global section is the window's own `_global` half with no second read. The RESOURCE is the one that reads past its window: its section is that half plus a second `_global`-only read capped at 15 with the window's rows filtered out of it, so the section is not capped at 15 and the block's memory rows are bounded at 20 + 15 — and it exists because the SessionStart instructions key their trust guidance on the `## Global (applies to all projects)` heading by name, so a cross-project row printed under `## Memories` lost the one thing that guidance points at. It is keyed on the row's own project rather than on a comparison against the requested one, which is what makes `project_id: "_global"` fall out as an empty own-half and one Global section — and that path SKIPS the second read rather than the section, because the pre-existing `projectID != memory.GlobalProjectID` guard, written to stop a bucket being counted as a project, also discarded the window's own rows and answered a full store with the never-saved census. An EMPTY `_global` window is a fourth case: `projectContextOwnRowsNote` refuses that id, so every surface that reads the bucket answers from the assembler's VERDICT — the abstention when rows were withheld, and a census OF the cross-project rows when the window was empty — and none of them answers the project census, which is a claim about a project. `ghost://memories/global` joined that set when it moved onto the assembler, and no count is involved on its path because `projectContextGlobalBudget` sets no `IncludeGlobal`: the bucket IS the population, so there is no second population for the verdict to be wrong about. Its census keeps its OWN wording rather than the block's — "No global memories saved yet. Use ghost_save_global…" — because it is the surface an agent is told to write to, so the empty answer is where it is told where; both wordings are gated on the same verdict, which is the part that has to agree. Both surfaces, because fixing one and forgetting the other is how the defect survived a round: the tool's own empty branch asks `projectExists`, which is `true` for a bucket, so it shipped the "is registered but has no memories" census over the same store the resource now describes correctly. `projectContextEmptyNote`'s `ghost_memories_list` pointer is CORRECT on this path and is asserted, since the tool resolves `_global`; the clause that would be false is the "Ghost holds N memories for this project" one, which is the one `projectContextOwnRowsNote` builds and this function does not. The `as_of` branch reached the same union through `MemoriesAsOf`'s `ProjectScoped` read, so it splits too — `splitMemoriesByProject` is the same predicate written against `memory.AsOfRow`, because `assemble.Run` does not produce `memory.Memory` and `MemoriesAsOf` does not produce `assemble.Item`. `validateTags` REFUSES a control character, an invisible FORMAT character (the whole of category Cf, #1663), a backtick or a `«»` in a tag on all four tools that write one, naming the position and the tag through `assemble.Token`; the FORMAT character is refused for the reason `assemble`'s renderers escape it rather than print it — a tag of `gh` + U+200B + `ost` is a different label from `ghost` while a reader sees one word — and it is the same class `validateScopeKeyValue` and `blankWriteText` already refuse; it refuses a list LONGER THAN TEN tags rather than cutting it — a silent cut leaves a label unaccounted for behind a success answer, and the count is a number the caller can act on — and trims only the LENGTH, because a shortened label is a different label and not a different row (#1041). Each tag as the caller sent it, not the cut one, goes through `memory.RejectSecret` under the store's own field name `tags[i]`, because the store's token check sees only the cut tag and a token placed past byte 64 would otherwise be cut out of its sight and the save would answer as a success. That count is a WRITE-BOUNDARY rule and not a property of the store: a fold or a portable import can leave a row carrying more tags, and such a row cannot be re-submitted as-is through `ghost_memory_update` (omitting `tags` keeps the list, so nothing is corrupted) That refusal is on the WRITE path on purpose: the same class on `ghost import` put the memory in danger of falling out of every `ghost export`, because the exporter applies the importer's own checks, and nothing on the write path had refused such a tag for the whole life of the feature. A tag is therefore carried byte for byte by import and export, and `assemble.TagsLabel` is what makes a stored one safe to read. FREE TEXT IS REFUSED AT WRITE TIME WHEN IT IS BLANK OR MALFORMED: `ghost_memory_save`, `ghost_save_global` and `ghost_memory_update` refuse a `content` that is blank, not valid UTF-8, or carrying a NUL byte, `ghost_task_create` refuses a `title` on the same rule, and `ghost_decision_record` refuses a `title`, `decision` or `rationale` — the old `value == ""` check at each site accepted `"   "`, `"\n\t"` and `"a\x00b"` and stored them verbatim, and BLANK includes a value holding only invisible FORMAT characters (`U+200B` ZERO WIDTH SPACE, `U+2060` WORD JOINER, `U+FEFF` ZERO WIDTH NO-BREAK SPACE), which are category Cf rather than Unicode spaces so a `TrimSpace` check misses them. The refusal names the field and never the value, because an invalid-UTF-8 or NUL-bearing argument is caller text an error string must not carry. A refused `ghost_memory_update` leaves the stored content byte-identical to what it was (#1038). The rule is a WRITE rule, like `validateTags`, and is NOT in `CheckImportedMemory`/`CheckImportedTask`/`CheckImportedDecision`: an artifact carries a blank `content`, `title`, `decision` or `rationale` byte for byte, and a store already holding one keeps round-tripping. A `PROJECT_ID` IS CAPPED AT WRITE TIME, AND THE CAP IS A WRITE RULE ALONE: `ensureProjectFor` refuses a `project_id` that is whitespace-only, not valid UTF-8, NUL-bearing, or over `maxProjectIDLen` (4096 — POSIX `PATH_MAX`, and a project id is routinely a filesystem path, so a wider one is a payload wearing a project's clothes and `ghost_list_projects` renders names uncapped). The cap deliberately does NOT live in `memory.CheckImportedProjectID`, which has no length bound ON PURPOSE (#844): a refusal there CASCADES — a failed project step never records its id, so every memory, task and decision naming it is rejected and a whole project is lost on restore. The gate at the call site is creation too, so an existing project addressed by a long recorded path resolves before this is asked. `ghost_memories_list` resolves the same way and had the identical `Project ""` wording, and its read was never guarded — `ListMemories` widens to `project_id = ? OR project_id = '_global'` whenever a category or retention filter is present, so a FILTERED browse of an unknown project returned the global rows. Both are fixed on their own branch ([#781](https://github.com/wcatz/ghost/pull/781)) rather than here: a different tool on a different code path, and not a consequence of this migration. A PASSIVE `Result.Abstention` makes no promise about a note, because it is bytes a CALLER renders and the callers do not agree on what follows it: the search surface writes `assemblerNotes(res.Notes)` after the sentence so "the note below breaks the removals down per stage" is true there, while `ghost_project_context`, the project-context resource and `recall_project` return the abstention as the ENTIRE answer and render no notes. The assembler does not own the rendering, so the promise is dropped from the passive half only; the search half keeps it, and the clause that carries the reason without the notes is inside the sentence ("withheld as out of date, their validity windows having closed or not yet opened"). The project-context census is gated on the PROJECT'S OWN ROWS, not on the block: `projectContextBudget` sets `IncludeGlobal`, so the block is populated by `_global` whenever the store holds any — and `cmd/ghost/bootstrap.go` seeds the global memories on every real store — and a block that admits no row of the requested project says so (`projectContextOwnRowsNote`: how many the project holds, that none is above, and where to browse them). The count is `CountMemories`, which covers rows left out for ANY reason — validity, the cap, deduplication, resolution — so the sentence names no cause. The population split has to live at the caller, because with one bucket holding two populations nothing above it can tell them apart: the live global is an admitted item, so the outcome is `answerable` and the reason is empty even when every row of the project was withheld. And that caller's gate is the ADMITTED ROWS, never the rendered text: `## Learned Context` is a direct read of `ghost_state` and not a memory row, so a project reflection has already summarised has a full block and an EMPTY read even when every memory of its own has been withheld (#788) — `## Recent Decisions` is that shape only once its companion memory is gone, since `RecordDecision` inserts a `decision_log` row in the same transaction. An empty item set therefore consults the project-scoped count, because the VERDICT describes the union window rather than the project: an empty item set with an exclusion reason can be describing `_global` rows, and a sentence read as a claim about the project needs a project-scoped fact behind it. THE COUNT IS THE SENTENCE'S INPUT, NOT A PERMISSION, and ALL THREE GATES GO THROUGH THE ONE FUNCTION THAT OWNS THE CHOICE (`projectContextOwnRowsNote`, which reads the count itself) — the two empty-block branches answer on the same union verdict and needed the same gate, and a gate a caller can pass without obeying is the failure this arrangement exists to prevent: a version read the count, was satisfied, and then rendered a different and false sentence for a project whose every row `ghost resolve` had withdrawn (the passive window binds `resolved_at IS NULL` and `CountMemories` does not, so the count is one and the window is empty, no stage withheld anything, and the abstention is false). TWO COUNTS, because the two sentences are claims about different populations: `CountMemories` (no `resolved_at` predicate) is what the project HOLDS and names no cause, while `projectWindowRowCount` (`CountActiveMemories`, a `windowCountCapableStore` assertion binding `resolved_at IS NULL` the way the passive key scan does) is what a WINDOW could admit, and only the abstention — which names a cause, and so is a claim about which rows that cause explains — may be rendered when it is above zero. A project whose every row `ghost resolve` has withdrawn reports one to the first count and zero to the second, and the second is what stops it being told its rows "were withheld as out of date" on the strength of aged-out rows that belong to `_global`. A count that errors is no sentence at all, because an unreadable count is evidence that the project holds rows neither way. A browse that resolves a project NAME that matches no `projects` row is answered with the not-registered sentence and reads NOTHING: `ResolveProject` answers an unknown name with `("", "", nil)`, so the handler keeps the RAW name for the message (formatting the resolved id produced `Project "" is not registered`, naming nothing the caller can act on) and SKIPS the `ListMemories` call. Skipping it is not tidiness — `ListMemories` widens to `(project_id = ? OR project_id = '_global')` whenever a category or retention filter is present, so a FILTERED browse of a project Ghost has never heard of returned the `_global` rows, and the not-registered sentence was unreachable for that call because the answer was never empty. The bound is exactly the cross-project rows: the widened clause binds `project_id = ''` — `ResolveProject` answers an unknown name with the EMPTY string — so only the `_global` disjunct can match, and a named project's own rows are never in the answer (an UNFILTERED read does not widen, which is why only the filtered case leaked). An unresolved project browses NOTHING rather than showing the cross-project rows, and that is a deliberate difference from `ghost_project_context`, which DOES append them for the same unresolved name: a browse is "this project's memories, optionally filtered" and a project with none has an empty answer, while a context block is what the SessionStart instructions send an agent to precisely when the directory matched nothing. Whether `ListMemories` should widen at all is a question about its own scope and is not settled here. `ghost_memory_search` sets `assemble.Request.Record` through a `retrievalCapableStore` capability assertion (#646) and a session id taken from `sessionIDFor(req)`, so the row is built in ONE place (the assembler) while only the handler knows which session the call arrived on; the assertion is `, ok` rather than a requirement, so a provider that cannot be audited still answers searches — a gap in a report is a smaller problem than a failed call, and the assembler treats a nil sink as "record nothing". THE TWO PASSIVE SURFACES RECORD TOO (#850), and for the first two releases only the search wrote a row, so every per-source denominator in the audit was a statement about searches and the `session_start` and `project_context` columns could only ever be zero. `ghost://memories/global` JOINED the project-context side of that when it moved onto the assembler (#581), which is a behaviour change rather than a wiring detail and is the reason it is written here: that resource used to read `_global` through `Store.GetTopMemories`, which ranked and trimmed in SQL and recorded NOTHING, so an agent reading the cross-project memories appended no row at all. It now owes exactly ONE row per read, under `source=project_context`, `project_id=_global` and an empty `query_hash` — a listing carried no question, and a digest of `""` would be the same constant on every read — pinned by `TestTheGlobalMemoriesResourceRecordsItsRead`. The attribution is `_global` rather than any requesting project because the row is a statement about the bucket it READ, which is the same reason the project-context resource attributes its Global section's own read to `_global` rather than to the project the call was about. The row carries verdicts and ids and never memory text, so recording a read of a global memory does not put that memory into every backup: `TestTheGlobalMemoriesResourceRecordCarriesNoQueryOrMemoryText` scans the raw bytes of every column for the rendered content, as a substring, because equality would miss a leak stored inside a longer value. `assembleProjectContext` sets the same two fields inside ITSELF rather than at its call sites, because the tool, the `ghost://project/{id}/context` resource and the `recall_project` prompt all reach the assembler through it, and a sink wired at the two callers would have recorded the tool and left a resource read unaudited — a resource read is a retrieval an agent acted on exactly as much as a tool call is. It records one row per READ and not one per block, which is why the tool owes ONE (its `## Global` section is the `_global` half of its own union window, not a second read) and the resource owes TWO (its section caps are per-section, so the second read is real). The second row is attributed to `_global` — the bucket it READ — and never to the requesting project, or that project's precision would be computed over a window holding none of its rows. Neither passive surface sets `SuppressRecordWhenLegsFailed`, which reads the OPPOSITE way from the search handler's choice for one reason: that handler CONVERTS a leg failure with nothing admitted into an ERROR, so the call never reached its caller as an answer, while a passive surface always answers. `ghost_memory_search` KEEPS `ReadOnlyHint: true` even though that path now writes, and the reasoning is at the annotation: the annotation is a claim a CLIENT acts on — auto-approve or ask the user — so it is about the effect a user would want to confirm, and a search confirms nothing (no memory created, nothing deleted, no answer changed); the row it appends is bookkeeping about the call, observable by no other tool. `ghost_project_context` is the opposite case and is NOT read-only: its first-contact import writes memory rows every passive surface then serves, so it declares `destructiveHint: false` explicitly (the spec defaults it to true once `readOnlyHint` is false; the import adds rows and overwrites nothing) and no `idempotentHint`, since a first call can import and a second does not (`TestProjectContextAnnotationsDoNotClaimReadOnly`). The search's session id is read through `sessionIDFor` and NOT through `provenanceFor`, because that function's other half is harness detection — `detectCallingSource` walks /proc on Linux and SPAWNS `ps` on darwin — and putting a process walk on every search to obtain a value that is `""` over the stdio transport Ghost ships is not a cost worth paying for a column nothing reads yet. Every id a tool prints goes through `assemble.Token` and every free-text field through `quoteData`, with `dataDelimiterNote` once per block and never on a row listing; a new listing tool or resource must use them. `shortID` abbreviates a well-formed id and shows a quoted one WHOLE, because eight runes of an escape is nothing a reader can act on. `ghost_health`'s RETRIEVAL block is the one place per-source figures reach an agent, and it is a block inside the existing tool rather than a 24th one: an agent debugging "search returns things I did not use" fetches `ghost_health` and never opens a terminal, so figures that live only in `ghost context --audit` are read exactly when nobody is measuring anything. It asserts on the concrete store (`s.store.(*memory.Store)`) because `audit.BuildReport`'s two readers are not on `provider.MemoryStore`, and it reads only — `writeRetrievalAuditBlock` writes nothing, which is what keeps this off the write-refusal path the store-version line is there to explain. It pools PROJECTS within a source through the ONE permitted `audit.MergeProjects`, and its per-source lines are `SourceReport.Summary()` rather than a second renderer, so the compact shape and the report shape cannot disagree. It goes LAST in the output because every line above it is already something an agent or a script reads by name. The `"ignored" is not a score` sentence is printed only once SOME source has a scored verdict and WITHOUT the `⚠` this tool reserves for trouble: a caveat attached to no figure is noise, and a fresh store carrying a warning glyph is how an agent learns to skip the warnings that matter (`TestHealthOnAStoreWithNoHistorySaysSo` holds that). **EVERY READ `ghost_health` TAKES IS REPORTED WHEN IT FAILS, NEVER DROPPED (#1461).** The embedding-coverage line, the memory-links line and each project's count used to fail in silence: coverage printed a bare `enabled` with no figure and so read as a healthy store, the links line vanished whole (its absence meant both "no links" and "a read cut short"), and an uncountable project was `continue`d past — no bullet — while the total excluded it and printed as complete. Each now names its failure in place (`**Embeddings:** enabled — coverage could not be read: …`, `**Memory links:** could not be read: …`, and a project bullet `count could not be read: …` with the total stating how many projects it leaves out), the same rule the pinned, history and retrieval blocks already follow; `ghost_list_projects` names a failed count the same way rather than printing `0`, because that listing is how an agent picks a project to save into. `TestHealthSaysWhenTheEmbeddingStatsReadFails`, `TestHealthSaysWhenTheLinkStatsReadFails`, `TestHealthNamesAProjectItCouldNotCount` and `TestProjectListingNamesACountItCouldNotRead` pin it. `TestHealthRetrievalBlockIsAdditive` asserts the tool count is still 23, which is the number four documents state in prose. `TestTheListingToolsRenderIdsThroughTheSafeToken`, `TestTheProjectListingsRenderANameRawIntoItsOwnLine`, `TestShortIDRendersAHostileIDWholeRatherThanAFragment` and `TestProjectContextQuotesItsFreeTextSurfaces` pin it. **The retrieval record names the HOST SESSION ONLY (#648, #971):** `Server.sessionIDFor` returns the transport's id when it has one (streamable HTTP). Over stdio, it returns `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID` ONLY when the current process IS the host session — the process the host started with the variable in its INITIAL environment. A child process (subagent, CLI call) inherits the variable, so its parent's initial environ also carries it. The server detects this by reading /proc/<ppid>/environ: if the parent has the variable, this process inherited it and returns ""; if the parent lacks it, this process is the host session and the variable is its own. `assembleProjectContext` applies the same host-session-only rule for all four project-context surfaces. This is the id the hook payload carries, so the audit can match a call to the scan that judged it. No other host's variable has been observed, so codex, opencode, goose and an `mcpo` bridge record `""` and their calls are unjudged; a session id that changes inside one server process (a clear) keeps recording the old one and its calls match no scan. All of these fail toward unjudged. Memory provenance (`provenanceFor`) is a separate concern and is unchanged. A test run under Claude Code inherits the variable, so a test asserting a record's session sets it explicitly with `t.Setenv`. The project-context block and resource end the memories section with a pinned-cut line when `Trace.PinnedCut` is non-zero (the tool's response byte cut adds its own pinned count, see `capResponseParts` below); the session-start header (`sessionCountsLine`) says the same, in wording that does not collide with the bench header parser. **A tool that writes under a project resolves it through `ensureProjectFor` — the ONE resolution-and-creation path — so a project the store has never seen is OPENED rather than refused, and the two guards are asked in exactly one place (#956).** **And a write that can be refused is judged BEFORE `ensureProjectFor` opens its project, so a refused save or decision writes nothing: no project row and no checkout binding.** `ghost_memory_save` and `ghost_save_global` ask `memory.CheckUpsertArguments` and `ghost_decision_record` asks `memory.CheckDecisionArguments` — the exported prologues `UpsertWithOptions` and `RecordDecision` themselves run first, so the handler and the store hold one predicate, not two — on the exact values the store will receive (clamped content, validated tags, `optScope`'s map, the provenance with its agent), and only then open the project. Opening it first left a refused call's project behind with no memory in it, and inside a git checkout that empty project took the checkout's binding, so every later session start there named the misspelling and the corrected save opened a second, unbound project. Project creation and the row's INSERT are still two transactions — `ensureProjectFor` routes through three store writers with their own transactions, and folding them into the upsert's would rewrite the resolution path — and the gap is closed by argument, not by atomicity: what the store can still refuse after the check (`CheckWindowOrder` on a fold) needs a row already in that project, because the duplicate probe is filtered by `project_id`, so it cannot fire on a project this call opened; `supersedes`' live-successor refusal is filtered the same way. A store failure (a lock timeout, a failed commit) after the project is opened can still leave it empty; that is an infrastructure failure, not a refusal of the caller's arguments. A new refusal added to either store writer goes into its `Check…Arguments` function, or this property is lost; `TestRefusedWriteOpensNoProjectAndBindsNoCheckout` holds one case per refusal shape. `ghost_memory_save` always worked this way; `ghost_decision_record` used to call `Store.ResolveProject` first and answer `project "<id>" not found` when nothing resolved, so an agent's first decision on a fresh store failed and it then recorded the decision under `_global` instead — a decision about one project answering for all of them, which is the harm the issue measured. Both tools now reach the same function, which is what puts the credential guard's key-shaped-id refusal and the hostile-shape refusal on every writer rather than on one, and what makes the two answers BYTE-IDENTICAL on a refused id — the assertion both halves of `TestDecisionRecordRefusesAProjectIDTheSaveRefuses` rest on. A project the store already holds is untouched: the resolution runs first and both guards are gated on its answer being empty, so a decision against an existing project resolves to the id it resolved to before, and the upsert's conflict arm keeps the recorded path rather than letting the write rewrite it. What routing through it ALSO does is make the repository route reachable for a decision, so a `*memory.BindingRefusal` is no longer unreachable there: the notice is appended to that result for the reason the save result carries it, because the decision landed in a project of its own while a same-named project kept the name, and "Decision recorded" is otherwise the same sentence as "you just stopped seeing the context of the project you named" (#613). `TestDecisionRecordOpensTheProjectTheStoreHasNeverSeen` is the parity half and it compares the project a decision opens against the project a SAVE opens on a store of the same shape, rather than only requiring the call to succeed; `TestDecisionRecordCannotReachARefusedNameBinding` in `refused_binding_test.go` was the test that pinned the opposite of this and is now `TestDecisionRecordReportsRefusedNameBinding`. **The session id a retrieval record carries is decided ONCE, in `New`, and every surface reads that one field (`Server.hostSessionID`; `sessionIDFor` for the search tool, `assembleProjectContext` for the project-context tool, resources and prompt).** On Linux it is `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID` from the environment only when this process is the host session itself: `ai.IsHostSession` reads the parent's INITIAL `/proc/<ppid>/environ`, and a parent that already carries the variable means this process inherited it (a subagent or CLI child), so the record carries "" and the audit leaves it unjudged (#971). Nothing reads `/proc` after construction (the answer would change if the parent exited), and tests reach the decision through the package-level `hostSessionResolver` hook, never a field on `Server`. Every uncertainty fails safe to NO id: `ppid <= 1`, an unreadable parent environ (EACCES, `hidepid` mounts), and a nested `claude` launched from inside another session (its parent carries the variable). On non-Linux platforms there is no `/proc`, so the environment's id is used as before, and the KNOWN GAP is that a child there still records the inherited id. `provenanceFor` is untouched by this rule. A project CREATED by a save from a name-shaped id is bound to the physical TOP LEVEL of the git checkout the server was started in (the directory is captured once in `New`; a directory outside any checkout, the home directory, an ancestor of it and a filesystem root are never bound) plus that checkout's remote, so the session start resolves it — and both answers come from `repo.GitCommand`, which drops from its child every inherited git variable that can relocate the repository or replace its configuration, so the checkout recorded is the one the server's own directory is in and never the one an inherited `GIT_DIR` or a `GIT_CONFIG_PARAMETERS` names (see `internal/repo`) — but only when no project already claims that directory by path or remote, and the directory passes the credential and project-shape guards as a recorded path; otherwise the project opens unbound as before. An existing project is never rebound, and a second new name from a claimed checkout is never folded into the first: the claim test, `checkBindPathConflicts` (bind's containment rules), the insert and `checkPathResolvable` (a path the resolver could never return, such as one of ten characters or fewer, declines the bind) are one write transaction (`Store.BindNewProjectToCheckout`), which declines rather than merges, because a merge would delete the row the save is about to write under. A changed decision is recorded with `supersedes`, and that rule is stated in the `ghost_decision_record` description and the `mcpInstructions` "Changed Decisions" section; `sameTitleDecisionAdvice` (backed by `Store.FindLiveDecisionByTitle`, which reads only id and title and counts a revisit decision as live) adds a same-title (case-insensitive, trimmed) note to a record that omitted `supersedes`, looked up before the write so the new row cannot match itself, and it is advice only: it never refuses and never links. A `supersedes` id the new project cannot mark is a PARTIAL failure and is an error RESULT (`IsError` set) rather than a warning on a success: the content keeps the committed `decision_id` and `memory_id` and says the new decision stands while the old one is NOT marked superseded, so a caller checking only `isError` cannot read the reversal as done (#1041). A supersede whose companion memory the resolve guard leaves standing — pinned, retention-exempt, a convention/preference, or ambiguous because more than one memory claims it — is the SAME partial failure, `IsError` set, with the left-live memory named; it used to be warning text on a success, which is exactly the shape #1041 removed for the failed supersede. The failure sentence names the `supersedes` id through `memory.RefusalArg`, and the id-carrying sentences in `SupersedeDecisionReport` render their ids through the same renderer, so neither the note nor the store error echoes a raw or oversized id (#1072). A `supersedes` id already superseded by a DIFFERENT decision that is still live (active or revisit, `Store.LiveDecisionSuccessor`) is REFUSED, not repointed (#1144): the repoint overwrote `superseded_by`, the only record that the live successor replaced it, and left both live with the same-title advice skipped. The refusal happens BEFORE the write, because the store's answer to a supersede it refuses is the partial failure below, with the new decision committed; it writes nothing, renders the caller's id through `memory.RefusalArg` and the stored successor through `assemble.Token`, and suggests `supersedes=<successor>`. **A decision that supersedes another is recorded and applied in ONE transaction**: the handler calls `Store.RecordDecisionSuperseding`, which runs the decision insert (`recordDecisionTx`) and the supersede (`supersedeDecisionTx`, the body `SupersedeDecisionReport` also runs) under one lock and one `decision-record` write. They were two writes on the request context, and a cancel between them left the new decision live beside the one it replaces, with the partial-failure answer never delivered and a retry writing a second live copy. Now an error from the call means nothing was written (a cancelled context, a database error, a commit failure all roll back both halves), so a retry is a fresh write; the ONE exception is a supersede the store refuses for what the rows say (`supersedeRefusal`: the old decision is not in the project, or it has a different live successor), which is decided before the supersede writes anything and commits the new decision alone, returned in `RecordedDecision.SupersedeErr` and answered as the #1041 partial failure, as before. Pinned by `TestRecordDecisionSupersedingCancelledAfterTheInsert` (an unexported `Store.afterDecisionInsert` hook cancels inside the transaction), `TestRecordDecisionSupersedingFailureRollsBackBothHalves` (a database error is not a refusal — a cancel alone cannot tell them apart, since a commit on a cancelled context fails by itself), `TestRecordDecisionSupersedingRefusalKeepsTheNewDecision` and, through the tool, `TestADecisionRecordCancelledBeforeItsSupersedeLeavesNoHalfWrite`.
