The supersede makes the successor check inside that transaction (`DecisionSuccessorError`), so a writer racing past the handler's read lands in the #1041 partial-failure result rather than a silent repoint; that result says the new decision stands unlinked and names the successor, and does NOT advise `supersedes=<successor>`, because the decision is already committed and recording it again would write a second copy (the store's error carries no advice for that reason). A target whose successor was itself superseded or no longer exists has no live successor to lose and is repointed. The same-pair no-op holds at the STORE level (`SupersedeDecisionReport(A, B)` twice): through the tool every call records a new decision, so a repeated `supersedes=A` is a different successor and is refused. The three listing tools judge their enum argument — and `ghost_memories_list` its retention tier — BEFORE resolving the project: `ghost_task_list` a status against the one `taskStatusValues` list `ghost_task_update` also uses, `ghost_decisions_list` a status against `decisionStatusValues` (active, superseded, revisit — the `decisions.status` CHECK), `ghost_memories_list` a category against `memory.IsValidCategory` and a tier through `memory.NormalizeRetention`, so a misspelt value is refused with the allowed list, rendered through `memory.RefusalArg`, instead of being answered "No tasks found.", "No decisions found." or a not-registered sentence (#1041). `ghost_memory_search` holds the same two rules: a category outside `memory.IsValidCategory` is refused with `invalidCategory` before the project is resolved, and a project name nothing resolves is refused as `project "…" not found` through `memory.RefusalArg`, as `ghost_task_list` and `ghost_decisions_list` refuse it, rather than searched as `_global` alone. Both checks sit above the `explain` and `as_of` branches, so all three paths refuse alike. It REFUSES rather than appending `projectNotRegistered` as `ghost_project_context` does because the assembler renders and measures the search answer: an appended sentence would ship bytes the response cap never saw, the assembler's own verdict advice would still be in the body, and the `explain` payload has no place for it. A handed-on unknown category answered `reason=all_out_of_category` with advice to raise the limit, and an unresolved project answered "widen the limit" or a `_global` row as a hit for the misspelt name. **A TASK'S PRIORITY IS A 0-4 RANGE AND IS REFUSED OUTSIDE IT (#1037):** `ghost_task_create` (whose documented default is 2) and `ghost_task_update` both refuse an integer outside 0-4 with `invalid priority <n> — must be between 0 and 4` and write nothing, rather than resetting it to 2. The reset was the bug rather than a tolerant default: a typo silently demoted a P0 task, and on create it deferred to the schema's own `CHECK (priority BETWEEN 0 AND 4)`, which surfaces as a raw SQLite constraint error instead of a named refusal. The range check runs after `optInt`, so the non-integer shapes it already refuses (a fractional number, a non-numeric string) keep their own message while a stringified `"3"` works — and the message is byte-identical to `memory.CheckImportedTask`'s, because the importer refuses the same range and two phrasings for one rule is the drift this file exists to prevent. **`ghost_project_delete` WITH `apply:true` ACCEPTS ONLY THE EXACT PROJECT ID ITS OWN DRY RUN PRINTED (#1048).** The dry-run side hands any identifier to `Store.DeleteProject`, whose `ResolveProject` accepts an id, a name, a canonical path prefix, a repository remote and a basename — the right shapes for a preview, but not for an irreversible delete, because an apply that passed the same fuzzy identifier through would delete whichever project those words resolved to at delete time. The handler therefore asks `ResolveExactProjectID` first and refuses a miss BEFORE `DeleteProject` runs: the refusal names the dry run (`apply:true requires the exact project id shown by the dry run, not a name, path or basename`), renders the value through `memory.RefusalArg("project", args.Project)` (the tool's own argument name), and deletes nothing. The id is the only identifier a caller can supply without evidence, which is why only it may cross into apply; the tool's description and its `project` jsonschema say so. The CLI path is unchanged by this: `ghost project delete` previews, confirms interactively, and applies the resolved id it printed, so it never applies an identifier the user did not just see resolved. Every read tool that renders its OWN rows — `ghost_memories_list`, `ghost_search_all`, `ghost_project_context`, `ghost_task_list` and `ghost_decisions_list` — is fitted to the same 16,000-byte response budget `ghost_memory_search` uses (`searchResponseCap`), through ONE render-level pass (`capResponseParts`, `responsecap.go`) rather than by re-running the assembler: a cuttable row is a memory line, a whole task or a whole decision record, everything else (headings, pinned lines, the learned summary, the not-registered sentence) is framing that is counted but never cut, rows go from the END — except that a PINNED row (only `ghost_project_context` marks pins, so the other four tools cut end-first exactly as before) is cut only once every unpinned row is gone, the way the assembler's fit treats it under `Budget.KeepPinned`, because its `## Global` section renders after the project's rows and an end-first cut removed the pinned global row it promised to keep — the answer ends with a notice naming how many went and the one lever that shows the rest (a lower `limit`, or this tool's own `category`/`retention`/`status` filter or query), and the pass is byte-identical to the uncut render whenever the answer already fits — so the project-context goldens and the resource/prompt surfaces, which are row-capped and NOT byte-fitted, do not move. A FRAMING THAT NAMES A CALLER-SUPPLIED PROJECT ID IS BOUNDED BEFORE IT IS RENDERED, because a framing at or over the budget keeps every row and the cap stops applying at all. The project-context tool's not-registered answer echoes the id the caller asked about, and that echo is framing: a 20,000-byte id made the answer about 180 KB with no cut notice, while the id was never echoed on the RESOLVED path (a registered id is not named in the block at all, so a long one was always inside the budget). The id is therefore rendered through the same 512-byte rule the context resource and the recall_project prompt use (`recallProjectPrefaceName`): at or under it the id comes back whole, because the sentence names the project the caller asked about, and past it the id is withheld, so the framing stays small enough for the cut to apply. An ordinary name's sentence is byte-identical to the one before the bound. A framing that cannot fit even with every row cut keeps its rows, mirroring the assembler's framing ceiling, and the FIRST row (the first PINNED row when any row is pinned) is never cut — an answer that holds rows always keeps at least one, so a single row larger than the budget (a decision whose two 8000-byte fields are clamped separately is about 16 KB) is shown whole with a notice that the budget could not be met rather than dropped for a notice over nothing; the budget bounds the answer and never what is stored. An answer that holds a pinned row says its rows were "cut from this answer, unpinned rows first" (`responseCapNoticeFor`) rather than "from the end", since the kept pinned row can render after the cut ones; a pin-free answer keeps the shared `assemble.CutNotice` sentence byte for byte. A pinned row the byte cut does remove is counted in its own statement after the notice ("N of the cut rows were pinned: the pinned rows alone exceed the budget"), and a section heading whose rows the cut all removed, and which carries no note, is not printed (`responseSection` records how many parts follow each heading; a heading that then opens the answer drops its leading blank lines). The `as_of` branch marks no pin, since a past reading reserves nothing, but it shares the heading rule. The `ghost://project/{id}/tasks` resource returns a failed `ListTasks` read as an error (rendering the project id through `memory.RefusalArg`) rather than answering "No open tasks for this project." **EMBEDDING NOTIFICATION VIA STORE ON-SAVE HOOK.** The server wires `Store.SetOnSave` to a non-blocking send on `projectCh` when `SetEmbedder` is called, so every store write that stores new text (Create, Upsert, RecordDecision, a content-changing update, first-contact import) notifies the embedding worker automatically. `ghost_memory_save`, `ghost_save_global` (as `_global`), `ghost_decision_record`, and `ghost_project_context`'s first-contact import no longer push `projectCh` by hand — the store's `onSave` callback fires for each. `ghost_memory_update` calls `onSave` from `UpdateMemoryWithOptions` when the content changed, outside any caller transaction — the same way Upsert does. A metadata-only edit (tags, importance, category, validity) does not queue the project because the text did not change and no re-embed is needed. The 2-minute sweep (`UnembeddedMemoryIDs`) still covers any rows the hook misses. **THE DECISION WRITE NOTIFIES EVERY SUBSCRIBABLE RESOURCE IT CHANGES (#1271).** `ghost_decision_record` pushes `notifications/resources/updated` for the project's `decisions` AND its `context` — the decision's companion memory and its `## Recent Decisions` entry both change that block — under both the hash-ID and the project-name URI a client may have subscribed with (`notifyProjectResource`), and does so AFTER its `supersedes` step, so the pushed update reflects the companion that step retired; a decision recorded under `_global` ALSO pushes `ghost://memories/global`, exactly as `ghost_save_global` does for its own write. The context resource is the one clients are told to pin at session start, so a client relying on notifications otherwise keeps a pre-write block. The convention is stated on `ghost_link_withdraw`'s notification ("Every other mutating handler in this file pushes that update after its write") and it is PER HANDLER rather than free — a write that serves a resource without pushing it is a defect. `TestResourceSubscription_NotifiesContextOnDecisionRecord` and `TestResourceSubscription_NotifiesGlobalOnGlobalDecisionRecord` pin the decision case, in the shape `TestResourceSubscription_NotifiesNameSubscriberOnToolSave` set. **EVERY WRITER OF A MEMORY ROW NOTIFIES EVERY RESOURCE WHOSE READ IT CHANGES (#1274, #1281).** `_global` is one project AND the bucket behind `ghost://memories/global`, so the context resource and the memories resource serve the same rows: a write whose row lives there must push BOTH `ghost://memories/global` and `ghost://project/_global/context`, or a subscriber to one keeps a stale list or block. The rule is ONE helper, `notifyMemoryRow(ctx, projectID)` — it pushes that project's context resource and, when the id is `_global`, the memories bucket — and every writer of an ordinary memory row calls it after a successful write: `ghost_save_global` (as `_global`), `ghost_memory_save`, `ghost_memory_update`, `ghost_memory_pin`, `ghost_memory_delete` (its plain path, its `purge_history` branch, and `purgeDeletedMemoryHistory` for an already-deleted tombstone), `ghost_link_withdraw`, the resolve pass behind `ghost_resolve apply`, and `ghost_project_context`'s first-contact Claude import, which notifies only when the run wrote at least one row (a run with no files, or whose every file was refused, failed or already present, changed nothing and stays silent), so the update, pin and remaining gaps left by #1279 and #1285, each of which pushed only the context resource, are closed by construction rather than by a per-handler check the next writer can miss. `ghost_memory_promote` is the writer that MOVES a row rather than editing one in place: the row leaves the source project and enters `_global`, so it calls the helper twice — once for the source project's context, and once for `_global`, whose context resource AND bucket the destination rows reach. `ghost_resolve_mark` is NOT one of these: the store refuses to mark in `_global` at all, so that tool can never change a `_global` row and its only notification is the context resource; the resolve writer that CAN reach `_global` is the `ghost_resolve apply` pass. `TestSaveGlobalNotifiesProjectContextSubscriber` and `TestMemorySaveToGlobalNotifiesGlobalMemoriesSubscriber` pin the two save directions; `TestMemoryUpdateOnGlobalNotifiesGlobalMemoriesSubscriber` and `TestMemoryPinOnGlobalNotifiesGlobalMemoriesSubscriber` pin update and pin; `TestMemoryDeleteOnGlobalNotifiesGlobalMemoriesSubscriber`, `TestMemoryDeletePurgeHistoryOnGlobalNotifiesGlobalMemoriesSubscriber` and `TestMemoryDeleteAlreadyDeletedOnGlobalNotifiesGlobalMemoriesSubscriber` pin the three delete paths; `TestMemoryPromoteNotifiesGlobalContextSubscriber`, `TestLinkWithdrawOnGlobalNotifiesGlobalMemoriesSubscriber` and `TestResolveApplyOnGlobalNotifiesGlobalMemoriesSubscriber` pin promote, withdraw and the resolve apply, and `TestProjectContextImportNotifiesContextSubscriber` pins the import with `TestProjectContextWithoutImportDoesNotNotify`, `TestProjectContextRefusedImportDoesNotNotify` and `TestProjectContextOnProjectWithMemoriesImportsNothingAndDoesNotNotify` as its silent controls — each subscribing a real MCP client before the write, since a notification is emitted once and cannot be retried. The cut notice every whole-row response cut ends with is now ONE sentence in `assemble.CutNotice`, called by `mcpserver.responseCapNotice` for the five listing tools and by the assembler's response-fit post-pass for `ghost_memory_search` (#1289), so a search answer and a listing cannot spell the same cut two ways. The injected decision surfaces read a project's LIVE decisions (#1272) — active or revisit, superseded excluded, active first — through `Store.LiveDecisions` (the `ghost://project/{id}/decisions` resource and the `## Recent Decisions` section of the context resource and the `recall_project` prompt; `ghost_project_context` renders no decisions section, which `ghost_decisions_list` and the decisions resource serve) and through the session-start block's own `status != 'superseded'` query, and a revisit row renders with ` (revisit)` after its quoted title, the marker `memory.DecisionRevisitMarker` puts there; the three renderers DELEGATE to that one function rather than each restating the rule, because the label's parity across the surfaces is a documented claim and two copies of a rule are two rules (it sits in `internal/memory` beside the status vocabulary it reads, since the dependency runs `assemble -> memory` and a renderer in `internal/assemble` cannot be reached from below). An active-only filter disagreed with `FindLiveDecisionByTitle`, `LiveDecisionSuccessor` and the same-title advice, which all read live as "not superseded", and hiding a decision flagged for reconsideration is the wrong direction on a surface whose whole job is to inject context. The active-first order is applied before the row cap closes, so a revisit row never displaces an active one. **`ghost_memory_delete` DECIDES OWNERSHIP INSIDE THE WRITE, ON EVERY BRANCH (#1154, #1191).** The handler's `GetByIDs` read and, for an id with no live row, its `MemoryHistory` read answer the caller's errors and never decide the write: a live delete carries the project into `DeleteOptions.ProjectID` / `Store.DeletePurgeInProject`, and the already-deleted purge calls `Store.PurgeDeletedMemoryHistoryInProject`, which reads inside its own `purge-history` transaction that the id still has no live row and that its newest history row is the project's, and refuses otherwise with the ids rendered through `memory.RefusalArg`. A deleted row is NOT gone for good — `ghost reflect --restore` reinstates it under its own id and a promote can then move it to `_global` — so a purge that checked the tombstone on the pool reported "already deleted" over a live row still holding the text, and let a project-scoped call erase a `_global` memory's history. `ghost history purge` takes the same scoped path for an id it found deleted, passing the project its newest history entry names, so the CLI refuses a row that came back in the same window; the unscoped `Store.PurgeMemoryHistory` keeps no production caller that acts on a pool read. **A `resources/read` ERROR NEVER REPEATS A TOKEN-SHAPED URI, EVEN ONE NO GHOST HANDLER SAW.** A URI that matches no registered resource or template (a bad escape, a raw `/` in the project segment, an unknown scheme) is answered by the SDK's own `Resource not found`, whose `data.uri` is the URI as sent. `withholdResourceReadURI` (`resource_read_uri.go`), a receiving middleware on `resources/read`, judges the URI and its leniently decoded form with `memory.RefusalArg` against `strconv.Quote`, and when either would be withheld (a token, or a value over `memory.ArgEchoMaxBytes`) sends the error without `data` and with every copy of the URI in the message replaced by the placeholder, keeping the wrapped wire code. It judges the URI rather than searching `data` for it, and it never adds text back, so a refusal a handler already withheld stays withheld; an error for a URI with no token is sent untouched. **A PROJECT RESOURCE URI CARRIES ITS ID AS ONE PERCENT-ENCODED SEGMENT, DECODED EXACTLY ONCE.** `ghost://project/{project_id}/...` is an RFC 6570 simple-string template, so a client expands the id with every byte outside the unreserved set percent-encoded; a `/` in the id (a checkout-path id, or a name like `team/api`) arrives as `%2F` and a space as `%20`. `parseProjectIDFromURI` therefore splits the segment out of the path AS SENT (`url.URL.RawPath`, falling back to `EscapedPath` when Go kept none) and calls `url.PathUnescape` on it once. Splitting the already-decoded `u.Path` and decoding again was a cross-project leak: `team%2Fapi` read project `team`, `team%252Fapi` reached `team/api`, and `%2Fsrv%2Fu%2Frepo` was refused as missing its id. Every notification URI for these resources is built by ONE function, `projectResourceURI`, the inverse of the parser, because MCP delivers `notifications/resources/updated` by exact string match and the SDK's template regexp only routes a segment of unreserved characters, `,` and `%XX`: it encodes every non-unreserved byte of the UTF-8 id as uppercase `%XX`, which is what a template expansion emits, so the URI pushed is the URI a client reads and subscribes with. `url.PathEscape` is not that encoding (it leaves `:` `@` `+` `=` raw, which the template rejects). `notifyProjectResource` still notifies under BOTH aliases, the id and the name, each in that encoded form and deduplicated by the final URI, and `ghost_project_delete`'s two direct notifications go through the same function. The unencoded form is NOT also notified for an id that needs encoding: the template never routes it, so no client can read it, and a subscription to it was a subscription to nothing. An id of unreserved characters only — every hash id, `_global`, most names — encodes to itself, so those URIs did not move. Subscriptions are compared as strings, so a client that subscribes with lowercase hex or a raw `,` reads the resource but is not matched by the uppercase, fully-encoded notification. `TestParseProjectIDFromURI_DecodesSegmentOnce`, `TestProjectResourceURI_RoundTrips` and `TestProjectResourceURI_EncodesLikeTheTemplate` pin the two functions; `TestProjectResources_EncodedIDReadsTheNamedProject` reads each project through a real client and fails on a row of the prefix project; `TestResourceSubscription_EncodedIDNotifiesAReadableURI` subscribes to encoded URIs beside the prefix project's, saves, and re-reads the delivered URI; `TestResourceSubscription_EncodedNameAndIDBothNotified` pins both aliases when the id and the name each need encoding; `TestResourceSubscription_ProjectDeleteNotifiesEncodedURI` covers the delete path. **A RESOURCE OR TEMPLATE REFUSAL CARRIES A DEFINED JSON-RPC CODE, NEVER 0.** A plain Go error returned from a resource or template handler goes on the wire with code 0, so the refusals of the caller's input are `*jsonrpc.Error` values returned unwrapped (the wire encoding keeps a wrapped error's code but drops its data): `parseProjectIDFromURI`'s three refusals (of which only the empty segment is reachable over the wire: the SDK answers a bad escape with its own `Resource not found` before the handler runs, so `TestParseProjectIDFromURI_BadEncodingIsInvalidParams` pins that refusal on the parser directly) and the `recall_project`/`record_decision` missing-argument refusals go through `invalidParamsError` (-32602), and the `tasks` and `decisions` resources answer an unregistered project with `projectResourceNotFound` — `mcp.ResourceNotFoundError`'s code and `data.uri`, with `: project "…" not found` appended to its message. `data.uri` is a second echo of the project argument, so it takes the same `memory.RefusalArg` decision as the message, and data is omitted when the id is withheld. A failed store read and a `ResolveProject` failure stay plain errors. The `context` resource and `recall_project` do NOT answer not-found for an unregistered project: their `## Global` half does not depend on the project and the context resource is the one pinned at session start, so they answer the block plus the not-registered sentence; the rule all three resources share is that a read with nothing true to return is not-found. `TestProjectResources_UnknownProjectIsResourceNotFound`, `TestProjectResources_MissingProjectIDIsInvalidParams`, `TestTemplates_MissingArgumentsAreInvalidParams` and `TestProjectResources_NotFoundDataWithholdsATokenShapedID` read the code, message and data back through a client; `TestProjectResourcesAndTemplates_SuccessPathsUnchanged` is the control. Every `ghost_search_all` row names its own bucket as `project=<assemble.Token(ProjectID)>` inside the row string (`searchAllLines`), never a per-project heading, so the fused cross-project ranking order is what the reader sees and the response budget counts the label as part of each cuttable row. `ghost_memories_list` now renders through `searchAllLines` too when its browse is FILTERED (category or retention set), because `ListMemories` widens to the project plus `_global` in that case and those rows come from more than one bucket; an unfiltered browse stays on `memoryLines`, which is unlabelled, because it is the project's own rows alone. The token guard runs on the unclamped text before the cap cuts it: `ghost_task_create` (title, description), `ghost_task_update` (description), `ghost_decision_record` (title, decision, rationale, every alternative before `memory.BoundAlternatives` drops the ones past the twentieth), `ghost_memory_save`, `ghost_memory_update` and `ghost_save_global` (content) call `memory.RejectSecret` on the caller's text before `memory.ClampContent`, `truncateUTF8` or `memory.BoundAlternatives`, as `Store.CompleteTask` and the portable importers do (#1238, #1226), so a token-shaped value past the 8000- or 300-byte cap is refused with nothing written rather than cut out of sight behind a truncation warning; `token_before_cap_test.go` places the token just past each cap, and its controls show oversized clean text is still stored cut with its warning.

**A TOOL RESULT THAT NAMES A STORED ID OR PROJECT NAME RENDERS IT THROUGH THE SHARED KEY RENDERER (#1377).** Every answer sentence in `mcpserver.go` that names a stored memory id — `ghost_memory_update`'s confirmation, both save tools' duplicate and fold clauses and the id they just wrote, the promote confirmation, the delete purge report, the decision record's two ids, the task create's id, and `ghost_memory_flag`'s flagged-but-unreadable refusal — goes through `assemble.Token` (`shortID` where a listing abbreviates, `memory.RefusalArg` in a refusal), and `ghost_project_delete`'s dry-run summary names the project through `assemble.Label` for its name and `assemble.Token` for its id, the way the project listing does. `ghost_health`'s pinned-contradictions block is the one place a stored id is rendered through `memory.RefusalArg` rather than `assemble.Token` — both the pinned memory's id and the contradicting memory's id — so a token-shaped stored id is withheld rather than printed in full, the same rule the history cap warning in the same answer already follows. The reason is the store's, not the writer's: `ghost import` writes an artifact's ids verbatim and an older store, a restored snapshot or a hand edit can already hold one with a line break, a `«»` or a control character, so a sentence that prints such a value with `%s` puts a stored value at the head of a line of the answer a model reads, where it forges a line of Ghost's own. A stored id is not exempt for being stored, which is why the four resource readers that wrap a store failure with the resolved project id (`list decisions`, `get memories`, `get learned context`) route it through `memory.RefusalArg("project_id", …)` rather than a bare `%q` — byte-identical for an ordinary id, withheld for a token-shaped one. An ordinary 32-hex id stays byte-identical, because `assemble.Token` writes an allowlisted value bare. The rule is held by `TestNoToolResultPrintsAStoredIDOutsideTheRenderer`, a structural scan over every non-test file in the package that fails on a formatting call whose operand is one of the stored-value spellings (the local ids `id`, `duplicateOf`, `memoryID`, `memoryIDs`, `decisionID`, `taskID`, `projectID`, `resolvedProjectID`, `requestedProjectID`; an id field on any receiver such as `p.ID`, `l.SourceID`, `args.TaskID`, `summary.ProjectID`; and the name field `summary.ProjectName`; and, since #1637, the CALLER's own project argument — `args.Project`, `args.ProjectID`, or an unresolved local `projectID`, together with any local one was assigned to — because a handler that names the project the way the caller spelled it is held to the same rule as one that names a stored id; the scan also fails on that value handed to either repair-command sink, `followup.ResolveCommand` and `repairInstructions`, which shell-quote the project into a command an agent may paste), and by the over-the-wire `TestToolResultsRenderAStoredMemoryIDThroughTheSafeToken`, which drives the tools the audit named plus the project-delete summary.

**A SCHEMA REFUSAL IS THE SDK'S OWN, AND THE CALLER'S TEXT IN IT IS WITHHELD LIKE ANY OTHER (#1713).** The SDK validates a tool call's arguments against the tool's advertised input schema BEFORE any handler runs, and its refusal repeats the caller's value and any unexpected property name verbatim: `validating "arguments": validating root: validating /properties/pin: type: ghp_… has type "string", want "boolean"` carries the whole value, and a property name of any length comes back whole inside `unexpected additional properties`. It is the ONE refusal on this surface that never reaches `memory.RefusalArg`, so the token guard and the 256-byte echo bound every other refusal obeys were bypassed on the one refusal a client reaches with a single wrong-typed argument or one extra property — and it is produced before Ghost sees the call, so no handler could have refused it differently. `withholdSchemaRefusal` is a RECEIVING MIDDLEWARE, the same shape as `withholdResourceReadURI` and for the same reason: a refusal the SDK produces on its own never reaches a Ghost handler, so it bypasses every renderer on this path. It does NOT re-validate the arguments and does not re-word the refusal — the SDK owns the wording — it withholds the CALLER TEXT the wording carries, which is the only part of it that is not the schema's, and it bounds what is left. Every rendering of a value the SDK prints comes from the arguments (the value itself, and a property name), so the renderings to withhold are the caller's own, found in the arguments the request carries, and they are withheld through `memory.RefusalArg`: a token-shaped value comes back as a placeholder naming the format the guard found, one over 256 bytes as a placeholder naming its length, and an ordinary value is left exactly as the SDK printed it, because a refusal that names an ordinary value is the diagnostic an agent needs. BOTH renderings of a withheld string are replaced, because the SDK prints a value RAW and a property name QUOTED, and the LONGEST rendering is replaced FIRST, because a value that contains another withheld value is printed whole. The label in the placeholder is a CONSTANT, and it cannot be the caller's own name for the argument: on this path the caller's name for the argument IS the text being withheld. The refusal is bounded as well (1024 bytes, then cut and marked), because a caller can send as many unexpected properties as it likes and the SDK prints every name. A refusal this file does not recognise is left exactly as it is: it is either Ghost's own sentence (which the guard already reached) or a shape with no rendering here.

**A PROMPT'S OWN ARGUMENTS ARE RENDERED, NOT INTERPOLATED (#1712).** `record_decision` puts two caller-supplied values into text the client hands to a model — the `project_id` and the `topic` — and only the first goes through a renderer. The topic used to be interpolated raw into the DESCRIPTION the client shows beside the prompt's name and into the USER MESSAGE it sends: a topic holding a quote closed the quoted slot it sat in and added a sentence of its own to the instruction — `x" for project "other".` made the message name two project ids before it asked for the decision — and a token-shaped topic came back whole where the project identifier beside it was withheld by `memory.ProjectArg`. The topic goes through that same renderer now (`memory.ProjectArg("topic", …)`), so a credential-shaped topic is withheld behind a placeholder naming the argument and the credential's FORMAT, and any other topic comes back QUOTED — ONE quoted span, which is what a quote or a line break inside it cannot escape, and what keeps the message one line and one instruction. The quoting escapes the invisible classes too: a control character and a FORMAT character (category Cf) come back as Go escapes, so a RIGHT-TO-LEFT OVERRIDE cannot reorder the line and a ZERO WIDTH SPACE cannot hide a spelling inside it — while an ordinary non-Latin topic comes back as itself, because escaping is not mangling. It is `ProjectArg` and not `RefusalArg` because the topic must come back WHOLE: it is what the decision is ABOUT, and a placeholder would leave the agent no subject to record. An ordinary topic is byte-identical to what it was, because `ProjectArg` returns exactly `%q` for a value the guard does not recognise — and the DESCRIPTION grew the same quotes its sibling prompt's description already had, because a renderer for a quoted slot and a second unquoted spelling of the same slot would be two rules. The property is held by the prompt's own tests, which read the span the template leaves for the topic and require it to be ONE quoted span that unquotes back to the caller's topic.

**THE TWO DIAGNOSTIC TOOLS READ THROUGH THE DIAGNOSTIC VIEW, AND EVERY OTHER READ TOOL KEEPS THE STORE'S OWN READS (#1738).** `ghost_health` and `ghost_list_projects` are the two tools whose whole job is to report the store's state, so they are the two that must not wait behind a write: they read through the store's read-only handle, in ONE read transaction, and every figure in the report — the project list and its counts, the store's schema version, the embedding coverage, the link figures, the pinned contradictions, the history growth and the retrieval figures — comes from that one snapshot, so the report's numbers agree with each other and the whole answer arrives while the write it describes is still in flight. The retrieval block and the store-version line take the store as an argument for that reason: they read the concrete store, so they read the same view as the rest of the report rather than the primary handle behind it. The view is held for the report's STORE STATEMENTS only, because the read handle has one connection and the retrieval snapshot needs it: the embedder's liveness and model probes are network calls, so they run before the view is opened, and a report that held the connection across a probe would make a concurrent search wait for it. Every OTHER read tool keeps today's design (the primary handle and `s.mu`): a reader that wants its own writes back inside one tool call has to stay on the primary handle, and the vector-cache epoch reads are bound to it — moving them is a design of its own, not this fix. A store that cannot offer a view (a test double, or a store built without a read handle) falls back to the store's own reads, so both tools still answer. Guarded by `TestHealthAnswersWhileAWriteIsInFlight`, `TestListProjectsAnswersWhileAWriteIsInFlight` and `TestTheDiagnosticToolsWorkWithoutAReadHandle`.

- `internal/hostevent/` — Normalized host-event contract (v1) and the transcript scanners; every parse or validation failure is an error the caller turns into "allow the stop", and a payload past `maxPayloadBytes` is one of them (nothing else on the path bounded it — the hook read stdin with an unbounded `io.ReadAll`, and `Parse` retains the payload decoded and again verbatim in `Raw`). The hook now reads through `hostevent.ReadPayload`, which stops one byte past the ceiling, so the bound limits the allocation and not only the parse. Field values are text the host did not write, so only the contract's own fields decide the envelope: an unknown field cannot become a known one, an instruction-shaped `hook_event_name` normalizes to nothing, and the goose native aliases (`event`, `working_dir`) bind only for `--source goose` and only per field where the dialect field is absent. `GhostSaves` counts STRUCTURE, never prose — a save tool named inside a text part, a shell command or an opencode Code Mode `execute` body is not a save, because that count is the save-nudge's evidence. `ScanAudit` is a SECOND registry beside the save-nudge's, and it has to be separate: the two want different things from the same transcript and only one of them can be right for a given host — the nudge must not read a tool's arguments (or prose naming a save tool would block a stop) and the audit must not skip a tool's output (or every use would be invisible), so bundling them into one struct would make every host's shape a compromise of both. `TestScanAuditRegistryCoversEveryScanFormat` is what keeps the two in step, because a format added to one and not the other would silently disable the audit, or the nudge, for that host's users. An unregistered format returns ok=false and NEVER a signals value, so a caller cannot persist a verdict derived from nothing; a PARTIAL read returns the signals it did read marked degraded, because the lines that were read are real evidence and discarding them would turn a partial answer into none. The token hasher is a PARAMETER of every scanner rather than something they build: a scanner handed no hasher would produce a Signals with no tokens, which is `Empty`, and the caller would read that as "this transcript held no evidence" rather than as "no key" (`TestAScanWithNoKeyProducesNoTokens`). **The working-moment events are `message-submit` and `edit` (#973), spelled `UserPromptSubmit` and `PostToolUse` in a payload, and they are gated by `Capability.WorkingMoment`, which only `claude-code` has:** another source's payload for them is a silent no-op, never output a host would misread.

- `internal/mcpinit/` — `ghost mcp init`, `ghost mcp status`, `ghost hook <event> --source <host>` (contract-v1 lifecycle dispatch; the session-start block's MEMORY ROWS are `assemble.Run`'s (`loadSessionPassive`, `session_passive.go`) and its bucket policies are ONE statement, `sessionPassiveBudget`, because the project bucket and `_global` genuinely disagree and two copies of a policy is how they drifted; the project slice is CONDITIONAL on a project matching and the global slice is not, which is the case the old shape got for free — the global loader ran above the project resolution, so an unrecognised directory still rendered a Global section. THE BLOCK ALSO RECORDS (#850): `loadSessionPassive` takes an `assemble.RecordSink` and `loadSessionContext` opens one per resolved session through `sessionRecordSink`, because the block a session is OPENED with is a retrieval like any other and the audit's per-source denominator had a `session_start` column that could only ever be zero. That handle is a SECOND one and has to be: the reads above run through `memory.OpenReadDB`, whose DSN is `mode=ro`, so wiring the record to the store already in hand would compile, satisfy `RecordSink`, and fail on every session — with the failure swallowed by the very fail-open contract the record is written under, which is the worst combination available (every behavioural test stays green and the audit is simply never written; `TestTheSessionStoreCarriesTheRecordToo` is the source assertion that pins the difference). It is `bumpSessionCount`'s construction — `rwDSN`, the `os.Stat` guard so a missing database is never CREATED by the write meant to describe it, `TightenPermissions` before and after — but it builds through `sessionStore` rather than `memory.OpenDB`, because `OpenDB` is the constructor that MIGRATES and a hook must not migrate a store behind a live server's back; #746 is then free, since the write runs `Store.RecordRetrieval` and that checks the store version inside the record transaction. An UNMATCHED directory records NOTHING, and says nothing either: `RecordRetrieval` refuses an empty project id, and neither `_global` nor a placeholder is an honest attribution — a store that cannot record must not print a refusal on every session a user opens in a directory Ghost has never seen. `SessionID` stays empty here and on the search path alike, because over stdio — the transport Ghost ships — it is `""` for both; `source` is the exact discriminator. Everything else the digest carries — the learned summary, the open tasks, the active decisions and the interaction count — is still a direct read, because the assembler's `Item` has no field for any of it; the two TOTAL counts are not a read at all, `sessionTally` counting them from the trace of the retrieval the block was just assembled from plus one `Store.PassiveEligibleCount` per bucket (the same `passivePopulationSQL` population the key scan reads, so a withheld row is never counted twice and the rows beyond the window are attributed to the ranking), so "of M total" is the store's eligible rows and never the over-fetch window's size — and that count is CAPPED at `PassiveEligibleCountCap`, past which it reports the cap as a LOWER BOUND (`capped`), `BucketTally.CountedAtLeast` marks the tally `AtLeast`, and the header reads "of 10000+ total — 9985+ not shown", never the window's size (#1531; `TestPassiveEligibleCountCapIsBounded` pins the cap's size); the two `COUNT(*)`s this package used to run disagreed with the window, and a header built from one while the rows came from the other is exactly how a block ended up calling rows "ranked out" that a stage had withheld. The all-withheld sentence is `assemble.EmptyNote` (the same function `ghost_project_context` prints) and `BucketTally.WithheldNote`, which asks `abstention` for the cause rather than keeping a sentence table of its own ([#897](https://github.com/wcatz/ghost/issues/897)), and BOTH buckets render it ([#1316](https://github.com/wcatz/ghost/issues/1316)): the `_global` half used to drop its section entirely when every one of its rows was withheld, so a block whose cross-project rows had all retired read exactly like one for a store that holds no globals while `ghost://memories/global` said those rows were withheld — it now keeps its `Global (applies to all projects)` heading, the same withheld sentence and the same `ghost_memories_list` pointer the project half prints, with the origin-guidance sentence dropped because there is no shown row for it to describe; a store with no global rows at all keeps today's output (no heading, no note). That section survives an unmatched directory on every surface: `RenderSessionContextAt` and `SessionBlockAt` return early on "no project and no rendered globals" only when `tally.globals.WithheldNote()` is empty too, the current reading's form of the `--as-of` path's `withheldAtT == 0` guard, so `ghost context`, opencode's block and `ghost bench --passive` print exactly the hook's block, less its trailing newline (`TestUnmatchedDirectoryKeepsAWhollyWithheldGlobalSectionOnEverySurface`). The per-item PREVIEW budget stays with the renderer rather than riding on `Slice.ClampBytes`: a preview has to SAY it was cut, `ClampBytes` cannot append an ellipsis, and a caller cannot tell a clamped row from one that happened to be exactly its budget, which is the distinction the ellipsis needs; the stored row is untouched either way. Every query this package still issues against `memories` is a `COUNT(*)`, asserted by a source scan — there is none since #897, and the scan stays for what a count names no row, while a row projection is a choice about which memories a session is shown, and a choice made here is a second implementation of the assembler's stages (the scope and tier schema-version probes that lived here for two releases are gone with the fetch they guarded, because `internal/memory` owns those substitutions in `passiveColumnsFor` and the store reads the version through its read SNAPSHOT); installers: claude-code, opencode plugin, codex TOML+hooks.json, goose Agent-Plugins package; goose field aliasing lives in hostevent.Parse). Every write to a file the user owns — settings.json, codex config.toml and hooks.json, the opencode plugin, the goose package, the MEMORY.md redirect — goes through `writeFileAtomic`/`writeFileAtomicPrivate` in settings.go, never a bare os.WriteFile: temp file in the same dir, flushed (data and mode) with `f.Sync()`, then rename, then the parent directory flushed, so an interrupted write cannot truncate a config, a reader holding it open still sees a whole document, and a power cut can leave neither an empty config nor a missing one. A temp-file flush failure stops the write and is reported; a directory-flush failure is not (the rename already landed, and Windows has no directory flush at all). The rename carries the permission bits across and nothing else — not the owner, POSIX ACLs, xattrs or hardlinks, so a hardlinked config in a dotfiles repo silently diverges from its repo copy. `writeFileAtomicPrivate` additionally clamps the mode to 0600 and is used only by settings.json, because that is the ceiling every pre-refactor save enforced; the other files keep whatever mode the user chose, even where a codex `[mcp_servers.ghost.env]` sub-table can hold a credential. Ghost's own marker/scratch files are exempt. Inside settings.json's `hooks`, `addHook` and `replaceHookCommand` keep every per-event entry and every hook action as raw JSON (#1214): adding a hook appends one entry, and the stale-path repair rewrites only the one `command` string, so a user's `timeout`, `async`, a non-command hook's own payload field and any key the host adds later survive — the `hookEntry`/`hookAction` structs are for building Ghost's entry and for reading, never for re-marshalling a user's array (`TestUserHookFieldsSurviveInit`, `TestUserHookFieldsSurviveStaleRepair`). **THE PRE-CONTRACT MIGRATION APPLIES THE SAME EXECUTABLE TEST AS THE STALE-PATH REPAIR (#1322):** `migratePreContractHook` rewrites a bare `… hook session-start` / `… hook stop` onto the contract form only when its leading token is a ghost binary (`isGhostBinaryName`), so a wrapper or another tool whose command happens to end in the same argument tail keeps its command on every init or re-run; the migration runs ahead of `reconcileHook`, so without the guard the foreign command never reached `isStaleGhostHookCommand`'s own check and was silently replaced (`TestMigratePreContractHook_LeavesAForeignCommandAlone`, for both events). **A HOOK GHOST DOES NOT OWN IS NOT A HOOK GHOST HAS, AND A WRAPPED GHOST INVOCATION IS ONE GHOST OWNS (#1326).** `reconcileHookMatcher` decides ownership by the EXECUTABLE, not by a substring: the command reconciled for an event is one that carries that event's `hook <event>` tail IMMEDIATELY PRECEDED, token for token, by a ghost-binary token — the one this run writes, under whatever name it carries (`isBakedGhostBinary`, the same recognition the Codex merge applies), or a `ghost` basename under any path and any quoting (`isGhostBinaryName`) — reading EVERY token of the command with the same quoting `splitHookCommand` applies to the leading one (`hookCommandTokens`). Reading every token, not only the leading one, is what makes a hand-edited WRAPPER ghost's own hook: `env X=1 /path/ghost hook session-start` and `cd d && ghost hook stop` do run ghost, so init leaves them byte-identical and adds NO second entry beside them — two entries would fire the session-start context and the stop hook twice. A command that does not run ghost — another tool's command that merely contains the words `hook session-start`, with no ghost-binary token before them — is left exactly as written AND is never counted as ghost's: ghost's own entry is added beside it, because "leave it alone" and "treat it as ghost's hook" are different decisions and the second reported an event as already configured over an install that had written nothing, so Ghost's session-start context and stop hook never ran under a success line. The read that produced this was positional and by substring (`findHookCommand`, still there for reading, never for deciding): another tool's command ending in the same argument tail sorted first as often as ghost's own did, and whichever won decided what the reconcile saw. Scanning the whole event list for ghost's own command is also what keeps the fix idempotent: the foreign entry still sorts first on the second run, so a version that only appended beside the FIRST match would add a second copy of ghost's hook on every re-run. Only a command whose LEADING token is ghost's binary is eligible for the in-place rewrite — the exact-string Windows legacy-quoting migration, and `isStaleGhostHookCommand`'s leading-token stale-path repair — so a wrapped invocation is recognised, never rewritten. `isGhostHookCommand` is the one ownership predicate, shared by both arms; `TestEnsureHook_InstallsItsOwnHookBesideAForeignCommand` runs the whole `ensureHook`/`ensureStopHook` path for both events and asserts the foreign command survives, ghost's is added once, and a second run adds nothing; `TestEnsureHook_RecognisesTheBinaryItWouldWrite` pins the baked-binary arm of the ownership test, where a test on the basename alone would install a second entry beside the first and the hook would fire twice; and `TestEnsureHook_TreatsAWrappedGhostCommandAsItsOwn` and `TestEnsureHook_TreatsAWrappedGhostCommandAsItsOwn` pin the wrapper arm for both events in an `env …`, a `cd … &&`, a leading-whitespace and a quoted-argument shape — untouched, exactly one command, a fixed point on the second run. **A QUOTED ARGUMENT THAT CARRIES THE WHOLE INVOCATION IS READ, BEHIND A GATE, AND A TRAILING UNCLOSED QUOTE COSTS NOTHING (#1337).** `ghostHookTail` failed closed on two shapes that `splitHookCommand` hides: `sh -c "ghost hook stop …"` and `bash -lc "…"` put ghost's command in ONE quoted token, so `sh`'s argument was read as a bare word and the event looked unowned; and a command whose LAST token's quoting never closes threw away the tokens read before it (`ghost hook stop --source claude-code; echo "oops`). Both made `reconcileHookMatcher` append a second entry, which fires the hook twice. The interior of a quoted argument is therefore read, but only when the word before it makes it a command list — a shell's own command-list flag, a single-dash flag ending in `c` (`-c`, `-lc`) DIRECTLY AFTER A SHELL NAME (`sh`, `bash`, `zsh`, `dash`, `ksh`, `ash`, `busybox`, `carriesCommandArgument`), because the flag shape alone belongs to any tool: `othertool -c "ghost hook stop …"` and `othertool --sync "…"` hand the invocation over as data to another tool and stay foreign (#1339) — or a shell name taking the script positionally (`busybox sh SCRIPT`) — and only where the ghost binary is in COMMAND position there (first token, or after a connective, an `X=1` assignment prefix, or a word that runs the command behind it unchanged — `sudo`, `env [-i] [NAME=VALUE …]`, `nice`, `command`, `exec` — the scan walking back until something else appears, so `echo sudo ghost hook stop …` still runs echo, `startsCommand`, #1339). That gate and that position rule are what keep another tool's ARGUMENT foreign: `othertool --note "the ghost hook stop …"`, `othertool --note "run: ghost hook stop …; done"` and `othertool -c "ghost hook stop …"` are not gated and not command position, and `sh -c "echo ghost hook stop …"` runs `echo`, not ghost. `hookCommandTokens` returns the tokens it read even when a later token will not parse, and `ghostHookTail` uses them (`TestEnsureHook_AnimatesAQuotedInvocationAndAFailSoftCommand` pins all three arms: every recognised shape untouched with one command and no re-add, the same shapes never rewritten by the legacy or stale-path repair, and every foreign argument still added beside; `TestEnsureHook_RecognisesTransparentPrefixesInsideAShellScript` pins the #1339 shapes in both directions — the prefix forms inside a script recognised, untouched and status-wired, the same words behind another tool's command and another tool's `-c` or `--sync` still foreign). One ambiguity remains accepted and inherent, at the top level only: the spec's own `env X=1 ghost …` requirement means an unquoted ghost binary in command position reads as ghost's own, whatever tool put it there. The flag-shape ambiguity a `-c` of any tool used to create — handed a complete ghost invocation as that flag's value — is closed by the shell-name requirement (#1339). **The SAME ownership rule is what `ghost mcp status` reads (#1326):** `contractHookWired` — and `ReportStaleIntegrations` behind `ghost upgrade` — asks `ghostHookTail` for the event's tail before it looks for the `--source` token, so the diagnostic and the reconcile answer the same question about the same file. A reader that matched the tail alone, as this one did, reported a foreign command as `✓ configured` over a file init was about to add to, and reported a wrapper that does run ghost as missing and sent the operator to a `ghost mcp init` that rewrites nothing; `TestStatus_ForeignHookIsNotWired` and `TestStatus_WrappedGhostHookIsWired` pin both directions. **A LEADING UTF-8 BYTE-ORDER MARK IS STRIPPED BEFORE A CODEX `config.toml` IS SCANNED, AND PUT BACK ON WRITE (#1370).** U+FEFF is not whitespace, so a mark a Windows editor left in front of the first line hid a BOM-prefixed ghost table from `findCodexTOMLTable`, the ambiguous-header check and the dotted-key check alike — `ghost mcp init --client codex` read the table as absent and appended a SECOND `[mcp_servers.ghost]`, which TOML rejects as a duplicate key, while printing success, and the status reader read the file the same way, so the broken install was reported healthy. `splitCodexLines` removes one leading mark from the body before splitting — ONE, matching a TOML parser, because a U+FEFF anywhere else is content — the merge and `codexMCPEntryStatus` both read through it, and the merge re-prepends the mark at the very start of the joined output so the repaired file keeps its encoding and the mark sits where the parser expects it; only the leading mark is touched, so every other byte of a config the user also edits by hand is preserved. `TestRunCodex_TOMLLeadingBOM` pins the repair in place with one header, the preserved mark, a second run as a fixed point, and both other guards (a dotted definition and an unparseable header are refused rather than appended beside). The JSON readers split by host: Claude Code reads a BOM-prefixed settings.json, so `loadSettings` strips one leading mark, remembers it on `settingsFile.bom` and `save` writes it back first; codex hooks.json (`decodeCodexHooksConfig`, `loadCodexHooksRules`; serde_json rejects the mark) and opencode `parseOpencodeConfig` (its JSONC reader reports an invalid symbol at offset 0) are refused by `leadingBOMError`, which names the byte-order mark, and never rewritten. `TestRunMergesBOMSettings`, `TestCodexHooksJSONBOMIsRefusedByName` and `TestParseOpencodeConfig_BOMIsRefusedByName` pin it. Two files get a one-time `.bak` through `writeBackupOnce`: settings.json on its first save, and codex `config.toml` the first time Ghost writes to it — the first init, which appends the table to a file the user already had, so the copy is the pre-ghost file (a repair-only backup could never be, since a repair can only find a table that is already there). Neither is ever rolled forward, so a later run cannot overwrite the only pristine copy with Ghost's own output, and a 0-byte config.toml is skipped rather than copied — an empty backup would stand forever and block the real one. It also owns the read-only stale-server scan (`ReportStaleServers`, #746), which reports running `ghost mcp` processes whose executable is unlinked or is not the binary this command is, so `ghost mcp status` and `ghost upgrade` can name the clients to restart. The session hook's `bumpSessionCount` is the ONE write into a memory store that does not go through `internal/memory`'s guarded seams — the structural test behind those seams walks that package, and this one opens its own `sql.Open` handle, as `sessionRecordSink` does for a different reason — so it reads `PRAGMA user_version` itself and skips the write when the store has moved past this build. Every write connection it opens (`rwDSN`, which `sessionRecordSink` shares) sets `foreign_keys(ON)` like `memory.OpenDB`, and the counter's statement inserts only for a project that still exists, so a project deleted between the hook resolving it and the bump is nothing to count — `return 0`, silent — rather than an orphan `ghost_state` row the next migration refuses the store over (#1155); `TestRwDSNEnforcesForeignKeys` reads the pragma back from a live connection because `TestMultiProcessSharedDatabase` never opens this DSN. It is checked inside the write's own transaction: `rwDSN` carries `_txlock=immediate`, so `db.BeginTx` takes the write lock at BEGIN, `PRAGMA user_version` is read through that transaction, and the upsert runs in the same one, so a migration cannot commit between the check and the write. The project-existence check is inside the upsert statement itself. The counter is still best-effort: every failure path rolls back and is `return 0`. The skip is silent, because a line printed on every session start lands in front of an agent on every start, and `ghost_health` plus `ghost mcp status` already say it where a human is looking. Four properties are load-bearing. It NEVER signals, kills or restarts anything, because only the operator knows which client is safe to interrupt. It detects by EXECUTABLE IDENTITY (the kernel's own `(deleted)` marker on `/proc/<pid>/exe`, then `os.SameFile`) and not by a version string, because a running process's version is text inside a binary this code must not execute, and the installed path plus the deletion marker are facts the kernel already keeps. It reports a THIRD state, "could not answer", as silence on a non-Linux platform and as `checked=false`, so an unreadable tree is never rendered as an empty list that reads as "nothing is stale" — and UNKNOWN is kept distinct from a FINDING at the per-process level too: `sameFile` is three-valued, because a two-valued one cannot say "could not read", and a `ghost mcp` running from a root-owned `/opt/ghost` at 0700 has an `/proc/<pid>/exe` this user cannot stat. Rendering that as "a different binary" would tell an operator to restart a healthy client, so the process is skipped: unreported is a small loss, wrongly reported costs a session. And its WORDING is derived from the evidence each finding carries, because `installed` is `os.Executable()` — the binary running this command — and not a discovered installation, so a `go run` build, a `make build` output and a `~/bin` copy are all ordinary ways to invoke the command and all have current clients. Only `ExeDeleted` is a fact about the world and is reported as "replaced"; a plain mismatch says "a different ghost binary than this one", and nothing anywhere claims a binary "is no longer installed", which asserts an installation this code never looked for. `ghost upgrade` compares against the path it just replaced, not `os.Executable()`, which is the old image this process is still running. Its consequence is hedged ("writes may be refused") because the scan never opens the store and so cannot assert the refusal happened; `ghost_health` is where that is actually reported. The session-start block's project name goes through `assemble.Label` rather than `Token`, because a name is a label and is normally full of spaces, and it is agent-supplied: `ensureProjectFor` stores the caller's `project_id` argument as the project's name as well as its id. `TestTheSessionStartHeadingCannotForgeALine` pins the heading and asserts the spaced cases verbatim, which is what the choice was for. The retrieval audit rides this hook and is the one thing it writes: the sidecar is a temp file named `ghost-audit-*.signals` whose path is handed to the detached lifecycle child on its command line, which is why it is a `CreateTemp` name rather than one composed from a project id. What crosses the gap is IDS and KEYED TOKENS — never transcript text, query text or memory content — and the transcript side is a READ: no store, no config, nothing opened, because the hook runs synchronously and must not touch the database. It resolves the per-install key through `memory.ReadRetrievalKey` BEFORE opening the transcript, and that one failure is the single NOT-fail-open step: a scan without a key produces no tokens, so "no key" and "no evidence" would be the same answer, and the second of those gets persisted as a claim about the session (`TestNoKeyMeansNoSidecarAndStillALifecycle` — and the lifecycle chain still runs, because the audit is a passenger of phases that are worth running whether or not this turn produced evidence). Two files are cleaned up rather than left to a sweep, because both are this run's own artefacts: a sidecar whose `startLifecycleChild` failed is REMOVED (a file written for a child that never started has no reader and no expiry, `TestASidecarWrittenForAChildThatNeverStartedIsRemoved`), and the child deletes the sidecar it read ONLY after the read succeeded AND the file was named like one — `lifecycle --signals <any path>` used to delete whatever it was handed, and it is an internal subcommand, which makes it exactly as reachable as the other internal phases (`TestRunLifecycleDoesNotDeleteAFileItWasHanded`, `TestRunLifecycleStillDeletesTheSidecarItRead`). **Session-start records carry the hook payload's `session_id` (#648)** through `loadSessionContextFor` into `loadSessionPassive`; `loadSessionContext` is the wrapper with no session (`ghost context`, opencode's plugin) and records none, so that block is unjudged. The stop hook's `scanAuditSignals` stamps the same payload id on the signals (`TestTheStopHookNamesTheSessionInTheSidecar`). The session-start block has a BYTE CAP on the whole render (#969): `sessionStartByteCap` (9000) is measured by `Budget.Measure` on `formatSessionContext`'s own output for the rows kept so far (header, summary, rows, tasks, decisions, counts line), not on the assembler's search envelope, because Claude Code caps a hook's plain stdout and `additionalContext` at 10,000 characters and over it replaces the block with a 2,000-character preview; the 1,000-byte margin is what the render cannot see: the session-number line, a lifecycle alert on the same stdout, and the trailing newline. **THE ALERT IS INSIDE THAT MARGIN (#1723):** the alert line carries a byte cap of its own (`lifecycleAlertMaxBytes`, 900 of those 1,000 bytes), so the margin HOLDS rather than being slack the alert could spend — a marker written by hand names a 300-byte project and carries a long error, and the alert rendered the project TWICE beside that error, so the line alone reached about 1,200 bytes and the block's cap did not know the line was there. The cap is on the LINE and not on each field alone, because the fields are not independent: the project is printed twice on the line and the phase list is a list, so bounding the error alone left the line unbounded in the tail. The parts are paid for in the order the reader needs them — the sentence and the timestamp, then the error (it says WHY) and the phases — and the project takes what is left, halved because it is printed twice (`lifecycleAlertProject`), so an ordinary project renders exactly as it did before. The phase list is bounded too (`renderMarkerPhases`: the entries it can carry are named and the ones it left out are COUNTED), and the error field is bounded as it RENDERS (`markerErrorContent`: the quoting escapes a control rune to up to six bytes, so a content at its own cap can render six times as long — the longest prefix whose quoted form still fits is taken, so an error carrying two colour codes keeps almost all of its text). **EVERY CUT ON THAT LINE IS MADE ON THE RAW VALUE, AND THE ESCAPED FORM IS WHAT THE BOUND IS MEASURED AGAINST.** The renderer escapes a control rune to an escape sequence and a backtick to a backslash and a backtick, so cutting the ESCAPED form lands inside an escape sequence — “\x” where the value held “\x1b” — and a half-written escape swallows the retry command's own backtick, leaving the command the reader is told to run with an open span. The project's share (`renderMarkerProject`), a phase name (`markerPhaseEntry`) and the error line (`markerErrorContent`) therefore all cut the raw value at a rune boundary, searching over the ESCAPED length of a prefix (`markerPrefixWithin`, the ONE search the three share), and the ellipsis goes on after the escaping so it reads as the cut it is. **THAT SEARCH RETURNS THE LONGEST PREFIX THAT FITS, NOT THE FIRST ONE IT FINDS.** The bytes are walked in halves, so the candidate is moved to a rune boundary — down to the boundary before it, and, when the whole stretch below the candidate is one character, UP to the boundary after it. Stopping at the lower bound instead loses every boundary above it, and a value led by a multi-byte character puts all of them out of reach: an emoji at the front of an error emptied the whole field, and one at the front of a project rendered it as an ellipsis although several bytes of it fitted. The search is measured against the brute-force longest fit over rune boundaries, over the three measures the cuts use (`TestMarkerPrefixWithinFindsTheLongestFit`, `TestMarkerPrefixWithinMatchesTheLongestFitOverRandomValues`). A phase entry that cannot be shown at all — not even one rune of it fits once it is rendered — is dropped and counted rather than shown half (`TestSessionStart_ProjectCutNeverSplitsAnEscape`, `TestSessionStart_ProjectCutLeavesTheRetryCommandSpanned`, `TestSessionStart_PhaseEntryKeepsItsQuoting`). `TestSessionStart_AlertAndBlockStayUnderTheHostLimit` is the case the cap exists for — a store whose block reaches the cap and a marker naming a 300-byte project with a long error, whose session-start output was over the host's limit — `TestSessionStart_AlertLineIsBounded` pins the line's own cap, `TestSessionStart_AlertNamesOnlyThePhasesItCanCarry` the list's bound and its count, and `TestSessionStart_AlertKeepsTheReason` the order the parts are cut in. The cap sits in the shared load, so it applies to every host that renders this block (Claude Code, codex) and to `ghost context` / `RenderSessionContext` output alike. **THE HISTORICAL READ CARRIES THE SAME TWO BUDGETS (#1706).** The `--as-of` half of `RenderSessionContextAt` (`renderHistoricalSessionContext`) does not go through that shared load — it reads the recorded set through the store and builds its own rows — so neither the per-item PREVIEW nor the whole-block cap reached it, and a project holding fifteen memories at the store's own 8,000-byte content cap produced a block of over a hundred thousand bytes; a host over its 10,000-character limit replaces the whole block with a 2,000-character preview, so the reader got a fragment of a block rather than the block. It now reaches BOTH through the current path's own pieces, because a second copy of either rule is a second rule: the rows are the assembler's own (`historicalSessionItems`), so the PREVIEW is the row builder's (`sessionRowsFrom` — 200 bytes for a project row and 300 for a global one), and the CAP is the fit rule's (`assemble.FitItems`, the ONE implementation the response-fit post-pass itself runs: the lowest-ranked row goes first, an UNPINNED one before any pinned one, the block is re-measured after every cut because the count line grows with each cut, and a framing already over the host's own 10,000 keeps its rows rather than cutting them for nothing). The rows it gives up are counted as cut FOR SIZE (`BucketTally.ByteCut`), so the count line says which rows the cap took. Stage 8's response-fit pass cuts the lowest-ranked UNPINNED row first (`Budget.KeepPinned`) and a pinned row only when none other is left, recording every cut as a `response_fit` decision in the trace and the retrieval record, so a cut row is never recorded as delivered; the count line says so (`BucketTally.ByteCut`, a subset of `RankedOut`). A block under the cap is byte-identical to the uncapped one. The framing is counted, and three of its fields are bounded in bytes at the read (`loadSessionContextFrom`: learned summary 1,000, task and decision titles 120, descriptions 200 as before). That is NOT a guarantee the framing fits: `quoteData` folds each line break to a 3-byte marker, and the project name and ids are unbounded. Rows are cut down to none if that reaches the cap; only when the framing alone is at or over the host's 10,000 (`Budget.FramingCeiling`) does the fit pass keep the rows rather than cut every one for nothing. **The working-moment channel (`workmoment.go`, `workmoment_marker.go`, #973) emits NOTHING unless a project row clears its own keyword floor, and every error is that same nothing (exit 0, empty stdout, no stderr on the normal path).** Keyword-only by construction (`CondFTSOnly`, no embedding, no model). The floor is the channel's own because the assembler's cutoff always keeps the top row and the no-answer bar needs a cosine: a row clears it on its own text with one distinctive term (an identifier-shaped or file-name-shaped term of at least 3 characters, matched on word boundaries) OR three distinct ordinary words, the generic development words (`genericWords`: fix, test, build, run, update, docs, fail, error, change, add, remove, code, file, go, make and their forms) never counting, so a generic message delivers nothing. Output is `hookSpecificOutput.additionalContext` for BOTH events (PostToolUse plain stdout is not context), capped at 1,500 bytes measured on that string and at the session's remaining allowance; `_global` rows and a subagent payload (`agent_id`) are never delivered to. The per-session marker `working-moment-<digest>.log` lives in `config.DataDirPath()` (a refused resolve or an empty session id means no path and so no delivery, never a fallback), is 0600, and `recordSessionStartDelivery` sweeps those older than 7 days at session start. Its stem is `markerStem` — the first 16 bytes of the SHA-256 of the session id, lowercase hex — and NOT `sanitizeMarkerProject`, which folds every character outside `[A-Za-z0-9._-]` onto `_` and caps the result at 80 bytes: "sess a" and "sess_a", and two ids sharing their first 80 characters, folded onto ONE file, so the second session's delivery found the row already delivered and the first session's bytes already spent, with no error and exit 0 (#1724; `TestTwoSessionIdsThatFoldOntoOneNameKeepSeparateLedgers`, `TestTheSessionIdIsDigestedIntoTheMarkerName`). A marker an older build wrote under the sanitized stem is never read — that name is not injective, so reading it would give those two sessions one ledger again — and the sweep removes it with the others (`TestAMarkerUnderTheOldNameIsNeverReadAndIsSwept`). It is APPEND-ONLY and a claim wins by file order (`readMomentState`): an entry is valid when none of its ids was claimed by an earlier valid entry and its bytes fit what earlier valid entries left, so parallel PostToolUse hooks agree on one winner and the loser stays silent. The session-start block writes the first entry (rows rendered, bytes spent including a lifecycle alert line), and a marker without one assumes the start spent `sessionStartByteCap`; the shared allowance is `sessionHostLimit`. A delivery is recorded once with source `working_moment` and the withheld rows as dropped verdicts; a silent turn records nothing. `ghost mcp init` registers the pair with `reconcileHookMatcher` (matcher `Edit|Write|MultiEdit` for PostToolUse) and the plugin manifests carry them; `ghost mcp status` reports them as optional and does not count them against health. WHETHER CUTTING CAN HELP IS DECIDED ONCE, FROM A FRAMING THAT DOES NOT DEPEND ON THE CUTS (#987): the pass asks the ceiling question on the first over-cap iteration and never re-asks it, and it asks it against the framing as it stands with EVERY row cut (`allCutTrace`, a COPY of the trace carrying one `response_fit` decision per row handed to `Measure` beside an empty item slice — the longest the count line can ever be, and the only reading that does not change as rows are removed). The framing is not independent of the cuts — the count line gains a clause per row cut — so a per-iteration reading asked a different question each time and the order of the two readings was the bug: a framing just under the ceiling passed the first iteration, a row was cut, the clause that cut earned pushed the framing over the ceiling, and the next iteration stopped, leaving the block over the cap with rows recorded as cut for nothing and a count line claiming the cut had kept it under a limit the block was still over. A block whose framing reaches the host limit even with every row cut therefore keeps every row, and the count line never claims a cut that did not keep the block under the limit; `TestSessionStartFitPassDecidesTheCeilingOnce` (a 3,184-byte project name, so the framing sits in the band a single cut crosses) and `TestTheFramingCeilingIsDecidedFromTheAllCutFraming` pin it from the two sides, and `TestTheFramingCeilingStillCutsWhenTheFramingCannotReachIt` pins that a once-only decision is not a decision never to cut. **A SESSION-START FIXTURE STAMPS EVERY ROW FROM ONE INSTANT AND NEVER LEAVES `created_at` TO `datetime('now')` (#1002). The decay ranking's last key, after score, importance and pin, is `created_at DESC`, so filler rows that tie on all of it are ordered by which side of a wall-clock second each INSERT happened to land on — and the insert loop is long enough to straddle one: fifteen rows run about 10 ms on an idle laptop and far longer on a loaded runner, so a second boundary falls inside the loop regularly. When the boundary falls between the last two INSERTs, the promoted row changes WHICH filler the 15-cap cuts, which is exactly how `TestSessionInjectionUsesDecayRanking` read as intermittent on ubuntu-26-canary and on windows-11-arm: every other assertion held and the one that named a specific filler did not. The bench corpus had the same hole before #708 and closed it with `corpusStamp`, and the golden's guard is `TestGoldenFixtureIsStableAcrossRuns`. So the fixture derives every stamp — the facts' and the gotcha's alike — from one fixed instant, which makes the fixture a function of the fixture and the gotcha's 400-day age a reading of that instant rather than of the host clock. The second half of the rule is what the test asserts: with ties legitimately broken by `id`, WHICH filler the cap cuts is the diversity share's answer and not the ranking's, so a test pins the gotcha's POSITION against every fact rather than naming the fact it expects to be cut — an assertion that names one row fails on a selection that is correct and merely re-sorted. **A DRY RUN AND A STATUS RUN ARE READ-ONLY (#1049):** both resolve the data directory through `config.DataDirPath` and open the store with `memory.OpenReadDB`, so neither creates a missing store nor migrates an older one — the preview that ends "No changes made (dry run)" must not be the irreversible write it previews, and a diagnostic must not upgrade the store out from under a live server whose writes the new schema then refuses. The wiring steps keep the same tense as the read-only promise they sit under (#1264): every permission and hook step reports a change it did not write as `~ would add …`/`~ would migrate …` and prints the `+ ` marker only when it wrote — a step that printed the applied marker on a run that wrote nothing described a change the user never got, and the only marker that outlives the run is the one the write earned. Because a read-only handle runs no migration, both read `PRAGMA user_version` first and skip the store-dependent work on a mismatch: `importMemories` skips the import preview and says so, and `checkStoreHealth` reports the mismatch — a failed check for a store from a newer Ghost, an informational `-` line for an older one — instead of selecting columns it does not have. The Ollama line inside `checkStoreHealth` follows the same precision rule the embedding worker's probe does: `checkOllama` returns the tri-state, and the `ollama-down-since` duration is printed only beside a CONCLUSIVE `Unreachable` — never beside an `Inconclusive` probe, whose missed deadline is an undetermined endpoint rather than an outage and whose marker a busy machine can leave stamped for hours. The ordinary (non-dry-run) import keeps the read-write open, because importing memories is a real write. The lifecycle failure marker is one file per project, `lifecycle-last-failure-<stem>.json` in `config.DataDirPath()`, and the stem is `markerStem`: the first 16 bytes of the SHA-256 of the project, lowercase hex — never the project itself, and never `sanitizeMarkerProject`, which folds every byte outside `[A-Za-z0-9._-]` onto `_` and so keyed "my project" and "my_project" on one file, letting one project's clean run erase the other's still-active failure (#1118; `TestPunctuationDifferingProjectsKeepSeparateMarkers`). `WriteLifecycleFailure`, `ClearLifecycleFailure` (and through it `FinishLifecycleRun`) and `readLifecycleFailure` all name the file through `projectMarkerFile`. A marker an older build wrote under the sanitized stem (`oldProjectMarkerFile`) is still read after the digest names and cleared by its own project's clean run, but because that name is not injective it counts only when the `project` it records is the session's (read) or the one being cleared (clear) — another project's old-stem file is passed over on read and left on disk on clear (`TestOldStemMarkerIsReadAndCleared`, `TestOldStemMarkerOfAnotherProjectSurvivesAClear`, `TestAnotherProjectsOldStemMarkerDoesNotHideOwnLegacyMarker`). The marker's JSON `project` field is the resolved id, or for an operand nothing resolves the operand itself, unless the token guard finds a credential in it: then it records `<project withheld: it holds a FORMAT>` (`markerProjectField`), while the file stays keyed on the raw operand's digest, so two token operands keep two files and the same operand's clean run still clears its own (#1130; `TestATokenShapedOperandNeverReachesTheMarker`, `TestATokenShapedOperandsCleanRunClearsItsMarker`). The self-clean uses TWO bounds: a marker older than 30 days is expired by the age horizon, and a marker dated more than 24 hours AHEAD — past `lifecycleMarkerClockSkew`, the tolerated clock skew — is expired on the forward bound, a marker up to 24h ahead still alerting (`TestSessionStart_SlightlyFutureMarkerStillPrints`, `TestSessionStart_FutureMarkerWithinClockSkewStillPrints`) and one past it swept (`TestSessionStart_FutureMarkerPastClockSkewSelfCleans`). A future stamp — a clock set forward when the marker was written, a VM resumed with a skewed clock, or a marker copied in from another machine — has a NEGATIVE age, so an "older than" horizon alone never expires it: it would alert at every session start, unchanged, until wall time caught up. The no-project read also RANKS rather than dates it: `newestMarker` puts a stamp dated after the read's now BELOW every stamp dated at or before it, so a future-dated marker never outranks one dated at or before the read — the newest wins among the rest, and among future stamps the earliest (the least skewed) wins, so being further ahead buys no rank and a future marker still shows when it is the only candidate (`TestSessionStart_FutureMarkerDoesNotOutrankARecentFailure`, `TestSessionStart_FutureDistanceBuysNoRankInTheNoProjectRead`, `TestSessionStart_FarFutureMarkerDoesNotHideANewerFailure`, `TestSessionStart_TwentyDayFutureMarkerDoesNotHideARecentFailure`). The read also passes OVER a marker older than `lifecycleAlertMaxAge` — kept on disk but deliberately silent — rather than ending on it, so a stale marker the ranking put first, and any in-tolerance future marker it outranks, still cannot suppress a current one behind it (`TestSessionStart_SilentMarkerDoesNotSuppressAnInToleranceFutureFailure`; the pass-over is `markerSilent`, applied inside `readLifecycleFailure` and `newestMarker`, so it costs the self-clean bound no pass and cannot exhaust it on files that are never removed — `TestSessionStart_SilentMarkersConsumeNoSelfCleanPass`). A MARKER IS ONLY EVER OPENED AS A REGULAR FILE (#1638): `readMarkerAtPath` goes through `openMarker`, which opens with `markerOpenFlags` (`O_NOFOLLOW|O_NONBLOCK` on unix, plain read-only on Windows, which has neither), fstats the descriptor and refuses anything that is not a regular file before a byte is read. The data dir is writable by every local process, so a FIFO at a marker path used to hang the session-start alert, `ghost context` and the clear path in open until a writer appeared — the host either waited or killed the hook at its own timeout — and a symlink was followed to whatever it named. A marker is always a regular file this package wrote, so anything else reads as no marker on every surface that reads it: the alert, the no-project glob read and the clear path (`TestReadMarkerAtPathRefusesWhatIsNotARegularFile`, `TestSessionStart_AFIFOAtTheMarkerPathAlertsNothing`, `TestSessionStart_ASymlinkAtTheMarkerPathAlertsNothing`, `TestSessionStart_AFIFOAtTheLegacyMarkerPathAlertsNothing`, `TestClearLifecycleFailureReturnsAtOnceWithAFIFOAtTheLegacyMarkerPath`; a regular marker beside a planted FIFO still alerts, `TestSessionStart_RegularMarkerStillAlertsBesideAFIFO`). A past-skew stamp is swept when it IS the candidate read (`TestSessionStart_FarFutureMarkerSelfCleans`, `TestSessionStart_FutureMarkerPastClockSkewSelfCleans`); a future stamp that ranks below a real failure is not the file read and so stays on disk until it becomes the candidate, because the self-clean removes only the file it read.
