The 30-day self-clean removes the exact file it read and nothing else, then reads the next candidate and judges it by the same rules, so a current marker behind an expired one still alerts: it does NOT call `ClearLifecycleFailure`, which is a clean run's clear and would delete a current name-keyed failure along with a stale id-keyed one (`TestAnExpiredIDMarkerDoesNotTakeAFreshNameMarkerWithIt`), and a withheld project names no file anyway (`TestAWithheldMarkerSelfCleans`). A clean run clears EVERY marker recording its project, not only the resolved id's file (#1168): a failure recorded while the operand resolved to nothing is keyed on the operand as given, and once the project exists under that name with a different id (an imported one) neither a clean run by name (re-resolved to the id) nor one by id (which never sees the name) reached that key, so the alert said consolidation was paused after the project had recovered. `ClearLifecycleFailure` therefore also clears the digest and old-stem files keyed on the operand as given and on the project's stored name (`resolveMarkerIdentity`), each only when the file records this project (`markerProjectField` of the key for the digest file, the raw key for the old stem) AND the key is not another project's id — the recorded project alone cannot tell an unresolved `shared` from the project whose id is `shared`, because both record the bare string (`clearCheckedMarker`; `TestACleanRunClearsAMarkerKeyedOnTheUnresolvedName`, `TestACleanRunByIDClearsAMarkerKeyedOnTheProjectsName`, `TestACleanRunLeavesAnotherProjectsMarkerKeyedOnItsName`; the self-clean of the same file once expired is `TestAnExpiredMarkerKeyedOnTheUnresolvedNameSelfCleans`). Only a clean run clears every key. A key the store cannot resolve CLEANLY — a name two projects share, which is `memory.ErrAmbiguousProject` and exactly what an unresolved write keyed its file on — clears NOTHING, the resolved id's own file included: an owner that cannot be determined is not read as "not mine", or one project's clean run erases the other's still-active failure (#1268; `TestACleanRunLeavesAMarkerKeyedOnAnAmbiguousName`). A resolution error that is NOT that one — a store-less or unreadable data dir — leaves the operand as the key, exactly as the writer's own fallback did, so the operand-keyed marker is still healed (`TestACleanRunStillHealsTheOperandKeyedMarkerWithNoStore`). The legacy shared `lifecycle-last-failure.json` is read last and cleared when its `project` is the run's resolved id, or when it is the stored name or the operand as given AND that spelling resolves back to this project — the pre-per-project writer recorded the unresolved operand, often a name, and a name two projects share (or a name that is another project's id) records the same string, so a bare spelling match is not ownership (#1268; `TestALegacyMarkerKeyedOnTheProjectNameIsCleared`, `TestAnAmbiguousLegacyMarkerSurvivesACleanRun`, `TestALegacyMarkerNamingAnotherProjectsIDSurvivesACleanRun`), and an unattributed marker is cleared only by a run that itself named nothing. An ambiguous operand returns before this check, clearing nothing at all. The opencode plugin bakes the binary path as a complete JS string literal (`jsStringLiteral`, JSON escaping with HTML escaping off, so ordinary paths render unchanged) through the bare `__GHOST_BIN__` placeholder, never by splicing the raw path between quotes, so a quote, backslash or newline in the path cannot break or inject into the plugin (#1218; `TestRenderedOpencodePluginPathIsAJSLiteral`). **A REFUSED REGISTRATION PUTS BACK THE ONE IT DELETED (#1216):** `registerMCP` removes the stale entry before it adds the new one, so a `claude mcp add-json` that failed left the user with NO ghost server at all until a re-run landed — every Claude Code session started with no ghost tools — and the error did not say the entry was gone. `rollbackRegistration` re-adds the previous registration from the `claude mcp get` output the same run already read, through `parseClaudeRegistration` (`Type:`, `Command:`, `URL:`, `Env`/`Environment:` and `Headers:` are read, a JSON array as the args verbatim and a JSON object as the environment or the headers verbatim; it reports false only when there is neither a Command line nor a URL, because an http entry prints no command and the rollback can still put it back, and a bare multi-token `Args:` line is split on whitespace AND named as dropped because that rendering cannot say one argument holding a space from two), and the returned error then tells the truth about the re-add rather than promising a clean one: the previous registration was RESTORED when every line the get output carried came across, it was RE-ADDED WITHOUT the parts the rendering could not carry (its environment, its headers, its arguments, or a field it does not recognise) naming them and pointing at a re-run, and only a registration the get output cannot describe at all is REMOVED and could not be restored. Four cases are NOT rolled back, and each says which one it is rather than guessing: nothing was registered, where the error is byte-identical to the one it always was, so the rollback is a property of the repair path and not a change to every failure; a registration the get output cannot describe, because `alreadyRegistered` requires a `Command:` line and so a URL-type entry is never removed in the first place; a remove that itself failed, where the previous registration is most likely still in place and re-adding it would collide with itself, so the error names the failed remove and says the registration most likely survived; and a restore the client refuses, which is REPORTED as a removal and never retried, because a second attempt against the refused write is the failure that just happened. And the restore is the PREVIOUS config, never the new one: re-adding the registration that failed would fail again and would claim a restore that did not happen (`TestRegisterMCPRestoresThePreviousRegistrationOnAddFailure`, `TestRegisterMCPNamesTheRegistrationRemovedWhenRestoreFails`, `TestRegisterMCPDoesNotRollBackARegistrationTheRemoveLeftInPlace`, `TestRegisterMCPWithoutAPreviousRegistrationReportsNoRollback`), and the honesty clause has its own three, one per side of it: `TestRollbackRegistrationNamesWhatTheReAddDidNotCarry` (a bare environment block and an argument holding a space are carried best-effort but NAMED, so "restored" is never claimed over a drop), `TestRollbackRegistrationRestoresAnHTTPEntry` (a URL with its headers in JSON restores whole, which is the case the Command-line requirement used to call unrestorable) and `TestRollbackRegistrationCarriesTheEnvironmentAndSpacedArgument` (a JSON object environment and a JSON array argument come across exactly, so "restored" is a claim about nothing left behind rather than about nothing having been printed). **A JSON null where a settings.json object belongs is refused by name, never filled in (#1263).** A whole-document `null` decodes into a nil map with no error — an empty object leaves `loadSettings`'s initialized map in place, so nil means null and nothing else — and an explicit `"permissions": null` decodes into a nil map at the `permsMap` rebuild the same way. Both are refused before anything is written, each naming the file and the field (`settings.json is null`, `settings.json: permissions is null`) through Run's retry hint, mirroring the codex `hooks.json: hooks is null` refusal, because the alternative is a panic on assignment into the nil map part-way through init's ten steps — which leaves a registered MCP server with no hooks, no tool permissions and `autoMemoryEnabled` untouched, and prints a stack trace instead of a sentence. `addPermissions`'s `permsMap` rebuild keeps the `addHook`-style nil guard as well, so that write cannot panic even if it is reached another way. **A STRUCTURALLY INVALID settings.json IS REFUSED BEFORE ANYTHING IS REGISTERED (#1267):** `ghost mcp init` loads and validates the user's Claude Code `settings.json` before step 4 through `settingsFile.validate`, which calls the SAME readers steps 5-8 call (`getPermissions` and `hookEntries`) so the preflight and the readers are one parser and print one message. It covers the document, `permissions` and `permissions.allow`, `hooks`, and only the events init reads (`settingsReadHookEvents`: SessionStart, Stop, UserPromptSubmit, PostToolUse). A document that is not a JSON object, a whole-document `null`, and an explicit null `permissions`, `hooks` or read event, are refused by name, as is a wrong-typed value (`{"permissions":[]}`, `{"permissions":"x"}`, `{"permissions":{"allow":"x"}}`, `{"hooks":[]}`, a non-list read event), instead of surfacing the raw Go unmarshal error or panicking on the nil map a null decodes into. A null `permissions.allow` is the one null that is NOT refused: it cannot panic, the merge has always filled it in with ghost's permissions, and it is treated as empty rather than invalid. Every OTHER event is deliberately not checked: `addHook` and `replaceHookCommand` decode only the event they are handed and carry the rest as raw JSON, so a non-read event with an unusual shape (`{"hooks":{"Notification":"x"}}`, a `PreToolUse` action whose `command` is a list) is written through untouched and must not refuse the install (`TestRunInstallsSettingsItCarriesThrough`). The pass runs before `registerMCP`, so a file init cannot wire is left byte-for-byte as written and no ghost server is registered with no hooks and no tool permissions; an event name in a refusal is rendered with `%q` because it is caller text. The refusal carries the edit that clears it (`Fix the named field in <path>, then re-run ghost mcp init`) rather than Run's generic re-run hint, which a re-run cannot satisfy: the file stays invalid until the user repairs it. `TestRunRefusesMalformedSettingsBeforeRegistering` pins each refusal against a recording `claude` stub that must never be invoked, and `TestRunInstallsWithValidSettings` / `TestRunInstallsWithMissingSettings` hold the installing side. **A SESSION AT A FILESYSTEM ROOT IS RECOGNISED ON EVERY PLATFORM (#1299):** the home-dir/root fallback asks `isFilesystemRoot` (`routing.go`), which compares the cleaned cwd against the cleaned volume root — `filepath.VolumeName(cwd)` plus the platform separator — so `/` on POSIX, a drive root (`C:\`, `D:\`) and a network share root (`\\server\share\`) all count. The comparison this replaced was against the literal `/`, which no Windows path can equal, so a Windows session at a drive or share root never took the `routing.default_project` fallback while a POSIX root did. `TestIsFilesystemRoot` pins the volume-root rule on the running platform, and the windows-only `TestIsHomeOrRootRecognisesWindowsRoots` pins the drive and share roots, which no non-Windows build can produce. **The transient-transcript sweep accepts ANY temp base the host could have named, not `os.TempDir()` alone (#1305).** `cleanupTransientTranscript` recognises the plugin's `ghost-*` session directory by its parent being one of `os.TempDir()`, `TMPDIR`, `TMP` and `TEMP`, compared with `filepath.Clean` (case-insensitively on Windows), because the plugin's runtime and this hook do not resolve the temp directory the same way: the plugin strips one trailing separator Go keeps verbatim, and with `TMPDIR` unset it falls back through `TMP` then `TEMP` while `os.TempDir` reads only the system default (and on Windows the two take `TMP` and `TEMP` in opposite order). The basename must still begin `ghost-`, so a directory of that name outside every temp base is left in place — the widened base set removes nothing the single-base guard would not have removed had the runtimes agreed. **The sweep removes only the directory the plugin materialised, never any other `ghost-…` entry of the temp directory.** The temp directory is shared with Ghost's own pending audit sidecars (`ghost-audit-*.signals`, regular files), the harness scratch fall-back (`ghost-<kind>-<digits>`), the bench and eval directories and other sessions' entries, and the payload's path is not chosen by Ghost, so a `ghost-` prefix and a temp parent are not enough: the guard once deleted a pending sidecar and another session's directory from a bare `--source codex` payload. `pluginSessionDir` (`stophook.go`) now requires, in order: the file is named `messages.jsonl`; its directory's name is `ghost-` followed by exactly six `[A-Za-z0-9]` characters, which is what the plugin's `mkdtemp(join(tmpdir(), "ghost-"))` returns; the parent passes `insideAHostTempDir`; `os.Lstat` reports a real directory (a symlink is refused, never followed); it is owned by the current uid (`stophook_owner_unix.go`; the `!unix` build has no uid to compare and returns true); and it holds that one entry, a regular file. Removal is `os.Remove` of the file then `os.Remove` of the directory, never `RemoveAll`, so an entry that appears between the check and the removal makes the directory removal fail and leaves it. Only the two opencode formats are considered: no adapter materialises a codex rollout in the temp directory (codex hands over the host's rollout file under its own home), so a codex payload never sweeps anything. `TestSweepNamesMatchThePluginSource` reads the prefix and the file name back out of the embedded plugin, `TestSweepNameMatchesARealRuntimeMkdtemp` runs the same `mkdtemp` under bun and node when installed, and `TestSweepLeavesEverythingTheHostDidNotMaterialise` holds one case per check (sidecar, foreign directory, scratch directory, bench directory, codex payload, plugin-named file, symlinked directory, extra entry, other file name, symlinked file). The residual: another live opencode session's directory has the same name shape, owner, parent and layout, so it cannot be told apart from this session's; the payload's path is what names it. **The cooldown skip line renders its project, because the value is a STORED id and a stored id is not token-guarded (#1327).** `logLifecycleCooldownSkip` (`lifecyclecooldown.go`) writes the one line that records why a run did not start into `lifecycle.log`, a durable file in the data directory that operators and diagnostic tooling read, and it interpolated the resolved project id with a bare `%s`. That id is not this process's operand, so it was never a refusal argument and never reached a renderer — which is why it survived a rule that holds everywhere else in the package, and why the renderer being a RENDERER (the `RefusalArg` note above) is a coverage promise rather than an enforced one: the hole is a call site that never asked. The reason the value had to be asked about is that the write-side guards do not reach it: `memory.CheckImportedProjectID` refuses a control character, a backtick and a delimiter and never asks the guard, and `memory.CheckImportedProject` asks it of a project's name and path but not of its id — so `ghost import` stores an artifact's project id verbatim, and a session standing in that project's recorded directory then resolves to it. The id therefore goes through `memory.RefusalArg`, the renderer the lock's and the start stamp's own refusals already use, so an ordinary id is now `%q`-quoted (the same as neighbouring lifecycle.log lines), while a token-shaped one is withheld behind a placeholder naming the argument and the format the guard found — the same class as the marker's project (#1088, `renderMarkerProject`) and the lifecycle operand (#839), closed here on the one lifecycle call site that still printed the value raw. `TestSpawnLifecycle_CooldownSkipWithholdsATokenShapedProjectID` plants both projects in one store and reads one log, so the assertion pair is the point: the token-shaped id must be absent and an ordinary id must still appear, because a renderer that withheld everything would keep the token out and leave the line unable to name the project it skipped. The session-start block's decisions read is the same LIVE one the other injected decision surfaces use (#1272): active or revisit, superseded excluded, active first, and a revisit row labelled ` (revisit)`; the block's rows carry the status as the tuple's fourth field so the renderer can mark it. The block also LEADS with the maintenance alert when a current failure marker exists, and BOTH session-start surfaces carry it: `runSessionStart` prints the one line ahead of the block, and `RenderSessionContextAt`'s current path prepends that same line, which is what keeps a host that cannot consume the hook's stdout injection at parity; the `--as-of` half returns before the read, so a historical render never reports the present's paused consolidation (`TestRenderSessionContextCarriesTheHookMaintenanceAlert` pins both directions). Every log Ghost appends to is opened through `openLogForAppend` (`logrotate.go`): a file at or over `logRotateCap` (5 MiB) is first renamed to `<path>.1`, replacing the previous copy, so the directory holds the current log and one rotated copy, and a missing file is created 0600 whatever the umask. `OpenRotatingLog` carries `GHOST_LOG_FILE` through the same path (`mcpLogConfig` in `cmd/ghost/bootstrap.go`) and keeps the cap while the server runs: the writer is mutex-guarded, closes the file before the rename (Windows), reopens through `openLogForAppend`, and never returns a write error to its caller (a failed reopen drops lines and retries), so the redirect is capped and owner-only like `lifecycle.log` (#1546). **A STORE-HEALTH STAT READ THAT FAILS IS A PRINTED `!` LINE, NOT A FAILED CHECK (#1462).** `checkStoreHealth`'s embedding-coverage and link reads each had an `err == nil` guard with no other branch, which dropped the line with no output at all — so a store whose vectors or links could not be counted printed every other check as passing with no sign of the gap. Each failure now prints `! embeddings: <error>` or `! memory links: <error>` and does NOT go through the check closure, because the store opened cleanly and its schema matched: this is not wiring `ghost mcp init` repairs, so failing the run would print a footer pointing at the wrong repair — exactly the rule `reportHistoryGrowth` already follows for its own unreadable read. The line is still printed, with the store's own error text, because a check that cannot run must not print nothing at all. writeRedirects rewrites a MEMORY.md only when it is byte-identical to a template Ghost generated; anything else is kept and reported as not overwritten.

**A READ-ONLY OPEN TIGHTENS NO MODE, AND THE STATUS RUN IS ONE (#1720).** `memory.OpenReadDB` runs neither the migration nor the permission pass, so a status run leaves the data directory's mode and `ghost.db`'s own exactly as it found them: a directory at `0750` and a database at `0640` are still there after the diagnostic that reports on them. `ghost maintenance status` opens through `memory.OpenDB` and does tighten them, so the two commands are NOT interchangeable for this. The permissions section of `docs/configuration.md` names them apart, because it listed the status run among the read-write commands — and a reader who ran the diagnostic to find out why the store was world-readable was told, wrongly, that the diagnostic had fixed it (`TestStatusLeavesTheDataDirectoryModesAlone`, `TestThePermissionsDocPlacesTheStatusRunOnTheReadOnlySide`).

**The one-time config `.bak` only ever holds content that predates Ghost (#1371).** `settingsFile.save` (`internal/mcpinit/settings.go`) and `installCodexMCP` (`codex.go`) skip the backup when the file on disk holds nothing but what Ghost itself writes: `settingsFile.ghostManaged`, decided at LOAD from the pre-merge document, and `codexConfigHoldsOnlyGhostOutput`, decided from the config's own lines in its read-time span. The reason is the slot's whole purpose — the `.bak` is the user's pre-ghost file and `writeBackupOnce` never rolls it forward, so a backup taken from Ghost's own output would fill it permanently and a user edit made later could never be captured. On a fresh install the first run creates the file from nothing (nothing to back up), and it is the SECOND run whose save sees a file — Ghost's own output — which is why the check is on the SHAPE of the document and not on whether the file existed before this run. Recognition is by EXACT equality with what init writes, not by this build's resolved path and not by the lenient ownership rule the reconcile uses: `holdsOnlyGhostManaged` accepts a `settings.json` only when it has no top-level key besides `permissions`, `hooks` and `autoMemoryEnabled` (false), an allow-only `permissions` object holding exactly Ghost's tool list, and, per event, exactly ONE hook entry that `ghostWrittenHookEntry` finds identical to init's output — the keys `matcher` and `hooks` and no others, the matcher init uses for that event (empty, or `Edit|Write|MultiEdit` for `PostToolUse`), a single action with exactly the keys `type` and `command`, and a command EQUAL to `shellQuote(binary) + " hook <event> --source claude-code"` for a binary named `ghost`. A `timeout` or any extra key, a `&& tool` tail, a wrapper, a second entry or a foreign event keeps the `.bak`, because `isGhostHookCommand` would still call those Ghost's hook while the file is no longer Ghost's own output. A command naming an OLDER ghost path still counts only when it equals what init would have written for that path, so the stale-path repair after an upgrade still reads as Ghost's own output; and `codexConfigHoldsOnlyGhostOutput` accepts an `isGhostBinaryName` command with the canonical `args = ["mcp"]` — so the stale-path repair after an upgrade moved the binary still reads the file as Ghost's own output and leaves the slot free. Drifted bytes Ghost never wrote — different table args, a sub-table, another key, another permission, a foreign hook, another top-level key — keep their `.bak`, and anything not affirmatively Ghost's own answers false, so an unusual shape costs a harmless extra backup rather than a skipped one.

**The codex hooks.json merge owns ghost's own ACTION, not the rule around it (#1217).** `mergeCodexHooksConfig` (`internal/mcpinit/codex.go`) rewrites an action IN PLACE: what it replaces on an action that is ghost's own is what ghost renders for it — `command`, `type` and `timeout`, the last present only for the event that has one of its own — and it identifies such an action by the ghost binary — the path this run bakes for it under whatever name it carries (#1249), or its native `.exe` or the `.bat`/`.cmd` launcher shim a Windows install may place on PATH — under any path and any quoting, running `hook <eventToken>`, so a rule the user wrote that runs ghost beside their own command keeps that command, the rule's matcher and every field either of them carries, and a rule running any other ghost subcommand, or ghost's hook for a DIFFERENT event, is the user's and is carried across verbatim. **THE CODEX MERGE SHARES THAT OWNERSHIP RULE WITH THE CLAUDE SIDE, AND A WRAPPER IS NEVER PRUNED OR REWRITTEN (#1345).** `codexIsGhostHookAction` now applies the SAME rule the Claude installer and its status share — `ghostHookTail`: every token of the action is read with the quoting `splitHookCommand` applies to the leading one, and a quoted argument is read inside only behind the command-list gate (a flag ending in `c`, or a shell name), so `env X=1 '<bin>' hook session-start`, `cd d && '<bin>' hook stop` and `sh -c "'<bin>' hook …"` all count as ghost's own wherever they sit in the action with their binary in COMMAND position, and no second rule is appended beside them — codex runs every matching hook, so a duplicate fires the session-start context and the stop hook twice per session. **THE COMMAND-POSITION WALK IS APPLIED AT THE TOP LEVEL TOO (#1358).** `ghostTailOver` used to accept a ghost binary at ANY position at depth 0, which made a ghost binary that is only an ARGUMENT of another program (`echo /opt/bin/ghost hook stop …`, which runs echo) read as ghost's own wired survivor: the installers pruned or suppressed the canonical rule and status reported the hook configured, so nothing fired while the install said it was wired. A ghost binary now counts only where `startsCommand` reaches it — the same walk the interior of a quoted command list already used — so the wrappers that genuinely run ghost (`X=1 ghost …`, `sudo ghost …`, `cd d && ghost …`, `env -i ghost …`) keep their command position and stay recognised, while the argument-position shape is foreign and gets ghost's own rule beside it. That read is shared by the Claude reconcile, `ghost mcp status` and the codex merge, so all three answer the same question about the same file; `hookCommandTokens` also drops leading whitespace rather than emitting an empty first token, so a hand-edited `   /path/ghost hook stop …` still tokenises as the invocation it is. The transparent prefix words are compared by basename, the way `shellBehind` already resolves shell names, so a path-qualified `/usr/bin/env ghost …` or `/usr/bin/sudo ghost …` keeps its command position rather than reading as foreign and gaining a duplicate entry beside a wrapper that already fires the hook. The rest of the wrappers that run ghost are the same walk, extended rather than special-cased: a NEWLINE separates commands exactly as `;` does, so `hookCommandTokens` normalises it to that connective before the split (`cd /d` then a newline then `ghost hook stop …`); a connective GLUED to a word ends the list it is in (`(cd /d; ghost …)` leaves `;` on `/d`), so the tokenizer cuts an UNQUOTED operator into a token of its own (`splitUnquotedOperators`: `d;` is `d` and `;`, `cd&&` is `cd` and `&&`, `build|tee` is three tokens), while a `;`, `&` or `|` inside a QUOTED token is the user's text (`--note "a;b"`, a grep pattern `'x|y'`, a URL with `&`) and is left whole, because crediting it would read an argument-position ghost binary as ghost's own and leave the event unwired; an `&` glued to a `>` belongs to a redirection (`2>&1`, `&>file`) and is left whole for the same reason, since `othertool 2>&1 ghost …` passes ghost to othertool. The boundary test is therefore a connective TOKEN and never "contains `;&|`", the opposite direction from `codexTokenEndsCommand`, where a boundary means "do not credit" and the safe direction is to append the canonical rule; a group opener (`(` or `{`) opens a command list, so the first word inside it is in command position (`{ ghost hook stop …; }`); and the transparent list carries the further commands that run their operand unchanged — `nohup`, `time`, `doas`, `stdbuf`, `ionice`, `chrt`, `taskset` and `timeout` — with `prefixPositionalSlots` giving the ones that take a plain slot of their own before the command (`timeout 5 ghost …`, `taskset 0x3 ghost …`, `chrt -f 10 ghost …`) and `prefixOptionOperands`, keyed BY COMMAND, giving the options that consume a separate following word (`timeout -k 5 10 ghost …`, `stdbuf -o L ghost …`, `doas -u user ghost …`). That table is keyed per command because the same spelling is an operand for one of them and nothing at all for another — `env`'s and `sudo`'s `-i` and `sudo`'s `-e` take none — so a shared table would read `sudo -e ghost …`'s binary as a flag operand and then treat the real command as somebody else's. The word a prefix command owns is stepped over only when such a command is actually to its left (`prefixRegionBefore`), so `echo 5 ghost hook stop …` stays foreign: echo owns no slot, and the binary is its argument. Recognising a wrapper is a COUNT, not a rewrite: only a command whose LEADING token is a ghost binary (`codexLeadingGhostHookAction`) is eligible for the in-place repair, so a wrapper keeps its prefix and its path byte for byte — the bound `isStaleGhostHookCommand` draws on the Claude side — and pruning is equally discriminating: a wrapped action is never removed, only canonical leading-token ghost actions are treated as duplicates and dropped, so when an event carries a wrapper the user's wrapper (which may chain ghost with its own command) is the deterministic survivor whether it sits before or after a canonical copy, and an action whose only extra copies are wrappers is left intact in its entirety; with no wrapper present the earliest canonical survives as before and any later canonical is pruned. A command that names ghost only inside another tool's ARGUMENT is still the user's and still gets ghost's rule beside it, and `codexContractHookWired` asks the same `ghostHookTail` before its `--source` scan, so `ghost mcp status --client codex` and the merge answer the same question about the same file — a reader that matched the tail alone reported a wrapper that does run ghost as missing and sent the operator to an init that rewrote nothing. That scan reads only ghost's OWN arguments (`codexTailNamesSource`): it stops at the first token that ends a command list, connective or standalone, so a `--source codex` belonging to a command chained behind `&&`, `;` or `|` is never credited to ghost — the wrapper is left beside the canonical rule instead, and ghost's own `--source` in `… hook stop --source codex && other` still is. `TestMergeCodexHooksTreatsAWrappedGhostActionAsItsOwn` runs assignment-prefix, `cd`-and and `sh -c` wrappers over all three events with byte-identical fixed points, `TestMergeCodexHooksAddsItsRuleBesideAForeignCommand` pins the foreign controls, `TestMergeCodexHooksNeverRewritesAWrappedAction` pins the repair bound, and `TestRunCodex_HooksWrappedGhostRuleIsNeverDuplicated` and `TestStatusCodex_WrappedHooksAreWired` the installer-level repro and the status agreement. `codexIsGhostHookAction` judges the EXECUTABLE and the EVENT and never `--source`, so a ghost action another host's config contributed — `hook stop --source claude`, say — is still ghost's own and is rewritten to run `--source codex` here. The merge this replaced classified a whole RULE by one of its actions (any action invoking the ghost binary made the rule "ghost's") and then dropped it, so a mixed rule lost the user's action and the matcher with it on every `ghost mcp init --client codex` run, with nothing printed beyond `+ updated hooks.json` and no `.bak` to restore from — hooks.json is one of the written files that gets no backup. A rule whose every action was a further copy of ghost's own disappears with them, because **ghost owns exactly ONE action per event**: the first one found is rewritten where it sits, a later one is pruned rather than left to fire twice, and the canonical rule is appended only when no rule holds ghost's action at all (`TestMergeCodexHooksFiresGhostOncePerEvent`, `TestMergeCodexHooksLeavesAGhostCommandGhostDidNotRender`). The fixed point holds for a binary baked under any name — `/g`, a renamed `.exe`, a `.bat`/`.cmd` shim — because an action is matched against the path being rendered and not only a `ghost` basename (#1249): `TestMergeCodexHooksFixedPointForAnyBinaryName` runs the merge three times with each and the second pass changes no byte. A rewrite also keeps the file's KEY ORDER (`codexObject`, decoded in document order and re-encoded in it), and that is what makes the merge a fixed point: a Go map re-encodes an object with its keys sorted, so rewriting the action a fresh install just wrote would reshuffle `type`/`command`/`timeout`, and the install's `string(existing) == desired` compare would then report a change on every second run. The two #1217 cases are pinned at the function that does the work: `TestAuditCodexMixedRuleKeepsUserAction` (a stale path inside a mixed rule is repaired while the user's action and matcher survive) and `TestAuditCodexMixedRuleCurrentPath` (already current: nothing is lost, no second rule is appended, and the merged document is a byte-level no-op). **A `"hooks"` value of the JSON literal `null` is REFUSED, not merged into (#1219).** `json.Unmarshal` decodes `null` into a nil map with no error, so the merge's assignment into the decoded map panicked with "assignment to entry in nil map" — under a dry run too, because the merge runs before the dry-run check, and with it every client after codex in a multi-client run, breaking the "one broken client never blocks the rest" contract. The merge now asks `isJSONNull` — the guard the Claude settings path already uses, so the two sides refuse the same value — and returns `hooks.json: hooks is null` before the decode, so the file is named and the failure is an ordinary refusal the installer reports rather than a crash; `TestAuditCodexHooksNull` pins it. **The same guard runs one level down (#1247):** a MANAGED event's value being the JSON literal `null` is structurally-invalid-but-present data too — `json.Unmarshal` decodes it into a nil `[]json.RawMessage` with no error, and the merge would then append ghost's canonical rule over the user's declaration as if the event were empty, silently replacing it. `decodeCodexHooksConfig` refuses it before the per-event `json.Unmarshal` and before `mergeCodexEventRules`, returning `hooks.json: hooks[%q] is null`, and it lives in the shared decode because that is what keeps the install pre-flight and the merge refusing the same shapes: `validateCodexHooksFile` refuses the file before step 3 registers the MCP server, the way the top-level guard does. The guard covers only the events ghost manages; an explicit null on any other event is the user's and is carried through verbatim (`TestAuditCodexHooksNullEventValue`, `TestRunCodex_NullEventValueRefused`, `TestMergeCodexHooksLeavesAnUnmanagedEventNull`).

**A blank codex hooks.json is WRITTEN, not refused (#1296).** A 0-byte or whitespace-only `hooks.json` carries nothing to merge, so `installCodexHooks` treats it as absent and writes the canonical document, the way a 0-byte `config.toml` is appended to rather than refused; the alternative — refusing it by name — would leave every re-run refusing the same file until the user deleted it. A file that is present but structurally invalid is the case that IS refused, and `RunCodex` validates it with `validateCodexHooksFile` BEFORE step 3 registers the MCP server in `config.toml`, as `Run` validates Claude's `settings.json` before it registers anything (#1267): the pre-flight and the merge share `decodeCodexHooksConfig`, so a document the pre-flight accepts is one the merge can act on and both callers refuse the same shapes with the same parse errors. The refusal names the file and carries the edit that clears it — repair it or delete it — rather than the generic re-run hint, which a re-run cannot satisfy while the file stays invalid; a refused file is left exactly as written and nothing is registered (`TestRunCodex_BlankHooksJSONWritten`, `TestRunCodex_RefusesMalformedHooksBeforeRegistering`).

**A blank Claude settings.json is POPULATED, not refused (#1684).** A 0-byte or whitespace-only `settings.json` carries nothing to merge, so `loadSettings` (`internal/mcpinit/settings.go`) returns the empty document init populates, exactly as it does for a missing file and as `installCodexHooks` treats a blank `hooks.json` (#1296). The decode used to be the difference: `json.Unmarshal` of an empty document failed with `settings.json must be a JSON object`, and `validateSettingsFile` appended the field hint, so init named a field a blank file does not have and every re-run refused the same file until the user deleted it. The blank file is not decoded, and the empty document is what `holdsOnlyGhostManaged` reports, so `save` does not capture it as the one-time `.bak` — an empty backup would stand forever and block the user's real file, the reason a 0-byte `config.toml` is skipped rather than copied. Nothing else about the pre-flight changed: a document that is present but structurally invalid is still refused before registration, naming the file and the field (`TestLoadSettings_BlankFileIsPopulated`, `TestRunInstallsWithBlankSettings`).

**A codex hooks.json whose DOCUMENT is the JSON literal `null` is REFUSED (#1673).** `json.Unmarshal` decodes `null` into a nil map with no error, so the `hooks` member then reads as absent, the pre-flight passes, and the merge writes Ghost's canonical document over the user's file — the user's own content gone behind an exit `0`. A null document is not an empty document to fill in, so the shared `decodeCodexHooksConfig` refuses it by name (`hooks.json is null`), the way the Claude settings path refuses a null `settings.json`, and the refusal lands in the pre-flight, before step 3 registers the MCP server, with the file left exactly as written (`TestMergeCodexHooksRefusesANullDocument`, `TestRunCodex_NullDocumentIsRefused`).

**THE CLAUDE CODE INSTALLER STOPS ON A REFUSED DATA DIRECTORY (#1321).** `Run` resolves the data directory once, after the settings pre-flight and before step 1, and returns the refusal as its error, so a run that used to report the refusal as a `(continuing)` warning and then print `Done!` and exit `0` can no longer claim success: the client is not registered and no configuration file is written. The two steps that resolve the directory for real — step 2's scratch root (`ensureScratchSpace`) and step 9's import (`importMemories`) — both downgrade a failure there to a best-effort warning, deliberately and for their own reasons (the harnesses fall back to the inherited temp dir; a per-project import failure is not fatal), so the guard is asked up front and only its refusal is fatal. `config.DataDirPath` is the one place `config.CheckDevDataDir` runs and it is a pure query that creates nothing (#721), so resolving it here leaves no phantom directory. Only `Run` resolves it: `RunCodex`, `RunOpencode` and `RunGoose` never call `config.DataDirPath` or `config.DataDir` — no scratch root, no memory import — so no refusal can reach them, and a multi-client install still wires the other clients. `TestRunStopsOnAForbiddenDataDirBeforeReportingDone` pins that a refused run errors, names the variable and the directory, prints no `Done!`, invokes the recording client stub not at all, and creates no data directory — and that the same sandbox installs when nothing is refused.

**THE HOOK ONLY EVER OPENS A REGULAR FILE FOR A TRANSCRIPT (#1411).** Both transcript opens on the stop/session-end path go through `openTranscript`: the nudge scan in `runStop` and the audit scan in `scanAuditSignals`, the second of which also runs at session end. `openTranscript` `stat`s the payload's path and refuses anything that is not a regular file BEFORE opening it. The path is the host's, not Ghost's, and opening a FIFO for reading blocks until a writer appears: a blocked open is the opposite of the fail-open contract, where anything unexpected is one `logFailOpen` line and the session continues. A directory, device or socket is refused unopened for the same reason — a read of it cannot mean anything — and a symlink is followed, so its target must be regular. It is `ReadBackupManifest`'s guard and its reason, kept as one helper so the two audit sites cannot drift apart, and the stage string names the open (`open transcript`, `open transcript for the audit`) so the refusal is attributed to the check rather than to the scanner.

**A HOST CONFIGURATION FILE IS ONLY EVER READ AS A REGULAR FILE (#1674).** `readHostConfigFile` (`internal/mcpinit/hostconfig.go`) is the ONE reader for the host configuration files — Claude Code's `settings.json`, codex's `config.toml` and `hooks.json`, opencode's config files (`opencode.json`, `opencode.jsonc`, `$OPENCODE_CONFIG`), goose's plugin package files (`plugin.json`, `mcp.json`, `hooks/hooks.json`), the MEMORY.md redirect, and the Claude plugins registry (`installed_plugins.json`) — and every reader of those goes through it: `loadSettings` (which `ghost mcp init`, `ghost mcp status` and the plugin's first-run finalization all read the Claude document through), `installCodexMCP`, `installCodexHooks`, `validateCodexHooksFile`, `loadCodexHooksRules`, `codexMCPEntryStatus`, `opencodeMCPEntryStatus`, `installOpencodePlugin`, `installGoosePackage`, `writeRedirects`, and `PluginInstalled`. They used to read with a bare `os.ReadFile`, and a FIFO at one of those paths stops the command: opening a FIFO for reading blocks until a writer appears, so `ghost mcp init` and `ghost mcp status` hung with no output at all, and a character device such as `/dev/zero` was read until memory ran out. The open carries `O_NONBLOCK` on unix, so the open of a FIFO returns at once instead of waiting for a writer, and the fstat on the open descriptor refuses anything that is not a regular file BEFORE a byte is read — the shape `internal/config` uses for a config layer, and the same reason (#1550). There is deliberately no `O_NOFOLLOW`, unlike the marker readers: a symlinked `settings.json` or `config.toml` is an ordinary dotfiles setup, and the check is made on what the link names, so a link to a regular file is read exactly as before. The refusal NAMES the file and says it is not a regular file, and it is a refusal rather than a skip: the file is the user's own, the run stops there with the file left exactly as it was and nothing registered, and the advice is the edit that clears it (`Replace it with a regular file, or delete it, then re-run`) rather than the generic re-run hint, which a re-run cannot satisfy while the path is still not a regular file. A status run REPORTS such a file instead of reading it, and reports it as unreadable rather than as missing: a file that cannot be read is not a file that is absent.

**AN EXPIRED MARKER IS SWEPT WHEREVER THE READ REACHES IT, AND BOTH READS REACH EVERY MARKER THEY LOOK AT (#1717).** The self-clean removes the file the read returned, so an expired marker was removed only when the read returned one. EVERY read returns one marker: the project read returns the FIRST candidate it can use and never reads the ones behind it, and the no-project read RANKS the markers and returns the one that ranks first. An expired marker behind either was therefore never returned and never removed — the dead file stayed on disk for as long as the marker in front of it did, and came back as news when that one aged out. Both reads now sweep their expired markers first (`sweepExpiredMarkers`): the no-project read over every marker in the data directory, the project read over its own candidates (`markerCandidates`), on the SAME expiry rule the alert applies (`markerExpired`: past the 30-day horizon on the age side, or past the 24-hour skew on the forward side), so the readers cannot disagree about which marker is expired. A marker in the kept-but-silent band is not expired and stays on disk, and a marker inside the tolerated skew is a real, recent failure and stays too. The sweep is bounded by the same `lifecycleSelfCleanMaxPasses` the alert loop works under. A marker the directory REFUSES to remove is reported the way the alert reports it: the directory is named as the blocker, never the recorded cause — but a refused removal NEVER HIDES a current failure. The sweep tries EVERY expired candidate, because a refusal says nothing about the file behind it, and the lookup carries on past all of them (each is expired and stays on disk); they are reported only when there is nothing current to show AND the first of them is this session's to report: another project's expired marker — the shared legacy file, an old-stem file — must not speak for this session. A marker that is ALREADY GONE is not a refusal — two session starts can walk the same expired marker, and the one that loses the race finds the file removed rather than a directory refusing — so the sweep carries on to the next candidate and the current failure is still shown, instead of the blocker line standing in for a marker that is already gone.

**A DEAD MARKER NEVER ENDS A READ, AND NEVER OUTRANKS A CURRENT ONE (#1734).** The no-project read RANKS every marker in the data directory and returns the one that ranks first, and the ranking puts every stamp dated at or before now above every stamp dated ahead of it — a stamp dated ahead (inside the tolerated skew) is a real, recent failure, and the newest stamp at or before now outranks it. A marker the sweep could not remove is expired and stays on disk, and one 40 days old is expired whatever the sweep did with it, so a REFUSED expired marker ranked first: the read returned it, the alert tried to remove it, the removal failed, and the session was told that the data directory was the blocker — or told nothing at all — instead of being told about the failure that was actually current. The read now drops every DEAD marker before the ranking: one the sweep could not remove (the sweep's own answer, passed in as `blocked`) and one past an expiry bound (`markerExpired`), whether the sweep removed it, could not remove it, or never reached it — the sweep's bound is a bound on REMOVALS, so the expired markers past it are still on disk, and the alert's own removal pass takes none of them on the way past. The per-project read already passed over every refused marker; the no-project read ranks over every marker in the data directory at once, so it has to drop the dead ones itself. A refused marker is reported only when there is nothing current to show, and then as the BLOCKER: the directory, never the recorded cause.

**A CLIENT'S MCP REGISTRATION IS JUDGED ON WHAT IT RUNS, NOT ON WHETHER IT EXISTS (#1710).** `ghost mcp status --client claude` reads the registration `claude mcp get ghost` printed and holds it to the two things the codex and opencode checks hold their own registrations to (`codexMCPCurrent`, `opencodeMCPEntryVerdict`): the command must resolve to the ghost binary this run found, and the arguments must be the contract's stdio invocation, `mcp` — so a registration carrying no arguments at all is drift too, because the client would start the CLI instead of the server. It used to answer the question by the presence of the text `Command:` in that output, which every registration named ghost carries — one pointing at a moved, deleted or unrelated program included — so a registration that leaves every session without ghost's tools read as "MCP server registered" and the run ended "All checks passed." The rendering is read through `parseClaudeRegistration`, the one reader of the `claude mcp get` output, so the status check and the rollback that restores a removed entry judge the same document; the identity of the command is decided by the FILE (`os.SameFile`, through `mcpCommandIsGhost`, the rule the opencode check shares), so a symlink onto the resolved binary is the same registration rather than a different one, a name-sibling beside it is not the ghost, and a binary that was moved away is nothing like it, and with no ghost binary on PATH there is nothing to compare against, so the command is accepted by NAME. The failed line names what the registration runs AND the one command that repairs it — `ghost mcp init --client claude` — because a drift the reader cannot see is the drift they cannot repair, and a line that says what is wrong without saying what to run about it leaves the reader to guess (`TestStatus_ClaudeMCPRegistration`).

**THE INSTALLER AND THE STATUS CHECK JUDGE THE CLAUDE REGISTRATION BY ONE RULE (#1711).** `claudeMCPVerdict` (`internal/mcpinit/status.go`) is the ONE judgement on a `claude mcp get ghost` rendering — whether the client holds a registration at all, whether that registration is this build's ghost binary run with the contract's arguments, and the drift in the status report's words — and BOTH commands call it: the status check reports the drift, and `registerMCP` repairs it. So the repair the failed line names repairs: a registration that is not this build's ghost binary, or not run with the contract's arguments, takes the remove-then-add path and is reported as updated, never as already registered. The installer's own judgement used to be two substring searches over the whole rendering — it contained `Command:`, and it contained the ghost binary path ANYWHERE — which read a foreign command as ghost's whenever the ghost path appeared in another line, read a path that merely BEGINS with the ghost's path as ghost's, and read the wrong arguments (or none at all) as ghost's because the Args line was never read; init then printed "already registered" and skipped the repair the status report had just asked for, so the operator followed the advice and nothing changed. The identity of the command is the FILE, so a symlink onto the resolved binary is the same registration and the installer leaves it alone, and a name-sibling beside it is not (`TestRegisterMCPRepairsARegistrationThatIsNotGhosts`, `TestRegisterMCPLeavesTheCurrentRegistrationAlone`, `TestStatusThenInitThenStatusRepairsTheRegistration`).

**A PID FILE IS ONLY EVER READ AS A REGULAR FILE (#1716).** `readPIDFile` (`internal/mcpinit/pidfile.go`) is the ONE reader of the PID files — the per-project lifecycle claim and the obsidian sync's own marker — and every reader of those goes through it: `isAlive` (the stop hook's spawn guard, the session-end hook's, `LifecycleLockHeld`'s and the obsidian sync's own) and `pidInFile` (the claim's release, which asks whether the file is still this run's to remove). They used to read with a bare `os.ReadFile`, and a FIFO at the claim path stops the command: opening a FIFO for reading blocks until a writer appears, so `ghost hook stop` hung with no output at all until the host's own timeout killed it, and a character device such as `/dev/zero` was read until memory ran out. The file is OPENED and the DESCRIPTOR is judged, never the path stat'ed and then read — a path swapped for a FIFO between the two calls would still hang the reader — and the flags are the ones `openMarker` opens a failure marker with (`markerOpenFlags`: O_NOFOLLOW and O_NONBLOCK on unix), so the open cannot block on a FIFO and a symlink is refused by the kernel rather than followed; the fstat on the descriptor then refuses anything that is not a regular file, which is NO CLAIM: the hook spawns the chain it was about to spawn rather than waiting on a claim that was never going to answer. The other two readers of the SAME claim file (`claimPidFileReclaim` and `unfinishedLifecycleRun`) stat it first because they need its mtime, and their liveness read is this one, so the file they judge is the file they opened. What is read is bounded at 4096 bytes, and a file over the bound is no claim: the pid and its token are a few bytes, and nothing else is one. The data directory is created 0700, so only the user's own processes can plant one.
**THE WORKING-MOMENT MARKER IS ONLY EVER OPENED AS A REGULAR FILE.** `readMomentState` (`internal/mcpinit/workmoment_marker.go`) reads `working-moment-<digest>.log` through `openMarker` (`markerOpenFlags`: O_NOFOLLOW and O_NONBLOCK on unix), so a FIFO planted at the predictable, session-id-derived path cannot block the `message-submit` or `edit` hooks in the read and a symlink is refused by the kernel rather than followed; a descriptor that is not a regular file is no marker. `appendMomentEntry` opens with the same O_NOFOLLOW and O_NONBLOCK and refuses a non-regular descriptor before writing, so the `D start …` ledger line cannot be written through a symlink to another file and a FIFO cannot block `recordSessionStartDelivery` after the context block has already been printed. The data directory is created 0700, so only the user's own processes can plant one.

**A CLAIM PUBLISH LEAVES NOTHING BEHIND WHEN IT FAILS (#1719).** `atomicWritePID` (`internal/mcpinit/stophook.go`) publishes the per-project lifecycle claim (and the obsidian sync's own marker) by writing a staging file beside it and renaming that onto the claim path, so a reader never sees a half-written claim — and the staging file is REMOVED when the publish fails. It used to be a FIXED `.tmp` sibling written with a bare `os.WriteFile` and renamed, which left two things behind. The first is the staging file itself: the rename fails whenever the claim path cannot be replaced (a directory sitting there is enough, and `claimPidFileReclaim` deliberately reaches the write for a non-regular entry at the claim path), and the data directory is never swept, so every failed publish left one file behind for as long as the store existed. The second is a NAME somebody else can point somewhere else: `os.WriteFile` FOLLOWS a symlink, so a link planted at that fixed staging name was written THROUGH — the claim text landed in a file the caller never named, and the rename then moved the link itself onto the claim path. The staging file is now UNIQUE (`os.CreateTemp`, the shape the failure marker and the start stamp publish theirs with) and it is removed on every failure path, so a refused claim leaves the data directory exactly as it found it and a planted link at a guessable name is never followed (`TestAFailedClaimPublishLeavesNoStagingFile`, `TestAClaimThatCannotBePublishedLeavesNoStagingFile`, `TestTheClaimPublishDoesNotWriteThroughAPlantedLink`, `TestLifecycleClaimPublishLeavesNoStagingFile`). The claim's staging name is `lifecycle-<project>.pid.tmp<random>`, which is why `longestDerivedNameOverhead` still counts the START STAMP's temp file as the longest derived name.

- `internal/claudeimport/` — One-time import of Claude Code auto-memory on first contact. The files are written by a session that read a checkout Ghost never chose, so the parse layer is an untrusted surface: a front-matter line gets its own bound (`maxFrontmatterLine`) because `bufio.Scanner`'s 64 KiB default stops the scan on a long description and the block's loss is silent (the type, and with it the category, went with it), a block that could not be read whole is imported as body text rather than half-parsed, and a file past `maxMemoryFileBytes` is skipped. A directory entry named `*.md` that is not a regular file — a symlink, FIFO, device or socket, judged by `entry.Type()`, which does not follow links — is refused unopened and named in its own `notRegular` group, apart from `refused` and `failed` because no retry can change it (a name `skipFile` already skips, such as MEMORY.md, stays silent whatever its type), and `ParseMemoryFile` itself `Lstat`s before opening with `O_NOFOLLOW|O_NONBLOCK` and refuses a non-regular descriptor, so a FIFO never blocks the import and a link never imports a file from outside the directory. The import writes nothing to disk at all — a fixture asserts the tree is byte-identical afterwards — and it never promotes: `categoryMap` is the only thing that picks a category, so a planted `source: manual`, `pinned: true` or `type: _global` reaches none of the fields consolidation filters on (`source`, `pinned`, `retention` or `resolved_at`, the four `consolidatable` and `ReplaceNonManual` exclude on) — the import's `UpsertWithOptions` binds the source to the package's own constant and writes an unpinned row at the default `project` tier with no `resolved_at`, so a forged exemption buys the row nothing and a forged `type: _global` lands on `categoryMap`'s `fact` fallback instead of a scope. `EncodeProjectPath` NAMES the directory Claude Code will actually read, so it mirrors Claude Code's own encoder byte for byte rather than approximating it: every character outside `[a-zA-Z0-9]` folds to `-`, not only the three path separators, so a `.`, `_`, space or non-ASCII rune each become their own `-` (the old three-character fold wrote `mcp init`'s redirect and the Claude memory import under a spelling Claude never reads, for every checkout under a dot-directory such as `~/.config`); the fold walks UTF-16 CODE UNITS, because Claude Code is JavaScript and its `[^a-zA-Z0-9]` regex matches each surrogate half of a non-BMP character separately, so one emoji yields two `-`; and a name over 200 units is truncated to its first 200 and suffixed with `-` plus the base-36 absolute value of a 32-bit `String.hashCode` (`h = h*31 + unit`, wrapped to int32) taken over the FULL path, so two paths sharing a 200-unit prefix stay apart. The exactness is the point and not a nicety: a name that differs by one `-` is silent — init reports a redirect it did not really place, the import finds no memory directory, `mcp status` checks the wrong place, and Claude never loads the redirect. `ClaudeMemoryDir` keeps its traversal guard, which is the second line behind the fold now that the fold no longer emits a separator. Re-runs are true no-ops: each file is saved with `UpsertOptions.SkipExactContent`, whose exact-content lookup is the first step inside `UpsertWithOptions`'s write transaction (see the Boundaries rule that a read deciding a write belongs there), so two clients that reach first contact together store a file once rather than once each (#1234), and the project read that opens the import is only a cheap skip, not the decision. Only a BYTE-IDENTICAL file is skipped: a near-duplicate of a stored row, or of a file earlier in the same run, still takes Upsert's ordinary fold — stored as its own row, linked to the row it matched by a 'duplicate' edge, and that row strengthened — exactly as before the skip moved into the transaction (`TestImportFoldsANearDuplicateIntoTheExistingRow`, `TestImportFoldsNearDuplicateFilesWithinOneImport`, `TestConcurrentFirstContactImportsEachFileOnce`). The write goes through `provider.MemoryStore`'s own method, so no store the import can be handed lacks the guarded path, and there is no unguarded fallback for a caller that discards the import's error to hide. ghost_project_context imports only on first contact, when the project holds no memories, and is therefore not annotated read-only; a run that stored at least one row pushes `notifyMemoryRow` for the project, as a save does. ghost mcp init is an out-of-process writer with no MCP session, so its import notifies no subscriber of a running server. ghost mcp init re-runs the import every time, and SkipExactContent makes that a no-op for content already present. A file the token guard refuses, or one that failed for any other reason, is reported by its name in two groups, never any of its text, to the server log, the ghost_project_context answer and ghost mcp init, through quoteData or memory.ProjectArg. There is no durable per-file record yet: a partly failed import, a user edit, or a deletion followed by a re-run is deliberately not retried or exempted automatically, because that correctness guarantee needs a schema change the owner has not approved. The issue is therefore only partially addressed (#1235).
- `internal/linking/` — Background worker linking similar memories into a graph. A SCAN IS RECORDED ONLY FOR THE VECTOR IT COMPARED (#1160): the worker passes the vector it read to `Store.MarkLinkScanned`, whose INSERT into `link_scans` is conditional, inside the same statement, on that blob still being the memory's `memory_embeddings.embedding`. A content edit deletes the vector and the slot together, so a scan that was in flight across the edit records nothing and the memory stays queued for its new text. The check lives in the mark rather than in `StoreEmbedding` because the re-embed of the new text can land on either side of the mark: clearing the slot on every store would cover the re-embed landing after the mark, but not the one landing before it, where no later write would undo the stale slot. A re-embed under a NEW identity owns the slot's clear as well, and the clear and the upsert must run in ONE `beginWrite` transaction (#1258): run as two, a mark for the retired vector could commit between them, pass its byte check against the retired bytes the row still held, and re-arm the slot after the clear — leaving a slot computed in the retired space that `UnscannedEmbeddedMemoryIDs` never re-queues. In one transaction a mark before it is cleared by the DELETE and a mark after it is refused by the byte check. The worker must pass the vector it scanned, never a fresh read at mark time, which would match the new vector and reintroduce the bug. **EVERY STORED MEMORY ID AND PROJECT ID IN A WORKER LOG LINE IS RENDERED THROUGH `memory.RefusalArg` (#1692).**
- `internal/embedding/` — Ollama async vectorization worker; every vector is stamped with the identity of the space that produced it (`<model>[:<dimensions>][+prefix]`, `embedding.VectorIdentity`) and nomic-embed-text is embedded with its `search_document: `/`search_query: ` task prefixes. A LIVENESS PROBE MISSING ITS DEADLINE IS NOT AN OUTAGE, which is why the probe is three-valued rather than a bool: `Client.Probe` answers `Reachable`, `Unreachable` (a non-200, or a refused connection — an ANSWER, so nothing is listening), or `Inconclusive` (the probe's own 2s deadline expired, so we learned nothing), and `Worker.checkAlive` returns that verdict while maintaining the `ollama-down-since` marker (`ghost mcp status` reads it for outage duration). The two errors are NOT symmetric, and every call site says which one it is making, which means the two gates DISAGREE on purpose: `EmbedPending` PROCEEDS on `Inconclusive` (its callers — a one-shot `ghost supersede` and a save-driven `processProject` — have no second chance, and skipping is what made #736 report a clean `0 candidate pairs`), while `SweepOnce` skips on anything but `Reachable`, because the daemon retries in one interval and a lenient gate there walks a HUNG endpoint through every project at `2 + 2·P + 32·P_pending` seconds, blocking `Worker.Run`'s select loop and dropping save notifications past `projectCh`'s 16-deep non-blocking buffer. `EmbedPending`'s batch is bounded in TIME as well as rows (`embedPendingBudget`, in the spirit of `supersedeEmbedBudget`): the budget is the deadline of every embed request in the batch, so a stalled endpoint costs at most that budget per batch, never one client timeout per remaining memory, and a spent budget logs "embed budget spent" and returns `Inconclusive` if the batch embedded nothing (the sweep ends) or `Reachable` if it made progress (the sweep moves on, so a slow but working endpoint cannot hold every later project behind the first one's backlog). Its in-batch re-probe after a failed embed stops on anything but `Reachable` (a part-done batch gains nothing from another 30s request), and an embed that times out while the re-check is still `Reachable` is either one slow row (the client's timeout on an over-long document) or the "pacified endpoint" shape where the server answers `GET /` but hangs `/api/embed`, so the row is recorded on the Worker (in memory, bounded by `maxSlowRows`) to be tried after every other row from then on and the batch moves on; only `stallAfterTimeouts` (2) consecutive timeouts, or a batch that ends on a timeout with nothing embedded, return `Inconclusive`, so `SweepOnce` ends the sweep and a pacified endpoint costs two stalled requests per sweep, not two per project, while a row already recorded as slow counts toward neither, so one row that always times out cannot end every sweep and starve the projects after it; ordinary per-row errors (a 400 for an over-long input, a model error for that one row) reset the count and continue to the next row. It RETURNS the verdict `SweepOnce` may ACT ON — the re-probe's own where a batch attempted work, `Inconclusive` for a stalled endpoint or a budget spent with nothing embedded, a CONCLUSIVE refusal otherwise, and an `Inconclusive` over no work collapsed to "nothing to act on" — so the sweep reuses one probe and a busy machine cannot end a tick over a project with no work. Reading a timeout as "down" cost a busy machine the whole index: both the daemon's sweep AND `ghost supersede`'s own pre-scan fill (`embedSupersedeCorpus`, #716) gate on this probe, so one slow round trip left the candidate scan with no vectors to score, `Result.Unscored` holding the entire corpus, and a clean-looking `0 candidate pairs` at exit 0 for a pair the operator could see (#736). The marker answers the OPPOSITE question, because a duration is only worth having for an outage that is real: written on a conclusive `Unreachable` at once and never re-written (so a still-down endpoint does not reset its own clock) — reachable only inside the DAEMON, which is the sole caller that builds a Worker with a data dir (`embedSupersedeCorpus` passes "" on purpose: the marker is the daemon's outage bookkeeping) — AND on `Inconclusive` after `inconclusiveStampsAfter` CONSECUTIVE unanswered SWEEP TICKS — a tick counts whether or not the strict gate let the sweep past it, so a hung endpoint that skips every project still keeps the clock running (three ticks is about six minutes at the daemon's 2-minute interval) — and one slow probe is a busy machine far more often than a dead endpoint, and a marker written on one is reported for as long as the machine stays busy, while the endpoints a refusal never describes (a wedged accept loop, a remote URL behind a dropping firewall) need that duration precisely because they never produce a refusal. The streak is counted PER SWEEP TICK (`SweepOnce` -> `noteSweep`) and NEVER per probe, because `checkAlive` is called more than once per tick — `EmbedPending` re-probes after every failed embed — so a per-probe count reaches its threshold inside ONE tick and stamps the marker on exactly the busy machine the streak exists to protect against. Any answered sweep SPENDS the streak, so a stall spread over minutes cannot accumulate into an outage that was never established; a stall never *clears* a real one, and an inconclusive probe never removes the marker at all. `Client.Alive` remains the two-valued form for the one caller that only warns a human (`ghost_health`); a caller that gates WORK on the answer uses `Probe`, because only there does a false "down" cost a skipped corpus rather than a conservative warning. `mcpinit`'s Ollama check reads the tri-state too, because `ghost mcp status` renders the OPPOSITE kind of claim to a warning line: the `ollama-down-since` marker's duration is a positive, timed outage assertion, so `checkOllama` returns the verdict and `checkStoreHealth` prints the duration only on a CONCLUSIVE `Unreachable` — an `Inconclusive` probe (a missed deadline, evidence of nothing) is reported as an undetermined endpoint and never as an outage with a start time, however long the marker says it has been stamped. `Client.Alive` would have flattened those two, which is why the diagnostic joined the tri-state callers. `Client.HasModel` reads the model-presence side of the same report, and it resolves every name the way Ollama does rather than comparing the raw strings: a leading `registry.ollama.ai/` and then a leading `library/` are dropped, EVERY OTHER QUALIFIER IS KEPT — a namespace names a distinct model, so `hf.co/alice/m:q` and `hf.co/bob/m:q` do not collide — and a name with no tag, including one left with a trailing colon, is filled in as `:latest`; that normalisation is applied to BOTH the configured name and each name the `/api/tags` listing carries, so a fully qualified `registry.ollama.ai/library/nomic-embed-text:v1.5` and a bare `nomic-embed-text:v1.5` are one installed model and neither a qualified nor a trailing-colon spelling is reported missing while embedding works A VECTOR IS STORED ONLY FOR THE TEXT IT WAS COMPUTED FROM (#1159): the worker reads a memory's content, waits on the endpoint, then writes through `Store.StoreEmbeddingForContent`, which re-reads the content INSIDE the write transaction and stores nothing (returning false) if it changed or the memory is gone, so an edit's deletion of the embedding is never overwritten by the replaced text's vector and the row stays pending for the next pass. `EmbedPending` and `EmbedOne` both use it; plain `StoreEmbedding` is for callers that just produced the vector for the current text. **EVERY STORED MEMORY ID AND PROJECT ID IN A WORKER LOG LINE IS RENDERED THROUGH `memory.RefusalArg` (#1692).**
- `internal/resolve/` — `ghost resolve`: LLM-classified de-weighting of resolved-evidence memories (drops from ranked injection, stays searchable); classifies via CLI harness (see Classifier backend below); the KEEP cache is keyed by `resolve.KeepStamp` and the audit evidence is read ABOVE its gate, once per pass (#880). A CLASSIFIER REPLY IS LOGGED THROUGH `memory.RefusalArg`, NOT RAW: `classifyChunk`'s two WARNs (unparseable reply, missing verdicts) render it with `logReply`, because the reply is model text over stored notes and the phase's stderr is appended to `lifecycle.log` on the detached path. That renderer rather than reflection's `previewContent`/`clipOpText` because it is the one resolve and supersede already import and use on these lines, it runs the token guard on the WHOLE value before any bound (a token is withheld and named by its format, never cut in half), and a value over `ArgEchoMaxBytes` (256) is withheld with its byte count, so the line still says how big the reply was; 256 holds a whole batch of verdicts, and a reply past it is a transcript. Pinned by `TestClassifyChunkLogsTheReplyBoundedAndGuarded`. THE VETO LINES NAME STORED IDS THROUGH THE SAME RENDERER: `Run`'s `resolve veto kept memory` and `Reassess`'s `reassess veto kept memory` and `reassess veto deferred to the classifier` lines render every id they name — `id`, and the deferring line's `newer` — through `memory.RefusalArg("memory_id", …)`, for the same reason and into the same detached `lifecycle.log`. Pinned by `TestResolveVetoLogWithholdsStoredIDs` and `TestReassessVetoLogsWithholdStoredIDs`. A stored note's content is folded to one physical line (by `assemble`'s line-break set) before batch classification, with the audit-evidence line after the «…» block on its own line, and a batch reply that repeats a line of stored content is re-judged note by note, so stored text cannot decide that note (#1633).
- `internal/memref/` — the ONE ref-resolution implementation, now for every surface that takes a memory by name, not only the ones that CHANGE a row: `ghost supersede --withdraw`, `ghost resolve --mark` / `ghost_resolve_mark`, and `ghost history` (#720), which only READS. Two entry points and ONE set of rules: `Resolve(ctx, store, projectID, which, ref)` scopes the read to a project (its callers are about to change what they name), and `ResolveIn(ids, which, ref)` applies the same rules to an id set the CALLER collected, which is what a store-wide reader needs. The difference is which ids, never how a ref is judged. `ResolveIn` names no project in its refusals because its set is not confined to one, and `FullIDLen` is only the boundary between two honest sentences about a miss, never a test of whether a ref is a whole id. `ErrNoMatch` distinguishes the refusal that says the set holds nothing from the ones that say it holds too much, and a caller branches on it rather than on the ref's length — which cannot tell them apart. The reasoning for all of it, and for why `resolveIn` applies the prefix match itself, is in [architecture.md](docs/architecture.md#memory-history). A ref is a full id, or a PREFIX of at least 8 CHARACTERS (a RUNE count, never a byte length — the id column is TEXT and SQLite's substr() slices by character, so a byte-counted floor on a multi-byte id refuses a prefix the store really has and accepts one that is a third of an identity; the two lengths agree for the hex ids Ghost mints, which is exactly why the bug survives a normal test). The floor applies to a PREFIX ALONE and is decided by COMPARING the match with the ref, never by inspecting the ref's characters — a full id is accepted whatever its shape, because `ghost import` writes an artifact's ids verbatim and nothing about the column says they are hex. A byte-exact match wins at any length (the id is a unique key, so the spelling the caller typed is decisive, which is what lets a short full id that also prefixes a longer one resolve), then a single case-folded one, then the prefix rules. AMBIGUITY IS A REFUSAL, never a choice, and it LISTS the matches up to `MaxMatches` — with ONE exception: a ref below the floor is refused WITHOUT the match list, because printing it would turn a refusal into a dump of the id set (a store-wide set is a LARGER thing to dump, not a smaller one). Two case-folded matches and no byte-exact one (a THIRD casing of ids differing only in case) is unaddressable by any spelling, so that refusal LISTS the two stored spellings instead of naming a remedy — the reader's next keystroke is a copy of one. The rules are shared rather than copied because two copies eventually disagree about which id one spelling addresses; a guess is worse on the history path than on the change paths, where it changes a row nobody named — there it also prints one memory's whole recorded TEXT as though it were another's. NOTE THE SCOPE, because the claim is not wider than it looks: `resolve --reassess --only` does NOT route here. `internal/resolve/scope.go`'s `Scope.Select` has its own rules, and it HAS to differ on one point — a selector matching nothing is a REPORTED MISS that lets the rest of the scope still be judged (#698's scoping judgement), where a change to a named row cannot be a miss at all, so a failed ref there is a refusal of the whole request. It also measures its 8-character floor in BYTES and truncates an ambiguity listing with `id[:8]`, both of which memref fixes, and unifying the two is a separate change worth doing on its own. It is worth knowing they are on the same round trip: `--mark`'s own follow-up hands the operator a `ghost resolve <project> --reassess --only <ids>` command The no-match refusal renders the ref through `memory.RefusalArg`, because it fires before the ref is known to name anything: a token-shaped ref is named by its format and an oversized one by its length, and the refusal still wraps `ErrNoMatch` so `resolveSource`'s holder fallback and `ghost history`'s full-id fallback still branch on it.
- `internal/supersede/` — `ghost supersede`: LLM-classified 'supersedes'/'causes' link creation over live memories, batched 8 candidate pairs per harness call with a content-keyed NEITHER cache (`supersede_checked`, schema v8, cascading FKs) so a converged project's fresh candidates make zero calls — except reversed ones, which are never cached and are re-asked every pass — a live edge whose endpoints HAVE moved is judged every pass so it keeps self-healing, and one whose endpoints have not is held quiet by skip-if-unchanged and judged by no pass at all, WHICHER WAY THE SCAN PROPOSED IT (#787: the orientation the scan AGREES with was the one that bypassed the test, so an unchanged live edge was re-billed and re-rolled on every pass even though that is the shape every real supersedes edge is in). The quiet is measured against the EDGE'S OWN `created_at`, and a verdict that re-confirms the edge moves that stamp (`memory.CreateLinkJudged`, #784) to the freshness of the content THAT VERDICT WAS MADE AGAINST — the later of the two endpoints' `updated_at` as the pass read them — and not to the clock, because the two are a classify call apart and an edit landing between the read and the write (reflect's consolidation rewrite, a save through a live `ghost mcp`) would otherwise be covered by a stamp written after it and the pair would sit quiet against text no verdict was given for — which is what makes the promise below true for an edge that has ever been edited, since a tag or importance edit bumps `updated_at`. `PromoteToGlobal` does NOT, and that is a decision rather than an oversight (#807): a promotion is a MOVE — the row's text, `created_at`, category and importance are all what they were — and `updated_at` is the column the two supersede readers above take to mean “the CONTENT of this row changed”. Measured, not argued: with the bump in place, promoting an endpoint of a live edge re-armed skip-if-unchanged and re-billed a pair nobody edited (a paid route to the re-rolling #779 measured), and on a CYCLE it flipped `orient` — the one shape with no stored direction to fall back on — so `ghost supersede --reassess` stood the backwards edge #641 found in the wild and withdrew the correct one, off a bookkeeping column. `TogglePin` does NOT either, and for the same reason (#820): a pin is a decision about how a memory is SURFACED, not about what it says, and the readers that act on it read `pinned` itself — the `_global` passive bucket's own order (`pinned DESC, importance DESC, updated_at DESC, id`), the composite ranking's decay exemption, and `ghost resolve`'s hold-back. The column `updated_at` means something else and two readers in another package take it to mean “the CONTENT of this row changed”. Measured the same way as the promotion: with the bump in place, pinning an endpoint of a live edge re-armed skip-if-unchanged and re-billed a pair nobody edited — and pinning is something an operator does MID-SESSION, while a stop hook's lifecycle phase is mid-flight, so this is the more ordinary of the two triggers — and on a CYCLE it flipped `orient` a second time, standing the backwards edge #641 found in the wild off a bookkeeping column. `pinMemoryTx`, the save-time `pin` on the `Upsert` path, already left the stamp alone; this brings the operator's explicit `ghost_memory_pin` into step with it. What no longer moves with a pin, named: the supersede pass's chronology and freshness test, above; `ghost prune`'s grace, whose `pruneActivitySQL` is `COALESCE(last_accessed, max(updated_at, created_at, expires_at))` — pinning a row no longer refreshes it, and `Touch` has no caller so the grace is measured from the row's last WRITE, which a pin is not; and the `updated_at` TIE-BREAK in that `_global` order, and nothing else there, because `pinned DESC` is that order's PRIMARY key and what is given up is only the ability of a pin to reorder two rows that tie on (pinned, importance). The pin is still recorded where a pin is read: `memories.pinned`, and no `memory_history` version, because that table records none of the columns this statement changes. The one reader whose view changes is `ghost prune`, which measures a row's grace from `updated_at` and so is no longer refreshed by a promotion; that is the right direction for a content-freshness column, and an operator who wants the row kept says so with a `persistent` tier or a pin. A classify call that FAILS is re-issued ONCE, after `classifyRetryDelay` (2s, injectable through `SetRetryDelay`) and counted by `Retries()` — it is on both reports and the reassess summary log — because a real `opencode run` that exited 1 once cost a whole `ghost supersede` pass in #699's rehearsal, and the rerun a minute later answered normally. ONE retry, not a schedule, and it decides nothing: the second failure is returned unchanged so a genuinely dead harness is exactly as fatal as before, `Run` still aborts and writes nothing, an UNPARSEABLE reply is not a transport failure and is not retried, and the wait is context-bounded so a cancelled run does not spend a second spawn to be told the same thing. Pairs are oriented by `updated_at` and then by `created_at` (`orient`), and a pair tying on BOTH is not proposed at all — the prompt also carries each note's `created_at` and offers a fourth verdict, `reversed`: a supersedes link only points newer→older, so a verdict saying the OLDER note is the current one is refused (never written, never cached) rather than written backwards (#641). THE UNIT THE PASS JUDGES IS THE UNORDERED PAIR, reconciled on `pairKey` across BOTH sources (#778), and that is a consequence of the harm rather than tidiness: a demotion lands on the target alone, so two 'supersedes' edges in opposite directions demote BOTH memories of one pair and neither withdraws the other.
