The old reconciliation keyed the ordered `{newer,older}` pair, so a live edge pointing the opposite way from the scan's own orientation for the same two rows matched nothing and both orientations were put to one classifier in a single pass — three dry runs over copies of three real projects proposed both directions of `02EA044F`/`74CE9D10`, and a verdict that cannot decline a direction wrote the CYCLE. Three rules within one pass, each a counted, named refusal on the report, none a guess: (1) a pair is classified at most once per pass, and a live edge decides its DIRECTION whichever way the scan ordered it — **and “a live edge” means an edge of EITHER relation, which is #823 and was the whole fix**: `causes` is written older→newer, so a pair holding one was reconciled by the same `pairKey` with NO direction override and its direction came from `orient`, i.e. from `updated_at`. So a `causes` edge running against the timestamps was re-proposed every pass in the flipped direction, paid a classify call for the re-ask for as long as the edge lived, and answered `causes` again — which wrote the SECOND direction of the pair. The edge's direction is the one a `reversed` verdict must be shown, since that is the wrong edge the pass and `--reassess` both have to reach, and a contradicting scan proposal is refused AS A DIRECTION and counted as one (`Result.OppositeLive`), which is deliberately a count of refused orientations and not of classifications, because the pair then faces skip-if-unchanged like any untouched live edge. `liveEdge`/`pairDirection` are where the two relations are normalised to one shape, because a shape carrying `(source, target)` would assert two different directions for one pair depending on which relation the read came from; the three answers `pairDirection` gives are three different things and not one answer with a flag (a `supersedes` CYCLE, case 1; a `supersedes` edge winning over a `causes` edge that contradicts it, case 2; a `causes` edge, or a `causes` cycle, case 3), and case 2 is not a refusal on purpose: only ONE of that pair's edges demotes, and a verdict settles both, so refusing it would hand a contradiction the ordinary pass used to clear for free to an operator. A pair a `causes` edge names is also never NEITHER-cache-skipped for the same reason one holding a `supersedes` edge is not — a cache skip is treated as a `neither` verdict, so skipping it asserts the edge is not there, which was graph-only staleness and harmless while a `causes` edge was only swept, and is not harmless now that its direction decides what the pass may write; (2) a pair the graph already claims in BOTH directions is refused outright (`Result.Bidirectional`), not judged, because no third direction exists and a SUPERSEDES would re-affirm one half of a cycle, with the report naming `ghost supersede <project> --reassess --consensus 3 --apply` (APPLIED, and GATED — the flagless one is a dry run and the ungated one acts on a single verdict) as the repair, since withdrawal is not this pass's job, as everywhere else here. **That refusal is `supersedes` and ONLY `supersedes`, and a `causes` cycle is judged instead of refused** — the one place the two relations differ, and the difference is the harm each does. A `supersedes` cycle demotes BOTH of the pair's memories and neither edge withdraws the other; nothing demotes on a `causes` edge. And `--reassess` loads live `supersedes`/`llm` edges and can never see a `causes` one, so refusing a `causes` cycle would leave a contradiction whose advertised repair cannot reach it — a worse state than the one the refusal prevents. Such a pair is therefore judged ONCE in the direction the timestamps give it, the only direction a pair whose two edges disagree has (`LinksByRelationSource` has no ORDER BY, so returning the first edge's direction would hand the pair's chronology to a coin flip on two hex ids), and the ordinary apply block converges it: an affirming verdict writes that direction and drops the other, a denying one drops both. It is held quiet by the edge's stamp exactly like every other linked pair, because a `causes` edge's stamp is a judgement time whatever else it is not. A pair whose ONLY live edges are `causes` — one of them or a cycle of them — and whose two rows ALSO tie on `updated_at` and `created_at` is counted as `Result.Unoriented` with everything else of that count, because there is no direction to ask “which is newer” about: a `supersedes` edge carries one and a `causes` edge does not, so a tied pair linked only by `causes` is the one live shape in the rule set that is not re-judged, which is why the report line says so. **AND A `causes` EDGE SUPPLIES A STAMP, NOT A DIRECTION — WHICH IS A TYPE, NOT A CONVENTION**: `pairDirection` returns FOUR NAMED states rather than a bool, and the two refusals are not the same refusal — a pair whose `supersedes` edges disagree is a `supersedes` cycle and is REFUSED, a pair whose `causes` edges disagree is a `causes` cycle and is JUDGED, but only when nothing else describes the pair. A pair holding a live `supersedes` edge **and** a `causes` cycle is exactly what a pre-#823 store holds (that writer shipped unguarded), and letting the `causes` half decide its direction is the #641 shape in a new costume: the live edge this pass, `--reassess` and resolve's piggyback can all reach is the `supersedes` one, so judging the pair the other way asks about an edge that is not there, and a denying verdict then invalidates `supersedes` in the TIMESTAMPS' direction — which is not where the live edge is — so nothing is withdrawn while the report says it was. A pass that FAILS reports the failure on stderr, and on the detached lifecycle path that stream is `lifecycle.log` — a file in the data directory that a later reading surfaces. Every supersede, reassess and relation error and log line that names a stored id therefore renders each id through `memory.RefusalArg("memory_id", …)`: the pair prefixes, the store-call prefixes and the pass's own pair log lines used to interpolate both ids with a bare `%s`, and a stored id is not token-guarded — `ghost import` writes an artifact's id verbatim and `memory.CheckImportedID` never asks the guard, so a `ghp_`-shaped 40-character id is a legal stored id. An ordinary id now reads quoted, which is the renderer's one visible change on prefixes that printed it bare, while a token-shaped or over-`ArgEchoMaxBytes` id is withheld behind a placeholder naming the field and the format the guard found, so the line still says a write failed without relocating what it was about. The states are named because a bool made the caller re-derive which refusal it was holding, which is how that got wrong the first time. The REPLY on the classifier's lines is rendered the same way as the ids beside it: `classifyChunk`'s two WARNs and the `errUnparseableVerdict` message go through `logReply` (`memory.RefusalArg("reply", …)`, the same rule and reasons as resolve's), and the error renders the reply where `Classify` BUILDS it, so `ClassifyBatch`'s lone-pair and fallback lines, and any later print of the error, cannot carry it raw; `errors.Is` still matches the sentinel. Pinned by `TestClassifierLogsTheReplyBoundedAndGuarded` and `TestUnparseableVerdictErrorCarriesARenderedReply`.

**A `causes` EDGE'S DIRECTION MAY NOT REACH A `supersedes` VERDICT, AND THAT IS A BLOCKER RATHER THAN A NICETY (A #641 HARM THE PASS PRODUCES)**: the first cut of #823 gave every live edge its own direction, and read through a `causes` override a live `causes newer→older` edge asks “does the January note supersede the September one”, the model answers SUPERSEDES, and the SUPERSEDES branch writes `supersedes older→newer` — an edge that DEMOTES the memory that is actually current and buries it, on a pair the store's own ranking then reads as settled. So `pairDirection` returns a `pairVerdict` whose `newer`/`older` and whose `stamp` are SEPARATE FIELDS, and the two `causes` cases leave the direction EMPTY: a `causes` edge settles the STAMP — which is what skip-if-unchanged reads, so the re-bill is fixed either way — and the `livePair` entry that keeps the pair out of the NEITHER cache, and nothing about the question. The `causes` case builds its candidate from the same `orient` a fresh pair uses, which is the pre-#823 behaviour for every pair without a live `supersedes` edge, so the prompt never labels the chronologically older note NEWER for a supersedes question. A live `SUPERSEDES` edge keeps its own override, deliberately: that edge IS the demotion, so the direction to judge it in is the direction it was written in, and a REVERSED answer is how the model declines a backwards claim. The consequences are named because they are behaviour, not accidents: `Result.OppositeLive` is `supersedes`-ONLY (a `causes` edge is never proposed against, because it is never a direction), so the `proposed the reverse` report line names one relation; a `causes` verdict on a pair whose live `causes` edge disagrees with the timestamps is a CORRECTION — the verdict drops the edge it contradicts and writes its own direction, so the pair converges in the pass that settles it, and `Classified.Reclassified` counts it (`TestACausesVerdictCorrectsALiveCausesEdgeThatDisagreesWithTheTimestamps`); the same verdict on a pair whose edge already agrees moves NOTHING and is not a reclassification, which is why the report's verb reads `re-linked` when the relation is unchanged AND a row of it moved and `re-affirmed` only when nothing moved (`TestACausesVerdictReAffirmsALiveCausesEdgeThatAgreesWithTheTimestamps`); and a pair linked only by `causes` whose rows tie on both timestamps is now `Unoriented` where #823 judged it, which is the same answer a fresh tied pair gets and is why the report line has to say which live shape it is. That refusal is counted ONCE, and counting it per half was a review finding: `order` is the union of the scan's fresh candidates and the live-edge pairs, so a tied `causes`-only pair is reached by both and a bulk-imported corpus — where every pair ties by construction — printed each one twice on a line documented as a per-pair count. `Selection` therefore carries `unorientedPairs` beside `Unoriented`, and the reconciliation suppresses its own increment for a pair the scan already refused; the SCAN's set is the one kept because it is the one built for the purpose, so a tied `causes` pair the scan never reached as a near neighbour is still counted here (`TestATiedCausesOnlyPairTheScanAlsoFoundIsCountedOnce`, and both halves of the guard are mutation-checked). A live `supersedes` edge never had the double count because the `pairDirected` path never calls `orient` — the edge carries a direction, so there is nothing to refuse. **AND EVERY VERDICT OWES THE PAIR'S *OTHER* EDGE, WHICH MAKES A RE-AFFIRMATION NOT AUTOMATICALLY A NON-EVENT**: the affirming verdicts write an edge in the direction they were asked about and the guard in front of the write closes a cycle, while the DENYING verdicts write nothing — so `sweepCausesBothWays` is the only thing that can, and a sweep stopping at one direction left the pair's other `causes` edge live while `ReclassifiedNoWrite` and the row's `already gone` marker both said the pair was unlinked. A SUPERSEDES verdict therefore drops the pair's `causes` edges in BOTH directions (one is the edge a supersession implies, the other the edge that CONTRADICTS it, and `supersedes A→B` beside `causes A→B` is a contradiction rather than a redundancy because a `causes` source→target edge asserts the reverse chronology), and so does every other verdict. Each second statement is gated on `claimsHold` — the pass having READ an edge that way — so the ordinary pair pays no second write. And because a verdict can KEEP the relation the pair was judged around and still remove a row of the other one (a `causes` cycle answered CAUSES keeps the direction it was asked about and drops the edge asserting the other; a SUPERSEDES verdict re-affirming a `supersedes` edge beside a `causes` cycle drops both of the cycle's edges), `Result.Reclassified` reads `verdict != liveRelation || verdictDrops(...)[either half] > 0` rather than the first test alone, and the CLI's reclassified-row predicate carries `c.CausesDropped > 0` (and, for the dry-run shape, `c.CausesDroppable > 0`) for the same reason. Since #845 that test is narrowed once more by `movedSomething`, because the withheld half of `verdictDrops` is no longer a change: a DENYING verdict on a pair whose only live edge was the withheld `supersedes` one moves nothing, and counting it prints `1 reclassified` over an `--apply` pass that wrote no link and deleted none. The relation test still differs for such a pair, so the exclusion cannot be folded into it and is its own term — `movedSomething = dropCauses > 0` — except on a CAUSES verdict, which is exempt because the `causes` write beside the withheld withdrawal really does land, which is the difference between a pair that changed and a pair that only declined. `verdictDrops` is a PREDICTION built from the same `claimsHold` predicates the apply block's sweeps are gated on, and it is computed BEFORE the apply block because a dry run runs no block and is left with only a prediction — a count only an applied run can reach is a count the preview does not preview — and the row's two clauses are INDEPENDENT rather than alternatives in a switch, because one verdict can re-link a pair AND drop an edge and a switch that took one branch printed the re-link over a run that also deleted a row. The UNGATED reverse sweep in the `causes` branch is the one guard with no observable effect in `Result`, and it is not for that reason untested: a sweep that can only move a row the pass has already seen is worth one write and not two, so `TestTheCausesSweepIsSkippedWhereThereIsNothingToSweep` counts the invalidations the pass ISSUES through a store spy rather than the rows that moved, which is the third question where `causesTakenStore` counts what moved and the row counts what was predicted. **AND THAT REPAIR WORKS, WHICH IS A SEPARATE INVARIANT AND A SEPARATE BUG**: `Reassess` groups the live edges by the UNORDERED pair before it asks anything, so a cycle is ONE question read as a DIRECTION — `supersedes` names one of the two live edges as current, so that edge stands and its reverse is withdrawn; `reversed` names the other, so the other stands; `neither`/`causes` deny the replacement in either direction and BOTH are withdrawn. Before #780 it judged every edge as an independent candidate, so a cycle was asked twice and a direction-blind verdict confirmed both (`Confirmed 2, Withdrawn 0`) — the pass that exists to remove wrong edges kept the worst one, and the command the creation pass advertised as its repair was a promise with nothing behind it. TWO outcomes decide NOTHING and both withdraw NEITHER, because withdrawing half of a cycle on a hunch leaves a live edge the pass never judged — the state the cycle itself is a report about — and they are SEPARATE values because the operator's next step differs: `CycleNoVerdict` (a failed call, an unparseable reply — the pass asked and got nothing, so a RERUN answers it) and `CycleUnoriented` (both notes share every timestamp, the #778 tie, so there was no direction to ask about and a rerun cannot change it, so the OPERATOR decides). One sentence claiming both was a review finding: it reported the transient #699 harness failure as a missing chronology and answered it with "delete an edge". `CycleOutcome` values are bare words, never prose, because the REPORT owns the wording and only the report knows whether the run was a dry run — a constant that spelled out "was withdrawn" put a past-tense claim under a list of "would withdraw" rows. The block's PER-EDGE verbs come from `apply` and from `WithdrawnEdge.Written` (`would withdraw` / `withdrew` / `already gone` / `not reached … STILL LIVE`), never from the outcome and never from `apply` alone — a failed InvalidateLink leaves the denied edge live. The line ABOUT the pair states the decision in words true in every mode ("so that edge stands and its reverse is denied"), so it can never contradict the markers under it; `ghost_link_withdraw` is named with all THREE of its parameters (`project_id`, `source_id`, `target_id`) because the handler refuses a call missing any of them, and a fallback naming two of the three would move the dead command from the CLI to the tool surface. `VetoSupersede` is NOT applied to a cycle: it asks one orientation's question and a cycle has two over the same two bodies. Every row it withdraws carries the SAME per-row sweep arm the ordinary path uses, and that is worth its own assertion per branch: a withdrawal that DENIES the pair also drops the 'causes' edge pointing into the note it just decided is current — except on a CAUSES verdict, which affirms that relation and sweeps nothing. `InvalidateLink` writes no history row for a non-supersedes relation, so a cycle row that lost its sweep is a second deletion with nothing in `memory_history` to show it, and `TestReassessSweepsTheCausesEdgeOfEveryCycleWithdrawalItDenies` pins all four arms (SUPERSEDES, REVERSED, both NEITHER rows, and the CAUSES row that must NOT sweep) plus the dry-run PREDICTION, which comes from a different read than the apply sweep's own count. The report's `cycle:` block is a LIST of ids and says which edge stands, because a headline `withdrew 0` over a pair with two live edges is the one sentence this report must not leave standing alone, and an undecided cycle prints the two `ghost supersede <project> --withdraw <src> <tgt> --apply` commands (through `internal/followup.WithdrawCommand`, the one place that decides how a project name is spelled as a shell argument — the creation pass's own cycle line goes through `followup.ReassessCommand` the same way, because a command printed with a literal `<project>` is one the operator has to edit, and an edit is where a repair lands in the wrong project); (3) a fresh pair whose two rows tie on `updated_at` AND `created_at` is never proposed (`Result.Unoriented`) — a bulk import stamps a whole batch with one stamp (33 dingo rows share `2026-09-20 09:26:05`) and the only remaining "chronology" would be two hex ids, so the old id tiebreak decided the direction of real supersessions by which hash sorted higher. A live edge naming a tied pair is STILL revalidated, because it already carries a direction: the tie refuses proposing one and says nothing about judging one. `created_at` is the tiebreak and never the primary order — it mislabels a note re-saved long after it was created, which is #641. **AND A FOURTH RULE, WHICH IS ABOUT TWO PASSES RATHER THAN ONE (#806)**: all three above are decided from what THIS pass read, and `ghost supersede` is a command an operator (or the stop hook's lifecycle phase) can run twice over one project at once. Two passes that read the same unclaimed pair, scan it in OPPOSITE directions and both reach a verdict wrote BOTH directions of it — the cycle #778 found in a real store, with a second process supplying the half that needed one. Opposite orientations need an endpoint's `updated_at` to move between the two scans, which is ordinary (a tag edit through a live `ghost mcp`), and the window is a whole classify call wide, because a pass reads the graph, scans, then spends seconds to minutes before its first write. So the fix is NOT a lifecycle lock — a lock is a convention between the processes that take it, and a command run twice is exactly where that convention has no enforcement; it is `memory.Store.CreateLinkUnopposed`, which re-reads the pair's REVERSE edge inside the SAME `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction that inserts this one. `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` takes the database write lock before the first statement, so the two writers are serialised and the loser's read happens after the winner's commit; a check outside the transaction would not close this at all, since both writers could read “no reverse edge” and both would insert. A refusal is a normal outcome (`Result.ReverseLive`, `Classified.OpposedLive`, and a `not written` marker on the row), not an error: the edge that exists is the earlier writer's, and the next pass judges the pair in the live edge's own direction. **AND A VERDICT IS NOT A WRITE, SO THE SUMMARY COUNTS THE MODE'S OWN FACT (#834)**: `Result.Created` has always been a WRITE count — 0 in a dry run — and `Result.CausesCreated` was a VERDICT count wearing that name, which since #823 became readable as a write count because the 'causes' write is guarded and can be refused in the same run that reached the verdict. `Result.CausesWritten` is the missing write count and `Created`'s twin, and it is incremented only where `CreateLinkUnopposed` for `causes` RETURNS TRUE. The summary's `N supersedes, N causes` therefore counts `Created` and `CausesWritten` under `--apply` and `Confirmed` and `CausesCreated` in a dry run, rather than printing both or picking one for both modes: under `--apply` the honest number is what the graph now holds, and the refusal has its own line a few lines below, so a summary that counted it as well would be claiming an edge the same report takes back. A refused write stays reported where it happened — `Result.ReverseLive`, `Classified.OpposedLive` and the row's `not written` marker — and the row is the only place a refusal is counted. `Result.WouldWriteLinks` keys off the VERDICTS and is deliberately left there: it answers 'would an --apply of THIS result have something to write', and in a dry run there are no writes to read, so swapping in `CausesWritten` would silence the `Re-run with --apply to write these links.` hint on exactly the pass whose verdict the operator most needs to hear about. The one process that can reach a `causes` refusal is the second one racing it, so the two-process harness is what proves the split (`TestTwoApplyPassesCannotWriteACausesCycle`: verdicts 2, written 1, refused 1, and `verdicts-written == refused`) and the report's own mode-dependent selection is held by `supersedeReportCountsEdges` over a `Result` a single process cannot produce. The guard is a SEPARATE method from `CreateLinkJudged` rather than a rule inside it, because the bench seeders and the cycle fixtures must still be able to WRITE a cycle — it is the state a real store holds, and the repair pass and its tests can only describe it by building it. It is ALSO relation-agnostic: `CreateLinkUnopposed` refuses the reverse of whatever relation it is handed, and the `causes` write goes through it too since #823. **The two halves had to land TOGETHER, and the order matters, because #819 shipped the `causes` write unguarded on purpose**: with the override built from live `supersedes` edges only, a pair whose `causes` edge ran against the timestamps was re-proposed flipped on every pass, answered `causes` again, and would have been REFUSED on every one — a refusal that never converges, at a classify call per pass for as long as the edge lives, which is worse than the contradiction it prevents. `TestTheCausesWriteIsNotGuarded` pinned that asymmetry from the pass's side so a later change could not guard both relations for the sake of symmetry, and it is `TestTheCausesWriteIsNowGuarded` that replaces it. The guard stays, and it is needed for a second reason once the `causes` direction stopped being an override: the guard is the only thing that can make a two-pass race impossible, and a pass that asks a pair and then REFUSES the write would re-ask it for ever. So a `causes` verdict still converges — it drops the edge it contradicts and then writes its own direction, unopposed — and the refusal is the one-off race `supersedes` has, reported as `not written` (`TestTwoApplyPassesCannotWriteACausesCycle`). Inside the `causes` branch the SWEEPS run BEFORE the write, and that order is load-bearing rather than stylistic — written first, the guarded write finds a cycle's other half live and refuses, the sweep then drops that half, and the pair ends up holding the OLD edge with its OLD stamp, so the graph looks settled and the pair is re-billed on the next pass for ever. Each sweep is additionally gated on the pass having READ an edge the other way (`claimsHold`), because a second write in the run that can only ever move a row the pass has already seen is the one worth making; and both affirming verdicts now sweep the OTHER relation in BOTH directions, because a verdict of SUPERSEDES denies a `causes` claim whichever end it is written from, and a pair holding `supersedes A→B` beside `causes A→B` is a contradiction rather than the redundancy the old one-directional sweep assumed. `TestTwoApplyPassesCannotWriteACycle` and `TestTwoApplyPassesCannotWriteACausesCycle` are the two-process reproduction for each relation (a built helper, two real stores on one file, a barrier held inside the classify call, a retag between the two scans, and the same fixture with `-verdict causes`), and `TestApplyPassWritesAnEdgeWhenNothingOpposesIt` is the other half: a guard that refused everything would pass both. **AND THE REPAIR PASS IS PARTIAL PER CALL, NOT PER PASS (#808)**: a project's open pairs are chunked across several harness calls, and a transport failure in one of them used to make every open pair `Unjudged` and throw away the calls already paid for. A call that fails answers nothing about the pairs IT carried and everything about the pairs the calls before it answered — complete, parsed, number-indexed verdicts — so `ClassifyBatch` returns the answered PREFIX with a `*PartialVerdictsError` carrying its length, and `Reassess` applies it and names only the failed chunk's pairs. All-or-nothing is not required for correctness, and the reason is structural rather than lucky: a cycle contributes exactly ONE candidate, so no pair's verdict is ever split across two calls. The error and the non-zero exit are unchanged, a reply whose verdict COUNT does not match is still no answer at all (the reply-to-pair mapping is exactly what is in doubt), and `Run` still treats a classify error as fatal rather than acting on a prefix — the creation pass's contract is that a transport failure cannot look like a successful empty pass, and its per-pair writes are the thing that pass exists to make. The `supersede_checked` NEITHER cache stays keyed by the ORDERED pair, because a cached verdict is about a direction; the unordered key is the pass's own, and the cache-skip test reads the unordered pair so a live edge keeps its self-healing whichever source proposed the pair. Since #823 that test also covers `causes` (above), which is the end of the graph-only-staleness bargain the cache was allowed to make: a cache skip is equivalent to a `neither` verdict, so it may not stand in for one on a pair the graph is linked in EITHER relation, and the sentence in `Run`'s doc comment that excused it is gone with the sentence it excused. The cache being content-keyed is what makes that affordable — a pin, a promotion or a project move does not change the pair's text, so none of them re-opens it (#820). The pass is KEEP-biased because the edge is not an annotation: the ranking guards demote its target and `ghost resolve`'s piggyback stamps `resolved_at` on it, so a wrong edge removes a live memory from every later session. Three rules decide that, from #686's 43%-precision measurement (every wrong edge joined two notes that were BOTH still true) and #779's re-measurement of the same shape over three more real stores (108 distinct proposals, 55% precision; 0.79 for edges proposed in all three of its runs against 0.33 for one proposed in a single run, so the wrong edges are an UNSTABLE classifier as much as a wrong one, and the count of runs that proposed an edge IS the correctness signal). **#779's rubric is a COVERAGE requirement over the same four answers, and it is four separate rules because a model can pass one and still fail another**: a `supersedes` must retire EVERY claim of the older note, not one of them (a newer note retiring some and leaving the rest standing is `neither` and NEVER `causes`, since `causes` requires the older note to remain independently true and a partly-retired note by definition does not) — and the `replaced:` field may then name several claims, semicolon-separated, so that is a widening of the VALUE and not a change of the reply format, which `TestClassifyReplyFormatIsUnchanged` holds verbatim against both parsers in both directions. Three shapes are named outright because each is separately false: a release, status, changelog or incident LOG never supersedes an earlier entry, and its one exception is an entry reporting the open issue CLOSED — scoped to that rule, and NARROWING is not closing; a RECURRING DEFECT is not a fix chain (only a note that says it is fixed, and fixes it, supersedes the note that reported it); and PARALLEL INVESTIGATION is not a chain (neither note retired the other, and the timestamps say only which was written last). No deterministic rule was added for any of them, and that is the load-bearing half: `VetoSupersede`'s error argument is that a false veto costs RECALL on the creation pass, and the same rule on the repair path deletes a correct edge and is deterministic, so the pair stays unlinked until a note changes. A rubric clause the model reads is reversible by a later pass; a second prose judgement in Go is not. The rubric is ONE Ghost-owned constant, `classifyRubric` in `internal/supersede/relation.go`, carried verbatim by BOTH shipped prompts (`classifySystemPrompt` and `classifyBatchSystemPrompt` = rubric + the numbered batch contract) and sent as the system prompt through `internal/ai`'s provider — the harness runs it and owns nothing in it, so a rule change is a change here and in one place, and a rule that reached only one of the two prompts would judge single-pair and batched calls by different standards. `TestClassifyRubricCarriesEverySupersedeRule` holds the shipped text to each clause in both prompts; `TestEveryRubricRuleIsMeasuredByALabeledPair` holds the clauses to `liveSupersedeCases` in BOTH directions, because a clause with no labeled pair is a rule nothing scores and a labeled #779 pair with no clause is a shape the prompt says nothing about. The rubric asks the falsity question — after the newer note, is the older note's claim false or no longer applicable, and two notes both still true are NEITHER even on the same topic — and a `supersedes` answer must name the claim in a `replaced:` field; missing/empty/placeholder reads NEITHER, which is a decision and therefore cacheable, and the NEITHER cache key prefix moves v2→v3 for it and v3→v4 for #779's coverage rule (a cache hit is a permanent skip for the life of that text, and every rule since v2 only ever narrowed the set of answers that are SUPERSEDES — so `TestSupersedeNEITHERCachePrefixMoved` holds EVERY prior prefix apart, not just the oldest, because a bump that cleared v2 while keeping v3 would leave exactly the #686-era rows in place). `replaced:` is the twin of resolve's `closed-by:` and is read by the SAME helpers (`resolve.ReasonedField`/`resolve.ReasonlessValue`), so a value that keeps a note in one pass's injection cannot bury it in the other's ranking; a synonym verdict is no way around the field. `VetoSupersede` is the free half, before any call: an older note stating an imperative that the newer note never names as retired or changed is settled as no-edge, and the imperative vocabulary is resolve's own (`VetoKeepImperative`, which `VetoKeep` also calls) rather than a copy, so one list protects a memory from being buried and from being demoted. Its error is in resolve's direction — a false veto leaves a stale note ranked, and #641's own "status-report-fix" fixture is what that costs, which the labeled regression set pins by name. A pair whose endpoints' scopes conflict is never classified: the scope reaches `SearchVectorScoped` so the neighbour budget counts only linkable rows, and `Run` re-checks the rule over every pair it is about to spend a call on — the reclassify path included, which is the only way a link written before the rule existed is reached at all. Beside it, at the same point and for the same reason, a pair with a KEEP-FOREVER endpoint (`retention`, see "Retention tiers") is dropped: `SelectCandidates` refuses such a pair on the fresh path, but the reclassify path re-proposes an EXISTING edge whenever an endpoint changed since the edge was written and never passes through it — so an edge written before the row was declared keep-forever would be billed a classify call and re-affirmed the first time that row was edited, which is the outcome the exemption exists to prevent. The edge is left alone: withdrawing it is `--reassess` / `ghost_link_withdraw`, and since #845 the creation pass deletes no graph history at all, so this exemption has nothing left to suppress. What the refusal stops is spending a call to keep it alive. The classifier is handed note text only, so it cannot see a `memories.scope` column; the guard is structural, not a rubric line. The reclassify half saves one call per endpoint edit rather than one per pass, because skip-if-unchanged already holds an untouched pair quiet AND a re-confirm moves the stamp that test reads (#784); without the second half the first was only true of an edge no endpoint had ever touched, and every edge past its first edit was re-billed and re-rolled on every pass after it. `memory_links.created_at` is therefore a JUDGEMENT stamp and not a claim about when the edge entered the graph — it has exactly one reader, that test — which is why the fix is the upsert's conflict clause and not a `validated_at` column: a new column would mean a schema v19 step, a version bump and a second place to keep in step, for a fact the existing column already held and nothing else reads. The stamp is a PARAMETER (`CreateLinkJudged`, with `CreateLink` as the empty-stamp wrapper every other caller uses) because only the caller knows what it judged; `datetime('now')` there would stamp the write rather than the judgement. The history row is keyed on the edge BECOMING active, not on this column, so a re-confirm still writes none. The scope refusal is not counted in `Result`: against the shipped store the query drops the conflicting rows before `SelectCandidates` sees them, so a count there would read as zero for work declined, while the veto IS counted (`Result.Vetoed`, printed) because a run that declined work it did not do must not report the same totals as one that found nothing to. An existing scope-conflicting edge is left in the graph and exempted at read time by `SupersedePenalties`, by `ghost resolve`'s supersedes piggyback, and by the two fold-target liveness checks — the piggyback is the one that would have been persistent, stamping `resolved_at` on the older endpoint. A vetoed pair's existing edge is left alone there — **AND SINCE #845 SO IS EVERY PAIR'S `supersedes` EDGE, WHICH IS THE PASS'S RULE AND NOT `--apply`'s: AN ORDINARY PASS REPORTS A WITHDRAWAL AND NEVER MAKES ONE, WHILE ITS WRITES ARE UNCHANGED**. Measured over a real store, 4 of 11 withdrawals the pass made were correct, 1 unsure and 6 wrong, and the wrong ones shared a shape — a newer note retiring ONE claim of an older note whose other claims still held. The verdict was a fair reading of the two bodies under the every-claim rule and the pass then deleted a correct edge, which is the one direction the KEEP bias below does not reach: the error surfaces as a stale memory promoted and a current one hidden, both behind a flag the operator set for WRITES, and a pass whose errors are invisible until a memory stops being reminded of is not a KEEP-biased pass. So `ordinaryPassWithdrawsSupersedes` is a package constant and NOT an `Options` field — it is not a threshold an operator tunes, and there is no setting under which a creation pass stops REPORTING a withdrawal it declines to make — and the removal belongs to the two commands a person asked for: `ghost supersede <project> --reassess --consensus 3 --apply` re-judges the edge under the current rules and withdraws what no longer holds, `--withdraw` removes the edge the caller names without asking a model. **THE `causes` SWEEP IS NOT WITHHELD, AND THE REPAIR COULD NOT REACH IT IF IT WERE**: `Reassess` — the repair every report here names, and the only one that re-judges an edge under the current rules — loads live `'supersedes'/'llm'` edges and can never see a `'causes'` one, so a `causes` edge left contradicting a supersession would be a contradiction in the graph under a repair line pointing at a command that cannot see it, which is the exact shape `Result.Bidirectional` refuses to create when it declines to judge a cycle; `--withdraw` can remove one, but only for a pair an operator has already named by hand, which is a targeted undo rather than a re-judge. Nothing in the ranking demotes on a `causes` edge either, so the harm #845 measures cannot come from leaving one live; and the ordinary pass's both-ways sweep is what converges a `causes` cycle (#823) at all. The withholding is ONE decision taken once: `withholdSupersedesDrop` zeroes the predicted `supersedes` count and `withdrawSupersedesEdge` returns 0 for a withheld row, so the prediction the row's clause is built from and the write the apply block issues cannot disagree, and all three denying branches reach the rule through the one function rather than spelling the guard out three times. A WITHHELD withdrawal is REPORTED in both modes (`Classified.WithdrawSuppressed`, `Result.WithdrawSuppressed`) and is NOT CACHED — `newNeither` skips every pair holding a live edge, and the withheld one is still live — so the pair is re-judged and re-reported on every later pass until an operator removes the edge (`TestASuppressedWithdrawalIsNotRecordedAndIsReportedAgainNextPass`). The flag is set in the classify loop, BEFORE the pre-write existence check, and it is CLEARED at the one site that learns the endpoint is gone (`supersede.go`'s `!pairAlive` `continue`), counter and all: `memory_links.source_id`/`target_id` are `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so a concurrent reflect's consolidation replacement took the edge with it, and a row still claiming a live edge — plus a `N pair(s) withheld` line saying the edge "is still live and still demoting its target" — would be the opposite of the truth for a memory that no longer exists. Before #845 that same stale row fell through to `already gone`, which is what had happened (`TestAStaleWithheldWithdrawalSaysTheEdgeIsGoneBecauseItIs`). The row is NOT excluded from the NEITHER cache write by this (`writable` does that): it simply never had a verdict to record, since the cache key needs the pair and a stale pair is re-judged next pass anyway. **AND THE RESIDUAL RISK IS DISCLOSED WHERE THE REPAIR IS RECOMMENDED, WHICH SINCE #862 MEANS NAMING THE GATE**: the repair acts on the same verdict #845 measured at 6 wrong withdrawals in 11, so every surface that prints the repair goes through ONE renderer, `followup.ReassessCommand(project, n)` — the withheld summary line, the `STILL LIVE` clause, the two creation-pass refusals that name the repair, the note that recommends the gate, `supersedeUsage` and the docs — because a repair line naming the bare form sends the operator to the weakest version of the command that exists to repair their graph. There is no ungated renderer any surface may call: the ungated spelling is what an OPERATOR types, not what a report prints. The gate flag and the `--apply` are rendered together for a reason an earlier version got wrong — appending `--consensus 3 --apply` to a string already ending in `--apply` printed `--reassess --apply --consensus 3 --apply`, which PARSES (both flags are boolean) and which the old `strings.Contains(out, "--apply --apply")` guard could not see; the guard now counts `--apply` per line and `TestSupersedeRepairCommandsParse` parses the rendered form. The two LOG lines in `internal/supersede` are the exception and name the command as TEXT rather than through this package (it does not import `internal/followup`, and a log line has no project name to render); they carry the gate for the same reason, and a test reads their wording. Disclosure is not what replaced the gate here; the flag did (see the consensus bullet), and the disclosure now carries the operator to it. What is still disclosed rather than gated is the UNGATED default itself: `Reassess` reads one verdict per edge, so the flagless `--reassess --apply` remains the weaker path, which is why `reassessConsensusNote` says so out loud after it withdraws something, and why every recommendation names `--consensus 3` rather than the bare repair. `--withdraw <source> <target> --apply` asks no model at all and is unaffected by either. The trade is named rather than absorbed: a backwards live `supersedes` edge, #641's shape, no longer self-heals in the ordinary pass, and `ghost supersede --reassess --consensus 3 --apply` withdraws it. **A pair the pass DID move is reported per edge, which is a separate rule from the counts above**: a live 'supersedes' edge re-judged into NEITHER, CAUSES or REVERSED used to be a real graph mutation through the same `InvalidateLink` that writes the `unsupersede` row, reaching the operator only as the `N reclassified` total; it is now a row naming an edge that is STILL LIVE, while the `causes` rows the same verdicts really sweep are still mutations, and `Classified.Withdrawn` is false for a withheld row through `withdrewLiveEdge` even where `CausesDropped > 0` — the edge the row is about is in the graph, and the `[+N causes edge]` clause names the ones that are not. So `Classified` carries what a report needs and a caller cannot reconstruct — `Reclassified` (the pair held a live edge, so the verdict WITHDREW rather than declined), `ReclassifiedFrom` (WHICH relation that edge carried, and the reason it is a field and not an inference: while a live edge could only be `supersedes`, “the pair held a live edge” and “the pair held a live `supersedes` edge” were the same fact and any verdict but SUPERSEDES was necessarily a change; a live `causes` edge makes both readings reachable — a `causes` verdict on such a pair RE-AFFIRMS it and a SUPERSEDES verdict REPLACES it — so `Result.Reclassified`, the reclassified-row rendering and the resolve follow-up's own filter are all keyed on the edge's relation rather than on the verdict), `Withdrawn` (this call's invalidation moved it: false in a dry run, false under `--apply` when a concurrent pass took it first, and false for a WITHHELD withdrawal even where the `causes` sweep beside it moved a row), `WithdrawSuppressed` (the verdict WOULD have taken the pair's live 'supersedes' edge away and this call declined to, #845 — the third of the three facts a reclassified row carries, and the only one of the three an operator can settle by re-running the same command, because a concurrent pass that took the edge first leaves nothing owed while a withheld edge is still live and still demoting its target; it is CLEARED again where the apply block finds an endpoint gone, since the cascade took the edge and there is nothing live left to claim), `CausesDropped` (the SECOND row the same verdict moves — a NEITHER or a REVERSED also drops the pair's live `causes` edge — counted from what the write RETURNED) and `CausesDroppable` (the dry run's FORECAST of that count, and it costs no read because it is counted off the edges the pass already loaded: forecasting a deletion with a prediction QUERY would put a second read's failure into a pass whose only fatal error is a write error, and a count only an applied run can reach is a count the preview does not preview). Both come from ONE function, `verdictDrops`, gated on the same `claimsHold` predicates the apply block's sweeps are gated on, so the prediction and the writes cannot disagree about whether there IS a row to move; it returns a THIRD value — what `withholdSupersedesDrop` (#845) decided about the `supersedes` half — rather than leaving each caller to re-derive it, because the ONE prediction, the ONE row field, the ONE counter and the ONE apply-block guard all have to be derived from the same decision and a suppression applied in two places is one a later change can half-undo; the two numbers CAN differ, because a concurrent pass may take a row in between, which is why the observed one is what `--apply` reports and the forecast is only what a dry run has. The reclassified-row predicate carries `c.CausesDroppable > 0` as well as `c.CausesDropped > 0` for the same reason it carries the second one: a `causes` cycle answered CAUSES keeps the relation the row is about and still deletes a row, and in a dry run that row has to be REACHED for the forecast to be printed at all (`TestSupersedePairLinesNamesTheEdgeACausesCycleRemoved` holds a dry-run fixture that a real dry run can actually emit — `Withdrawn` false AND `CausesDropped` 0 — because a fixture leaving the observed count at 1 is reached through a field a dry run never sets and would prove nothing about the dry run's own path) and `TargetProjectID` — the CLI renders one row per withdrawal in the SHAPE and the WORDING `--reassess` uses for its own (both ids, the verdict's reason, and a marker that claims a write only where one landed), and an `--apply` run hands the withdrawn targets to the same `withdrawnTargets` / `writeReassessTargets` / `supersedeReassessFollowup` block the two repair modes use, so a third surface cannot print a third version of the command (#785). That follow-up is built over `supersedes` withdrawals ALONE, and the RELATION is a filter rather than an assumption: the repair is a `ghost resolve --reassess`, resolve's supersedes piggyback acts on `supersedes`/`llm` edges only, and a withdrawn `causes` edge orphaned no `resolved_at` — so naming its target sends the operator to clear a memory nothing is holding down. A WITHHELD `supersedes` withdrawal is excluded from it for the same reason reached from the other side, and by its OWN field rather than by `Withdrawn`: the edge is still live, so the piggyback that stamped the `resolved_at` is still holding that target down and there is no orphaned resolution to clear, while a concurrent pass leaving the identical state behind left the edge NOT there. Listing a withheld pair would print a repair for a memory nothing needs re-hiding, and in a dry run it would also print `Re-run with --apply to withdraw these edges.` — a promise of a flag that does not withdraw it, which is how a report talks an operator into a second run that cannot help (`TestReclassifiedWithdrawalsNamesEveryOrphanedTarget` pins the empty list, because since #845 every `Reclassified`-from-`supersedes` + denying-verdict row this pass can reach is withheld, so that guard is the whole of the function's reach and deleting it would silently start printing follow-ups for edges that are in the graph). A `causes`→`supersedes` change is still REPORTED, by the row, because a report that hid a graph change would be the other half of the same lie. A `NEITHER` on a pair the graph never linked is NOT a withdrawal and is reported as none: no edge justified a resolution for it, so naming it would send the operator to re-judge a row for nothing. And `ghost supersede <project> --reassess [--apply]` (`internal/supersede/reassess.go`) is the repair path: it re-judges every live 'supersedes'/'llm' edge under the current rules and withdraws the ones that come back NEITHER, vetoed, CAUSES or REVERSED through `InvalidateLink` (which writes the `unsupersede` history row), printing each withdrawn edge with the rule that withdrew it, which half decided it (`veto, no harness call` vs `classifier`) and — because a withdrawal sweeps the pair's 'causes' edge as `Run` does on the same self-contradicting verdicts — how many `causes` rows that took (`WithdrawnEdge.CausesSwept` is the PREDICTION in a dry run and the OBSERVED count under `--apply` — a row never claims a deletion that did not happen, and a sweep that ERRORED sets `SweepFailed` and prints "unknown" instead of a count (`ReassessResult.CausesSweepFailed` counts those rows, separately from the swept ones, because "swept 0" and "could not tell" are different sentences); a `causes` VERDICT sweeps nothing, because it affirms that relation). The prediction is read through each pair's OWN endpoints (`GetLinks` on the older id), NOT through `LinksByRelationSource`: the sweep deletes by id with no project predicate, so a pair whose older note has been promoted to `_global` or moved by `ghost project merge` is still swept, and a project-scoped read (which joins on the edge's source, and a 'causes' edge's source is the OLDER note) cannot see it. A dry run reports the count it is about to print, not `Result.Withdrawn` (which is 0 by construction in a dry run — the same rule `reassessSummaryLine` follows on the resolve side), and a failed invalidation returns the edges that DID land with the error, because each is its own transaction and a later pass will not see them again. That 'causes' sweep is predicted by a read that ONLY a dry run makes: under `--apply` every row's count comes from the sweep itself, so the prediction is not read at all and a discarded read can never be the reason a repair failed (`TestReassessUnderApplyNeverReadsThePrediction`). A dry run whose prediction read fails marks the row's second deletion unknown (`WithdrawnEdge.PredictionUnknown`, counted as `CausesPredictionFailed`) instead of printing a count nobody looked for, and `SweepFailed` stays false — no write ran. A classify call that FAILED is not fatal to those rows either, and that is the direction of the fix (#699: one dead `opencode run` cost a real rehearsal every withdrawal, 76 of 87 edges waiting on a rerun, including the ones the veto had settled for free): the settled withdrawals apply, the pairs the classifier never answered move to `ReassessResult.Unjudged` with their edges LEFT ALIVE, and the pass still returns an error so the exit says "rerun me". Every open pair is unjudged, not the subset the harness named last — a call that failed answers nothing about anything it carried, and a verdict-count mismatch is the same state, so both take this path. That is the UNGATED rule, and the gate narrows it rather than widening it: under `--consensus N` the bar for "decide this pair" is all N answers, so a pair one answer short of a quorum is unjudged with its edge live and the report says `no quorum` rather than `no verdict` — which is why `unjudgedReason` and the `Unjudged` field's own doc are both worded on the gate rather than on the harness. Withdrawing is the safe direction and settling the veto needs no harness, which is what makes it the one case where a partial repair is still a repair. The withdrawal sweeps the other relation's edge too, as `Run` does on the same self-contradicting verdicts, but NOT under a CAUSES verdict: there the existing 'causes' edge may be saying something true. THIS IS THE ONE PLACE THE VETO'S ERROR ARGUMENT DOES NOT CARRY OVER: on the creation pass a false veto costs recall, here it deletes a correct edge, and since the veto is deterministic on the same two note bodies the ordinary pass re-fires it forever, so the pair cannot be re-linked until a note changes — which is why the report marks those rows. Withdrawing is only half the repair: the memory is still stamped `resolved_at`, and only `ghost resolve --reassess` can clear it, so the chain is pinned end to end in `TestReassessResolvesAChainIntoResolveReassess` `supersede.Withdraw` (`withdraw.go`) is the OTHER repair, for an edge those rules still accept: a pair that is wrong for a reason no rubric can see (the newer note is not a replacement of the older one at all) is confirmed by the classifier, so `--reassess` keeps the edge and only the caller's judgement can withdraw it. Nothing is judged there, so no harness is involved and nothing is billed — `ghost supersede <project> --withdraw <src> <tgt> [--apply]` and the MCP `ghost_link_withdraw` call the same core, through `linkCapableStore`, so an operator and an agent take one path. Its rules are the ones a delete needs: a ref is a full id or 8+ characters of one (every report abbreviates to eight, so requiring 32 would make it undrivable), matched as ONE literal, case-insensitive PREFIX query inside the project plus `_global` — which is what lets a full id of any shape resolve through the same call, and `ghost import` writes an artifact's ids verbatim, so a ref check that insisted on hex would make an imported endpoint unnameable; the 8-character floor applies to a PREFIX alone and is decided by comparing the match with the ref rather than by inspecting the ref's characters; an ambiguous ref is a refusal LISTING the matches, never a choice; a pair with no live edge is an error whose message names the target's live edges — every holder id, and the relations among them as one phrase, so a pair live in EITHER relation is answered rather than filtered; and the WHOLE request is settled before any write, so one bad pair out of five withdraws none of them (`TestWithdrawChangesNothingWhenAnyPairIsWrong`). **A PAIR NAMES A RELATION, AND WITHDRAW REACHES BOTH (#833)**: `WithdrawPair.Relation` is empty for auto-select, and `resolvePair` then walks the two directed relations in a FIXED order — `'supersedes'` then `'causes'` — because they are not equal and 'supersedes' is the one that buries its target and the one every pre-#833 call site meant. The order is a stated rule rather than whatever the read happened to return for a stated reason: a pair holding BOTH is precisely the case where it decides which edge moves. A PINNED relation is a FILTER and not a preference — it skips every other relation rather than sorting behind this one, because falling back is the wrong-edge withdrawal the pin exists to prevent — so the holder set `resolveSource` falls back to is deliberately NOT narrowed by it, which is what turns that case into a PRECISE refusal ('no live supersedes link A→B, still caused by A') rather than a refusal of the SOURCE ref as unresolvable. This is the second way a `causes` edge became a first-class claim: its DIRECTION started deciding which way a pair is judged (#823), so a pair whose only edge is a `causes` one is a pair a person has to be able to name — and the pair that most needs it is one the pass refuses for ever, the #778 tie whose two rows share both timestamps and so carry no chronology to orient by, which until #833 had NO repair at all through either surface. `WithdrawnLink.Relation` carries the resolved relation to the `InvalidateLink` write, because that write is relation-scoped: hardcoding 'supersedes' there would invalidate a row that does not exist and leave the `causes` edge live under a report that says it was withdrawn. It is a `supersedes` withdrawal that writes the `unsupersede` history row and draws the resolve follow-up, and a `causes` one that does neither, because a `causes` claim never held its target down. An edge belongs to the named project if EITHER endpoint does, and `_global` belongs to every project — but the two supersede reads are NOT the same rule, and the difference is the question each answers. `LinksInto`, which a TARGETED withdrawal decides on, takes EITHER endpoint: the caller has already NAMED the memory being un-buried, and its project may withdraw whatever buries it. It also takes the RELATION, and an empty one returns the two relations a withdrawal can act on ('supersedes' first, by `ORDER BY`, then `created_at`) — the widening #833 needs, stated as the same query rather than a second one, because a read that admitted 'supersedes' plus 'causes' and a read the selection then filtered in Go are two statements that can disagree about what is live. Only those two relations are in the empty form: a 'related' or 'contradicts' edge is not a supersession claim, nothing in the ranking reads it, and listing one in a refusal would send an operator after an edge neither surface was asked about. `LinksByRelationSource`, which feeds a PASS, takes BOTH: the pass judges a pair and then writes links on it, so judging an edge acts on both of its endpoints, and a read that admitted an edge whose target belonged to a third project would let `ghost supersede p --reassess` delete a claim in `q`'s graph. The shared scope is in both because a promotion creates it: `ghost_memory_promote` and `ghost reflect --promote-globals` move a memory into `_global` and KEEP its links, so a live edge is left with one endpoint in the project and one in the shared scope — and with the SOURCE alone every project-scoped reader lost that edge at once, so `Reassess` loaded 0, the ordinary pass never re-judged it and `Withdraw` refused, while `SupersedePenalties`, which carries NO project predicate at all, went on demoting its target and resolve's floor released the `resolved_at` the demotion was still justifying (#786). So the four `LinksByRelationSource` readers agree with EACH OTHER and with the ranking on every edge with BOTH endpoints in the caller's project or in `_global`, and `LinksInto` — used only by a targeted withdrawal — covers every edge with EITHER endpoint there; they still differ on an edge with NEITHER, which is another project's pair for that project's passes to judge. A project-scoped `--withdraw` cannot even NAME a source in another project (`ghost project merge` leaves those behind), because the refs are project-scoped and widening them would answer questions about a neighbour's corpus — so the target is resolved FIRST and the source ref then falls back to the edges already known to hold it (`memref.ResolveIn` over the holders, and only on a MISS, never on an ambiguity or a short ref). That fallback is deliberately the narrowest widening available: the extra ids come from a read the caller can already see, so the flag cannot be used to enumerate another project, and it closes the one shape that would otherwise be demoted with no single-command repair. `Withdraw`'s TARGET ref resolves with no project predicate when and only when the named project IS `_global` (`memref.ResolveIn` over `Store.MemoryIDsByIDPrefixAnyProject`, the live-row half of `AnyMemoryIDsByIDPrefix`), which is what makes both endpoints of a shared-scope pair nameable there. And the refusal for a pair with no live edge names the edges that DO bury the target while saying NOTHING about whose they are, because the read no longer establishes it. Under `--apply` it prints the follow-up through the SAME helpers `ghost supersede --reassess --apply` uses (`supersedeReassessFollowup` + `writeReassessTargets`, one formatter and one file writer for the three withdrawal paths, and the file's header names which command wrote it), so all three name the SCOPED `ghost resolve <project> --reassess --only <the withdrawn edges' target ids> --apply` plus a `--only-file` beside it — SCOPED TO THE PROJECT THAT OWNS EACH TARGET, not to the one the command named, because a resolve repair draws its pool from `ResolvedCandidates(projectID)`, which filters `project_id = ?`: a selector resolved against one project and repaired against another is a SILENT no-op under a block promising a clear. It is one project in every ordinary case, and `RepairableTargets`/`withdrawnTargets` return one group per project (both surfaces through `internal/followup.ResolveCommand`, which quotes a project name that needs it — there is no MCP tool for the repair, because `ghost_resolve` is the FORWARD pass and stamps `resolved_at`, so the MCP result names the CLI command and says so) — scoped because an unscoped repair re-judges every resolved memory in the project (#702 measured 143 rows proposed, ~35% stale). The list is deduplicated and holds only rows the call actually withdrew: a row it never reached has a live edge, so nothing was orphaned by it. The report itself carries no resolve hint and prints the target's own first line, so the reader can confirm the edge was the right one **THE CONSENSUS GATE IS A GATE ON THE WRITE, NOT ON THE QUESTION, AND IT RUNS AFTER EVERY FREE FILTER** (#779). `RunWith`'s `Options.Consensus` (N; 0 and 1 are the ungated pass, `Run` is the ungated entry point and takes the same decision) makes N independent `ClassifyBatch` calls over the SAME candidate set in the same order and acts only where all N verdicts are IDENTICAL — unanimity, not a majority, because #779's measurement is the argument: 0.79 precision for an edge proposed in all three of three runs, 0.56 in two, 0.33 in one, so a 2-of-3 majority writes exactly the middle row. THREE properties are load-bearing and each is separately falsifiable. (1) **The N passes are spent on `pending`, after the orientation refusals, the existence check, the scope and keep-forever exemptions, the veto, the NEITHER cache and skip-if-unchanged** — every one of those is a question about a pair's text and identity whose answer cannot change on a second look, so re-running them per pass would buy nothing and the multiplier would land on pairs that were never going to be asked; `Result.ConsensusPairsAsked` is `N × len(pending)` and NOT `N × Result.Candidates`, and the test that pins it is the one that also pins an unchanged live edge is not re-asked at all. (2) **Each pass is a real re-ask: the cache is READ once, before the first pass, and no row is written until every pass is in hand and the apply block runs.** Persisting pass 1's NEITHERs and letting pass 2 be served from them would be invisible in the report — the run would read as a model that changed its mind twice — and it would make the gate measure the cache rather than the model, which is what `TestConsensusAsksEveryPass` is for (the fake says NEITHER on pass 1 and SUPERSEDES on passes 2 and 3, so a served-from-cache pass 2 changes the answer). (3) **THREE outcomes, not two**, and they are `agreed`/`disputed`/`undecided` rather than a bool: unanimous means every pass's ordinary handling applies UNCHANGED, including that a unanimous `neither` is a decision and IS cached and a unanimous `reversed` is STILL refused and STILL never cached — unanimity does not license the #641 backwards link. `disputed` writes NOTHING: no link, no withdrawal, no NEITHER cache row, no row in the decided list, only a `Disputed` record carrying the ids and the tally. It is deliberately NOT a withdrawal and NOT a reversal, because N different answers is a statement about the model's stability and not about which endpoint is current, and a live edge on such a pair is untouched and can still be withdrawn on a later pass's quorum. `undecided` — no pass produced a readable verdict — is `Result.Unclassified` and NOT `NotAgreed`, because collapsing them reports a prompt or harness fault as model instability and sends an operator to raise the quorum for it. A pair the passes split on is therefore RE-ASKED next pass rather than frozen, which is the opposite of the NEITHER cache's bargain and is why it writes no cache row. The gate is OFF unless asked for on the CLI (`--consensus N`, N >= `supersede.MinConsensus`; 1 is refused rather than clamped, because a gate of one is the ungated pass wearing the flag of a safety control) and ON for the automatic phase through `reflection.supersede_consensus` (default 3), which is read ONLY inside the `AutoSupersede` branch and emitted ONLY when it is at least `MinConsensus` — an ungated phase must not print a flag asserting a gate it is not running, and the parser would refuse its own argv. `auto_supersede` itself STAYS FALSE: a gate raises the precision of an unmeasured classifier and does not make it measured. `--consensus` is REFUSED with `--withdraw`, which judges nothing — N passes would buy no agreement about a decision the operator has already made by naming the edge. Since #862 it APPLIES to `--reassess`, and that is the boundary this gate drew the other way: the repair is the only path left that DELETES a live `supersedes` edge (the creation pass has withheld that since #845), so refusing the gate there left the one deletion path in the product with the FEWEST answers behind it. It spends `N` `ClassifyBatch` calls through `classifyVotes` and settles each pair through `settleOne` on the ordinary pass's own `voteCount.quorum`, so there is ONE implementation of "the N passes disagreed" over #779's ONE measurement — a second would be a second rule that drifts. It changes WHERE the decision is made, not what a decision is, and THE ONE POPULATION IT DOES NOT COVER IS THE VETOED EDGE: `ReassessWith` runs `VetoSupersede` while it is still building `open`, appends every vetoed pair straight to `settled` and never offers it to `classifyVotes` (reassess.go), so under `--consensus 3` a vetoed edge is withdrawn after ZERO classify calls and no pass is asked to vote on it (`TestReassessWithdrawsAVetoedEdgeUnderTheGate` holds the zero calls, the withdrawal and `Consensus == 3` together, because a result reading 1 would mean the FLAG was dropped rather than the veto exempted). The reason is the veto's nature and not an exemption granted to it: it is a deterministic rule over the two note bodies, so N passes of a model cannot agree it more than one does, and asking three times to reach the same answer would buy nothing and bill for it. So the help, `docs/cli.md` and `docs/configuration.md` all name the exemption wherever they describe the gate (`TestSupersedeUsageNamesTheGateAndTheVetoItDoesNotCover` holds all THREE help surfaces separately, since a qualifier in one place and an absolute in another reads as the absolute), and this is the price of the arrangement rather than an oversight: this is the ONE place the veto's error argument does not carry over, so a gated `--reassess --apply` still deletes a correct edge on a false veto, the ordinary pass will not re-create it, and the report marks the row `veto, no harness call` and excludes it from `verdictWithdrawn` — so even the gate recommendation printed under the report counts only the rows a model decided. A unanimous verdict settles exactly as one verdict did, and a split moves NOTHING — no invalidation, no `causes` sweep, no outcome counter — and is REPORTED with its tally (`ReassessResult.NotAgreed`/`Disputed`, and `CycleNotAgreed` for a split cycle, which is a distinct outcome from `CycleNoVerdict` because the passes DID answer and the operator's next step differs). **THE THRESHOLD IS N, WHICH IS WHERE THE GATE MEETS #808's PARTIAL REPAIR, AND IT IS THE ONE PLACE THIS CHANGES THE PASS'S CONTRACT**: `classifyVotes` returns a `voteCount` per pair AND how many passes ANSWERED it, because those are not the same number and the difference IS the decision. A pair all N passes answered is read through `quorum`; a pair fewer than N answered has NOT been agreed by N passes, so acting on the answers it does have would be the ungated behaviour the gate exists to remove — a wrong withdrawal on one of three answers is exactly what #845 measured — so it is `Unjudged` with its edge LIVE and the pass still returns the error. A FAILING PASS STOPS the loop, and that is a COST decision rather than a correctness one: the pairs the failed call carried cannot reach a quorum from the passes that did answer, and re-asking against the harness that just died is what cost #699's rehearsal 76 of 87 edges. The pass returns the error, so the exit says "rerun me", and nothing is cached for an unanswered edge. **THE DEFAULT IS UNGATED, DELIBERATELY, AND THAT IS NOT A HOLE**: `Reassess` is the wrapper and `ReassessWith` the pass, so every existing `--reassess --apply` means one verdict per edge exactly as before, and a default of 3 would triple the classify calls behind a flag the operator did not type. So the gate is RECOMMENDED rather than imposed — `reassessConsensusNote` prints one line after an ungated `--apply` that really withdrew an edge, naming `--consensus 3` as a COMMAND and its cost, since that is the trade — and it is suppressed for a run that already gated, which is the case where printing it would be actively wrong. The automatic `auto_supersede` phase was already gated through `reflection.supersede_consensus` and is unchanged.
- `bench/longmemeval/` — LongMemEval-S retrieval-only harness, the one place the embedding pass can be STOPPED PARTWAY (`-embed-deadline`) without lying about it. A pass that hits its budget stops at a batch boundary with every vector it computed already appended to the `-embed-cache` file, prints cache progress INSTEAD of the metrics table, and exits `3` (`exitPartial`, distinct from the floor-violation `1`) so a caller saves the cache and re-dispatches. Two halves, both load-bearing: a partial run must never print a metric or check a floor (it covered a subset of the questions, so its numbers are not a result and would read as a regression), and the budget must count only time inside remote embed calls, never total process wall clock — a warm pass makes no remote call, so a clock-based budget would cut off a dispatch whose cache is already complete and re-dispatch would loop forever without progress. That is also why the CI job stops the pass itself rather than leaving it to the job cap, and the reason is a moving file rather than a skipped step: a cancelled step's shell is killed but its CHILD IS NOT REAPED, so the harness keeps appending after the step ends and any archive taken in that window is not a snapshot (run 36501751853: `tar` refused the moving file, and `actions/cache/save` downgraded that to a warning, so the step reported `success` over an entry that was never stored). What the self-stop buys is a QUIESCENT file and a bench step that COMPLETES, so `grew` is recorded and the save is a real snapshot. The reported `N/M` comes from `warmProgress` over the same `selectedQuestions` set the scoring loop used, so the denominator is the pass that actually ran; it counts the role-prefixed inputs, so a text embedded in both roles is two vectors, and a document-role embedding of a question never satisfies its query role.
- `internal/bench/` — `ghost bench`: retrieval-quality benchmark harness (graded dataset + sweep + staleness/recency/ranking-state/maintenance-state suites + the no-answer false-positive report, measured per condition BY the runner — `runCondition` does not skip a query with an empty rel, it measures it, and the deeper report reads that measurement rather than searching again). Each scenario/fixture asserts the SHAPE of what it can see, not today's numbers: `TestRecencyTrapAtDefault`/`TestDecayFrontier`/`TestSupersedeDemoteClearsFrontier` are scoped to the trap fixture's never-decay half (the invariance the frontier claims; the decaying half moves by design and is reported), the ranking-state suite asserts that decay and the demote are each non-inert, that they do NOT compose (the demote recovers none of the probes decay demotes, because the rows decay pushes down are the rows the demote promotes), while the headline corpus is still inert — asserted by TestDecayDoesNotPerturbGradedBench, which owns the 551-memory load and checks it in the two forms the property has: measured for decay, STRUCTURAL for the demote (no memory in that corpus declares an age_days or a supersedes edge). Do NOT re-seed or re-measure the headline corpus in the ranking-state test: this package was within ~10s of CI's 600s per-binary ceiling, and #677 added ONE more corpus-wide test — ~9s — which failed `build-and-test` at 600.038s. One corpus load is ~10s and #677's per-row validity and provenance writes are free, so the cost here is TESTS, not rows and not loads: it fits again only because TestBenchRegressionFloors, TestBenchDatasetReport, TestDecayDoesNotPerturbGradedBench, TestSweep and TestFalsePositiveReport now run CONCURRENTLY, so assume a new corpus-wide test costs ~10s of a shared CI budget and measure before you add one — `go test -race ./internal/bench -run '^Test[^L]' -count=1` reads 188s on a laptop, a different machine that flatters the answer, and `sweep_test.go` carries the measurement and the failed CI job. The fusion claim is a PAIRED 95% bootstrap interval whose lower edge has to clear -`fusionTolerance` (0.02 = 4.4 queries of NDCG at n=220) — not a comparison of two means, because the margin over the vector leg is 0.018 with an interval of [+0.003, +0.034]. A PUBLISHED NUMBER FROM THIS PACKAGE IS A FUNCTION OF THE FIXTURE, not of the run, and it takes two store properties to make that true: every seeded row is stored under `corpusID(project, key)` — `bench:<project>:<key>`, so the ranking's id tie-break resolves a collision by the dataset's own key order — and one `corpusStamp` per seeding pass, so every row of a pass shares a `created_at` and the decay factor cannot order a tied pair of different categories by which side of a wall-clock second they landed on. Both were drawn per run before #708 and the `vec=0.50` sweep row could not reproduce its own interval; `corpusID` and `corpusStamp` are in `corpusstore.go` and are the reason the four seeders go through `CreateWithID` rather than `Create`. Two tests hold it: `TestSweepReproducesAcrossTwoSeedsInOneProcess` (two seeds, one process, the affected grid point) and `TestCLIBenchSweepReproducesAcrossProcesses` in e2e (two processes, the whole six-point table) — the split is the package's CI budget, not a difference in what is claimed: a second full sweep in this package is ~75s of `-race`, and the package went 336s → 487s of Go's 600s per-binary default in CI when the in-process test landed, so ~113s of headroom is left here. The next corpus-wide test has to be measured against THAT number, not against the 263s this bullet used to quote. Two things now keep that number down, and both are scheduling and not coverage: the headline run (seed the committed corpus, score fts / vector / hybrid) is computed ONCE per package by `runTestdata` behind a `sync.Once`, on the model of `auditRun`, because TestBenchRegressionFloors, TestBenchDatasetReport and TestSweep each used to pay it (~47s of `-race` CPU apiece) for a result they only read; and the heavy tests that share no process state are `t.Parallel`, because Go runs the non-parallel top-level tests first and one at a time, and that serial phase was ~144s on one core before any corpus test started. Measured with `-race -count=1 -p 1`: 283s → 161s on 8 cores, 335s → 198s and 552s → 408s of user CPU at `GOMAXPROCS=4`. The shared run is computed on its own store with a background context, never through `newBenchStoreWithDB(t)` or `t.Fatalf` inside the `Once` (the first caller's cleanup would own a store the others read, and a fatal would mark the `Once` done with no result), and its error is reported by every caller. A test that calls `t.Setenv` (the passive suite, through `passiveEnv`) cannot be parallel and stays serial. TestSweepReproducesAcrossTwoSeedsInOneProcess does NOT use the shared run: two independent seeds are its subject. The QUERIES' labels are guarded rather than trusted: `TestTwinOfATopGradedAnswerIsGraded` requires that when a query grades one half of a #965 near-duplicate pair (`grafana_port`/`grafana_ingress_port`, `arc_runners`/`arc_self_hosted`, `time_decay_categories`/`ghost_decay`) at the query's maximum gain, its twin carries that same gain — the rule #965 applied by hand and the one #1400 found broken on `q_decay_rules`, where a correct top-five row scored as a miss. It is scoped to the maximum gain (a member graded below it is a distractor for that query) and it fails if a named key stops existing, so a renamed fixture cannot satisfy it by finding neither half.
  `ghost bench` (plain) prints the three-conditions table PLUS the top-row shares and R@1 ceiling after the table, then the no-answer false-positive table, the fused-vs-legs intervals, and the abstention baseline. The table itself (the fixed-width lines other tools parse) is UNCHANGED; the shares are new lines after it.
  `ghost bench --context` (#582) is a third report and the only one that measures the BLOCK rather than the order: context precision, contamination, result rate, budget adherence, per-bucket diversity and token cost, each measured by running the real `Store.Candidates` → `assemble.Run` path at the TOOL's own budget (`bench.ContextItems`/`ContextBytes` mirror `ghost_memory_search`'s `limit` 5 and `searchResponseMaxBytes`, and a metric taken at any other budget is a metric about a surface nobody ships). It is REPORT-ONLY and gates nothing, and the reason is the corpus rather than caution: the graded corpus holds no resolved, out-of-window or cross-bucket row and stage 2 drops the rest before admission, so its contamination figure is 0.000 BY CONSTRUCTION and a gate on it gates a tautology — the contaminated measurement is carried by the 8-row `contextFixture` instead, which is why the report prints "a zero here is a reading of THIS corpus" under an all-zero arm table. It adds NO corpus-wide test and must not grow one: the fixture is 8 rows, the new tests read 0.17s, and the package measures 11.7–13.5s over six runs after this against 14.36s on `98ffe9c5` before it, so the ~113s of race headroom this bullet quotes is untouched. Contamination and the trims are read from `assemble.Result.Leaks()` and `assemble.Trace.TrimmedByBudget()` and NEVER re-derived here (see `internal/assemble`); where those are unreadable the bench REFUSES instead of scoring — a missing trace, or an admitted row with no recorded verdict, is an unmeasured row, and a clean zero for one is the single output that report must never produce. Every ratio in the report is a fraction of a NAMED population, and the trim ratios are where that costs something: the query ratio's denominator is EVERY answered query, not the trimmed ones (counting only those makes the ratio its own numerator over its own denominator — `1.000` on a corpus where the cap binds everywhere AND on one where the budget was deleted, indistinguishable), and the two trims get SEPARATE row populations because a row the fit pass drops has already passed the item cap, so the cap saw every row and the fit pass only the ones the cap left. A trim that fired on nothing therefore reads `0.000` over a real population, never `n/a` — it ran and cut none — and the graded denominator is the graded rows that REACHED the stage, which is neither the admitted count nor `reached - cut` and coincides with each only when the other trim dropped nothing. `bench.SeedAt` takes the clock and `Seed` is its wall-clock wrapper; `bench.ContextInstant()` pins the CONTEXT report so two runs of one binary print the same bytes, while the ablation tables deliberately keep their wall clock, because pinning those would age the corpus by however long ago a constant was written and move published NDCG for a reason that has nothing to do with retrieval (#708's rule, still load-bearing). Since #955 `ContextRequest` carries the shipped `config.DefaultNoAnswerCosine`, so `--context` is measured with the no-answer bar on and its result rate is below 1.000 by exactly the answerable queries the bar refuses; the cutoff sweep's request (`contextRequestWithCutoff`) holds the bar OFF so that sweep stays a measurement of the cutoff alone. `ghost bench --no-answer-sweep` (`internal/bench/noanswer_sweep.go`) measures three rule families per setting over the graded corpus plus the 24 no-answer queries at the shipped cutoff: only the cosine floor is the assembler's real rule (each row runs `assemble.Run` with the bar set); the fused-score floor and the cosine-plus-keyword rule are judged from the block afterwards and are labelled "not built" so no second implementation of a rule can drift from the first. Plain `ghost bench`'s no-answer section is the retriever path and does not move with the bar.
