  `ghost bench --passive` (#581) measures the PASSIVE surfaces — session start and `ghost context` (one render), `ghost_project_context` and the project resource — and every rule below is about keeping that measurement the product's rather than the bench's. (1) It calls the production entry points, never a copy: `mcpinit.SessionBlockAt`, `mcpserver.ProjectContextAt` and `mcpserver.ProjectResourceAt` are the code the hook, the tool and the resource already ran, behind a function that takes the clock and omits the side effects of a session starting (Obsidian mirror, session counter, query-key write); `loadSessionContext` and the tool handler call the same inner functions, so a change to a budget, a policy or a renderer reaches the bench with no edit here, and a bench-local re-implementation is the one thing that would let it measure its own copy. (2) Rows are recognised in the RENDERED text by their backticked id, never read off `assemble.Result`: the caller receives text, and a figure about a value it never saw is not a figure about the surface. (3) The corpus (`passive_corpus.go`) is deterministic and offline, on `bench.ContextInstant()`: ids come from `corpusID`, ages from `corpusStamp`, windows sit years from the clock on both sides, and every validity stamp is in `memory.StoredStampLayout` and asserted to parse — an unreadable stamp is kept as unset, so a fixture of them would read zero leakage for the wrong reason. Withheld rows are the highest-importance and newest in their project (`TestPassiveCorpusGradesAreConsistent`), so a filter that stopped working puts them at the top rather than behind a cap. (4) Grades are the FIXTURE's, per surface: a resolved, expired or not-yet-valid row is withheld everywhere, an out-of-scope row only on a read that carries a scope (`injection.session_scope` ships empty and the tool carries none, and an unscoped request matching every row is the documented rule), and superseded and near-duplicate rows are optional because the project bucket reorders them and leaves the drop to the cap — grading them withheld would assert a contract the code does not have, and the duplicate rate measures them instead. (5) The honesty check compares the session-start header with the corpus's own counts and is REPORTED, never asserted: the golden pins whatever state the tree is in (it was FAIL before #897 and is PASS after), and `TestPassiveHonestyCheckReadsBothHeaderShapes` holds the parser to both the legacy and the tally header, because a check that fails everything makes the golden say FAIL for ever. Its truth follows the product's attribution rules (out-of-scope rows never enter the window; `_global` may drop demoted losers as withheld) so it asserts nothing the product does not promise. The pinned row is `optional` on the union surfaces, because a pin is not a documented slot guarantee there, and it lives in a decaying category because in a never-decay one the pin is a no-op. (6) The one assertion is leakage: zero on every surface, with a non-zero denominator, plus `TestPassiveMeasurementCatchesADisabledFilter`, which re-seeds the store with the validity windows (then the withdrawals) removed and requires the figure to go non-zero naming only rows of that kind — the in-test form of "disable the filter and the bench must notice", beside a scorer test that plants violations in a hand-built block. It gates nothing in CI beyond those tests, and the config it reads is the compiled defaults with the passive budget's knobs stated in code, so a `GHOST_*` variable cannot move the golden.
  `ghost bench --audit` (#929) scores the retrieval audit against TWO labelled corpora and prints both (`internal/bench/audit*.go`, goldens `testdata/audit_report.golden` and `testdata/audit_report_real.golden`, pinned by `TestAuditBaseline` and `TestRealAuditBaseline`; the synthetic corpus's `fp` call holds hand-written cases for the false-positive classes measured on real work, in their own domain so the other scenarios keep their denominators): the corpora's labels are hand-written (ids cited, memories restated, denied or saved), `audit.Run` is the thing under test and is called unchanged on `audit.Signals` built over a FIXED bench key, and the bench never reads a key file, a clock figure or the data directory; it prints per-outcome precision and recall (`used` split by identifier and token arm), a 5x5 confusion table and the corpus's headline lines, and gates nothing in CI beyond its tests. `RunAuditTemp` and `RunRealAuditTemp` are both wrappers over ONE `runAuditCorpus(ctx, c, label)`, so a third corpus is a constructor and a call and not a second copy of the harness — and no report is reachable except through it, which is what keeps the two comparable. The corpus is a PARAMETER and never a branch inside the measurement: nothing in `runAuditCall` reads which corpus it was handed, so the two runs differ only in their fixture. THE SECOND CORPUS IS THE ONE THAT MAKES THE FIRST HONEST (#1003): it is hand-labelled from REAL sessions (each `Domain` names the session a note was saved in, each turn is a clipped excerpt of the agent's own text in the turn a recorded retrieval matched it, and the comment above a turn carries the study's `USED`/`NOT_USED`/`UNCLEAR` label, the pattern and what the judge said), it was scrubbed before it entered the repository (no host name, no owner's name, no addressable local path — what looks like a path in the fixture text is the placeholder the scrub wrote, and it is fixture text nobody resolves), and its content is byte-identical to what the study supplied, because a measured baseline is only worth anything if the next run measures the same bytes. Only a pair labelled `USED` carries a label slice, so the corpus's Go-visible positives are FOUR (one cite, three restatements; one restatement precedes the only call that kept its memory, so the scored positives are three) and the other ninety turns are scored as expected-ignored, which is seventy-eight `NOT_USED` and twelve `UNCLEAR`, so the false-`used` figure covers undecided pairs as well as settled negatives — `TestRealAuditCorpusLabelsAreConsistent` checks the ids are 32-hex and unique, that every labelled turn names a memory the corpus holds, and pins the counts (36 memories, 94 turns, 23 calls, 1 cite, 3 restatements), and it deliberately does NOT carry the synthetic test's restatement word-share bar, because a turn here is a CLIPPED excerpt and the share of a long memory it can hold is a property of the clip and not of the label — the bar it uses is the comparison's own absolute floor of three shared words. THE REAL SET HAS NO SCENARIO NAMED `start` (`startScenario` matches that name, not a call's position), and that matters in both directions: `Headlines` reads its catch lines over EVERY call when `startScenario` finds none, `FormatAudit` prints neither synthetic-only domain line for it (`same-domain`, `spread`) because a `0 of 0` over a population the corpus does not have is a figure about nothing, and `TestAuditHeadlinesArePrinted` holds BOTH report shapes — the one that prints them and the one that must not. ITS BASELINE IS WHAT IT IS AND IS NOT TUNED HERE: the judge files no `used` verdict at all on the real set, so cited ids caught 0 of 1, restatements 0 of 2, false `used` 0 of 33 and token-arm precision `n/a` — each miss is a different documented rule (the one cited id sits in a save's arguments, which `AddSaveArgs` reads as nothing by design; the two restatements clear the token arm's absolute floor of three and fail its half-the-memory fraction, 30 of 258 and 19 of 153 words), and the 0 of 33 is where #984's fixes show, against the study's 60 wrong verdicts in 62. So `TestRealAuditFloors` holds the POPULATIONS (1, 2, 33 — a corpus quietly trimmed of its labels must not be able to meet a floor by shrinking) and the false-`used` CEILING of 0, which is the one direction a regression can move today, and states in its own comment that a recall floor cannot be written until the arm fires. The real-set report is built ONCE and shared (`realAuditRun`, one `sync.Once` like `auditRun`) because its 23 calls open 23 scratch stores and seed 36 memories into each: the audit tests in this package read **10.8s under `-race` before this change and 43.3s after it** (`go test -race -run 'TestAudit|TestRealAudit' -count=1 ./internal/bench`), i.e. **+32.4s** of a package that was at 487s of CI's 600s per-binary ceiling — so ~81s of headroom is left, and that measurement is why this file carries no stability test of its own (a second real-corpus run is another ~29s of race for a guarantee `TestAuditReportIsStableAcrossRuns` already holds) and why the baseline, the floors and the headline-shape test all share the ONE run. The next test added here is measured against THAT number, not against the synthetic set's.
- `internal/obsidian/` — One-way Markdown vault mirror (`ghost obsidian export|sync`). The store can hold ids, project names, tags and bodies Ghost never generated (`ghost import` writes an artifact's ids verbatim), so every name it builds is a name somebody else chose: `idToken` keeps the first 8 bytes of an id only when they name a single path component a person could type and hashes the id otherwise (a separator put the note outside the `Memories/` subtree prune manages and had it deleted; a tab or line break is a legal name nobody can open; a NUL failed the whole export with `EINVAL` on every run), `folderNames` replaces a folder over `maxFolderName` the same way it replaces one that would escape because `ENAMETOOLONG` failed the export just as permanently, and `hasGhostID` unquotes exactly what `yamlScalar` emits. What prune keys its RETENTION on is the note's vault-relative PATH (`keepSet`, keyed by `keepKey`), not the id read back out of the note and not the bare basename — the front matter still decides whether a file is Ghost's to touch at all, so the block is prune's ownership test, but the id inside it is not its key: `yamlScalar` flattens tab/CR/LF to a space so every key stays on one line, and that is lossy — three records can render one `ghost_id` line, and keyed on that line they would collide on it and two of the three would be deleted. A basename is not safe either, and for a different reason: the same slug occurs in two project folders, and a memory and a task with the same title produce the same name and can share an id fragment, so one live note anywhere in the vault would keep a stale copy alive everywhere. A key that decides what to keep should be one the system holds, not one parsed back out of a file written for a human. Every front-matter value still has to occupy exactly one line — that is what makes the note valid YAML and the ownership test's answer well-defined (it reads to the first `---` and takes the one `ghost_id:` line there) — but no value read out of front matter, forged or merely flattened, can now make prune drop a note it wrote. A NOTE NAME IS ONE RECORD'S, and an existing one never moves: a built name (`fileName`/`fileNameFor`, slug + `idToken`) is shared by two records with one title whose ids agree on their first 8 bytes (imported ids reach that), and Export used to write both to it and report success with one record missing from the vault. `noteNames` assigns the names for one directory (a project's Memories/, Decisions/ or Tasks/) together, compared case-folded: a built name only one record has is kept byte for byte, so no note in a collision-free vault is renamed; in a colliding group the smallest id keeps the built name and each other member is `<stem>-<8 hex of sha256(id)>.md`, so the winner depends on the set of ids and never on listing order, and a second pass rewrites nothing. A suffixed name that another record's built name already holds falls back to the full 64-hex hash, which no built name is long enough to equal. The keep-set and the memory wikilinks take the assigned names, so retention is still keyed on the path: when the record holding the built name goes, the survivor inherits it and its suffixed note is pruned as stale. A `--project` filter selects EXACTLY ONE project under `ghost export`'s rule (`selectProjects`): an exact id wins first, a name more than one project carries is refused naming every matching id through `memory.RefusalArg`, and the filter is resolved before `ensureVault`, so a refused filter leaves the vault untouched (#1164). **A STAGING FILE IS RECLAIMED BY THE NAME THE WRITER ACTUALLY MAKES (#1640).** `writeIfChanged` stages a note as `<note>.md.ghost-tmp-<random>` — the `os.CreateTemp` pattern `stagingPattern` builds from `tmpMarker` — and prune reclaims exactly that shape through `isStagingName`, which requires a note name ending in `.md` before the marker, takes the marker from the RIGHT so a name that merely mentions it is left alone, and also accepts the marker with no suffix (the fixed name a build before the `CreateTemp` form used). It reclaims one only once its modification time is older than `stagingGrace` (about a minute): a manual export and the auto-sync worker can run at once, so reclaiming a live staging file would delete the other pass's file between its `os.CreateTemp` and its `Rename` and fail that write with `ENOENT`. The writer and the reclaim read the one `tmpMarker` constant, so they cannot drift: the reclaim used to match a fixed `.ghost-tmp` suffix the `CreateTemp` writer never produced, and a staging file a killed export left behind held its note text in the vault forever. A non-regular entry (FIFO, device, socket or directory) carrying the Markdown suffix is never opened for reading in `prune`, `hasGhostID` or `hasGhostContent` -- `prune` skips it the way `pruneOrphanFolder` already skips anything that is not a regular file. `hasGhostID` and `hasGhostContent` open with `O_NONBLOCK` and follow symlinks (a symlinked note is still pruned), refusing on `fstat` when the target is not a regular file. `writeIfChanged` refuses only an entry that is neither a regular file nor a symlink, without opening it. It compares an existing note through a non-blocking open (following a symlink), leaves a note whose content is already identical alone, and otherwise replaces the path with a regular file, so a symlinked note with differing content, a dangling link or an unreadable note is replaced as before. Reads judge the open descriptor, not a separate stat, so a path swapped to a FIFO between the two does not block.
- `internal/portable/` — The portable JSONL artifact (`ghost export` / `ghost import`): one typed record per line behind a schema-version header, written projects-then-memories-then-links-then-tasks-then-decisions in id order so two exports of an unchanged store are byte-identical. A `--project` filter must match EXACTLY ONE project: matching none is an error, and matching MORE THAN ONE is refused naming every id it matched (#1093), because a project's name is not unique — `projects.path` and `projects.repo_remote` carry the only unique indexes — so widening to every project that answers to a name would capture a project the caller never named into an artifact handed to someone else. An exact id is matched FIRST, before any name, and always selects exactly one project (the precedence `ResolveProject` gives an id), because ids and names share the filter's one namespace and a store can hold a project whose id is another project's name. It is a wire format, not a backup — `memory.Store.Backup` is the restorable copy. Import never overwrites an id that already exists, so re-running is always safe; a record it cannot import is rejected individually and the run continues, so one hand-edited line does not abandon the file. **A LINE WHOSE PAYLOADS DO NOT MATCH ITS TYPE REFUSES THE FILE, IN BOTH MODES (#1511).** `requirePayload` refuses a record carrying no payload of its type and, through `otherPayloads`, one that also carries another kind's payload, and `readRecords` refuses a header carrying any payload; importing the payload the type names would drop the other and report a clean import of a file whose data it left behind, and which of the two the line meant is a guess, so it is refused like an unknown record type rather than rejected by line like an unparseable one. `TestALineWhosePayloadsDoNotMatchItsTypeIsRefused` pins the refusal, the line number and the kinds named, and an empty store, for the dry run and the apply. **A LINE THAT REPEATS A TOP-LEVEL KEY REFUSES THE FILE, IN BOTH MODES (#1513).** `readRecords` refuses a line whose top-level object repeats a key (`repeatedKey`): encoding/json keeps the last copy, so a line with two `memory` objects decodes to one record and the payload check cannot see the loss. Every top-level key is checked, not only the payload keys, and keys are folded the way the decoder folds them (`foldRune`), so `memory`, `Memory` and a Unicode case-fold spelling are one key; a nested object's keys are not checked. The error names the line and the key, never the line's text, and the pass costs one more scan of each line (`TestALineThatRepeatsAKeyIsRefused`, `TestAKeyRepeatedInsideAPayloadIsNotAFileRefusal`). **A `blocked_by` / `superseded_by` POINTER TO A RECORD THE RUN REJECTED IS DROPPED, NOT FOLLOWED (#1147).** `orderTasks` / `orderDecisions` drop a pointer to a record the artifact does not contain, but a target the artifact contains and then rejects is only known as the steps run, and the store's own pointer check sits after the dry-run return (a dry run writes nothing, so the target is never there to find) — so the dry run created the dependent record and the apply rejected it. `planRecords` keeps a per-kind map of the records that have LANDED (created or already present), with the project each landed in, and drops a pointer whose target is not in it, in both modes, reporting it on `RecordResult.DroppedRef`; the set is positive rather than a list of rejections because a record refused by the plan's own project check never reaches its store method. `TestATaskWhoseBlockerIsRejectedStillImports` and `TestADecisionWhoseSupersederIsRejectedStillImports` pin identical dry-run and apply outcomes, the drop, and a valid pointer kept. **EVERY DROPPED POINTER IS REPORTED, AND ONE THE STORE CAN RESOLVE IN THE RECORD'S OWN PROJECT IS NOT DROPPED (#1153).** The two drops ordering makes — a target in neither the artifact nor the store, and a pointer that is part of a cycle — used to be silent. Ordering now records what it cleared and why on the record (`droppedRef`, `dropReason`), the step reports it as `RecordResult.DroppedRef` with `RecordResult.DropReason` (`DropRejected` is the zero value, so #1147's lines are unchanged), and the CLI names the reason. Before any drop stands, the step asks the store (`Store.HasTask` / `HasDecision`, a plain read that answers alike on the dry run's read-only handle) and keeps a pointer whose target the store already held IN THE PROJECT THE RECORD IS IMPORTED UNDER, after project mapping. Both take that project id and filter on it: a target held in another project is dropped and reported as `DropAbsent`, exactly like one that exists nowhere, because the store's own readers follow these pointers only within one project (`liveDecisionSuccessor` joins on `s.project_id = d.project_id`) and a kept cross-project pointer was rewritten through `ON DELETE SET NULL` by deleting the other project (`TestAPointerIntoAnotherProjectIsDropped`; `TestAPointerIntoTheMappedProjectIsKept` holds the filter to the mapped id, not the artifact's). The same rule holds for a target IN THE ARTIFACT: its pointer is kept only when the target landed in the dependent's project, after mapping, and is otherwise dropped as `DropAbsent`. A created target landed in the project it is imported under; a skipped one is the stored row, so its project is read back with `HasTask` / `HasDecision` and a row held in any other project counts as landed elsewhere (`landedElsewhere`) — which the store-held restore then puts back when that row is in the dependent's own project. Both shapes are pinned in both modes with the other project deleted afterwards, an artifact carrying both projects (`TestAnInArtifactPointerIntoAnotherProjectIsDropped`) and a target id the store already holds under another project (`TestAnInArtifactPointerToARecordStoredInAnotherProjectIsDropped`), `TestAnInArtifactPointerIntoTheMappedProjectIsKept` holds a mapped same-project pointer, created or skipped, kept, and `TestAnInArtifactPointerToARecordStoredInItsOwnProjectIsKept` holds one kept when the artifact names the target under another project but the store holds it in the dependent's. The pointer checks inside `ImportTask` / `ImportDecision` stay unscoped: the plan only ever hands them a target in the record's own project (one landed there, or one the store held there), so they are a plain existence check behind it, and it is the plan's own checks, made alike in both modes, that keep the dry run and the apply agreeing. And only a target held BEFORE the run counts: one this run created is there in an apply and absent from its dry run, and counting it made the two disagree about a cycle. `topoOrder` clears only the pointers of a cycle's own members, decided for every record before any is cleared, so a record that merely points into a cycle keeps its pointer and the report's "part of a cycle" is true of each record it names. Membership is one linear pass (`cycleMembers` walks each record once), and the passes after it work on numbered ids rather than copied records or slice scans, because a long chain hanging off a cycle takes one pass per link: a per-record walk made a dry run of 4000 such tasks take minutes (`TestCycleMembersIsLinear` and `TestTopoOrderOfAChainIntoACycleIsBounded` count accessor calls rather than time, and `TestCycleMembersMarksOnlyCycles` pins exact membership). `TestAPointerToARecordNowhereIsReported`, `TestAPointerInACycleIsReported` and `TestAPointerToARecordOnlyInTheStoreIsKept` pin all three in both modes. **A REPEATED ID IN ONE ARTIFACT IS A SKIP IN THE DRY RUN EXACTLY AS ON APPLY (#1096).** The store's create-or-skip answer is a presence check against the database, which an apply run has written to and a dry run has not, so the second record of a repeated id would otherwise read as a create in the preview and a skip in the run it previews. `Import` therefore tracks the ids the run has created, per kind (`createdIDs`, read and written by `step.run`), and a later record holding one is answered as a skip without asking the store. The check sits AFTER the importer's own project check and BEFORE the store call, which is the order the apply run answers in: the project check runs ahead of the store in both modes, so a repeated id naming a missing project is refused in both, and the store's presence check runs ahead of its record checks, so a repeated id whose second record is invalid is a skip on apply and must be one in the preview. The apply run's verdicts are unchanged by this; only the preview moves. The set records what the run actually CREATED rather than what the artifact repeats: a repeated id whose first record was refused wrote nothing under it, so the second is judged on its own record. `TestADryRunAndItsApplyAgreeWhenAnArtifactRepeatsAnID` drives all four kinds plus those cases and asserts the preview's report lines are the apply run's, record for record. **A RECORD'S LINE HAS NO CEILING OF ITS OWN, WHICH IS THE ONE BOUND THE FORMAT SUPPORTS (#1084).** A memory's `content` is capped at `MaxContentLen`, but its `evidence` list is not — one row is appended every time the same fact is saved again (`Upsert`'s fold branch) — so an exporter's own output can pass any ceiling derived from the content cap. The cap this package used was exactly that one, and it refused the exporter's own artifact as a WHOLE file, before any record was read, so an artifact that looked complete stopped restoring at all and the one bloated record made every other record in the backup unimportable. The line bound is therefore the format's own, which is unbounded: `readRecords` reads through a `bufio.Reader` that grows to fit the line, and the seam fails in the direction that loses nothing — the claim is not "any line is a valid record" but "a line too large to read is one the exporter did not write, which is this build's bug rather than the file's". `TestImportReadsARecordTheExporterCanWrite` is the pin: it seeds a memory whose evidence list is over a megabyte, exports it, and imports it into a fresh store, and it asserts the fixture is in fact over the ceiling rather than passing because it never got there. A dry run opens the store read-only, so the preview cannot migrate or seed what it previews — which means openReadOnlyTransferStore must also refuse a store whose PRAGMA user_version is behind or ahead (memory.DBUserVersion / memory.SchemaVersion), or the first query fails with SQLite's "no such column" instead of a version mismatch. The artifact deliberately omits Ghost's own _global builtin seeds: they are written per-install under hex(randomblob(16)), so every importer's id-only dedup would duplicate them permanently, and the destination already re-creates them by content. Imported memories are stamped `source = "onboarding"` and unpinned unless `ImportOptions.TrustProvenance` is set: an artifact is a file that arrived from somewhere, and on its own authority it must not be able to plant rows the store exempts from consolidation by name (`manual`, `builtin`) or by pin. Embeddings and the resolve KEEP cache are not in the artifact: both are derived and rebuilt by their workers, and so are the DERIVED edges of `memory_links` — the `related` ones the linking worker writes and the `duplicate` ones a near-duplicate fold writes. The `supersedes` and `causes` edges are NOT derived and DO travel, for the reason the rule below gives. Two further omissions are NOT derived, so nothing rebuilds them and a restore loses them: `memory_history`, whose change log starts fresh at the single `import` row `ImportMemory` writes in the same transaction as the insert, so `ghost history` on a restored memory shows just that row; and `memory_flags`, one agent's append-only `wrong`/`stale` claim about a memory, which a restored memory simply does not carry. `ghost backup` is the full-fidelity copy that keeps both, and carrying either in the artifact would be a format change of its own. Import report lines render the id through `assemble.Token` in both `labelOrID` and the CLI's `printRecordLine`, and the detail is quoted on BOTH switch arms rather than only when an id is present, so a store written before the shape check cannot forge a report. `TestTheImportReportNeverPrintsAForgedLine` and `TestTheImportRecordLineNeverForgesALine` pin that, and pin the ordinary report line byte-identical to what an operator has been reading. EXPORT CALLS THE IMPORTER'S OWN REFUSAL PREDICATES, ONE PER RECORD TYPE, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE ROUND-TRIP GUARANTEE: `internal/memory.CheckImportedProject` / `CheckImportedMemory` / `CheckImportedTask` / `CheckImportedDecision` (internal/memory/import_check.go) are the ONLY place a portable record is judged, and `internal/portable.Export` calls the same four functions `ImportProject` / `ImportMemory` / `ImportTask` / `ImportDecision` do, so an export cannot exit 0 with a record its own importer will refuse (#813). #796 got the id-SHAPE checks shared and documented the gap it left — an empty project name or path, credential-shaped content, tags, `source_ref`, `agent` or evidence, and every value-set check still exported at 0 and failed on a restore — which is why the rule is now a function with two callers rather than two functions kept in step by hand. A predicate judges a RECORD and never a STORE, which is what lets the exporter call it: the three refusals that ARE about the destination (a project this store already records the same checkout for, a record whose project is absent, a task's unresolved blocker) and the recorded-history check stay in the importers, and each one's reason for having no export-side counterpart is a ROW in `TestEveryImportRefusalPathHasAnExportCounterpart` — the recorded-history one in particular could not be screened at export even in principle, since every write appends a history row and so every memory a store exports has one. A decision's `tags` is the twelfth refusal path the one shared predicate carries for it (#835), and it is the only one whose field is a TAG LIST rather than a column of its own: a credential-shaped tag makes the DECISION unbacked-up, which the credential rule accepts for content, `source_ref`, `agent`, `session_id` and evidence on a memory and on the four other decision fields, because the value is the leak and the `!` line naming the field is a repair rather than a silent loss. A record left out is NAMED with the IMPORTER'S OWN MESSAGE (never a second phrasing, which is the thing that drifted), rendered through `assemble.Token`, and `SkippedRecord.Secret` marks the credential refusals so the report can name the EDIT rather than only the delete (and `SkippedRecord.Orphan` marks a child left out only because its project was, which the report advises as a consequence of that project — fix it and re-export — and never re-keys or deletes, since the child's id is fine and it is the only copy) — and THE FIELD LISTS IN THAT ADVICE ARE DERIVED, from `mcpserver.EditableFieldList`, which reflects over the same argument structs the three field-editing tools are registered with. A transcribed list is the same mistake as a second refusal predicate and it drifted the same way: the first version promised `ghost_memory_update` could edit a memory's `agent` and `session_id` and that `ghost_task_update` could edit a task's `title` and `notes`, none of which any of them takes, so four of the credential-guarded fields were sent to a call that rejects them. THE UNEDITABLE HALF IS ONE SENTENCE PER GROUP, NOT ONE LIST, because the groups do not share a reason or a route: a memory's `agent`/`session_id` are written by the update from the EDITING SESSION's provenance and a caller must not be able to name their own author; an EVIDENCE row (`evidence[N].agent`/`session_id`/`source_ref`, which `CheckImportedMemory` guards and `ghost export` refuses just as it refuses `content`) is written by nothing at all, and the only surface reaching one is `ghost history purge`; `CheckImportedMemory` also judges each evidence `id` (the `memory_provenance` primary key): a repeat among non-empty ids of one memory (an empty id is minted, as before), a whitespace-only id, a control or line-break character (U+0085, U+2028 and U+2029 included), a backtick or a « » guillemet (the record-id character rule), or a length past `MaxImportedIDLen` is refused naming `evidence[N].id` and never the id, so the dry run and the apply agree (#1409); export applies the same predicate, so a stored memory with such an evidence id is skipped and named, not exported; an evidence id that is well-formed but already stored on ANOTHER memory (in the store, or on an earlier memory of the same artifact) is MINTED afresh by `importEvidenceTx` rather than refused, so the dry run and the apply both create the memory and a re-import of the same artifact, whose memories are skips, writes no second copy of the evidence (`TestImportedEvidenceIDHeldByAnotherMemoryIsMinted`); a task's `title` is written at insert; a decision has no update tool; a project's `path` is written by `ghost project bind` — and for THAT one the obvious-looking alternative, `ghost project delete`, CASCADES every memory in the project away, so advice that loses data is worse than the wrong field list the derivation removed — while a project's `name` is never rewritten at all. `cmd/ghost.secretUnfixableRoutes` states each with its own reason and route, because silence would leave the operator holding a row the report refused with advice covering every field they can see and not theirs. A CREDENTIAL refusal names the FIELD and never the value, on both the export and the import side: the value reaches a terminal, a CI log and a paste, so the report and the struct between them hold no copy of it — the FIELD name is what makes the line actionable without the value. A PROJECT'S RECORDS GO WITH IT when it is left out, because the importer resolves each record's project against the artifact. The file is KEPT and the command EXITS NON-ZERO, so a partial export cannot read as a complete one. **The artifact CARRIES `memories.retention` and the import decides what it becomes (#1081).** `PortableMemory.Retention` is the column itself — selected by `PortableMemories`, scanned by `scanPortableMemory`, and written on every record, because a field the artifact omits is a field a restore loses: a `persistent` row is the one thing a user declared keep-forever, and a round trip that dropped it turned that row into an ordinary one at the moment the artifact was needed. It is a plain string with no `omitempty` and no pointer, because the two states that would need telling apart fold safely: an absent key and an empty string both mean "the record states nothing", and `NormalizeRetention` resolves both to the column's own `project` default — so a v1 artifact still imports unchanged, while every record this build writes names the tier it holds. The VALUE SET is checked with the save tool's own `InvalidRetentionError` and not a second wording, so a hand-edited fourth tier is refused by name rather than failing the INSERT with SQLite's CHECK; the empty string is NOT one of those refusals, because it is an absent claim rather than a bad one. **A MEMORY'S VALIDITY TRIPLE AND CONFIDENCE ARE JUDGED BY THE SAVE TOOL'S RULES, AND EXPORT NAMES A ROW THAT BREAKS THEM.** `CheckImportedMemory` reads the triple through `memory.ImportedValidity` and the rating through `memory.CheckConfidence`, so a hand-typed RFC 3339 stamp is normalized rather than stored as text every reader treats as no claim, a bare `valid_until` means the end of its day, and an unreadable stamp, an inverted window or a confidence outside 0 to 1 rejects the record by name in the dry run and the apply alike. Because `Export` asks the same predicate, a stored row holding one (an older import, a restore, a hand edit) is left out and named rather than carried into every later round trip. A stored-layout stamp is kept as its raw text, so an export of a store the writer tools wrote re-imports byte-stable. A bare date is not: export writes it verbatim, and a bare-date `valid_until` an older import stored comes back as `23:59:59` of that day, so a row today's readers expire at that day's midnight reads as live through the end of the day after a round trip. That direction is intended, because it is the save tool's reading of the same text. `SkippedRecord.Validity` marks such a refusal, and the report gives `validityAdvice` (replace the value with `ghost_memory_update`; delete and re-save only to clear a stamp, which the tool cannot remove) instead of the re-key advice, whose delete would lose the row's links and history (`TestAnExportThatLeftAValidityRowOutAdvisesAnUpdateNotARekey`, `TestAValidityRowBesideAnIDRowGetsBothRepairs`, `TestTheValidityAdviceNamesFieldsTheToolTakes`). `TestImportRejectsAStampTheSaveToolRefusesByName`, `TestExportImportKeepsValidityStampsByteIdentical` and the three stamp grounds of `TestEveryStoreReachableRefusalIsLeftOutAndNamed` pin it. **AN EVIDENCE RECORD'S OWN CLAIMS ARE JUDGED BY THE SAVE TOOL'S RULES, AND EXPORT NAMES A ROW THAT BREAKS THEM.** `CheckImportedMemory` reads each evidence record's stamps through `memory.ImportedEvidence` and its rating through `memory.CheckConfidence` — the same rules the memory's own triple and confidence are read by — so an artifact is not the way around them: a stamp is a moment exactly as a memory's own is, and a rating outside 0 to 1 is a broken scale. Because `Export` asks the same predicate, a stored row holding one (an older import, a restore, a hand edit) is left out and named rather than carried into every later round trip, and `SkippedRecord.Evidence` marks the refusal so the report gives the delete-and-re-save its own advice names — no tool edits an evidence row, so the in-place repair `validityAdvice` names for a memory's own claim would be refused. `TestImportRejectsAnEvidenceRecordWhoseStampsAndRatingAreUnreadable`, `TestExportImportKeepsEvidenceStampsAndRatings`, `TestAnExportThatLeftAnEvidenceRowOutAdvisesTheDeleteNotARekey` and the two evidence grounds of `TestEveryStoreReachableRefusalIsLeftOutAndNamed` pin it. An import downgrades nothing it was asked to keep for free: `ImportMemory` normalizes the stated tier and then, unless `ImportOptions.TrustProvenance` is set, overwrites it with `RetentionProject` in the same breath as the source and pin rewrite — because `persistent` is the tier every lifecycle pass spares and `session` is a row scheduled for deletion by its own derived expiry, and a file that arrived from somewhere must not be able to plant either. A `session` row restored under the flag takes its expiry from `sessionExpiry`, the one derivation every save uses, because the artifact carries no `expires_at` and a session row with none is one no prune can ever reach (`TestARestoredSessionRowStaysPrunable` runs the prune and catches the row). The tier is a REWRITE the report states rather than a refusal, so it rides the `downgraded` flag and the CLI's per-record and summary lines name it beside the source and the pin. `RetentionExempt` is the only exemption the column buys and it is asked at every write, so an imported `persistent` row is exempt exactly as a saved one is — which is the whole point of restoring it. Every import refusal that names a column value renders it through `memory.RefusalArg` (token-guarded, bounded at 256 bytes) and never `%q`: an evidence `kind`, a task or decision `status`, the declared record `type`, a record's `project_id`, `blocked_by`, `superseded_by`, and the transfer commands' own `unknown argument` / `unknown flag` errors. The refusal runs BEFORE the secret guard and the report prints it, so a hand-edited artifact could otherwise carry a credential to the terminal; the sentence names the field and the allowed values instead (`TestImportRefusalsNeverEchoHostileColumnValues`, `TestImportDanglingReferenceRefusalsNeverEchoTheValue`, `TestTransferArgRefusalsNeverEchoHostileArgv`). **THE ARTIFACT HEADER DECLARES THE MUST-UNDERSTAND FEATURES AN IMPORTER NEEDS (#1535).** `record.Features` is the only key a header may carry for this; an importer refuses the whole file when it does not understand a listed feature, so an added field can be marked must-understand without bumping `SchemaVersion`. A field that is not listed here is one an older build may ignore, which is why a must-understand one is listed. `headerLine` derives its list from `knownFeatures`, the single source of truth, so the written and accepted sets cannot drift. **A FILE EXPORT IS PUBLISHED ATOMICALLY (#1148).** The artifact has no trailer, so a file cut on a record boundary is indistinguishable from a whole one and imports with exit 0; `runExportCore` therefore writes a 0600 temporary file in the destination directory, syncs it, and gives it the requested name through `exportPublish` (a hard link, so an existing file is still refused and never replaced, with an O_EXCL copy fallback on link-less filesystems, which never replaces a file either) only on success. Every failure removes the temporary file and leaves nothing at the destination name; `--out -` streams as before. A trailer record is a possible follow-up and is not part of this. **A PROJECT THE ARTIFACT ITSELF CREATES CLAIMS ITS CHECKOUT.** `resolveProjectMapping` maps an artifact project onto a store project with the same path or normalized remote, and onto an EARLIER project of the same artifact the same way: the apply inserts the first and its in-transaction collision check would refuse the second, while the dry run reads an unchanged store and would promise both, so a store-only check made the preview and the apply disagree and took the second project's children with it as "not found". Only a project the store's dry-run verdict (`ImportProject` with `apply=false`) would create claims its path and remote; a refused one writes nothing and claims nothing, as `createdIDs` treats a refused repeated id, and a repeated project id is mapped by its first record the store would accept, since a refused one settles nothing (`TestProjectsThatCollideWithinOneArtifactMapOntoTheFirst`, `TestARefusedProjectDoesNotClaimItsCheckout`, `TestARepeatedIDIsDecidedByItsFirstAcceptedRecord`, `TestDistinctProjectsInOneArtifactBothCreate`). **AN EDGE TRAVELS WHEN NOTHING RECOMPUTES IT, AND IT TRAVELS ONLY BETWEEN TWO MEMORIES THE ARTIFACT CARRIES (#1707).** `memory.CarriedLinkRelations` is the one list of the relations the artifact carries — `supersedes` and `causes` — and it is a list rather than a rule about the table because the rule is about the WORKERS: the linking worker writes the `related` edges from the cosine similarity of two vectors and the near-duplicate fold writes the `duplicate` ones, so a worker rebuilds those after an import, while the `supersedes` and `causes` edges are the supersede pass's CLASSIFIER JUDGEMENT about a pair of memories and NOTHING recomputes them. An artifact that dropped them restored a store in which a memory a `supersedes` edge had demoted was no longer demoted — `SupersedePenalties` reads those edges, so the ranked reads stopped applying the penalty and `ghost supersede --withdraw` and `--reassess` had no edge to act on — and neither report mentioned the loss. A link is a RECORD (`type: "link"`), written after the memories and imported after them, so both its endpoints exist before it does: an edge travels only when BOTH its endpoints are memories the artifact carries, and one whose endpoint is missing is left out and COUNTED (`Stats.LinksLeftOut`) rather than dropped in silence, because the demotion it caused is lost with it. The import refuses such an edge BY NAME (`memory.CheckImportedLink`, the importer's own predicate, which `ghost export` calls as it calls the other four), and `ImportLink` never overwrites an edge the store already holds and never REVIVES one it holds withdrawn — a withdrawal is an explicit act and an import is not a claim about it. A `supersedes` edge files the same history row `CreateLink` files, in the same transaction, so the change log says the claim landed. **THREE SHORT CLAUSES RIDE ON THAT: AN IMPORT NEVER OVERWRITES OR REVIVES AN EDGE** (the edge the store holds is the live copy, and a withdrawal somebody took back stays withdrawn), **AN EDGE TRAVELS ONLY WITH BOTH ITS ENDPOINTS**, and **THE HEADER DECLARES THE `links` FEATURE** — so a build that cannot interpret these records refuses the file by name rather than reading it as a store whose demotions are missing. The format's `schema_version` is 3: it was bumped once because the feature list arrived after the `links` and `retention` fields it protects, and the feature list is the mechanism for any later must-understand addition. `TestASupersedesEdgeTravelsWithTheArtifact`, `TestACausesEdgeTravelsWithTheArtifact`, `TestARelatedEdgeIsNotCarried`, `TestALinkNamingAMemoryTheArtifactDoesNotCarryIsRejected`, `TestADryRunAndItsApplyAgreeAboutTheEdges`, `TestAnEdgeWhoseEndpointIsOutsideTheExportIsLeftOutAndCounted`, `TestASecondImportOfTheSameArtifactCreatesNoLinks`, `TestTheImportRefusesAnEdgeItWouldNotStore` and `TestAnExportLeavesOutAnEdgeItsImporterWouldRefuse` pin the halves, and `TestCheckImportedLinkRefusesWhatTheStoreWillNotHold` and `TestImportLinkIsIdempotent` pin the store's own side of them.
- `internal/reflection/` — Memory consolidation: LlmConsolidator + SQLiteConsolidator. The LLM tier speaks per-id operations, not free text (see "Reflection operation contract"). `persistent`-tier rows are never its input or its output: `ReplaceNonManual` excludes them from the snapshot and the replaceable set, which is the only way "a consolidation never saw this text" is true (see "Retention tiers"). `duplicateClusters` is the ONE near-duplicate rule: the SQLite tier and `PlanGlobalFold` (`ghost maintenance consolidate-global`) both call it, so a project and `_global` cannot disagree about what a duplicate is. The `_global` fold adds only pairwise guards (`samePolarity`: negation, opposed stances, differing `prefer` objects, and `swapped`, the generic catch for a word swapped rather than added; the stance list is conservative and a miss is a false cluster, not a leftover duplicate) and applies through `Store.FoldRows`, NOT `ReplaceNonManual`: the whole-set replace matches emissions to rows by text alone, so a byte-identical agent row traded columns with a survivor, and a row edited or deleted between plan and apply used to be reverted or resurrected — the corpus the whole-set replace now carries (id, content, updated_at, category, importance, tags) is what keeps such a row intact rather than overwriting its copy of it (#1051). `ghost reflect _global --apply` still reaches `ReplaceNonManual` over `_global` by resolving it as an ordinary project operand; the fold is the only caller that targets `_global` on purpose. It plans only `source = 'reflection'` rows and a dry run opens the store read-only. `inferGlobalScope` keeps a fact that names one host, node or cluster by a concrete identifier (`relay-3`, an IPv4, `*.internal`) in its project; a bare noun is not a marker. One consolidation's input is bounded for every tier by `maxConsolidationInput` (2000, `cmd/ghost/lifecycle.go`): a harness tier is sent the whole set as text and the SQLite floor compares every pair. Above it nothing is written; a manual `ghost reflect` exits 1 and says what to do, and the lifecycle's `--skip-unchanged --apply` form reports a skip and exits 0, so the bound never leaves the lifecycle failure marker (`TestConsolidationBoundIsTwoThousand`, `TestLifecycleReflectAboveTheInputBoundIsASkip`). The harness's `learned_context` is held to the memory guards (`guardLearnedContext`, run last in `readOpResponse`): an unknown commit SHA (`unknownSHAs` over `knownSHAs`), a project name absent from the input (`foreignNamesIn` over `forbiddenProjectNames`) or an identifier none of the input memories, commits, project name or language carry (`unknownIdentifiers`) drops the WHOLE paragraph and keeps `CurrentContext`, the mechanical tier's own fallback; it is never repaired and never fails the response. The stored `learned_context` grounds NOTHING: `inputCorpus`, `knownSHAs` and the paragraph's identifier sources all leave it out, because it is the harness's own earlier output and counting it let one answer license a foreign name in every later round, as a memory and as the next paragraph. The refusal is a WARN naming the check and its tokens plus the paragraph's byte count, never its text; the reflection input quotes the stored paragraph with `quoteData` like each memory, and the stored-data notice names its section. Commit subjects in the reflection input are data, not structure: `BuildReflectionPrompt` renders each `LastCommits` entry through `commitLine` (`gitinfo.go`), which folds every line-ending rune (LF, VT, FF, CR, FS/GS/RS, NEL, LS, PS, CRLF as one) to `⏎` and every other C0/C1 control to a space and then wraps the line in `quoteData`, so a subject written by anyone who can push to a branch the user pulls cannot start a line with a forged `## ` heading or a `- id:` memory line. The fold is applied in the builder, not in `CollectGitContext`, because `LastCommits` is also the SHA source for the fabrication and contamination guards and callers set it directly; and it is the commit line's alone, since memory content keeps its real line breaks for the rewrite (`TestTheReflectionPromptCarriesAStoredNewlineUnchanged`). The preamble names exactly the sections it calls stored data and only headings the builder writes — `Recent Git Activity` and `Existing Memories`; the retired `Recent Exchanges` section is no longer named (`TestThePreambleNamesOnlySectionsTheBuilderWrites`, `TestAHostileCommitSubjectCannotForgeTheReflectionInputsStructure`, `TestTheBuilderFramesACommitLineItWasHandedRaw`)
- `Store.ReplaceNonManual`'s corpus guard (#1051): the whole-set replace takes the corpus the pass READ — `fed []CorpusRow`, the id, content, updated_at and the metadata the reuse branch writes (category, importance, tags) of every replaceable row it saw — and inside the write transaction treats a candidate that is not in it, or whose content, write clock or metadata has moved since, as CONCURRENT: excluded from the reuse map and from the delete set, kept exactly as it is, and reported in `preserved` so no skip fingerprint absorbs it. An emission is DROPPED, not written, when a row it claims changed or is gone: a keep claims its source byte-for-byte by text, and a merge or a rewrite claims the ids it names in `ReplacesIDs`, and a dropped merge keeps ALL of its sources, so the round loses nothing it was not allowed to claim. The guard is the same shape as `FoldRow.UpdatedAt` but is a WHOLESALE corpus check, not the `consolidatedSince` one, which is chronological and only saves rows CREATED during the round trip: a row EDITED (age intact) or DELETED by a concurrent writer is invisible to it — the gap #1051 closes. THE METADATA COLUMNS ARE THERE BECAUSE updated_at IS NOT THE WHOLE SIGNAL: `UpsertWithOptions`' fold (`strengthen memory`, issue #560) raises importance WITHOUT moving updated_at, so a row can sit at the write clock the pass read with its importance already drifted, and the reuse branch would then write the stale value back over the fold. Comparing category, importance and tags — exactly what the reuse branch writes — closes that gap. Scope is deliberately absent, because the reuse branch reaches a scope only through COALESCE and can therefore never erase a concurrent one. `updated_at` is a write clock, not a content clock (#879), and covers every other column an edit moves: a metadata-only edit through `UpdateMemory` (a retag, a reweight, a scope narrowing) moves it while the text stays put, reads as concurrent, and is kept while its emission is dropped — the safe direction, because the pass cannot otherwise see the change. A caller that carries no corpus (nil) keeps the pre-#1051 behaviour exactly. THE GUARD COVERS BOTH HALVES of an apply: `ApplyReflection` builds it over the project's replaceable set BEFORE the replace, and applies it to the cross-project candidates too, because a promotion writes into `_global` where the project's reuse map is never consulted — a concurrently deleted project row otherwise came back in `_global`, and a global emission merging a concurrently edited project row was written beside the edit. A dropped candidate is dropped entirely: it is neither promoted nor recovered into the project, since the recovery would resurrect the row a concurrent writer deleted. The two halves SHARE ONE view of that round — `applyView` in the transaction context carries the concurrent rows AND the fold boundary, so a cross-project candidate the replace refuses in its SECOND pass (it guards the folded list again, and the marks this very pass makes decide it) is counted back against `globalDropped` rather than `projectDropped`: the caller's cross-project component subtracts only the global half's drops, so leaving that refusal in the project count makes the Applied line claim a candidate was kept project-scoped beside a total that never counted it. The refusal MOVES between halves, it is not counted twice — the sum is what the fingerprint decision and the all-dropped check read. `runReflect` states the delete count `ApplyReflection` returns on the `Applied:` line (`, N removed`, only when N > 0) through `writeAppliedReport`: a round whose every emission was refused still deletes the rows nothing claimed, and `0 memories consolidated` alone would hide it (#1124).
- **THE SAME-SECOND WINDOW IS A REFUSAL, NOT A CHANGE (#1745).** `concurrentSinceTx` marks every row saved at or after the consolidation window's own stamp concurrent, and the comparison is `created_at >= consolidatedSince`: the `>=` is deliberate, because the stamp and `created_at` are both second-precision, so a row written in the SAME SECOND the pass started cannot be told from one that arrived during the round trip. The pass refuses a proposal over such a row, and that refusal STANDS — the row is kept exactly as it is, nothing is written, and no skip fingerprint is recorded, exactly as for a row that really moved. What changed is what the operator is told. `EmissionDrops` carries the two apart (`Changed` for a claim whose row really changed or was deleted, `SameSecond` for a claim whose row the pass read unchanged and that is concurrent only because it shares the window's second), and `fedGuard` decides between them: a row is in the same-second set only if the pass READ it and it has not moved since (`CorpusRow.sameAs`), so a row edited during the pass is a change however it was stamped, and a claim that fails on a real change as well is a change too. `Total()` counts both, because a held-back proposal is still a refused one and the caller's all-dropped check reads that number. Nothing is applied either way: the rows refused before are refused now, and only the reason differs (`TestReplaceNonManualCountsASameSecondRefusalApartFromAChange`, `TestReflectASameSecondSaveIsHeldBackAndCountedApartFromAConcurrentChange`, `TestRunReflectReportsASameSecondSaveAsHeldBack`). The window stamp comes from the store's own clock, the same clock as created_at, and reflectWindowStamp exists only so the command-level test can pin it.
- `Store.FoldRows` (`internal/memory/fold_rows.go`): the targeted fold behind `ghost maintenance consolidate-global`. ONE write transaction (`beginWrite`, immediate) for the whole run that re-reads the survivor and every folded row BY ID and refuses (skips and reports) a row that is not in the project, not `source = 'reflection'`, pinned, resolved, persistent, created at or after `since`, or changed since the plan (content or `updated_at`); a refused survivor skips its whole cluster, and every cluster is checked before anything is written. On the rows that pass it takes ONE snapshot through `snapshotReplaceableTx` (the SAME code `ReplaceNonManual` uses and of the whole replaceable set, never only the folded rows: `RestoreSnapshot` deletes every reflection row it does not find in the snapshot, so a narrower one would make a restore destructive), carries the folded rows' evidence and claims onto the survivor BEFORE the delete through `absorbSourcesTx`, the helper the reuse branch and a promoted merge share (the survivor is an existing row the folded rows merge into, the reused row's position; its provenance stamp writes nothing, because the survivor is already `reflection`, and every folded row is consumed, so none is kept live): the folded rows' decision links are APPENDED to the survivor's `source_ref` through `boundedDecisionRefs`, and the validity window (as a unit), a readable `verified_at`, confidence, agent and session are filled only where the survivor states none, with no history row and no `updated_at` bump for them, exactly as `fillReusedClaimsTx` does on the reuse branch. A fold that dropped the links left a promoted merge's `decision:<id>` on no live row, so a supersede of that decision retired nothing and answered clean. A cluster whose decision links, the survivor's own included, would not fit in one `source_ref` (`decisionLinksOverflowTx`, the check `ReplaceNonManual` makes per emission) is refused in the check loop before anything is written, every row of it skipped with the reason `decision links do not fit in one source_ref (N link(s), 512-byte bound)`, so its rows stay live as companions and the other clusters still fold; refusing later would make `errDecisionRefsOverflow` roll back the whole run. It then writes a `delete` history row per folded row and links it to the survivor, prunes old snapshots as the replace does, and updates the survivor's importance (max), tags (union) and the longest retention tier, with one `reflect` history row and an `updated_at` bump whenever any of the three changed; the survivor's content is never written and no other row changes in any column (`TestConsolidateGlobalApplyTouchesOnlyTheCluster` diffs every table).
- `Store.UpdateTask` / `Store.CompleteTask` (`internal/memory/tasks.go`): a task edit is ONE write transaction that READS the row it is about to write (#1083). `UpdateTask` used to read the task on the POOL, merge the caller's fields in Go and then write every mutable column back from that copy, so two handles editing different fields of one task each wrote the pre-commit value of the other's field and one edit was lost with BOTH calls reporting success — `s.mu` is per `Store` and closes nothing across processes, and two MCP servers on one database is a documented, supported mode, so a per-`Store` mutex was never an answer. The read now runs inside `beginWrite("update-task")`, and `_txlock=immediate` holds the write lock across it, which is the arrangement the Boundaries note requires and the one `UpdateMemoryWithOptions` already followed; the merge is therefore taken against the row as it stands when the write lands, so the second of two concurrent editors writes the first's status AND its own priority, and the `completed_at` stamp a status move earns survives a later priority-only edit that used to clear it. `resolveTaskID` stays on the pool deliberately: it NAMES a row and decides no value, and the prefix expansion is shared with the read paths. It follows memref's rule that a byte-exact match wins at any length: an id equal to a stored task id resolves to that task first, and only a non-exact input goes through the prefix scan and its ambiguity refusal, because `ghost import` writes an artifact's ids verbatim and a task `T1` beside a task `T10` is otherwise ambiguous under every spelling (`TestResolveTaskIDExactMatchWinsOverALongerPrefixedID`). The `RowsAffected` of both task UPDATEs is now read, and a 0-row update is an ERROR rather than a reported success — `CompleteTask` discarded it the same way, so a task deleted between its read and its write produced a completion the caller could not detect. The tests drive SQLite's own write lock rather than a timing race, in the shape `upsert_concurrency_test.go` established for #560: a third handle holds an open write transaction, the writer under test is proved BLOCKED (`requireTaskBlocked` — without that a writer which never reached the lock would run entirely after the commit and look correct against a fix that changed nothing), the delete is committed by the very commit that releases the lock, and the assertions are `TestUpdateTaskKeepsAConcurrentEditToAnotherField`, `TestUpdateTaskReportsATaskDeletedUnderItsWrite` and `TestCompleteTaskReportsATaskDeletedUnderItsWrite`. The MCP caller's claim that the read-merge-write cannot be overwritten is now true of the store rather than of a lock, and `ghost_task_update` inherits it. `CompleteTask` is idempotent on a done task (#1143), matching the `completed_at` rule `UpdateTask` follows (#1040): one UPDATE keeps an already-done task's stamp and an empty `notes` argument keeps the stored notes, while a non-empty one replaces them; `TestCompleteTaskIsIdempotentOnADoneTask` and `TestGhostTaskComplete_RepeatKeepsNotesAndStamp` pin it. The note is capped at `MaxContentLen` by `CompleteTask` itself, on its unclamped text after the credential guard, and `ghost_task_complete` warns when the cap cut it — the same cap and marker `ImportTask` applies, so a note saved through the tool and one restored through an artifact store the same bytes (#1238).
- `internal/followup/` — the one renderer for the command that completes a supersede repair (`ResolveCommand`: `ghost resolve <project> --reassess --only <the withdrawn edges' target ids> --apply`, full ids, the project quoted through `--project` unless it is one bare shell word, and the ids quoted too — `ghost import` writes an artifact's ids verbatim, so an id is caller-supplied text like a project name. It returns TWO further values: the ids it could not carry because they hold a comma (the `--only-file` beside it reads one id per line and never splits, so that surface reaches them) and the ids it could not carry because they hold a NEWLINE, which NO surface can (`--only` splits on commas, the file is one id per line, and a `#` in an `--only-file` line starts a comment when it BEGINS THE LINE or FOLLOWS WHITESPACE, so an annotated `<id>   # note` keeps working — a selector holding a trailing comment is not a prefix and would make the repair refuse the whole file — at the cost of truncating an id containing ' #') — a memory with such an id stays resolved until the row is rewritten, and every surface says so rather than claiming a repair; the id file is REFUSED outright when every id in the set is one of those, because a file naming nothing is a list `readRefSelectors` rejects (one reader for both `--only-file` and `--mark-file`, differing only in the flag it names in its errors — a second reader would be a second place for the two to disagree about which ids a file can name). The command is EMPTY when no id is carriable, and that is deliberate in the other direction too: printing the command without `--only` is the project-wide re-judge #698 measured, and both surfaces print what comes back here as THE repair. A comma therefore reaches the id file; a newline reaches nothing, and is named. `RenderUncarriedIDs(viaFile, unnameable)` prints BOTH buckets — it takes both and returns both, so a caller cannot spell one through `assemble.Token` and the other through `%q` — and all FOUR surfaces call it, because the ids are a stored value at the START OF A LINE and that is the one place a reader takes what they see for Ghost's own: a comma bucket is by definition ids `ghost import` wrote verbatim, and #791's refusal covers a control character, whitespace, a backtick and a « but deliberately NOT a comma (a comma breaks a selector rather than a line), so such an id can still carry all three and `Token` is what makes them data (a « becomes `«`, so it cannot open a block of its own). The MCP pair used to print that bucket raw and the newline bucket with `%q`, which keeps a printable non-ASCII rune as itself — so the same stored value read differently depending on which surface an agent happened to be holding, on the most injection-exposed surface in the codebase. It is its own package because FOUR surfaces print that command and none may render it differently: the CLI under its per-edge list, the CLI under `resolve --mark`'s report (`resolveMarkReport`, whose follow-up lists only the rows THIS call stamped — a row already resolved before the run is not something the command would change), and the MCP tools `ghost_link_withdraw` and `ghost_resolve_mark`, where it is a CLI command rather than a tool call because no MCP surface exposes the repair at all — `ghost_resolve` is the FORWARD pass and stamps `resolved_at`, so pointing an agent at it would bury more memories than it restores- `internal/secret/` — Credential-shape detection for stored text (`Detect`); shape-based, never keyword-based
- `internal/provider/` — Interface contracts: LLMProvider, MemoryStore
- `internal/config/` — Layered YAML + env config (koanf). `Load` returns an error for a config file that exists but does not parse, for a `GHOST_*` value that is not readable as its key's type, and warns on file keys no `Config` field binds (env names are not key-checked — most `GHOST_*` variables are not config keys); CLI subcommands call it and fail. `embedding.dimensions` of zero or less is refused there (`checkEmbeddingValues`), enabled or not, because the width is both the stamp in `embedding.VectorIdentity` and the length the client requires of every vector — at zero the identity loses its stamp, the whole corpus reads stale, and no vector can be rewritten; `decodeFallback` runs the same check and replaces the value with the compiled default and a warning, because the fallback keeps the environment and would otherwise carry `GHOST_EMBEDDING_DIMENSIONS=0` straight to the server. It does the same for every `[0,1]` value `checkValueChecks` refuses, one key at a time through `valueChecks` so two bad values are both replaced: the context floors, `search.min_similarity` (the vector-leg floor, which is applied inside the leg so an out-of-range value empties it and leaves hybrid search answering from the keyword arm alone) and `linking.threshold` (the cosine the MCP server's linking worker requires before it creates a `related` edge, so the refusal keeps a NaN, infinite or out-of-range value out of link creation). `GHOST_CONTEXT_RELEVANCE_CUTOFF`, `GHOST_CONTEXT_NO_ANSWER_COSINE`, `GHOST_SEARCH_MIN_SIMILARITY` and `GHOST_LINKING_THRESHOLD` parse as any float, and an out-of-range one reaching the assembler or the store fails every search request. A rejected `no_answer_cosine` is treated as unset, so the model tie (`resolveNoAnswerBar`) picks its default rather than applying 0.62 to a model it was not measured on. `LoadForHook` is the host-session path (SessionStart/Stop hooks, obsidian auto-sync, routing, the scratch budget): it reports the same problem on stderr and returns `FallbackConfig` — the compiled defaults plus the `GHOST_*` environment, i.e. every layer that does not read a file — so a typo cannot fail someone's session and cannot undo an environment opt-out. `envOverrides` lists the `GHOST_*` names the generic `GHOST_` prefix + `_`→`.` transformer cannot map, each with the parser for its key's type. `SetWarningWriter` redirects the warning sink, which `runMCP` points at its log channel; the sink is atomic because harness spawns warn from goroutines. A config layer that is not a regular file (a FIFO, a device, a directory) is skipped with the unreadable-file warning: `readConfigLayer` opens it with `configOpenFlags` (O_NONBLOCK on unix, so a FIFO cannot block the open until a writer appears) and refuses it on the fstat of the open descriptor before reading a byte; there is deliberately no O_NOFOLLOW, because a symlinked config.yaml is a legitimate setup and the check applies to the link's target.
- `internal/procgroup/` — the process-group mechanics shared by the two places Ghost confines a child that may spawn helpers of its own: the lifecycle phase spawn (`cmd/ghost`) and the LLM-harness spawn (`internal/ai`). `SetGroup` puts a child in a group of its own; `TerminateGroup` asks that whole group to stop (SIGTERM on unix, the direct child's kill on Windows, where there is no portable group signal); `KillGroup` force-kills the whole group; `GroupInhabited` asks whether anything is still in it. `TerminateGroup`, `KillGroup` and `GroupInhabited` are called by both spawn sites — the phase's `terminatePhaseProcess` / `killPhaseProcess` / `phaseGroupInhabited` are thin named wrappers — so those halves cannot drift apart. **`SetGroup` has one deliberate caller asymmetry.** On unix both spawn sites call it, so each child sits in its own group with its own deadline sweep. On WINDOWS only the HARNESS calls it (`internal/ai`'s `setHarnessProcessGroup`, which sets `CREATE_NEW_PROCESS_GROUP` so a helper cannot hold the harness's stdout and a Ctrl-C is not delivered to the third-party CLI with Ghost); the PHASE's Windows `setPhaseProcessGroup` is a NO-OP, exactly as before this package existed, because a phase is `ghost` itself and `maintenanceContext` relies on it catching the console Ctrl-C so it can cancel the harness calls it is blocked on before it exits. Giving the phase that flag on Windows would turn its own Ctrl-C handling off. The force-kill is what a grace period escalates to: a harness whose group ignores SIGTERM is killed by its own Cancel after the grace, because os/exec's own escalation kills only the direct child and would leave a helper running as an orphan (see “a harness call is bounded by its whole deadline”).
- `internal/procstat/` — process identity for the two places Ghost must not trust a bare PID: `StartTime(pid)` returns an opaque creation-time token — per-platform, from the `linux`, `darwin` and `windows` files plus a `!windows && !linux && !darwin` stub that returns `("", false)` — and `IsAlive(pid, wantToken, haveToken)` reports liveness from two variants only, POSIX signal 0 and the Windows handle/exit-code form. The token is opaque — never interpreted as wall-clock time, never compared across platforms or machines, only tested for exact equality against a token obtained the same way — and the load-bearing rule is that liveness FAILS OPEN: when the token cannot be read (an unsupported OS, an unreadable proc entry, permission denied) `IsAlive` reports alive, so a transient token-read failure can never flap a live process to "dead" and let `Reap` or `claimPidFile` delete what it should not. Linux's raw source is boot-relative ticks, which is not reboot-safe on its own while `.pid` files and `.owner` markers outlive a reboot, so the machine's `boot_id` is prefixed into the token. Windows treats a handle that opens with an exit code other than `STILL_ACTIVE` as not alive, because `OpenProcess` succeeding does not mean the process is still running. Both consumers go through it and neither re-implements it: `internal/mcpinit` (a `.pid` file holds `pid:token`, or a bare pid when no token was available) and `internal/scratch` (the `.owner` marker's `start` line). The extraction was forced by the second: `scratch` needs the same token but `mcpinit` already imports `scratch`, so a shared package was the only way to reuse the implementation instead of duplicating it — `mcpinit` keeps two unexported wrappers so its own call sites and platform tests did not move
- `internal/scratch/` — the temp space Ghost's LLM-harness children are confined to. The root is `<dataDir>/scratch`, or `$GHOST_SCRATCH_DIR` when set and NON-EMPTY (a blank override is treated as unset, so an exported empty variable cannot silently move the root to the process's own working directory), created 0700 explicitly because `MkdirAll` leaves an existing directory's mode alone and the override may name a pre-existing shared dir. It is under the data dir rather than the shared system temp because opencode writes a hidden ~4.7 MiB JIT-cache shared object into its temp dir on EVERY spawn and one consolidation lifecycle spawns hundreds of harness processes: left in the system temp those droppings accumulate until the filesystem fills, at which point every LLM-backed phase fails at once. `Root` resolves the data dir through `config.DataDir`, whose #721 `GHOST_DEV_FORBID_DATA_DIR` refusal sits in `config.DataDirPath`, so that refusal reaches this root too and the scratch dir cannot be planted where the store is forbidden. `Open` mints one `<pid>-<16 hex>` directory and writes a `.owner` marker (`pid=`, `token=`, `start=`); `Release` is best-effort, idempotent and nil-safe, because it also runs on early returns and on paths `Reap` may have already collected. WHICH variables a child gets is the spawn site's business — `internal/ai` pins TMPDIR/TMP/TEMP — and nothing here reads or writes the inherited temp dir, because this package must keep working when the system temp is full or absent, which is the situation it exists to survive. `Reap(maxAge)` (once per lifecycle in `cmd/ghost`, and from `ghost mcp init`'s `ensureScratchSpace`, both with a 24h age) scans the root's DIRECT entries and never the root: only entries provably created by Ghost are eligible — a readable marker, or a markerless name of exactly `Open`'s shape, and a markerless one only once its mtime is past `maxAge`, which is what protects an entry whose marker is being written right now — and anything else is left alone, because the override may point at a directory shared with somebody else. THE SHAPE IS EXACT DOWN TO THE PID SEGMENT: `Open` names its directory `<pid>-<16 hex>` with `strconv.Itoa(os.Getpid())`, so a pid is a POSITIVE integer whose decimal form never carries a leading zero, and a markerless name whose pid segment is `0`, or a pid written with a leading zero, is a name no Ghost run can have produced — it is somebody else's entry, and it is left alone however old it is, because a markerless entry is removed on the strength of its name ALONE. The follow-up id file's run token carries the same pid segment and ONE rule decides both (`isPidSegment`), so a name Ghost cannot write cannot authorize a deletion in either place. A SYMLINK is never followed, because the root is Ghost-owned but a link can point anywhere. The `.owner` marker inside an entry is read the same way a sidecar is (O_NOFOLLOW, O_NONBLOCK, then an fstat that refuses anything that is not a regular file), so a FIFO planted where the marker belongs cannot block the open and a symlink there is not followed to whatever it names. A marked entry is owned-and-live only when its pid is alive AND, where the marker carries one, its `start=` creation-time token still matches the live process's (the same pid-reuse token `internal/mcpinit`'s `.pid` files hold); a dead pid, or a live one whose token differs, is abandoned and removed regardless of age. A `start=none` sentinel says the platform cannot supply tokens at all, so liveness alone shields it and that shield is UNCONDITIONAL — no age ceiling, no mtime consultation — because a phase timeout can be configured to 0 (unbounded) and any ceiling could delete a live invocation's scratch. Only a marker with NO start line at all predates tokens, and only that form falls back to the age backstop. `EnforceBudget()` runs before each spawn (`internal/ai`'s `harnessCommand` is its one call site) in a fixed order: measure, and only if already over `scratch.max_bytes` reap, re-measure, then warn LOUDLY through slog naming the root and the bytes-over, and record the bytes and reap count to `maintenance_runs` when the check fired at all. Every failure path warns and RETURNS: enforcement must never BLOCK a spawn (maintenance is not stopped by its own hygiene) but must never be quiet either. `scratch.max_bytes <= 0` disables the check; an unset key is the compiled 512 MiB default (`config.DefaultScratchMaxBytes`). The one markerless regular file Reap collects is a follow-up id file, `ReassessTargetsName`'s `supersede-reassess-<project>-<utc>-<suffix>.ids`: it carries its own 14-day window (`idFileStaleAge`) rather than maxAge, because it is an operator's pending repair that must stay readable for days, and Reap takes it with `os.Remove` (one file, never descending), so a directory wearing the name survives. cmd/ghost and scratch share one name builder so the writer and the reaper cannot drift. **A MEASUREMENT THAT FAILED IS NOT AN EMPTY MEASUREMENT (#1718).** `Size` returns `(0, 0, err)` when its walk meets an entry it cannot read — a directory the owner cannot enter, a file it cannot stat — because a partial count would be a claim about bytes it never saw. So the zeros that come back with the error are a FAILED measurement, and every caller has to say so instead of printing them: `EnforceBudget` logs "cannot measure root" and skips the check rather than comparing a failed measurement against the budget, and `ghost maintenance status` names the failure in its header ("could not be measured: …") rather than printing "0 bytes in 0 files" for a root it never managed to read — which is what it used to do, silently and with exit 0. The header prints a size only when a size was measured; an ABSENT root is the other state, and `Size` reports that one as `(0, 0, nil)`, because a root that does not exist really does hold nothing. The failure and the root path are rendered through the LABEL renderer, because the entry the walk could not read is a NAME any local process can choose and `Size` puts that name in the error it returns: printed raw, a name holding a line break ends the header line and the rest of the name becomes a second line of output, wearing the product's own voice.
- `internal/secret/` — credential-shape detection for text Ghost is about to store, and the only place that answers "does this value look like a secret". It answers from the SHAPE of the value and never from the words around it, because a memory store is prose ABOUT credentials ("rotate the deployment password quarterly", "the tokenizer keeps a 30k vocabulary" are ordinary knowledge) and a keyword heuristic refuses all of them — `internal/reflection`'s `looksLikeSecret` asks the different question (is this text safe to replay into every project) and must never reject a save, only narrow a scope. So every rule needs a value: a fixed provider prefix at a realistic length, a complete PEM block, a labelled Cardano key field, an unbroken credential-length hex or base64 run, a whole word sequence from the embedded BIP-39 list, or a credential-shaped NAME assigned to a value that could not have been written by someone documenting the setting — every length floor chosen against the longest innocent value of the same shape (`longHexFloor` 128, so a 32-byte Cardano public key, a payment credential and a stake credential all stay storable; `mnemonicWords` 24, not 12, and the cost is a documented false negative). A `Finding` names the format and NOTHING ELSE — never the matched text, because a Finding reaches the log file, the saving agent's context and a third-party model's prompt, so quoting the value relocates the secret instead of containing it — and the rules are ordered so the first match wins and a specific format must precede a general one. Precision is not optional: the refusal is loud and blocking, so a false positive trains a user to route around the control within a week. The performance shape is measured and pinned rather than assumed (no keyword prefilter, because `LiteralPrefix` returns empty for a pattern starting with a word boundary, so a derived prefilter would be useless and a hand-written one a silent false negative — wrong in the strict direction, and a rule that never fires in this control is indistinguishable from a rule that does not exist; `TestDetectIsLinearInLineLength` and `TestDetectDoesNotRescanTheLinePerAssignment` hold the line there). The writers that reach it are named at each call site rather than being all of them **THE VALUE THE DETECTOR JUDGES IS THE VALUE A READER SEES, AND THE INVISIBLE FORMAT CLASS IS WHY.** A value carrying a Unicode FORMAT character (category Cf — U+200B ZERO WIDTH SPACE, U+00AD SOFT HYPHEN and the rest of the class) is judged TWICE: as the reader sees it, with those characters removed, and as the bytes are. The FIRST reading is what closes the hole the class opened: the rules matched the raw bytes, so ONE invisible character inside a token-shaped value broke the match (`sk-ant-api03-…` was recognised and the same value with a U+200B in the middle was recognised as nothing), so the value was stored — by the MCP save tools, by `UpsertWithOptions`, by `CheckImportedMemory` and by `ghost import`, which refused the unsplit value and accepted the split one — and it was then printed whole by every listing, replayed into every later session and written into the next export. The SECOND reading keeps the verdict from ever being WEAKER: a rule that needs a word boundary reads the bytes, and a FORMAT character is not a word character, so the token beside it starts a word; removing the character joins the two and the boundary goes with it, and a guard that stops recognising is indistinguishable from a guard that is not there. The class is the WHOLE of category Cf rather than a list, so a character a later Unicode version adds is covered without a change.
