The predicate that names it (`secret.IsFormatRune`) LIVES HERE rather than in `internal/memory`, because the dependency runs `internal/memory` -> `internal/secret`: the detector has to see THROUGH the class, so the rule cannot live above the package that needs it, and `memory.IsFormatRune` delegates to it — so the renderers that escape the class, the write boundaries that refuse it and the detector that sees through it share ONE implementation. **THE DETECTION-ONLY SET IS BROADER: it is category Cf, plus Mn/Me combining marks, the Hangul fillers (U+115F, U+1160, U+3164, U+FFA0), the object replacement character (U+FFFC), the braille blank (U+2800), and all of category Cc except tab, newline and carriage return.** This set is used ONLY to build the copy the detector judges; the renderers and write boundaries still use the Cf-only predicate. The class is pinned EXACTLY by `TestTheFormatRuneClassIsExactlyUnicodeCategoryCf`, exhaustively over every code point, and the two readings are pinned by `TestDetectSeesThroughAnInvisibleFormatCharacter`, `TestDetectKeepsRefusingWhatTheBytesRefused` and `TestDetectAllowsProseCarryingAnInvisibleCharacter`.
- `internal/repo/` — repository identity read out of the filesystem, two functions: `DetectRemote(dir)` runs `git -C dir config --get remote.origin.url` and `TopLevel(dir)` runs `git -C dir rev-parse --show-toplevel`, both under a 2s `detectTimeout`. It exists to keep process-spawning out of `internal/memory`, which is deliberately a pure storage layer that never spawns a process, while the only place that can say which repository a path belongs to is a caller willing to ask git — so the capability is INJECTED rather than called: `cmd/ghost/main.go` hands this function in through `memory.SetDetectRemote`, `nil` restores a no-op returning `""`, and a store built without one resolves exactly as it did before repository identity existed. The load-bearing rule is that every failure returns `""` rather than an error — not a repository, no `origin` remote, an unreadable directory, no `git` binary, a hung `git` — because detection runs on save paths and "no repository known" is a legitimate answer, not a reason to fail a memory write; the timeout is that same rule applied to a network-mounted worktree or an NFS-backed checkout, which must never stall a write. `-C` walks up to the enclosing repository, so a path pointing inside a checkout still reports that checkout's remote. `internal/mcpserver` calls the same detector, and only when a `project_id` contains a path separator — a project NAME says nothing about a repository, so an ordinary named save never crosses the boundary, which is what lets its tests replace the package var and prove it. `ghost project bind` passes the same function, so the remote recorded there is the one resolution later compares against rather than a second spelling of one repository

  **EVERY GIT CHILD GHOST RUNS GOES THROUGH `repo.GitCommand`, WHICH DROPS THE INHERITED GIT VARIABLES THAT CAN RELOCATE THE REPOSITORY OR REPLACE THE FILE ITS CONFIGURATION IS READ FROM.** Two families, one rule: the `-C` directory a caller names is the only thing that decides which repository answers, and no parent process can point the child at a config file, or hand it config values, for a question the checkout's own config already answers. The LOCATION family tells git WHERE a repository is rather than how to behave — `GIT_DIR`, `GIT_WORK_TREE`, `GIT_COMMON_DIR`, `GIT_INDEX_FILE`, `GIT_CEILING_DIRECTORIES`, `GIT_OBJECT_DIRECTORY`, `GIT_ALTERNATE_OBJECT_DIRECTORIES`, `GIT_NAMESPACE`, `GIT_PREFIX`, and `GIT_DISCOVERY_ACROSS_FILESYSTEM`, which is in the same family as the ceiling because it WIDENS where discovery may walk to and so changes which repository a directory resolves to just as the ceiling narrows it. Inherited, each of them overrides the directory a caller named with `-C`, so a server started from inside a git hook — or from any shell that exports one — resolves a different top level or a different remote than its working directory implies, and the project a save opens is bound to a checkout nobody is working in. The CONFIG family decides WHICH configuration the child reads: `GIT_CONFIG_PARAMETERS`, which is what `git -c key=value` exports to the child it spawns (a hook included), and `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT` with `GIT_CONFIG_KEY_n`/`GIT_CONFIG_VALUE_n`, which is the same job in an indexed spelling; plus `GIT_CONFIG`, `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL` and `GIT_CONFIG_SYSTEM`, which replace the files that are read. That layer OUTRANKS the checkout's own `.git/config`, so an inherited one names the `remote.origin.url` that the one key `DetectRemote` reads — `GIT_CONFIG` because it replaces the read outright, and the global and system ones because they still supply a key the checkout does not carry, which is the ordinary case for a repository bound before its origin was recorded. The scrub is what makes the `-C` directory the only input that decides which repository answers, and it is the reason the two detectors above and `internal/reflection`'s commit log all build their child the same way: one helper, so there is one spelling of the rule and no second site that forgets it. The COUNT and the indexed pairs go together or not at all: dropping only `GIT_CONFIG_KEY_`/`GIT_CONFIG_VALUE_` while leaving the count makes the child fail outright (git reports a missing config key and exits non-zero), which answers "no repository known" rather than answering about the directory it was handed — a partial scrub would silently drop repository identity on any host whose hook exports these. **What the scrub does NOT reach is stated with the same precision: the checkout's own `.git/config`, and the user's OWN global and system config.** `git config --get` reads local first, so a checkout's own value still wins, and when the checkout carries none the user's own `$HOME/.gitconfig` answers — which is exactly the answer git gives at the user's own shell, and which no PARENT process can point anywhere. Scoping the read to `--local` would close that remainder and is deliberately NOT what this does: it also drops an `[include] path` out of the checkout's OWN config, so a repository that carries its origin the way a dotfile-managed checkout does would stop answering at all. **The one cost of dropping the three file pointers is named rather than hidden:** they are also the supported way to RELOCATE that config (a container or a CI sandbox pointing the global at a file of its own), and a relocation is lost — the child falls back to `$HOME/.gitconfig` and `/etc/gitconfig`, which is where `safe.directory` normally lives anyway, so a checkout owned by another uid still resolves and one whose `safe.directory` was parked only in the relocated file stops resolving and answers "" on both detectors. The same cost applies to a `safe.directory` supplied through `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT`/`GIT_CONFIG_KEY_n`/`GIT_CONFIG_VALUE_n` or through `git -c` (`GIT_CONFIG_PARAMETERS`): it is no longer honoured either, and that is the common CI and container spelling. The remedy is to set `safe.directory` in `$HOME/.gitconfig` or the system config. These go because the alternative is a parent that can name the global file or supply the key. `GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM` is deliberately kept: it can only DISABLE the system config, never point the child at a different one, so it cannot redirect the remote. The rest of the scrub is NARROW and deliberately so — it takes location and config pointers and nothing else, because the variables that are about neither have to reach the child to keep it working at all (`PATH`, `HOME`, and git's own `GIT_AUTHOR_*`/`GIT_TEMPLATE_DIR`, which name a commit and a template rather than a repository). The risk is contained rather than absent: the store's claim and containment guards still apply to whatever path the scrub lets a wrong-but-guarded binding record, so this is a wrong directory inside the guards rather than a cross-project write. Pinned by `TestInheritedGitLocationVariablesAreIgnored` (two repositories, the location variables pointing at the wrong one), `TestInheritedGitConfigOverridesAreIgnored` (the exported parameter string, the indexed pairs, each config file, and all of them at once the way a hook's environment carries them), `TestTheDroppedConfigFilePointersFallBackToTheUsersOwnConfig` (the fallback that keeps a relocated `safe.directory` working), and `TestWithoutGitOverridesKeepsOtherVariables`, which also holds the indexed prefixes and the discovery variable that no behavioural test can observe
- `internal/selfupdate/` — `ghost upgrade` self-update from GitHub Releases. The PRODUCER side of release authenticity (#694) is asserted from here, not from the workflow: every asset `.goreleaser.yml` publishes and every zip the `plugin` job uploads carries a GitHub artifact attestation minted by `actions/attest-build-provenance`, and that action is pinned by full commit SHA because it is the one release step whose output clients are told to trust; every other action the release, docker and plugin jobs invoke is now pinned the same way, so no write-scoped job runs third-party code from a movable tag (`TestReleaseJobAttestsExactlyWhatGoReleaserPublishes` DERIVES the expected subject paths from `.goreleaser.yml` rather than transcribing them, in both directions, so a new archive format or a renamed checksum file cannot merge as an unattested gap; `TestPluginJobAttestsExactlyWhatItUploads` reads the `gh release upload` step and compares; the two attestation scopes are job-scoped, never workflow-scoped, or the `docker` job inherits an OIDC token). The plugin attestation runs BEFORE `gh release edit --draft=false`, so a failure leaves an unpublished draft a re-run completes rather than a public release whose zips have no provenance — that ordering is asserted too, because it is the difference between a recoverable and an unrecoverable release. A DERIVATION only catches what it derives independently: an extra file's path is taken verbatim from `release.extra_files` because GoReleaser resolves that glob from the project root and never copies it into `dist/`, `TestEveryLiteralAttestationSubjectExists` then asks of every literal subject path whether it names a file that will exist at attest time (a repository file, the checksum file, or one this job's own steps build), and `TestTheReleaseTimeGuardFailsOnASingleZeroMatchPattern` EXECUTES the workflow's guard step so a guard that never fails is not mistaken for a guard. That last pair exists because `actions/attest-build-provenance` only errors when the COMBINED glob result is empty, so a single wrong path is dropped silently and the release ships an asset nobody attested. The CONSUMER side lives in the same package (`attestation.go`) and has three load-bearing rules, all of them about which states may be collapsed into which. (1) Nothing is hand-rolled: the bundle is parsed and the certificate checked by sigstore-go, and the trust root comes from that library's TUF client — a hand-written chain check is exactly the thing that is subtly wrong in ways no test here would catch, and the whole value of an attestation is that it is a signature nobody in this repository made. (2) The three failures are NOT one failure, and the boundary between them runs through the HTTP status code, not through the call site. `ErrNoAttestation` (the service holds none) is distinct from a 500, an unparseable response, a 404 on a presigned `bundle_url` and a trust root that would not fetch, because only the first is a fact about the release and only the first is something a user can permanently do anything about; the verifier reports the first as `AttestationAbsent` and everything else as `AttestationUnreachable`, and a client that collapsed them would turn a Sigstore outage into a permanent belief about a release. `attestationGet` takes `absenceOn404` for exactly this reason: ONLY the index's 404 is the answer to "does this release have an attestation", so a blob 404 must not be — and it is a parameter rather than a convention because a blob's presigned lifetime has nothing to do with whether the release is attested. A member that cannot be read is collected as a reason instead of aborting the lookup, so a release with several attestations and one dead blob link still yields the bundles the others carried; the reverse would let anyone who can publish one extra unreadable attestation make a release uninstallable. `AttestationUnverifiable` — a bundle exists and did not verify — is fatal whatever flags exist, because a loud override that can wave through a failed verification hands the feature to anyone who can mint one bundle of their own. (3) A refusal never quotes the identity that was REJECTED. A certificate SAN is attacker-supplied text and sigstore-go's own mismatch error prints the value it got, so `classifyAttestationFailure` reduces every failure to this package's own words and the required identity is named instead. The same rule reaches the reason strings: a blob 404 must not leak the absence wording into a message a user reads, because a correct state classification with a misleading sentence is the same defect told differently. The cutover constant `FirstAttestedVersion` is applied inside `AttestationVerifier.Check` rather than by its callers, so no call site can forget it, and an unorderable version counts as REQUIRING an attestation — "cannot tell" must never read as "old enough to skip". The trust root is fetched only once a bundle is in hand, from a data-directory resolver rather than a path, so a pre-cutover upgrade pays neither a TUF round trip nor the data-directory guard — and it is fetched under its own `attestationTimeout` rather than the caller's, because the caller in production is the whole 12-minute run budget, which is both a long wait and the wrong explanation for a stalled Sigstore endpoint. `install.ps1` is held to the SAME three states and the same override, and `TestInstallPS1AttestationDecidesTheSameWayGhostUpgradeDoes` EXECUTES the script's decisions against a stubbed `gh` while `TestTheTwoBoundariesAgree` and `TestTheReleaseWorkflowIdentityIsTheSameStringInBoth` compare its boundary constant and its certificate identity against this package's, because the script cannot import a Go constant and two copies of one rule drift. The boundary is a MIRROR of `parseVersion`/`coreVersion` rather than an approximation of it, and the two once disagreed in the only direction that matters: PowerShell's `^\d+(\.\d+)*$` accepted any number of components and leading zeros, so `0.42.9.1` and `0.042.9` — both UNPARSEABLE to the client — were read as OLDER than the cutover and installed on a checksum alone while `ghost upgrade` refused the same release. Two rules carry that: exactly three components, and each is `0 | [1-9][0-9]*`; and the table that holds them together needs rows on an OLDER line, because every adversarial row on or above the cutover answers `true` whether or not the parse is right, so a stricter-or-broken parser passed them all. The suite runs in CI on the `windows-plugin` job, which is the only job that can run a Windows script — the skip in `requirePwsh` is for local runs only, and its comment once claimed there was no Windows job at all, which was false. AND every comparison the boundary makes is CASE-SENSITIVE and WHOLE-STRING, because PowerShell's defaults are the opposite of Go's and the difference is invisible in ordinary text: `-replace` and `-notmatch` are case-insensitive with .NET Unicode case folding, so `V0.42.9` lost its capital and `0.42.9-K` (U+212A KELVIN SIGN) folded onto a plain `k` — both accepted as versions older than the cutover, both skipping the check. A `$` anchor also matches BEFORE a trailing newline, so `0.42.9\n` parsed, and an `[int]` cast threw on a component past Int32 where `strconv.Atoi` accepts one. The script therefore uses `-creplace`/`-cnotmatch` with `\A…\z` and `[long]::TryParse` throughout the version path, and the table carries U+212A, U+0130, U+017F, trailing-newline and past-Int32 rows. ONE trap is not in the table and cannot be, because the table chooses its own inputs: the version the installer actually checks is DERIVED by `Get-LatestReleaseInfo`, which has its own `-replace`, so a tag of `V0.42.9` was reaching the check as `0.42.9` while every boundary row stayed green. The case file drives that function with `Invoke-RestMethod` shadowed by a stub, which is the only way that site is covered at all. It cannot reuse the verifier: there is no Sigstore library in Windows PowerShell, and the attestations API serves each bundle snappy-compressed, which PowerShell cannot read — so it shells out to `gh attestation verify`, which needs `gh auth login` even for a public repository. So `install.ps1` splits the failures in two where this package splits them in three, and it does so ESTABLISHING which happened rather than mapping an exit status to a verdict: `gh` answers 'did not verify', 'nothing to verify' and 'I could not reach GitHub' with the SAME status, so the script proves `gh` is logged in, probes that this `gh` has the `attestation` subcommand at all (a gh from before it existed would otherwise be a permanent unoverridable refusal that blames the release for a property of the machine), and `Test-GhMachineFailure` recognises a transport failure by specific text — returning FALSE for anything it does not recognise, because a matcher that cannot be recognised must never be the one that lets something through. That is the fail-CLOSED direction, chosen on purpose: a DNS failure or a 500 must not be reported as a fact about the archive, and an unrecognised failure must be treated as a refusal. The list is therefore a DOWNGRADE list, so every entry has to be a string an attacker cannot put into gh's error text about a certificate — gh quotes the certificate's subject and the repository back in its messages, which is why 'SSL' and 'EOF' were removed from it after a control proved the first is matched by 'not from a trusted CA'. 'Unchecked' is a property of the MACHINE and is the only state `-SkipAttestation` (or `$env:GHOST_SKIP_ATTESTATION`) reaches, for the same reason `AttestationUnverifiable` is unreachable: an override that reached the other one would be handed to anyone who can publish a bundle of their own. The env var exists at all because the documented way to run the installer is `iwr … | iex`, where a parameter cannot be passed; and the check runs after the download (the lookup is keyed on the digest of the bytes that arrived) and before `Expand-Archive`, so nothing nobody has vouched for reaches a decompressor. EVERY PARENT-DIRECTORY DERIVATION IN THE INSTALL PATH IS `filepath.Dir`, so a binary or symlink directly under a filesystem root keeps that root as its parent (`/` for `/ghost`, `C:\` for `C:\ghost.exe`) instead of collapsing to `""` or `C:`. A relative parent is not a cosmetic difference: it sends `Replace`'s staging file to `os.TempDir()` — an EXDEV rename when the temp filesystem is not the install's — and it breaks `resolveSymlinks`' promise that every path it returns is absolute, so the caller's working directory could change where an update lands. `installNewBinary` never overwrites an aside it could not clear: when `<target>.old` cannot be removed, the returned note names that FIXED path and says a running `ghost mcp` client **may** hold the old binary (restarting the client releases it), and the target then moves to a unique `<target>.old-<timestamp>` sibling so the upgrade still installs rather than failing. `sweepOldAsides` runs twice within that one install — before the move and after it, never only on a later run — and deletes exactly the `<base>.old-<digits>` names: the suffix must be non-empty ASCII digits, and the entry must be a regular file, so `ghost.old-notes`, a directory, a symlink and the plain `<base>.old` itself are all left alone. The aside a successful install has just created is the image the upgrading process is still executing on every platform that needed the move, so removing it always fails and is deliberately NOT reported: the returned note is produced only by the fixed `.old` an earlier run left behind, which nothing in this run can account for. `install.ps1`'s `Install-Ghost` mirrors the same fallback and reported errors: a fixed `ghost.exe.old` that cannot be removed switches to a unique sibling, unique `<base>.old-<digits>` names are swept case-sensitively and skipped when they are reparse points, and a move or restore failure is reported distinctly from the install error. It extracts only the archive's root entry named `ghost` or `ghost.exe` when the entry's reported length is non-zero, and cannot detect a link entry on the .NET Framework compression API it uses.

- `eval/storyline/` — LOCAL-ONLY multi-session storyline runner (#339), reusing `eval/cycle`'s substrate (scratch XDG roots, seeded opencode auth, the built `cmd/ghost` binary, the embedding drain, the chronology restamp) rather than extracting it: the duplication is deliberate, a refactor would not have fit the diff, and a shared helper that both runners depend on is a follow-up, not a prerequisite. Five rules are load-bearing. (1) **The grade reads STORE STATE, never CLI stdout** — `memory_links` rows and `memories` stamps; stdout is kept in the report because a warning is evidence, but a phase that prints a verdict it did not write cannot pass a check. (2) **The block is rendered BEFORE that stage's own records are seeded**, so what a session received is exactly what sessions 1..N-1 left behind; seeding first hands every session its own answer and makes `carry-forward` pass for the wrong reason. (3) **A supersedes edge's DIRECTION is read from the store's `created_at` stamps, not from the ids' order** — and a pair whose chronology cannot be read FAILS rather than passes, because the arc's direction is the one thing a direction-blind check would hide. (4) **Every id the grade resolves came off the `Result`, the OPENING records' included** — they are saved before any session and belong to none, and `Validate` lets a storyline reverse one (`stageOf` answers -1 for an opening record, so the "must be a later stage" rule passes). An id held only in a local slice makes `supersede-edge` fail on a store that got the edge perfectly right, which is a runner defect wearing a finding's clothes. (5) **The model is resolved ONCE, in `resolveModel`, rather than at each use** — `internal/ai` reads `GHOST_OPENCODE_MODEL` out of the RUNNER's own environment when the pin is empty, and `scratchEnv` rewrites only the CHILD processes' environment, so a stale export would decide the sessions while the phases used the default and the report named a third model. Beyond those five, two limits bound what a passing run can claim: the `stale-original` match is deliberately narrow (the record's own verbatim text) and the opt-in `-judge` covers paraphrase; the arc's lifecycle runs AFTER the last session by construction, so the runner shows what the block does with a reversal already stored, not what a supersede caught mid-arc would have changed. A stage marked `Lifecycle` runs the arc phases between sessions, so a stale line after it is about a store the lifecycle has read. `-fake-harness` replaces every model with a fixed session answer and a fake `opencode` whose supersede verdict is an ORACLE built from the storyline's own annotations — so its findings are about Ghost's pipeline, not a model — and the answer lines are not graded under it. A storyline's `KnownFailing` entries are reported and non-gating, a listed check that passes is reported as an unexpected pass, `stale-original` passes when the stale record's Mark is absent OR when every line carrying it holds `assemble.MaybeReplacedMarker` AT ITS FIELD and the replacement's Mark is on an earlier line. `carriesReplacedMarker` walks `Item.Line`'s prefix in its emitted order — importance, ` [pinned]`, the ` tags:` list DECODED as JSON so a bracket inside a tag cannot end it, ` [resolved]` — and wants the marker next; a substring test before the first `«` let a tag holding the marker's words pass. An unmarked line, a marked line with its replacement after it or missing, a marker inside the stored text and a marker forged as a tag all fail, and `main` runs with `Run.StageGap` (`stageGap`, 1.1s; zero in the unit tests) so one stage's first save is never in the same `created_at` second as the previous stage's last — a scripted stage saves well inside a second, a real session starts minutes later, and a tie is a store with no chronology for the read side to order a replacement by (only the time still owed is slept, so a real-harness run pays nothing), and `TestKnownFailingNamesAreRealChecks` refuses an entry naming no real check. Nothing here is wired into CI (#338), and a non-zero exit is a finding about Ghost, not a runner bug

- `.github/scripts/` — the reviewer's own scripts, and the ONE place the review pipeline decides anything. They are read from the BASE branch (the default branch's copy, moved out of the workspace by the reviewer workflow before the pull request's tree lands on top of them), never from the pull request's own tree: a same-repo pull request that edited `post_review.py` would otherwise get its edited version executed as the sweeper bot, and a pull request that edited the OUTCOME HELPER would otherwise decide whether its own review is judged. The scripts are stdlib-only Python, and the ci workflow's unittest step runs every `test_*.py` beside them — which is why a decision that belongs to a script belongs in a script and not in workflow shell, where no test can reach it.

  **AN OUTAGE OF THE REVIEW MODELS IS NOT A VERDICT, AND THE REVIEW-GATE IS LEFT PENDING WHEN IT IS ONE.** The Reviewer workflow has two paths to a review — a free path over several anonymous models and one paid fallback — and when neither produced a result the run failed and the sweeper published a FAILING `review-gate` check run, so an outage read exactly like a review that could not be trusted, and the gate stayed red until somebody re-ran the job by hand. The classification is ONE tested helper (`.github/scripts/review_outcome.py`, `decide()`), and it answers with one of three outcomes: `success` (a result was produced on either path), `unavailable` (no result, because the models were unavailable) and `failure` (everything else, exactly as before). It is UNAVAILABLE when the paid fallback failed on the shared Go usage window (whatever the free path did), or when every free candidate failed ONLY on a rate limit or a dropped connection and the paid step produced no result; a model that answered with an unusable findings document, an agent that did not register, a missing trusted script and a refused review all stay FAILURES, and so does any reason the helper does not recognise — the rule fails closed, because an unrecognised reason is a verdict nobody read. On `unavailable` the reviewer names the cause in the job log and in the title of the gate, and the gate is published as a PENDING check run (`in_progress`), never as a completed failure and never as a success: a green gate on a pull request nothing was judged on is the harm this rule exists to prevent. **A REASON MAY CLASSIFY AN OUTAGE ONLY WHEN THE MODEL PRODUCED NOTHING, AND A MODEL THAT ANSWERED OR ACTED IS NEVER AN OUTAGE.** The reasons the classification reads are the opencode process's error events and its stderr, and the stderr is NOT purely the provider's: opencode echoes a rejected tool call there, and the command it names is the model's own text. So the run is marked as ACTED when the event stream holds anything that is not an error event, or when the stderr names a tool attempt or a permission decision — and a stream that does not parse counts as acted, so the test fails closed. An ACTED run, and a run whose validation produced any text at all, are FAILURES on BOTH paths, before the usage-limit and transport branches are asked. That order is the whole point, because a model can write anything, including the provider's own phrases: a diff that steered a model into answering `Go usage limit exceeded`, or into trying a shell command by that name, must not be able to choose whether its own review reads as an outage. A real outage fails before any model output, so nothing is lost. The MODEL's document is written apart, and it is read only as the sign that a model ANSWERED. The outage patterns are therefore the provider's own messages and nothing looser, and the usage window is matched by its exact phrase. **THE CAUSE IS A FIXED LABEL, NEVER A REASON.** The gate title is a place a reader trusts, so it says which of two things happened — `paid fallback usage limit`, `free models unreachable` — and not what a provider said about it; the reason text reaches neither the title nor the job log. The gate is published by a JOB OF ITS OWN, so the permission to write check runs never exists in the job that runs a model on the pull request's own diff, and the review job keeps exactly the permissions it had. The sweeper leaves that pending gate alone while NO review judged the head — it asks the same marker `post_review.py` writes and the Reviewer workflow reads back — and completes it as soon as a review does judge the head, so a review that finally lands clears the gate and a gate left over from an earlier attempt never outlives the verdict. A scheduled workflow (`.github/workflows/review-retry.yml`) re-runs the review for an open pull request whose CURRENT head carries such a gate, BOUNDED by the attempt counter of the run that published it, so a permanent outage cannot loop forever; a new push starts a new run with its own counter and the ordinary review, which every push still triggers, is untouched. A base branch that predates the helper falls back to the behaviour the workflow had before it, so the change takes effect only once the helper is on main.

  **THE QUESTION "DID THE MODEL ACT?" IS ONE TESTED RULE, AND ALL THREE PLACES ASK IT THE SAME WAY.** The workflow asks it in three places — the model selection step, the free review loop and the paid fallback — and each place used to carry its own copy of the answer: a `jq` test over the run's event stream plus a grep of the run's stderr against a pattern written out three times with identical text, with nothing to keep the copies in step. The copies drifted — the selection step read only the stderr, so a model that produced something in its event stream was read as one that produced nothing — and the shell side of the decision had no automated test at all: only `decide` was tested, with the answer passed in. The rule now lives in ONE script (`.github/scripts/model_acted.py`, `acted()`), read from the TRUSTED copy of the scripts like every other script the workflow runs, and the three places ask it with the event stream AND the stderr. The stderr is read NARROWLY, because the broad pattern matched the bare words `permission` and `bash`: the phrases are the refusals themselves — opencode's ask notice, a refusal naming the tool, a declined tool call — and each of them is more than one word, so an outage whose stderr carries a stack trace mentioning a permission or a shell is an outage again and leaves the gate pending instead of failing it. One phrase is not a refusal wording and is kept anyway — `not permitted` — because it can only force ACTED, so it fails the gate closed rather than leaving it pending. A stream that does not parse counts as acted, so the rule fails closed, and so does a stream or a stderr that could not be READ: an absent file and an empty file are the run's own silence (the workflow pre-creates the empty files), but a file that exists and cannot be opened — a directory, a file nobody can read — is an unknown, and an unknown is acted. And a run whose rule could not be consulted AT ALL — a base branch that predates the script — is never an outage either: it fails closed with the default cause, because no cause can be supported from a run nobody read, and the marker says `unknown` so the classification never claims the model acted when it did not.

  **A WORKFLOW STEP'S SHELL IS RUN OVER FIXTURES, BECAUSE A STEP NOBODY RUNS FAILS SILENTLY.** The classify step once wrote its classification to the job log instead of the step output, so every review failed with "no valid free or paid review result was produced: a review result was produced" until it was caught on the pull request's own run. `.github/scripts/test_reviewer_steps.py` reads a step's `run:` block out of the workflow file and runs it over fixture outputs — the free and paid reasons, the validation files, and the marker the rule itself wrote — and asserts what the step put in `$GITHUB_OUTPUT`: the classification, the cause, and the rule that a run nobody read is never an outage. It runs a CALL SITE's own shell too — the model selection step, over a fake opencode — with the rule beside the trusted scripts and with it absent, and asserts the marker the call site wrote (`acted`, or `unknown` when the rule could not be consulted) and the outcome the classification reached from it. The same suite holds the three places that ask whether the model acted to the same question, so a copy of the pattern cannot come back into the workflow.

- `e2e/` — the end-to-end layer: the BUILT binary, run the way a user runs it, behind the `e2e` build tag. **THE SANDBOX'S PATH NAMES ONLY DIRECTORIES THE SUITE CREATED, SO NO MCP CLIENT THE HOST INSTALLED IS REACHABLE FROM INSIDE A SANDBOX (#1759).** The PATH is the fake-harness directory and a tools directory, both inside the sandbox. The tools directory holds a link to the binary under test — so `ghost` resolves to it by name wherever the build lives, a `GHOST_E2E_BIN` in an installed prefix included — and links to the host programs the sandboxed program calls by name: the POSIX utilities the fake harness scripts use (`cat`, `date`, `grep`, `head`, `rm`, `sed`, `sleep`, `tr`, required, because a shell script that calls `grep` needs a `grep` to call) and the ones Ghost resolves itself (`git` for repository identity, `lsof` and `fuser` for the scratch cleanup probe, `ps` for the darwin source-detection fallback, linked only when the host has them, so a machine without one degrades as it does everywhere else). A host directory on the PATH used to put the developer's own programs within reach: a real `claude` or `opencode` installed into `/usr/local/bin` was found by `ghost mcp init`, which then DROVE IT — the suite running a real client where the end-to-end rules say the harnesses are fakes — and the "no client on PATH" case answered with a success instead of the actionable error. The rule is pinned from both sides: `TestSandboxPATHNamesOnlySuiteOwnedDirs` asks the environment the sandbox actually hands over and fails on any PATH entry outside the sandbox root, and `TestSandboxCannotReachAClientOutsideIt` plants the suite's own fake harness as a client OUTSIDE the sandbox, asks `ghost mcp init` to report that there is no client on PATH, and proves the planted program was never run. Closed is not empty: `TestSandboxFakeHarnessRunsUnderTheClosedPATH` asks the fake harness to answer under that PATH, so a utility the tools directory forgot fails here — naming the utility — rather than halfway through another test.

## Key patterns
- Memory categories: architecture, decision, pattern, convention, gotcha, dependency, preference, fact
- Untrusted parse surfaces (`claudeimport`, `hostevent`, `obsidian`): a value somebody else wrote is DATA. It is stored exactly as parsed, it names at most one path component inside the directory its surface owns, and it never decides the parse — the parser's own vocabulary does, and a payload that does not fit is rejected or mapped to the documented fallback, never obeyed. `internal/adversarial` states the invariant once and holds the corpus all three suites drive (#585). Two rules that look like filters and are not: a planted payload stays retrievable (hiding the tampering is worse than showing it; quoting into the data block happens on the way out), and every `hostevent` refusal fails open, because "allow the stop" is the only response the hook contract emits. Defects here were silent rather than loud — a front-matter line past `bufio.Scanner`'s default cost a memory its category, a separator in a record id cost it its note, a quoted `ghost_id` cost it a second prune pass — which is why each is a fixture and not a line of prose
- Backup and portable transfer: `ghost backup` is `Store.Backup` → `memory.vacuumInto` (SQLite `VACUUM INTO`, the same one implementation the pre-migration copy uses), so both claim the destination with `reserveBackupPath` (a `0600` `O_EXCL` create, before any byte is written) and both refuse an existing path. The vacuum is handed `vacuumTarget(dest)` — a percent-encoded `file:` URI for the absolute path — never `dest` itself: the live connection was opened through a `file:` URI, so SQLite reads a bare `VACUUM INTO` argument as a URI too, and `file:snap.db` or `:memory:` would send the copy somewhere the reservation, the chmod, the size check and the count never looked (#1186). A failure after the vacuum (stat or count) goes through `afterVacuumFailure`: an empty file is the run's own reservation and is removed, anything larger is a copy and is kept and named in the error, as a failed manifest keeps it. `VACUUM INTO` names no mode, so creating the file first is what keeps a full copy of the memory database out of a group-readable window on an `--out` outside the `0700` data directory. The write lock is held for the vacuum and nothing longer. It counts the snapshot by opening the written file read-only, never the live one — the counts a restore is checked against have to describe the file that was written. The portable artifact (`internal/portable`) is the other half: `memory.PortableProjects` / `PortableMemories` are separate reads because `ListProjects` has no `repo_remote` and an artifact needs the columns in an order it owns (the other reason this line used to give — that the ordinary list readers drop the validity triple — stopped being true when `memory.Memory` gained `valid_from`/`valid_until`/`verified_at`: retrieval hydrates all three, and `PortableMemories` selects them for itself), and `Import*` are separate writes because no existing writer can preserve an imported id, `created_at` or pin. Every `Import*` takes an `apply` flag, so a dry run runs the same validation as the run it previews. `ImportProject` writes the project's `ghost_state` row in the SAME transaction as the `projects` row, the way the other two creation routes do (`EnsureProject`, `createRepoProjectTx`), because a project with no state row has no `learned_context` to write: `UpdateLearnedContext` is a plain UPDATE and checks `RowsAffected`, so a missing row is a REPORTED failure rather than a silent no-op that discarded a reflection run's summary on an imported project, and `IncrementInteraction`'s `RETURNING` already failed outright on the same store. The artifact leaves out Ghost's own shipped seeds, and it identifies them by their known CONTENT — the sentence `SeedGlobalMemories` writes, from `shippedSeedContents` — and never by `source = 'builtin'`: `builtin` is a legal `memories.source` for a user's own row (`ghost import --trust-provenance` stores an artifact's source verbatim), so a source test silently dropped that row from every artifact while the export reported success. A `builtin`-sourced row in `_global` whose content is not a shipped sentence is the only copy of itself and exports.
- Time-decay scoring: convention/preference/fact never decay; architecture/pattern 45-day; decision/gotcha/dependency 30-day
- Retention tiers (schema v19, #587): `memories.retention` is `session|project|persistent` with a CHECK, default `project`, and `memories.expires_at` is DERIVED for a session save (now+`SessionTTL`) and stated by nobody else — so no save can schedule the memory it just wrote for deletion, and a NULL expiry is the unprunable direction. `migrateV19` adds both columns and updates no row: a memory that persisted until somebody resolved it WAS a project memory, which is what every pre-v19 row reads as afterwards. `memory.retention` (the column, in Go) is not `Memory.Retention` (the field): the field normalizes an unset column to `project` in `scanMemoryRow`, because one reader forgetting the column would otherwise hydrate a session row as "no tier stated" and a prune could not see it — which is why the six full-row readers share ONE list (`memoryColumns`, plus `memoryColumnsPrefixed` for the two FTS queries that alias `memories`). `DecayFactor(category, retention, pinned, ageDays)` is the Go mirror of `DecayRankingSQL` and both carry the bounded session decay (tau 7 days, floor 0.5, formatted from the same constants); a durable row's score is exactly what it was before tiers, which is the whole reason the multiplier is 1.0 there, and `explain` names it per row as `retention_factor`. `persistent` is the one tier a default may not take back, and the ONE default that is allowed to take it back is the artifact's, which is why the portable record carries the column and `ImportMemory` rewrites it to `RetentionProject` unless `ImportOptions.TrustProvenance` is set — a `session` row must not arrive unprunable either, so a trusted import derives its `expires_at` through `sessionExpiry` and a restored row is a prune candidate like any other (`internal/portable`'s bullet carries the full rule). The exemption is asked at every WRITE and every ranking consequence, not only where a pass selects inputs: `ReplaceNonManual` (snapshot, replaceable set, concurrent-save set, output delete), `RestoreSnapshot`'s UPDATE, `ResolveCandidates`/`ResolvedCandidates`/`SetResolved`, `supersede.SelectCandidates` (before the classify call, so the verdict is not paid for), `SupersedePenalties` and `DemotionPenalties`, which take the caller's PROTECTION map rather than a list of pins — because a stale `duplicate` or `supersedes` edge would otherwise sink a row every other pass spares, and because both lookups run on read-only handles (`GetTopMemories`, `explain`, and the assembler's near-duplicate stage over a passive bucket), where a statement naming the column fails in full. The two maps are NOT the same: `DemotionPenalties` (the near-duplicate demotion) protects a plain `pinned` row the way it always did before tiers and extends that to keep-forever rows, while `SupersedePenalties` protects ONLY the `persistent` tier — a `supersedes` edge asserts that one claim replaced another, and a pin keeps a row visible without declaring its claim current, so a pinned supersedes target sinks exactly as it did before #587 (the pin-sparing supersede variant is issue #739, kept out of the tier PR; `TestAPinDoesNotStopASupersedesEdge` and `TestASupersedesEdgeDoesNotSinkAPersistentRow` pin the boundary). EVERY caller builds the map from rows it has already read, and a caller handed ids it did not hydrate is a caller that cannot protect them (`TestTheDemotionLookupsDoNotNameTheTierColumn` drops both v19 columns and calls both). A fold RAISES the surviving row's tier and never lowers it (`mergeRetention`): a protection the caller asked for and did not get is a false report, while lowering would let a `session`-tier restatement schedule another memory's durable row for deletion. `ghost prune` is the ONLY thing that removes a `session` row, it is never run for you (no lifecycle phase, hook or scheduler calls it), the dry run is a read-only PREVIEW built from the same predicate, scope and order every apply batch re-derives (so it cannot describe rows the apply would not select), an APPLIED report is counted and listed from `RemovedIDs` rather than from the candidate list — every batch re-derives the predicate under the write lock, so a row a save pinned, re-tiered or re-saved between the preview and its own batch is correctly spared and is STILL in `Candidates` (`TestPruneSparesARowThatStoppedMatchingMidRun` puts such a write in that window through a tombstone trigger, because the `pruneBeforeDelete` seam cannot: it runs inside the batch transaction, which both holds the write lock and has already chosen the batch's ids), the rows it did not take are NAMED as spared and the line names the change rather than the outcome (a write that removed the row outright leaves nothing behind), the apply deletes in bounded batches (500 rows per transaction, each batch with its own tombstones and its own rollback), and every removal appends a `delete` tombstone before the DELETE. Its activity term is `COALESCE(last_accessed, updated_at, created_at)` — nothing in production writes `last_accessed` (`Touch` has no caller), so the grace is measured from the row's last WRITE, and `retention` is NOT carried by the portable artifact, so an imported memory arrives as `project` (kept longer, never pruned)
- Empty-set guard: never replace all memories with empty reflection output, and a reflection response carrying no operations fails the LLM tier rather than relying on that guard alone
- The gate lives in `TieredConsolidator`, NOT in the LLM tier — so naming a backend bypasses it unless the tier is wrapped. `ghost reflect --tier cli` and `--tier opencode` used to hand the bare `LlmConsolidator` to the apply path and had no floor at all, while the default `auto` tier and the unattended path were bounded; `eval/cycle` measures with `--tier opencode --apply`, so the suite was grading an ungated consolidator against a gated production one. Both branches now go through `gatedLLMTier` (`cmd/ghost/lifecycle.go`), which calls `reflection.NewGatedConsolidator` — one tier, no fallback added, so "exactly this backend" still means that and a too-small answer is a failed run rather than a quiet drop to Jaccard. It is a named function because `runReflect` calls `os.Exit`, so the tier switch is unreachable from a test and the behaviour has to be pinned at the seam. The wrapper reports the tier's OWN name, not the `tiered:` prefix (`TieredConsolidator.gated`): `Consolidator: cli` must keep printing `cli`, because a script or saved log-grep keyed on that label breaks silently otherwise, and wrapping a tier changes how it is bounded, not which one runs. The prefix marks WHICH CONSTRUCTOR ran, not how many tiers were in the list — `auto --require-llm` is a one-tier list and still prints it, so do not read the prefix as a claim about fall-through
- Reflection reduction warning counts survivors, not one scope: `reportReductionWarning` (`cmd/ghost/lifecycle.go`) measures `reflectRetained`, which adds the cross-project candidates back in when promotion is off because `applyReflection` folds them into the project. Reading `len(projectMems)` understates retention by the whole candidate set and fired `>50% reduction` on rounds that lost nothing — a warning that cries wolf on the one path where nobody reads it is worse than none. The input side is `len(consolidatable)`, not a re-count of non-`manual` sources: `consolidatable` already dropped `manual`/`builtin`/`pinned`/`persistent`/resolved, so every row left is one `ReplaceNonManual` would delete. **AN APPLY COUNTS AFTER THE WRITE, IN BOTH PROMOTION STATES, FROM THE ROWS THE PROJECT ENDS UP HOLDING, INCLUDING THE SOURCES A REFUSAL LEFT LIVE; A ROUND THAT WROTE NOTHING COUNTS NOTHING.** The write is where a proposal stops being one: `applyReflection` drops a proposal whose content holds a token-shaped value before the store sees it, and a proposal that was never written is not a memory the project holds, so the count is taken from the POST-drop sets (`keptProjectMems`/`keptGlobalMems`) and not from the caller's pre-drop lists. `ReplaceNonManual` can refuse a proposal too, and a refused merge takes none of its sources: the store reports `EmissionDrops.Retained`, the corpus rows still live plus the rows the replace wrote, and that is the count `reflectRetained` uses after the write. The corpus is re-read for that count rather than taken from the replace's own kept-live set: a row the pass read and a concurrent writer pinned, resolved or made persistent while the harness was answering is no longer a replaceable candidate, so it is in neither the delete set nor that set — and it is still a row the project holds, so skipping it understated the count and could raise a reduction warning on a round that lost nothing. A corpus row deleted mid-round is gone and is not counted. Counting one per refused proposal understated an N-source refusal by N-1 and announced a reduction beside the refusal note saying every source was still live (`TestRunReflectCountsAScopeRefusedMergeAsTheSourcesItLeavesLive`). The refusal kinds are covered at the store, where the count is computed: a scope conflict, a decision-link overflow, a claim on a row that moved, a corpus row pinned or resolved while the round ran, and a plain successful merge as the control, each against the rows the project really holds (`TestReplaceNonManualReportsTheRowsItLeavesLive`). A global-only apply runs no replace, so the store reports no project retention at all — that zero means no replace ran, and the count is the rows the project still holds (`TestRunReflectCountsAGlobalOnlyApplyAsTheRowsTheProjectStillHolds`). The pre-drop count said the corpus kept more than it did, in both directions: a round whose every proposal was dropped wrote nothing and still announced a reduction, and a round that kept a third of the corpus after a partial drop reported the pre-drop count and stayed silent about the compression that happened (`TestRunReflectReportsTheReductionTheApplyMade`, `TestRunReflectDoesNotReportAReductionItDidNotMake`). A dry run reports before the write (the call sits in the `if !apply` branch, above the dry-run return), which is deliberate — the preview reports the compression it would apply — and it counts the proposals BEFORE the drop, because the drop of a token-shaped proposal happens inside `applyReflection` and a dry run never calls it, so a dry run's count can differ from the count the apply prints; only from `reductionWarnMinInput` (6) up, which is where the quality gate stops judging and this becomes the only signal
- Validity and provenance are written by the tools, or by nothing: `ghost_memory_save`, `ghost_save_global` and `ghost_memory_update` share one `validityArgs` struct (`internal/mcpserver/validity.go`) and one resolver, so the six optional arguments mean the same thing in all three schemas. `valid_from`/`valid_until`/`verified_at` take RFC 3339 or a date and are stored in `memory.StoredStampLayout` (`verified: true` is the same field as "now", and `verified` together with `verified_at` is refused rather than resolved); a window that does not end after it starts, a `confidence` outside [0,1], an unreadable stamp and a `source_ref` over `MaxSourceRefLen` are tool errors, because stage 2 would otherwise read them as claims nobody meant. Nothing is invented: an omitted argument is absent, and absent is NULL, so a store can still tell "nobody said" from "said to be nothing" — which also means a stamp can be replaced but not removed. Four surfaces filter on a window: `ghost_memory_search`, the session-start block, which since #766 runs the same stage 2 rather than rendering its own rows, `ghost_project_context`, and the `ghost://memories/global` resource — so a memory whose `valid_until` has passed or whose `valid_from` has not arrived stops being offered at every session start and stops appearing in either listing. That is a change in what a session is told, and it is the correct one — the block used to inject a retired row as a plain present-tense line, which is a lie about it — but it arrived by INHERITANCE rather than by decision, so it is stated here: `ghost_memories_list` and `ghost_search_all` still do not run the pipeline and still render one marked `expired` instead (assemble.ValidityStateOf), and no surface carries a validity marker on a line it is filtering on. That list has to be read, not remembered: a surface that starts assembling is removed from it in the SAME change, because a tool description naming it as a browsing surface is a claim an agent then relies on when it decides whether a dated claim is still worth trusting. `unverified` is still kept, because `verified_at` is a flag rather than a predicate; a row nobody has re-checked is still true as far as the store knows. Saying otherwise in a tool description is a lie the next surface inherits — and a surface that renders its own rows does not get the shared renderer for free. `agent` comes from `provenanceFor` (client name, then process ancestry) and `session_id` from `req.Session.ID()`, which is "" on the stdio transport every Ghost host actually uses — NULL is the honest record there, and a made-up id would be indistinguishable from a real one. `memory.UpdateMemoryWithOptions` writes the columns with `COALESCE` (and `NULLIF` on the provenance strings) rather than a read-merge-write, so an omitted field is the stored value by construction and two concurrent edits cannot lose each other; `provider.MemoryStore` is not widened for it, so `mcpserver` reaches it through the `updateCapableStore` assertion. Stage 4's multiplier stays `1.0`: the renderer shows confidence and the writer records it, and nothing ranks on either
- Durable knowledge, not repository facts (#674): a memory is for knowledge that survives the conversation and is expensive to rediscover, and one rule is stated in five AGENT-FACING places that must be reworded together — the MCP server instructions, the `ghost_memory_save` and `ghost_save_global` descriptions, the reflect ops prompt, and the resolve classify rubric — plus the two docs that describe them (`docs/mcp.md` agent guidance, `docs/cli.md` resolve section), which go stale silently because no test reads them. "foo.go contains HandleFoo()" is the bad example (the repository is authoritative, so the note goes stale silently and is cheap to re-read); "Production schema changes require explicit approval." and "Deployment keeps database migrations separate from application rollout, on purpose." are good ones. The rule GUIDES and never refuses: the only deterministic half is `repoFactHint` (`internal/mcpserver/durable.go`), one advisory sentence appended to a response that ALREADY reports a stored id, on the three paths that write caller-supplied memory text and say so: `ghost_memory_save`, `ghost_save_global` (the instructions route the cross-project case there) and `ghost_memory_update`. NOT `ghost_decision_record`, whose companion memory's text is composed inside `memory.RecordDecision` — duplicating that format string in the MCP layer to judge it would let the two drift apart silently, and it needs both a reference token and a containment predicate in the SAME sentence — one without the other fires on exactly the durable notes the rule protects, since a note that cites a path and adds a reason is the case to leave alone. The equivalent reflection guidance is an obsolete-drop CANDIDATE only: the unattended path's answer to an unexplained drop is the verbatim re-add, so the new rule splices the mode-dependent `staleTail` and claims nothing the apply does not do under `--allow-drops`. Resolve gets RUBRIC text and no new veto, and NO new veto pattern: a veto is a second vocabulary for what a memory is for, `internal/supersede` reads the imperative half of the same list for the opposite decision, and `ghost resolve --reassess` runs that veto over already-resolved rows, where a false one un-hides a note permanently (#702). The predicate list is LOCATION verbs only for the same reason — a path-anchored behavioural claim ("`lifecycle.go` calls `os.Exit`, so the tier switch is unreachable from a test") is a reason, not a repository fact. The REFERENCE half is PATH-LIKE for the same reason: "it contains a dot" is not a property of code, and a bare `Capitalised.Name` fires on durable notes (an independent review measured five — `U.S. regulators`, `Bob.Smith`, `Node.js`, `Terraform.State`, `Jane.Doe`, every one carrying a real containment predicate), so a bare `Stem.ext` needs a LOWERCASE stem and a bare dotted identifier needs a Go shape (lowercase package qualifier, or a final segment with an internal capital). Measured residual: 0/612 on the committed bench corpora. **A flagged sentence is exempted in two ways (#960):** (1) the note carries a reason connector anywhere (`because`, `so that`, `on purpose`, `never`, `must` — case-insensitive, word-bounded), which is checked over the WHOLE note with no category gate; (2) the sentence is a rule (`always`) filed under a rule category (`convention`, `decision`, `gotcha`). The words `must` and `never` are already caught by the note-level reason check, so only `always` reaches the category-gated rule check. The category is the one the save will carry: `ghost_memory_update` judges an omitted category against the stored row's, so a note already filed as a rule is not re-judged as a fact because the edit left the field alone. Neither exemption can refuse a save; both only drop the sentence.
- Save-time consolidation opt-out: `ghost_memory_save`'s `pin` maps to `UpsertOptions.Pin` and pins inside `Upsert`'s existing `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction, so a memory can opt out of `ghost reflect` in the call that stores it rather than in a second `ghost_memory_pin` call — nothing an agent writes is excluded by `source`. A fold pins BOTH rows (the copy just stored and the existing row the text merged into, which is the one a later consolidation is most likely to absorb), so the save result says so rather than letting the caller infer the pin from the id it returned; `FoldOnly` stores no row, so the target is the only thing the request can mean. `pinMemoryTx` deliberately leaves `updated_at` alone: the row's age and timestamp are load-bearing for decay and the `--skip-unchanged` fingerprint AND THE OTHER PIN WRITER AGREES (#820): `Store.TogglePin` — what `ghost_memory_pin` and the MCP pin tool call — writes `pinned` and NOTHING ELSE, so the operator's explicit call says the same thing about the stamp as the save-time one does. It used to carry `updated_at = datetime('now')` beside the flag, and that column is `ghost supersede`'s chronology (`orient`) and its change detector (skip-if-unchanged), so pinning an endpoint of a live edge re-armed a reclassify nobody asked for — a classify call for a pair whose two notes had not changed since the edge was judged, which is #779's measured route to withdrawing a correct edge and re-creating it on alternating passes — and on a pair live in BOTH directions it flipped the direction `ghost supersede --reassess` asks about, which is what stands the backwards edge #641 found in the wild. A pin is a decision about how a memory is SURFACED, and the readers that act on it read `pinned` itself: the `_global` passive bucket's order (`pinned DESC, importance DESC, updated_at DESC, id` — `pinned DESC` is its PRIMARY key, so a pin still reorders what it names, and only the tie-break between two rows equal on (pinned, importance) is given up), the composite ranking's full decay exemption, and `ghost resolve`'s hold-back, which refuses to stamp `pinned` rows. What a pin no longer moves, named: that supersede pair of readers; `ghost prune`'s grace, whose `pruneActivitySQL` is `COALESCE(last_accessed, max(updated_at, created_at, expires_at))` and which is therefore measured from the row's last WRITE — `Touch` has no caller, so nothing in production writes `last_accessed`, and a pin is not a write of the note, so an operator who wants a row kept for good says so with the `persistent` tier. `TogglePin` files no `memory_history` version and neither does `pinMemoryTx`: the change log records content, category, importance, `resolved_at` and `source`, and `pinned` is none of them, so a pin is recorded in the column the readers read and in no audit row. `PromoteToGlobal` is the same decision about the same column, and the reasoning is on the `internal/supersede/` bullet.
- Upsert dedup is one write transaction: `UpsertWithOptions` opens its `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction BEFORE the FTS duplicate probe and commits the writes the probe decides on, because a probe outside it leaves a window another ghost process can use — a save that strengthened the same row in that window had its increment overwritten by arithmetic on a value read before the fact, and a row committed in that window was invisible to the probe, so two saves of one fact became two unlinked rows (both are cross-process; the per-`Store` `s.mu` cannot close either). The strengthen is therefore `SET importance = MIN(1.0, importance + ?)`: the value it adds to is the one the row holds when the statement runs. Never query the pool inside an open transaction on the same handle — `OpenDB` pins one connection, so it deadlocks; go through the `sql.Tx`. `FoldOnly`'s re-verify of its target happens in that same transaction, which makes it a statement about the probed row, not a race guard. A REPLACEMENT IS NOT A DUPLICATE: both dedup probes skip a candidate for which `statesDifferentValue` (`internal/memory/value_conflict.go`) holds, before the score is taken, so a compatible candidate ranked below it can still take the fold. The rule is lexical and deterministic: each side's VALUE TOKENS (whitespace-split, surrounding punctuation trimmed, a trailing `/` dropped so `internal/memory/` is `internal/memory`, a digit-grouping comma joined so `1,000` is `1000`; a token with a digit, an inner `.` `/` `_` `=` `:` `@` `#` `\` with three or more letters or digits, an upper-case letter after its first rune, a capitalised word AWAY from a sentence start, or any word inside a backticked span, so `` `make deploy-prod` `` against `` `make deploy-stg` `` is refused whatever the case. A sentence starts at the text's start, at every line, after a leading list or heading marker (`-` `*` `+` `N.` `N)` `#`…, never a value itself) and after `.` `!` `?` `:`, so `The cache …`/`Cache …` and `## Setup` / `Run …` are grammar while `Redis`/`Postgres` is two values; never a hyphen alone. A version with one more component is a different value on purpose: `v1.2` against `v1.2.0` is refused. The KNOWN LIMIT is a plain lowercase word outside backticks: `uses pgx` against `uses sqlx` still folds, and `TestStatesDifferentValue` pins that row so a change to it is deliberate) are compared lowercased, a number with a unit suffix of up to three letters matches the bare number (`3600s`/`3600`) while two suffixes on one number do not (`16gb`/`16mb`), and the fold is refused only when EACH side holds a value token the other lacks. A text that adds a value to the other, or rewords the prose around the same values, still folds. A RETIREMENT IS NOT A DUPLICATE EITHER: both probes also skip a candidate the incoming text RETIRES (`retires`, `internal/memory/retirement.go`), a note saying the candidate's subject is gone with nothing in its place. A retirement holds no value token the claim lacks, so the value guard alone folded "SendGrid was removed from parcel-notify: internal/mail is deleted …" into "Order emails go out through SendGrid: internal/mail wraps …", linked it `duplicate` and strengthened the removed dependency (0.70 -> 0.84). The rule is lexical, deterministic and directional: ONE SENTENCE of the incoming text (the `valueFields` sentence boundaries, `.` `!` `?` `:` and a new line) holds a phrase from the closed list `retirementPhrases` (the PAST passive `was`/`were`/`been` + `removed`/`deleted`/`dropped`, `is gone`/`are gone`, `no longer`, `not used anymore`, `decommissioned`, `deprecated`, `retired`; never a bare `removed`/`deleted`/`dropped`, a change-log verb with any object, and never the present passive `is removed from`/`is deleted`, which describes live behaviour such as "a job is removed from river_job on success"), a value token the candidate also holds (the `sharesValue` anchor, in the same sentence, so a change log that names the subject in one sentence and removes something else in another is no retirement), no plan or condition word (`retirementPlanWords`: `once`, `will`, `when`, `after`, `until`, `should`, `if`, `plan` …, so "the LEGACY_AUTH flag is no longer needed once SSO ships" is a plan and folds as before) and no negation outside the phrase (`retirementNegations`); and the candidate holds NO retirement phrase anywhere, so two "no longer" notes are not ordered by it and a retirement re-saved in a second wording still folds into the first. KNOWN LIMITS, beside the plain-word one: a retirement in the ACTIVE voice ("We removed SendGrid", "dropped Memcached", "deleted internal/mail") is not detected, because the bare verbs are left off the list as change-log verbs with any object; a retirement that names its subject only in plain lowercase words, or only by a capitalised word at a sentence start ("Redis was removed"), shares no value token and is not detected, and a real retirement written with a plan word ("After the migration, SendGrid was removed") is missed — the safe direction, two rows a supersede pass can still relate (`TestRetires` pins the must-trigger and must-not-trigger rows, `TestUpsertDoesNotFoldARetirementIntoTheClaimItRetires`, `TestUpsertCrossCategoryDoesNotFoldARetirement`). Before it, a save that changed a version, path, endpoint, store or number of the fact it resembled cleared `mergeScore` (0.52–0.69 on the storyline eval's three replacement arcs), was linked to the claim it replaces as a `duplicate` — the edge `nearDuplicatePenaltyRows` exempts from the strength threshold — and strengthened the STALE row (importance +20%, access_count +1), so the one write that recorded a change in truth made the retired claim rank higher until a supersede pass ran (`TestUpsertDoesNotFoldAReplacementIntoTheClaimItReplaces`, `TestUpsertCrossCategoryDoesNotFoldAReplacement`, `TestStatesDifferentValue`). It is the safe direction to be wrong in: a false refusal is two rows supersede or reflect can still relate; a false fold strengthens a stale claim. It is a candidate filter AFTER each probe's `LIMIT 15`, as `foldOnlyEquivalent` is, so it shares the window limit #665 closed for scope (a replacement that spent a slot costs the slot, not the fold of a compatible row inside the window). The same-category probe still folds into a resolved row and either probe into an expired one when the values agree — the resolved case deliberately (`TestUnresolveOnWrite`); the expired case is not excluded and not documented either way, and a fold carries a window the caller states onto the target (`TestUpsertFoldAppliesTheCallersValidityToTheTarget`), so excluding expired targets would change that path too. Save-time and reflection-time dedup now differ on two shapes, a value swap and a retirement: the SQLite tier's `duplicateClusters` has neither the value guard nor `retires`, so it has no value guard
- The seed's existence probe is inside its own write transaction, for the same reason and with a different cost: `SeedGlobalMemories` runs on EVERY open, so two handles meet one file — an editor-launched `ghost mcp` on first use and a CLI command, both of which bootstrap — and the per-`Store` `s.mu` closes nothing between them. Its probe used to run on the pool as an autocommit read before `seedMemoryTx` opened its transaction, so it answered about a moment before the transaction existed: two handles both read zero and both inserted a byte-identical pinned `source = 'builtin'` _global row. Nothing repairs that copy — re-seeding skips by content, consolidation does not touch a pinned row (`ReplaceNonManual` deletes only what a reflect pass replaced and only asks for `pinned = 0`, and the fold path deletes just the rows it absorbed), `PruneSessionMemories` reads `retention = 'session' AND pinned = 0` and so never names this row either, and `MergeProject` and `DeleteProject` both refuse `_global` outright, so the only route that removes THIS row is an explicit delete by id (`Store.Delete`, `DeleteWithOptions`, `DeletePurge`, the three behind the delete tool). A duplicate therefore stays until a person goes looking for it — and the seed lands in the Global section of every session-start block and every project context, so it doubles a shipped rule in every later session. The probe now reads INSIDE the `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction that writes (`tx.QueryRowContext`, never `s.db`), which SQLite's write lock serializes across processes: a concurrent seed either commits first and this probe then sees its row and writes nothing, or it sees the empty store and the other handle's probe then sees this one's row. Two copies cannot both be first. A probe that cannot be read fails the seed rather than writing into a store whose existing rows it could not see. `TestSeedGlobalMemoriesIsOneTransactionAcrossHandles` is the two-handle concurrent case, and it pads `_global` so the probe's COUNT has real work to do — a scan over a handful of rows finishes in microseconds and the ordering it exists to catch would otherwise turn on scheduler luck
- A newer schema is refused before it is written to: `memory.OpenDB` reads `PRAGMA user_version` and rejects a store from a newer Ghost BEFORE `initSQL` runs. `initSQL` is `CREATE … IF NOT EXISTS`, so on a store that merely has more than this build knows it is nearly a no-op — which is what hid the ordering — but an object the newer build renamed, replaced or dropped is recreated there, in a store the same call then declares unreadable. A fresh store has no stamp to read and is initialized and stamped; a store behind is backed up (`memory.vacuumInto`) and then migrated. A store behind is backed up and migrated by ONE opener at a time (`memory.acquireMigrationLock`): an exclusive OS file lock (flock on Unix, LockFileEx on Windows, `migratelock_*.go`) on `<db>.migrate.lock`, taken only when the first read finds the store lagging and held to the end of `OpenDB`, after which `user_version` is READ AGAIN and that read decides — a waiter that finds the store current skips the backup and the migration both, and one that finds it newer is refused by the same `refuseNewerSchema` as the first read. Without it two processes starting together after an upgrade both backed up, collided on the one-second `<db>.pre-migrate-<unix>` name (`reserveBackupPath`'s `O_EXCL`), and one failed to start, while both ran `migrate()` from a stale `from`. SQLite cannot be this lock: `VACUUM INTO` refuses to run inside a transaction and `migrate()` commits one transaction per step with `foreign_keys` toggled between them, so no write transaction spans the sequence. Making the backup name unique instead would hide the race (both processes would still copy and migrate); `TestConcurrentOpensOfALaggingStoreMigrateOnce` and `TestMultiProcessConcurrentOpenOfALaggingStore` (the multiproc helper's `open` role) require every opener to succeed AND exactly one backup, so dropping the lock or the re-read fails them. The lock wait is unbounded (a large store's copy legitimately takes long; the OS drops a dead holder's lock), the file is never removed (unlinking a lock file another opener waits on breaks the exclusion), and a current store never takes it or creates it
- A RUNNING server re-checks that on EVERY write, because the open-time check answers a question that a later migration invalidates (#746): a `ghost mcp` opened the store hours before a newer binary migrated it underneath, and kept writing for ~40 hours with no history row, no version for an edit, and no tombstone for a delete. There are TWO seams, because a check that only guards a transaction misses every write that never opened one: `memory.beginGuardedWrite` for a write TRANSACTION (what `beginWrite` delegates to) and `memory.execGuardedWrite` for a single AUTOCOMMIT statement. The second is not a nicety — the issue's own reproduction is a save, and a lone `Exec` on the pool is how a lone statement reaches SQLite; a version that was cached "somewhere per write" and a statement that never asked are the same hole. `TestEveryWriteRefusesANewerStore` walks the package with go/parser and requires every write to be one of the two or to carry a recorded reason, so a new write path cannot opt out by accident. Classifying writes by METHOD NAME alone is not enough to earn that claim, and the first version of this test was wrong in exactly that way: `CreateTask` (behind the `ghost_task_create` tool) and `IncrementInteraction` both wrote through `QueryRowContext` — a `RETURNING` clause, which is a write the method name denies and the SQL admits — and both succeeded against a store a newer Ghost owned while the scan passed. So the scan now reads the STATEMENT for a `Query*`: a leading word on a small read-only allow-list (`SELECT`, `PRAGMA`, `EXPLAIN`, `VALUES`) is a read, and everything else — every write keyword, `WITH` (which reads like an opener and can introduce an INSERT), and any statement text the scan cannot resolve at all — is a write needing a seam. Resolvable means a literal, the format string of an `fmt.Sprintf`, a concatenation rooted in either, or a name bound to one, read from its FIRST binding only because `+=` cannot change the leading word; a variable assembled anywhere else is unresolvable and therefore a write. `TestTheScanCatchesTheWriteShapesItWasBlindTo` pins each of those shapes against synthetic source, `TestTheScanStillCallsAReadARead` pins the other direction so the holes cannot be closed by calling everything a write, and `TestOnlyTheMigrationIsExemptByFile` keeps the whole-file exemption a single named decision rather than a shape that spreads. The scan also unwraps a parenthesised receiver and follows a bound method value (`f := s.db.ExecContext`), both of which reached SQLite with no selector at the call site; it tells a transaction from a statement the other way round, following `*sql.Tx` parameters, the values a tx-returning function hands back, and the executors and prepared statements derived from one, and it treats anything it cannot PROVE is a transaction as a write — the bias is deliberate, because the cost of guessing wrong is a failure naming a line and the cost of guessing the other way is a silent hole. Exemption keys are receiver-qualified, because this package declares a free function and a method with the same bare name and a key that named both would exempt both. `pinMemoryTx` therefore takes a `*sql.Tx` rather than the `sqlExecutor` interface that also admits a handle. The check runs AFTER `BeginTx` and THROUGH the transaction, which is what makes it race-free rather than merely likely: the DSN asks for BEGIN IMMEDIATE, so the write lock is already held, and a migration is itself a write that needs the same lock — the window is closed, not small. It is not a property a test can pin, and the variant that looks equivalent is wrong twice over: a pre-BEGIN read leaves the gap (and on this `MaxOpenConns(1)` pool it DEADLOCKS rather than racing, since the open transaction holds the only connection the probe would need). READS keep working, because refusing them would turn "restart your client" into "you cannot search"; a `ReadOnly` transaction is exempt for the same reason and the scan says so explicitly. The cost is one `PRAGMA user_version` per write and THERE IS NO CACHE, which is a decision rather than an omission: the pragma that looks like a change counter, `PRAGMA data_version`, is a per-CONNECTION counter rather than a property of the file, so on a connection the pool replaced underneath a long-lived server it can carry the value the previous one had (measured: two handles on one file both report 2, and a brand-new database also reports 2), and a cache keyed on it answers "unchanged" for exactly the stale write it was meant to prevent. What it saved was a memory read of a page the open transaction already holds. A probe that FAILS refuses the write and says which probe failed: an unreadable version is not a licence to write, and not a licence to claim the store is newer either, since that claim sends an operator hunting for a build that may not exist. `ErrStoreNewer` names both versions and says to restart the CLIENT rather than to upgrade, because the binary on disk may already be current and the running process is the stale half — an operator told to upgrade runs `ghost upgrade`, watches it report "already up to date", and learns nothing
- Project shape is refused where a project is CREATED, by the exporter's own predicate (#824): `memory.CheckImportedProject` is the ONE rule for a project's id, name and path, and it is asked at both store creation routes (`ensureProjectLocked`, behind `EnsureProject`/`EnsureProjectWithRepo`, and `ResolveOrCreateRepoProject` → `createRepoProjectTx`) as well as at `mcpserver.ensureProjectFor`. Both store routes and the MCP route exist because there are three of them — a path-shaped `project_id` with a detectable remote takes the repository route instead of `EnsureProject` — so the second and third are the ones a fix on the first misses. The check is on CREATION and nothing else, which is harder to honour than "ask the predicate on the way in" because NONE of the three routes is reached with the caller's own argument: `ensureProjectFor` resolves that argument by exact id, then basename name, then longest path prefix, and hands the RESOLVED id down, so a legacy project with a hostile id and an ordinary recorded PATH is addressed by its path every day and arrives at the store carrying the hostile id. Judging the record above that resolution refuses a save into a project that exists and holds the user's memories — "invalid project_id" for the project the session is working in. So at the MCP boundary the check follows the FULL resolution — not just the exact-id lookup, because a project can hold a refused character in a field the write boundary does not own (`ghost project bind` is the only writer of `projects.path`, and since #840 it asks `CheckImportedProjectText("path", …)` on the path **about to be recorded** — never on the stored one, so a project that already records such a path stays writable and stays repairable by binding it to a directory that passes. Before that it asked only `storedPathIsUsable`, i.e. whether the path is absolute, and «, » and a backtick are all legal directory names, so a clean id could sit above a hostile recorded path that the session's own directory resolves to) — and at BOTH store routes it sits on the arm about to `INSERT`, in the same `beginWrite` transaction as the `INSERT` it gates (an existence check outside the transaction is a check-then-write another process can interleave into).
