The refresh arm is then not judged, and that is safe rather than merely convenient: the upsert's `ON CONFLICT(id) DO UPDATE` touches only `path`, `repo_remote` and `updated_at`, it keeps the stored `path` whenever the caller passed the id in its place (which is what `ensureProjectFor` does, so a hostile id is never written back), and `repo_remote` is normalized, and the creation predicate judges it only on the INSERT arm (normalization drops the userinfo on every spelling, the query string and the fragment; a token in the URL path is not recognisable to it, the three INSERT arms hand the normalized remote to `CheckImportedProject` through `createdProject` and refuse it, but the refresh arm and the bind routes (`bindRepoRemoteIfUnsetTx`, `BindProjectPath`) do not judge it, so a path token can still reach an existing project that way; the backstop that always holds is import and export, which judge every row's remote as stored: a token-shaped remote, whether stored by those routes or by an older build, is refused on import and named and left out of export, and is not rewritten in place). `createdProject` is the one place that judges the record as it would be STORED (`ensureProjectLocked` normalizes an empty path to the id, because `projects.path` is UNIQUE and MCP callers pass `path=""`). The MCP refusal names the refused `project_id` through `assemble.Token`, which the store's does not: the store's message is also the export report's, where quoting the caller's text is noise and a hazard, so it says THIS SENTENCE does not repeat the value rather than claiming the value is never shown — one message serving two surfaces has to be true on both. Both guards are gated on that one question, including the credential one: a bound path carrying a token must not make a project unwritable by the address a session uses. The credential guard is asked a SECOND time on the resolution's own error path, because `ResolveProject`'s two ambiguity refusals interpolate the caller's argument (`"%q matches multiple projects"`, `"%q has tied path-prefix matches"`) and that sentence reaches the tool answer — so the rule is not "ask before the resolve" but "ask at every point where the argument can become an answer", which is the only form of it that survives the resolve being hoisted. The repository refusal needs no such treatment because it names a `NormalizeRepoRemote`d remote. Naming is bounded by what a value can survive, so on the CREATION path the credential guard is asked BEFORE the shape predicate (`memory.RejectSecret`, the one `rejectSecret` seam under an exported name) and a `*SecretContentError` is returned untouched: a path-shaped `project_id` carrying a token is an ordinary agent mistake, and appending the value to the one message that says Ghost never stores credentials would relocate the secret. Asking FIRST rather than branching on the returned error is what closes the gap, because the predicate judges SHAPE before credentials and a value that is both returns a shape error with the credential behind it. A SPACE stays allowed and any LENGTH is allowed at the STORE, because a project id is routinely a filesystem path; `TestProjectCreationStillAcceptsAPathShapedID` is what holds that against an over-eager fix. The refused class is a control character (Cc), a line separator (`unicode.Zl`/`unicode.Zp` — U+2028 and U+2029, which `unicode.IsControl` does not cover, #1363), a backtick or a «», so the boundary and `assemble.Label`'s escape test name the same line-ending class; before that, a project whose name or id held U+2028 was accepted by the boundary and rendered raw by the label, which is how the forged heading was reached. The MCP write boundary tightens that in two WRITE-ONLY ways — it refuses a whitespace-ONLY id and one over `maxProjectIDLen` (4096, POSIX `PATH_MAX`) — because a blank id opens a project nothing can name and an unbounded one is rendered uncapped by `ghost_list_projects`; the store predicate itself stays space-tolerant and bound-free ON PURPOSE (#844), since a refusal there CASCADES on restore. A project row the write boundary will no longer create can still be in a store — a pre-guard save, a `RestoreSnapshot`, a hand edit — and every renderer still has to make it safe, so those fixtures plant it in SQL
- A rendered label is bounded by the RENDERER, whatever writer produced the row (#821): `assemble.AgentLabel`, `SourceRefLabel` and `TagsLabel` each cut what they print and mark the cut, because the renderer is the one layer that cannot assume its input came from a writer that enforces a cap. `MaxRenderedTagLen` is 64, the number `mcpserver.tagMaxLen` cuts at, and the DIRECTION is the invariant: the print bound must never be TIGHTER than the write bound, or a tag the writer accepted whole and stored whole would print shortened — a display bound quietly becoming a data change. A looser print bound costs nothing, so only the direction is pinned, and it is pinned by a test (`mcpserver.TestTheRendererNeverShortensATagThisWriterAccepts`) that can see both numbers, since `tagMaxLen` is unexported in a package `assemble` imports. The cut is `clampBytes`, not a byte slice, because this bound handles exactly the untrusted non-ASCII text a writer would have refused and a cut through a multi-byte rune would put an invalid byte inside the JSON array; it happens BEFORE the marshal, so the marker sits inside the string like every other character of it, and each tag is bounded on its OWN — bounding the list as one value would have to drop or merge entries, and dropping a tag is worse than showing part of one. `boundTags` allocates only when it has to, because it runs on every row of every listing. The bound NEVER touches the stored value: it hands back a list to marshal, and the three writers that deliberately do not reach `validateTags` (`RestoreSnapshot` in SQL, `CreateFromCorpus` through `insertMemory`, `ReplaceNonManual` inheriting its source's union) keep their value whole
- **A STORED TASK OR DECISION IS BOUNDED BY THE CAPS ITS OWN WRITERS APPLY, AND THE PORTABLE IMPORTERS ARE WRITERS OF THOSE FIELDS TOO (#1226).** `ImportTask` and `ImportDecision` store a title through `TruncateUTF8(…, MaxTitleLen)` (300) and a description, notes, decision and rationale through `ClampContent` (`MaxContentLen`, 8000, plus the explicit marker), and a decision's `alternatives` through `memory.BoundAlternatives` — at most `MaxAlternatives` (20) entries, in artifact order, each cut at the title cap. Those are the numbers `ghost_task_create`, `ghost_task_complete` and `ghost_decision_record` apply, and they live in `internal/memory` beside `MaxContentLen` for the reason `MaxContentLen` lives there: the cap is one rule with several writers, and a second copy of the number in the importer is how an imported row stops being what a save of the same text stores. `mcpserver.maxTitleLen` is now `memory.MaxTitleLen` and the MCP tool's alternatives bound is the same `BoundAlternatives` call, so neither can drift. What this protects is the row-rendering read tools' shared premise: `capResponseParts` keeps the FIRST row whole rather than dropping it, which is sound only because a row is bounded by construction, and an artifact carrying 100 KB per field broke that premise about twentyfold — one `ghost_task_list` row came back at about 200 KB and one `ghost_decisions_list` row at megabytes (25 oversized alternatives), with no `limit` able to narrow either. The caps are in the IMPORTER and not in `CheckImportedTask` / `CheckImportedDecision` for two reasons that are one rule: a predicate judges what a build ACCEPTS and `ghost export` calls it, so putting a cut there would have the exporter cut stored rows on its way out — a second transformation in a predicate that exists to report refusals — while `ImportMemory` has always clamped its `content` after its own predicate for the same reason. And it is a CUT, never a refusal: a size refusal in those predicates costs the RECORD, and since the exporter applies them, it would drop the task or the decision AND its companion memory out of every artifact — the #844 cascade — on the day the operator needs the backup, and a restore must not refuse the stores it exists to restore. The cut is explicit in the stored row for the same reason a save's is: `ClampContent`'s marker is IN the value, and a title's cut appends nothing because the writer's does not. They sit ABOVE the `apply=false` early return, so a dry run previews the apply run exactly, and the credential guard still runs first on the UNCLAMPED text, so a token past the cap is refused rather than quietly cut out of sight. **AND THE REPORT NAMES EVERY FIELD A CAP CUT (#1232).** `ImportTask` and `ImportDecision` return the fields they cut, `RecordResult.Clamped` carries those names, and `printRecordLine` prints them as `…(truncated at the cap: title, notes)` — on a dry run's create as well as an apply's. The marker in a body value is not enough to say this, because a title's cut appends nothing and the alternatives past the twentieth are not in the row at all, so for those the stored record is silent and the report is the only place the loss can be named; and it has to be named BEFORE the write, since the reader of a preview is approving a run, not auditing a finished one. It is a LIST rather than a flag for the same reason every refusal in this command names a field and never its value: a report that says "something was truncated" sends the operator through the artifact looking, and one that quoted the text would carry the cut value into a terminal and a pasted log. The names are the artifact's own field names, in the record's own order, rather than the caps' — `title` is a field the reader can go and shorten, `MaxTitleLen` is this build's number — and they come from the calls that made the cut rather than from the cap constants applied a second time, which is what `memory.AlternativesClamped` is for: `BoundAlternatives` decides on it and the importer reports from it, so the list cannot name a cut which did not happen or miss one which did. A SKIP and a REJECTION name nothing, because neither wrote a row; `RecordResult.Clamped` is empty for both whatever the artifact carried. `tags` is untouched: a tag list is still carried byte for byte in both directions, whatever its length (#822). **AND THE DOWNGRADE NOTE AND THE CUT NOTE ARE INDEPENDENT, SO ONE LINE CARRIES BOTH (#1275).** `printRecordLine` builds the provenance note and the cap note as two separate steps rather than as arms of one mutually exclusive switch, because a memory can be both: an artifact record over `MaxContentLen` imported under the DEFAULT policy has its source, pin and tier rewritten AND its body cut, and before #1275 the downgrade arm won the switch so the cut arm never ran. That is the common restore path — the artifact that is not the user's own export is exactly the one that is always downgraded — so the body was shortened with no note at all. The order is fixed: the downgrade clause (with the tier it carries) first, then the `…(truncated at the cap: …)` clause. `TestTheImportRecordLineNamesACutField`'s combined case and `TestADefaultImportNamesBothTheRewriteAndTheCut` pin the two notes and their order, the second driving the real importer through a dry run. The `provenance kept as exported` clause is stated only when NEITHER rewrite happened: a downgrade or a cut has just said what became of the artifact's own value, and claiming it was kept beside either would contradict it.
- A decision's tags follow the memory rule, and a tag's SHAPE is the one place this codebase says **never refuse** (#822): a tag is refused at WRITE time by `mcpserver.validateTags` on the four tools that take a tag list — `ghost_memory_save`, `ghost_save_global`, `ghost_memory_update`, `ghost_decision_record` — and NEVER on export or import, for shape or for anything else. `CheckImportedMemory` says so in its own comment, and neither it nor `CheckImportedDecision` judges a tag's characters; the silence on SHAPE is the point rather than an omission: `ghost export` applies the importer's own predicates, so a tag shape refused at import drops the decision AND the companion memory `RecordDecision` wrote beside it out of every artifact, on the day the operator needs the backup, over a label the user cannot even see. Nothing refused such a tag for the whole life of the feature, so any store written through it can hold one and the tags are carried byte for byte in both directions. Safety is the renderer's (`TagsLabel`, and `internal/reflection`'s larger class in the consolidation prompt), and a legacy decision carrying `["«urgent»"]` round-trips whole. A CREDENTIAL-shaped tag is the one thing this never covered is not extended to, and that is the second half of the same rule rather than an exception to it: `CheckImportedMemory` and `CheckImportedDecision` each ask `rejectSecretList("tags", …)`, so a token in EITHER tags column is refused on import and, because the exporter calls the same predicate, named and left out of every artifact (#835). The distinction is the question being asked — a character is a fact about a RENDERING and the renderer already neutralises it, while a credential is a live value that the next export copies into a file about to be pasted somewhere — and it is why an ordinary tag list still round-trips byte for byte and a shape still never costs you the row
- Project lookup: canonical path-prefix match decided in RESOLVED form (`resolveByPath`): the caller's directory is resolved once per call with `canonicalPath`, every recorded path that `storedPathIsUsable` accepts is compared in its resolved form, and the deepest survivor BY RESOLVED LENGTH wins while equal resolved length is the ambiguity error. Nothing is decided by recorded text, so a project recorded through a symlink or a Windows 8.3 short name is found whichever spelling the session reports, a child recorded through a link nests under a parent recorded at the real spelling, and two projects recorded as two spellings of one directory tie instead of the longer text winning. The textual `pathCandidatesQuery` filter (`LENGTH(path) > 10`) is untouched because `savingRepository` mirrors it; neither the resolver nor bind uses it. Each recorded path's resolved form comes from a per-Store memo (`canonMemo`, keyed by the stored path string, so a database write cannot make an entry name the wrong project: only the filesystem can). Three rules bound it: every SURVIVING path is re-resolved fresh before it routes a caller, which guards only against a false inclusion — a stale entry can still EXCLUDE a deeper project (a child recorded as a symlink retargeted into the caller's directory with no ghost write), and the caller is then routed to the shallower one for up to `canonMemoTTL`, a bounded regression against resolving fresh every time; the write seam (`beginGuardedWrite`) clears the whole memo for every operation not listed in `opsThatLeaveProjectsAlone`, which only shortens that window in the writing process because a database write cannot make an entry wrong (`ensure-project` and `repo-project` are listed because they run on every save and only add a project, which has a new key); and entries expire after `canonMemoTTL` (30 s), an UNRESOLVABLE entry after `canonMemoRetry` (2 s), because a project is routinely recorded before its checkout exists. A caller directory that does not resolve ends the path step without reading the table. Cost with 200 projects (50 of them deleted), per path resolve: about 0.6 ms on a hit and 0.75 ms on a miss with a warm memo, against 0.45 ms and 0.35 ms before #1005 (and 8.5 ms on a miss for the first design, which re-resolved the caller once per row); about 4.5 ms with a cold memo, which every short-lived process (the SessionStart hook, the CLI) pays per resolve, and which is one `EvalSymlinks` per recorded path once per TTL or after a clearing write; stored rows are never rewritten. Bind and lookup apply ONE rule (#1016): `checkPathResolvable`, which `BindProjectPath` and `BindNewProjectToCheckout` run on the row just written, asks `storedPathIsUsable`, compares every recorded path in resolved form (`canonicalPath`, `samePath`), drops a remote conflict and ranks by resolved length exactly as `resolveByPath` does, so a path of ten characters or fewer is bindable because lookup finds it, and the textual `pathCandidatesQuery` is no longer part of bind. A second spelling of a directory another project records (a symlinked parent) is refused before the write by `checkBindPathConflicts`, which compares PHYSICAL paths and returns `ErrBindPathClaimed`, so lookup never reaches the ambiguity error through a bind; a resolved tie that some other writer created is refused by `checkPathResolvable` itself. A row another writer made (import, restore, `EnsureProject` with a path) is not judged by bind. A CHECKOUT IS A DIRECTORY, and the resolved string alone does not decide that: `canonicalPath` (`EvalSymlinks`) resolves a regular file exactly as it resolves a directory, so `BindNewProjectToCheckout` stats the resolved path and declines a non-directory before its transaction, the same refusal `ghost project bind` already makes at the CLI, because a file is not a location a session stands in and a project bound to one is bound to nothing. `TestBindRefusesASecondSpellingAndLookupStaysUnambiguous`, `TestBindAndLookupTreatAShortPathTheSame` and `TestCheckPathResolvableRefusesAResolvedTie` hold it) OR exact repository remote OR basename name fallback — a basename match needs exactly one surviving candidate, a recorded path containing the session's directory, and no remote conflict; ambiguous matches are errors, a name-shaped input skips location checks, and a project with no recorded location is reached by name only. A path-shaped MCP save detects the checkout's Git remote and may bind it to one uniquely named, unclaimed project, but a unique name is the weakest evidence available: a candidate that records a usable path binds only when that path contains the saving directory, so an unrelated clone named after the project cannot take over its remote. A refused binding is reported to the save caller — the store returns it beside the project the memory was written to, and the save result names what kept the name and where the memory went — because routing the save is right and losing the context of a same-named project silently is not. Only the recorded-checkout refusal is repairable and it takes `ghost project merge` then `ghost project bind`, in that order: the merge drops the repository with the row it came from and the bind is refused while the other project still records that directory, so a merge alone re-splits on the next save, and a bind alone loses the memory just saved. A path-prefix match routes a save from inside a checkout to the project that encloses it but may not identify the repository for it: a save identifies the enclosing project only when the saving directory IS its recorded root or reports the same remote, so a nested checkout — a submodule, a vendored repository — never lends its remote to the project around it, while an ordinary subdirectory of the same repository still does. The session's remote is derived at most once per resolve, and only by the steps that read it
- Global memories: `_global` project, included in every project's context
- Source provenance: `manual` marks direct user material; `builtin` marks Ghost-shipped pinned rules and is never presented as user-authored
- Credential guard: no write path stores a credential value. Stored content is embedded, replayed into every later session's context, mirrored to the Obsidian vault, and quoted into the next `reflect`/`resolve`/`supersede` prompt, so anything saved is already copied to a third-party model. `memory.rejectSecret` is the one seam (`internal/memory/secret_guard.go`), called before any statement by every path that writes caller-supplied text — `UpsertWithOptions` (so `Upsert`, `UpsertWithProvenance`, both save tools, the Claude import), `UpdateMemory`, `Create`/`CreateWithID`, `RecordDecision` (each `alternatives` entry and each `tags` entry too), the three task writers, `UpdateLearnedContext`, and the four portable importers (`ImportProject`'s name and path included — `repo_remote` is judged by `CheckImportedProject` with the name and path (the creating INSERT arms, import and export; not the refresh or bind routes): `NormalizeRepoRemote` strips the userinfo (on every spelling, including scheme-less `user@host/path` and `user:token@host/path`), the query string and the fragment before a write, and that predicate is the backstop for any token position, the URL path included. A remote an older build stored is not rewritten in place; it is refused on import and, because export asks the same predicate, named and left out of the artifact) — and returns `*SecretContentError`/`ErrSecretContent` naming the field and the format, never the value. The importers place the guard in a narrow window: after the id-presence check, so a record already in the store stays a skip and the portable format's re-run-is-always-safe promise holds; before the `apply=false` early return, so a dry run classifies a record exactly as the apply run it previews would. `internal/secret` decides by value SHAPE, never by the words around it: a memory store's ordinary vocabulary is "rotate the password quarterly" and "the access token lives in the runner env", and a keyword heuristic refuses both, which is why `internal/reflection`'s `looksLikeSecret` (a scope decision — is this safe to replay into every project — never a save decision) stays separate. Its rules whose verdict needs the matched text walk every candidate, not the leftmost one, or a `${DB_PASSWORD}` placeholder early in the save shadows a real credential later in it. An assigned credential needs BOTH a key that names a secret (split on `_ - .` and camelCase, so `existingSecret`, `token_url` and `token_sha` are references, not fields) AND a value that is not a reference: no dotted identifier path (every credential encoding excludes `.`, so `opts.OAuthClientSecretFromVault` names a place), no template, no code expression, no single-case word segment, and a length/entropy bar AND a value that is credential material (not a template, not a URL, no single-case word segment, and a length/entropy bar) — a Helm chart, a Grafana provisioning file and a CI workflow are mostly about credentials and record the NAME of one, never the value. Reflection drops rather than refuses, and the drop sits at the WRITE BOUNDARY (`applyReflection`), not in the tier: `ApplyReflection` replaces a corpus in one transaction, so a refusal would roll back the whole round. It must also be after the drop guard's audit — the audit asks which inputs the output failed to account for, and executeOps emits every unclaimed input verbatim, so a memory the tier carried forward is byte-identical to the output that carried it; filtering earlier makes that input look unaccounted for, and it is then either re-added verbatim (the drop is a no-op, after the audit printed its content to stderr) or silently deleted by `ReplaceNonManual` with no `--allow-drops`. At the boundary it is still accounted for, so `RetainGuardedDrops` does not re-add it and the drop is not a no-op; it IS deleted from the store, because `ReplaceNonManual` removes every replaceable row the emitted set no longer accounts for — and the report describes the mechanism without claiming a count, only when a project replace actually ran, since a drop that empties the set writes nothing — in which case the round reports `Applied: nothing`, names the surviving row, and records NO skip fingerprint, so `--skip-unchanged` does not skip the project forever. `applyReflection` returns `applied` for that distinction, and `dropCredentialProposals` returns fresh slices and `applyReflection` returns those post-drop slices, because the caller lists and counts them. `previewContent` in `internal/reflection` withholds a credential from the three tier log lines, which run before `cmd/ghost` sees anything. `--allow-drops` does not gate it — that flag authorises what the MODEL dropped. Every print site in `ghost reflect` goes through `displayProposal`, so a proposal or a guarded drop that holds a credential is shown as `<withheld: format, category, bytes>` rather than as 120 characters of it. A MEMORY'S SCOPE IS GUARDED AND BOUNDED ON EVERY WRITER THAT STORES IT (#1042): a scope key and a scope value are caller text printed on every listing outside the data delimiters, and were neither guarded nor bounded until then (a token saved as `scope{token=…}` was replayed into model context, and a 200 KB value was echoed in full). `memory.checkScope` (`internal/memory/scope_guard.go`) is the one function: it refuses more than `MaxScopeKeys` (16) keys, a key over `MaxScopeKeyLen` (64 bytes, a tag's cap) and a value over `MaxScopeValueLen` (128 bytes, an agent's cap) — refused, never clamped, because a shortened key is a different key; a blank or whitespace-only key or value, a NUL byte, invalid UTF-8, a control character, or a line separator (U+2028, U+2029, U+0085) — refused with the field named by position and never the offending text; then runs `rejectSecret` over every key (`scope_key[i]`, by sorted position), and every `key=value` pair (`scope[<key>]`, the key rendered through `SafeToken`) — the pair is a superset of the value alone, so a token under any key and an assigned credential whose halves are each harmless are both caught. The bounds and the key character rules run first and quote nothing, so an over-long token is refused for its length and never printed; the token guard then screens every key before a value character rule names one. A row stored BEFORE the character rules with such a scope (a blank, NUL, control or line-break value or key) is not rewritten: an export names it as left out with `SkippedRecord.ScopeChar` set, and the report advises deleting and re-saving it with a clean scope, not re-keying it; the renderers still neutralise such a scope on read, which `TestSearchExplainRendersStoredTextLikeTheAnswer` proves with a directly inserted row. It is called by `checkMemoryWrite` (`Create`, `CreateWithID`), `UpsertWithOptions` (both save tools; this is also the only path that rewrites an existing row with a caller's scope, through a duplicate save, and a refused scope leaves that row byte-identical), and `CheckImportedMemory` (so `ImportMemory`, its dry run, and `ghost export`, which skips and names a stored row the importer would refuse — including, for the bounds, a row stored before they existed, so such a row is left out of every artifact until its scope is fixed, as an over-long `source_ref` already is (this differs from a tag's SHAPE, which is carried byte for byte per #811, because a scope's size is a fact about the record, not a rendering) — `secretUnfixableRoutes` says `ghost_memory_update` takes no scope). No update tool takes a scope, so `UpdateMemoryWithOptions` has nothing to guard. Deliberately not reached, like the source_ref and agent caps: `RestoreSnapshot`, `CreateFromCorpus` and `ReplaceNonManual` (reflection emits no scope; `inheritedScope` resolves the scope a rewrite or merge inherits from its source rows — a single-source rewrite keeps the source's scope, a merge keeps the scope when all scoped sources agree, and a merge whose sources disagree, or mix scoped and unscoped rows, is refused with its sources kept and the refusal counted as a dropped emission — in `EmissionDrops.Scope`, never `Changed`, and with the kept sources left out of `preserved`, because no row moved and every later run refuses the same merge, so the round still records its skip fingerprint (#1492); the shared rule is `scopeFold`, which `inheritedScope` reads row by row and `FoldSourceScopes` exposes to the reflect accounting. A row any refusal (scope or overflow) keeps live without its having moved is still claimable by a VERBATIM re-emission — no `ReplacesIDs`, no stated scope, byte-identical text, the drop guard's re-add — which lands on it as a keep that writes nothing, rather than being refused as a moved claim or INSERTed as a fresh copy that, with no scope of its own, would apply in every environment. An emission that restates an input row verbatim — a keep, a pass-through, a grounding-refusal re-emit, the drop guard's re-add — names that row in `reflection.ReflectMemory.RestatesID` (set only by `executeOps`' `verbatimMemory` and `RetainGuardedDrops`, never read from the model's answer), and `cmd/ghost`'s `reflectMemoriesToMemory` gives it NO `ReplacesIDs` from the TEXT join: an unnamed row whose content equals a merge's text otherwise inherited the merge's sources on its own keep, `verbatimOfKept` refused it for naming sources, and `fedGuard.dropReasonFor` counted it as a claim on a row that moved, so a merge refused for overflow or scope printed the concurrent-edit line and withheld the skip fingerprint although no row changed. The join stays text-keyed for merge and rewrite survivors, so a real concurrent edit — of a merge source or of the twin itself — is still the corpus guard's drop. What a restatement DOES take is the ids of the drops that named IT as their successor, and those are keyed on the successor's ID (`replacedIDsFor`), so a kept successor claims the row it replaces: the dropped row is deleted into it rather than left as a row nothing accounted for, its delete history names it, and the decision links the dropped row carried reach a live row instead of dying with it (see "A supersession's successor claims the dropped row"). A read or decode failure is NOT a conflict and fails the replace, the way `inheritedClaims` and `inheritedRetention` propagate theirs. `ReplaceNonManual`'s fresh insert writes the resolved scope; its reuse branch updates a row in place and writes scope through COALESCE, so it is held to the same rule against the REUSED row's own scope — chosen by content, which need not be one of the emission's sources — and a disagreement refuses the emission too. A single-source reuse keeps the reused row's scope. ApplyReflection's promotion half resolves the same scope from the source project's rows BEFORE the project replace deletes them — a row merged into a cross-project candidate is deleted by that replace — passes it to the `_global` upsert, and refuses a disagreeing candidate outright — it is counted as a dropped cross-project emission and its sources are held through the project replace, rather than written into the project unscoped (which would widen the scoped sources one level in) or promoted (which would widen them across every project)). `assemble.ScopeLabel` is the display bound for exactly those and for rows written before the cap: at most `MaxScopeKeys` keys printed (the rest as `…[N more scope keys]`), each key and value cut on a rune to `MaxScopeKeyLen`/`MaxScopeValueLen` BEFORE it is quoted and marked `…[scope truncated]`, using the writer's own constants so the label is never tighter than what a writer accepted whole. A DECISION'S TAGS FOLLOW THE MEMORY RULE ON EVERY PATH, which took a gap to reach (#835) and the gap is the instructive part: `RecordDecision` refused a credential-shaped tag from #656 and `CheckImportedDecision` did not, because a decision's tags are judged in a SHARED PREDICATE rather than in a writer's body — and a guard inside a shared function has no call site at the writer to grep for, so `secret_guard.go` counted the two portable importers as one and claimed "all five" tag-bearing writers while the decision route was unguarded. The reach is now a SET of six, counted from the predicates by `credentialFields()` rather than asserted: Create, `UpsertWithOptions`, `UpdateMemory`, `RecordDecision`, `ImportMemory` and `ImportDecision`, and `TestEveryTagBearingWriterIsGuarded` is the table. The four clauses, which a memory's tags and a decision's tags now satisfy identically: at WRITE, `rejectSecretList("tags", …)` refuses with the field named and the value withheld; at IMPORT, `CheckImportedMemory`/`CheckImportedDecision` ask it inside the narrow window above, so a DRY RUN classifies the record as the apply run will; at EXPORT, `portable.Export` calls the same predicate and prints a `!` line naming the field with `SkippedRecord.Secret` set, so the decision is named rather than silently absent from every artifact; and an ordinary tag list round-trips BYTE FOR BYTE, because the guard refuses a credential SHAPE and not a tag. What is deliberately still NOT checked on either predicate is a tag's SHAPE (#822): a character is a fact about a RENDERING, and a shape refusal at import would put an otherwise-valid decision outside every backup for a label the user cannot even see. So `tags` is the one column in the export report's advice whose fix depends on the RECORD KIND — `ghost_memory_update` edits a memory's tags and cannot reach a decisions row — and `secretUnfixableRoutes` names the field on the decision sentence for that reason.
- The same rule on the way OUT, and it is a RENDERER rather than a second check (#839): `memory.RefusalArg(field, value)` (`memory.ProjectArg` plus a bound, below) is how a caller-supplied project argument enters a refusal sentence, and it returns exactly `%q` for a value the guard does not recognise — so every ordinary refusal stays byte-identical to the one it replaces, which is the whole diagnostic (`project "foo" not found` is how an agent finds its own typo) — and a placeholder naming the argument and the credential's FORMAT, and no part of the value, for one it does. It is a RENDERER and not a coverage claim: a new refusal that quotes a project argument has to route through `RefusalArg`, and a package that interpolates the operand with `%q` is a hole in this rule, not an exception to it (`cmd/ghost`'s `parseLifecycleArgs` refusals for a second positional argument and an unknown flag were that until #1122; `ghost context`'s `--since`, `--as-of`, unknown-flag and stray-positional refusals were too until #1119, and the stdlib parse errors that quote their input are not wrapped there either; `internal/mcpinit`'s two lifecycle refusals were exactly that until #844's third round, and the renderer's own docstring had started claiming a surface no call site used; and `cmd/ghost`'s `runProjectMergeCore` echoed the STORE's resolved ids until #1343 — a bare `%q` in the self-merge refusal, which is byte-identical for an ordinary id because `RefusalArg` returns exactly `%q` for one the token guard does not recognise, and a bare `%s` in the `Merging …` line, whose ids are now quoted by that same renderer — because a resolved id is not the command's operand and the bound-free import predicate accepts an artifact's project id verbatim, so neither is safe to echo — the package's structural guard now keys on a format that names a project as well as on `was given twice`, so the next one fails a build rather than reaching a reader). It lives beside `rejectSecret` because the store and the handlers owe the same sentence: `ResolveProject`'s refusals are handed back by nineteen handlers as `resolve project: %w`, so a rendering per call site would be nineteen chances to get one wrong, and a rendering that is a CHECK would be a second opinion to disagree with the first. The label is the argument's own name where the boundary can see it (`project_id` for most tools, `project` for `ghost_resolve`, and the CLI's own `<project>` operand — a refusal naming a field the reader never typed is the failure) and `memory.ProjectIdentifierLabel` where the STORE cannot see it. Two different sentences get the neutral label, for two different reasons, and neither is a special case of anything: the AMBIGUITY refusals are the store's on EVERY surface, because every handler hands `ResolveProject`'s error back verbatim, so `ghost_resolve` is held to the neutral label for its ambiguity refusal even though its own not-found sentence says `project`; and `ghost_project_delete` is held to it for its not-found refusal because that sentence is the store's — the tool hands `args.Project` to `Store.DeleteProject` verbatim there, equally reached by the CLI's `ghost project delete <name-or-id>` — with the tool name in the wrapping `ghost_project_delete: ` error telling the agent which argument to change. The tool's ONE sentence of its own, the #1048 apply refusal, is a boundary sentence and renders the value through `RefusalArg` as `project`, which is the name of the argument its caller actually passes. Knowing the field is necessary but not sufficient to be allowed to use it, and `mcpserver`'s surface sweep holds both cases explicitly: `storeAmbiguityLabel` for the ambiguity shapes, and a `storeRefusal` flag for the one unknown-project sentence the store builds. Rendering is not withholding: `assemble.Label` neutralises characters for a line, it does not keep secrets out of one, so the bind refusal's directory is named through it and the bind refusal's CREDENTIAL is not named at all. `ghost_memory_save` is the exception that proves the rule — it already answers with the guard's own refusal rather than a rendered one, because there the credential IS the finding and there is no ambiguity to explain. `ProjectArg` itself is the whole-value renderer and stays unbounded, and is used only where the value must come back whole, because the prompts and the not-registered answers must name a project id whole (record_decision tells the agent to pass it back to a tool) and the `record_decision` prompt's `topic` must name the decision's subject whole (#1712). A surface whose answer is bounded BYTES bounds the value ITSELF rather than through this renderer, and asks for that bound where the answer is built: the project-context tool renders its not-registered answer inside the response budget, so it echoes a long project id through its own 512-byte rule and a longer one comes back withheld (the `internal/mcpserver/` rule), while `ghost_memories_list` still echoes an unregistered id whole through `ProjectArg`. A REFUSAL uses `memory.RefusalArg`, which adds a bound: a value longer than `memory.ArgEchoMaxBytes` (256) is withheld with its length, after the guard has judged the whole value, so a 200 KB argument is never repeated. Every MCP argument refusal follows it (token guard, then bound): a string value goes through `RefusalArg`, a value that is not the expected type is named by its Go type and, inside an array, by its position, never by its contents (a map or array prints its contents under `%v`). A memory id is rendered the same way on the refusals that fire before the id is known to exist, and on the ownership refusal that names a row the store holds under ANOTHER project: there the id is still the caller's OPERAND, and because `ghost import` writes an artifact's ids verbatim a stored one can be token-shaped or oversized, so echoing it raw is a hole in this rule and not an exception to it. An id the caller has successfully addressed in its own project, and a category or retention tier the store has already validated, are not caller text and are left as they were. THE STORE'S OWN REFUSALS THAT NAME AN ID FOLLOW THE SAME RULE, because a project, memory or task id can be a caller's operand OR an id `ghost import` wrote verbatim, and a store-level sentence cannot tell which it was handed: every `project %q …` refusal in `internal/memory` (`belongs to a different repository`, `repository changed concurrently`, `was already deleted`, `GetProjectPath`'s `not found`, the `_global` merge guards, the bind's not-found and different-repository refusals, `projectRef`'s pointer to the other project, `checkPathResolvable`'s longer-path pointer, and `reconcileMergeRepoTx`'s two ids in the `repository … is already recorded on project …` sentence) all render the id through `RefusalArg(memory.ProjectIdentifierLabel, …)`, which is the neutral label because the store cannot see a tool argument or CLI operand's name; a task id in `resolveTaskID`'s ambiguity refusal and in the two `was deleted before the write landed` sentences through `RefusalArg("task_id", …)`; and a memory id in a link's self-link refusal, the evidence writers' wrapped errors (`append`/`carry`/`import evidence for …`), the fold's `fold target … disappeared` and the restore/history/compact wrapped reads through `RefusalArg("memory_id", …)`. The importer's wrapped failures are the same class, and they are the ones an artifact drives: `import project %s`, `import memory %s`, `import task %s` and `import decision %s` render the record's id through `RefusalArg(memory.ProjectIdentifierLabel, …)` or `RefusalArg("memory_id"/"task_id"/"decision_id", …)` before the `%w`, so a token-shaped or oversized id the shape check let through cannot ride out in the failure it caused. A stored id is NOT exempt for being stored; that is the reading #1291 gave the memory ownership refusal, and this is the rest of the set. Where a sentence interpolated the id with `%s` (`GetProjectPath`'s `not found`, the evidence and importer prefixes), an ordinary id now reads quoted, which is the renderer's one visible change to those sentences; every sentence that already used `%q` is byte-identical for a value the guard does not recognise. The same rule holds on the CLI's lifecycle coordinator (#1087): `runLifecycle`'s lock refusal, `supersedeConsensusClampNotice` and `runAuditPhase`'s missing-project skip render their project operand through `RefusalArg`, and `phaseFailureCause`'s silent-death fallback renders the value after `--project` through `ProjectArg`. Those surfaces are the reason it matters: stderr on the detached path is `lifecycle.log`, and the marker's recorded argv is part of a durable data-directory file that persists for up to 30 days and is printed at every later session start. An ordinary operand is still named — quoted, like every other project-argument sentence — so the log line keeps saying which phase and scope ran. The rule also reaches `ghost history purge`'s refusals (#1344), and for the same reason a stored id is not exempt: `purgePrefixRefusal` renders the caller's prefix AND the stored id it names through `RefusalArg` — the id in both places it appears, the "memory it names" clause and the `ghost history purge …` next command — `parseHistoryArgs` renders the stray second operand the same way, and the one shared "nothing to purge" sentence renders the id it reports the miss for, whether that is the caller's operand or the stored spelling `purgeHistoryMemory` re-spells the argument to. `memory.RefusalArg` is `%q` for an ordinary id, so the full id the prefix names still prints; the two sentences that interpolated it with `%s` now name it quoted, the renderer's one visible change.
- The session-start alert RENDERS the marker's project too, because it is the same class of caller text on the same first-lines-every-session surface (#1088): `lifecycleFailureAlert` interpolated the marker's `project` with `%s`, and `WriteLifecycleFailure` stores the raw `--project` operand in the marker's own `project` field whenever the store cannot resolve it (the recorded argv is a separate copy, rendered on the write side since #1112), so `ghost lifecycle --project $'evil\n## x'` put a forged heading ABOVE the block's own «...» explainer. `renderMarkerProject` (`internal/mcpinit/lifecyclemarker.go`) is `assemble.Label` for a value the guard does not recognise — folded to one line, byte-identical for an ordinary project under the cap below, the rule the block's own project name already follows (#791) — and `memory.ProjectArg` for a credential-shaped one, which withholds it behind the placeholder naming the field and the credential's FORMAT. The guard is asked FIRST, so a value that is both hostile and credential-shaped is withheld rather than escaped: Label neutralises characters for a line and does not keep a secret out of one, which is the bind refusal's own line. The rendered value is capped beside the error line it shares that surface with (`lifecycleMarkerProjectMaxBytes`), for the error line's own reason: it is caller text printed twice on the same line at every session start, and nothing upstream bounds the raw operand. The cap is applied AFTER the branch, so a credential's placeholder is never cut. The retry command on that same line carries the capped rendering too, so an operand past the cap is suggested to the reader in its cut form — the same tradeoff the capped error line beside it makes, and the reason the cap sits high enough that no real project name reaches it.
- The marker's ERROR is untrusted text too, and the alert treats it so (#1167). It is the failed phase's output tail (`phaseTail`, the last 1200 bytes, cut from the head), so once the output runs past that the first line is the first WHOLE line after the cut of whatever the harness, the model or `runReflect` printed last — not the first line the phase printed. `phaseTail.String` drops the partial line the head cut leaves, and returns `[earlier output trimmed]` when nothing whole follows it, because that fragment can be a token without its prefix, which the guard cannot recognise (`TestPhaseTailDropsAPartialFirstLine`, `TestPhaseTailOfOnePartialLineIsAMarker`, `TestPhaseTailKeepsAWholeFirstLine`). `WriteLifecycleFailure` asks the token guard of the whole tail before its own 1500-byte storage cap, so a value that cap would split is judged whole, and stores `<error withheld: it holds a FORMAT>` on a match (`markerErrorField`); `renderMarkerError` asks the guard again, of the whole stored error rather than only the line shown, because a marker written by an older build holds the raw value, then picks ONE line (`markerExplainingLine`), caps it and frames it with `quoteData`, so instruction-like output is rendered as «...» data, not as Ghost's own words. The cap applies before the frame, so the closing delimiter is never cut. The line picked is the first one opening with `error: `, the report every reflect, resolve and supersede fatal path prints before `os.Exit(1)`, because a phase's first line is its banner (`Project: ...`) or a log record (`resolve prefilter`); it is found by prefix and not by being last, since the tail is fed by two pipes and a stdout line can land after the report, and the coordinator's own `lifecycle: X failed ... (continuing)` line never enters the tail at all. A stored error with no such line — a Go panic, a group SIGKILL after the grace period, the no-output fallback — is shown by its first line. An interruption is recognised only in the two shapes the coordinator writes, matched as the whole sentence (`mcpinit.InterruptedBySignal`, then ` while running ` or ` before ` and one phase name, to the end of the line): the whole first line (`interruptOutcome`) or the end of a line after `; ` (`applyInterrupt`, which need not be the report's line), so phase output that only quotes the words is not read as one. It is put first, so the cap cuts the report rather than it, and because that rebuilt line can put words side by side that were apart in the file, the guard is asked of the shown line too. The selection is the renderer's, so a marker an older build wrote is rendered the same way; the file keeps the whole tail. (`TestATokenInAFailedPhasesOutputNeverReachesTheMarkerOrTheAlert`, `TestAMarkersTokenShapedErrorIsWithheldAtRender`, `TestTheMarkersErrorIsRenderedAsFramedData`, `TestTheAlertQuotesTheLineThatExplainsAPhaseFailure`, `TestAnInterruptAfterAnEarlierFailureIsInTheAlert`, `TestALongFailureLineDoesNotPushTheInterruptPastTheBound`, `TestAnInterruptOnlyMarkerStillLeadsWithTheInterrupt`, `TestAQuotedInterruptPhraseIsNotAnInterrupt`, `TestTheShownLineIsAskedOfTheTokenGuard`). The failure marker's stored error has absolute paths replaced with `<path>` at write and again at render, so the alert never sees a filesystem path regardless of when the marker was written.
- The marker's failed-phase LIST is the third raw field on that same alert line, and it is rendered as a token for the same reason (#1382). `lifecycleFailureAlert` joined `m.PhasesFailed` with `strings.Join(m.PhasesFailed, ", ")` interpolated straight into the format string, while the project (`renderMarkerProject`) and the error (`renderMarkerError`) sharing the line were already neutralised, so a corrupt, hand-edited or older-build marker whose entry held a line break forged a line — a second `## Ghost context:` heading or a fake `Use project_id:` instruction — as Ghost's own prose outside the «...» framing. `renderMarkerPhases` renders EACH entry through `assemble.Token` before the join, so a line break prints as the Go escape `\n` and cannot start a line, while an ordinary phase name is byte-identical (`TestSessionStart_MarkerPhasesCannotForgeALine`, `TestSessionStart_OrdinaryPhaseListRendersUnchanged`).

- Four deliberate exclusions from this guard: `CreateFromCorpus`, `RestoreSnapshot`, `SeedGlobalMemories` and `ReplaceNonManual` are outside both it and `MaxContentLen`. `CreateFromCorpus` exists because a public benchmark corpus contains real leaked credentials (measured: three in longmemeval-s) and a retrieval benchmark cannot refuse to ingest them; it is off `provider.MemoryStore`, off the MCP surface, and reachable only from the three `bench/` dataset seeders. The first two write byte-exact data into a restored store, and dropping a row from a restore is worse than the leak. `ReplaceNonManual` is excluded because filtering there DELETES the stored row — an unattended nightly reflect would silently remove a credential from a pre-guard database with no report — and because `dropCredentialProposals` in `cmd/ghost/promotion.go` already drops a credential-shaped proposal at the write boundary, after the drop guard's audit — where the stored row goes with it, since the row is the value. Detecting what is ALREADY in a database is still a separate report-first job, not a side effect of consolidation. A new caller-supplied text writer MUST call `rejectSecret` first
- Reflection promotion: cross-project candidates stay project-scoped by default; `ghost reflect --promote-globals` opts into `_global`, and the project replacement plus global writes share one transaction. A promoted merge is the consolidation successor of the project rows the replace CONSUMES and inherits from them by the project path's own rule, not a copy of it (#1491): `ReplaceNonManual` runs the promotions (`applyView.promotions`) after its delete set, `keptLive` and `consumed` are fixed and before its delete, so the tier is `foldedRetention`, the provenance stamp, carried evidence and claims are `absorbSourcesTx` (shared with the reuse branch; a kept-live source gives its decision links alone), and each consumed source's delete row names the promoted row through the replace's own `successorOf`. Promoted after the replace, the row found its sources gone and inherited nothing. A global-only apply runs no replace and consumes nothing, so its promoted row inherits nothing — no link, claim, record, tier or successor — and a rerun carries nothing twice, because a consumed source is gone and a kept-live one gives no records. A row the replace could not touch (`replaceableRowSQL`: manual, builtin, pinned, resolved or retention-exempt) takes none of the sources' claims, links, records or provenance stamp, so a supersede cannot retire a row the user made or protected. A promotion that consumes a source carrying a decision link is therefore never folded into such a row: `UpsertOptions.foldReplaceableOnly` adds `replaceableRowSQL` to both duplicate lookups, before their window is cut, so the promotion folds into the best replaceable match (the carrier an earlier round inserted, which keeps a second round from adding a third row) and is inserted as a fresh `_global` row beside the protected one only when there is none; either row inherits by `absorbSourcesTx` and is named by the sources' delete rows, as a project emission is inserted beside a manual row. Every other promotion keeps the plain FoldOnly dedup and may still fold into such a row and strengthen it (importance, an observation, and a tier that only rises), and the consumed sources' evidence is then deleted with them, not carried. A promoted merge whose decision links overflow a source_ref is refused before the replace and its claims are kept, counted as the global half's `EmissionDrops.Overflow` and recorded in `applyView.overflowKept` so the replace does not report them as survivors (#1228). An overflow found only at the write, because the replaceable fold target's own links leave no room for the sources' (`promotionOverflowError`), is refused the same way inside the replace: the sources it would have consumed, and any row carrying its text, leave the delete set (a row an earlier emission already took keeps that successor), the drop is counted through `applyView.promotionDropped`, and nothing is written back into the project, so every decision link stays on a live row a supersede retires. A reflect of `_global` itself takes the replace path for its candidates, because a fold inside the replace over `_global` could land on a row that replace deletes. A failed promotion is written back into the project after the replace returns
- Reflection drop guard: every one of the eight categories is audited for a surviving merge target, not just the operational ones — an input under 45% token containment in the output it is compared against is re-added verbatim (`internal/reflection/drops.go`, `AuditGuardedDrops` + `RetainGuardedDrops`), and `ghost reflect --allow-drops` accepts the deletions instead. A source the guard re-adds is reused in place and stays live as its own row — as is any other candidate the pass names and keeps, a row kept as concurrent or by a refused emission — so the merge that named it takes none of its evidence, claims, retention or delete-history successor pointer (`sourcesNotKeptLive` in `ReplaceNonManual`): claims follow evidence, and a `verified_at`, confidence or window copied from a row whose records stayed behind would stand on the merge with no record of it. Its tier is read only when the emission folds no source at all (`foldedRetention`): a tier is backed by no record, and a rewrite of a session row whose only source stays live would otherwise default to a project row nothing expires. The one thing it STILL inherits is the source's `decision:<id>` links (a manual, pinned or retention-exempt source is never a candidate and is outside the split), through `boundedDecisionRefs`, because the store cannot tell whether the merge text still restates the decision — so both rows carry the link and the supersede reports them in `Ambiguous` rather than retiring one and answering clean. It is not the `manual` source that covers this: seeds are written `builtin`, agent saves `mcp` and reflection writes `reflection`, so nothing an agent writes is ever excluded from consolidation by that filter. With the per-id pass-through the guard no longer rescues omissions, and what it still covers is narrow: a MERGE's sources (`ReflectionResult.Merges`, carrying the ids it folded in AND the text it produced, scored against the text of THAT merge), an explicit `obsolete` drop, and the SQLite tier's absorbed duplicates (it names no ids, so all of its inputs are measured the strict way). A merge source is scored against its own merge and not against the union of the outputs, which #659 chose when the union was a handful of survivors and which the pass-through made into the whole project's vocabulary: every id the response never named is emitted verbatim, so a source whose substance its merge discarded passed containment on the strength of an unrelated memory sharing its words — the same class of loss the pass-through exists to remove, reintroduced through the merge branch (#549). The merge's own text is the whole witness available, because a merge source is consumed by its merge (its own text is never in the result) and the parser rejects an id claimed twice (so a sibling cannot be there either); scoring against the siblings could only LOWER the bar. A merge that is not in the result has no witness and its sources fall back to the strict per-output test. THERE IS NO EXEMPTION. Not for a rewrite and not for a supersession: **an unattended reflect never deletes a memory on the model's say-so alone.** A `rewrite <id> -> <text>` or a `drop <id> reason: superseded by <id>` is audited exactly like an `obsolete` drop — the corpus has to be able to SHOW the row is gone. `ReflectionResult.Replacements` still RECORDS the ids a response disposed of and the text it says took their place, because that is the only place the response's own account of a disposal is visible to a reader of a result, but nothing acts on it. The carve-out this replaced was the hole: a supersession disposed of a row whenever the named successor's text was in the result, and the parser's only rule is that the target survives the response — so naming a neighbour was a valid operation and two ordinary deployment notes in the same project, paired because they sat adjacent in the prompt, was a silent deletion with no warning, no `--allow-drops` and a zero exit status. Requiring the witness to be RELATED closed that, and then turned out to be redundant: the witness text is itself an output, so a row at 45% containment against it also passes the per-output scan. The whole branch was dead code. The rule is KEEP-biased on purpose, and the reason is repairability: a stale row that is KEPT can be demoted, because the lifecycle runs `resolve` and `supersede` immediately after `reflect` and demoting a genuinely stale row is their job, while a deleted row is gone. So the failure direction is a duplicate — the old text back beside its replacement — never a silent deletion. The cost, named: the "three issues are still open" class comes back as a kept stale row whenever the successor is reworded below the containment bar (measured: this repo's own superseded note about the Anthropic API client scores 0.429 against its replacement), and stays visible until resolve or supersede demote it, so a corpus of those grows the input each pass has to read. That is the right way round for a command that rewrites a memory store with no human in the loop. Because a re-added verbatim memory reaches `ReplaceNonManual` through the exact-content reuse path, retention must not be treated as consolidation: `reusePreservesAge` (`internal/memory/store.go`) keeps the stored `created_at` and `source` when the re-emission matches the row's content and category — for every verbatim re-emission, not only retention: a `keep`, a pass-through and the SQLite tier's unmerged (always verbatim) output keep their age too, so decaying categories now age out of injection instead of being refreshed by each applied reflect — and omitting a stale memory cannot restart its decay or relabel its source, while a genuine rewrite (new text, or the same text in another category) still gets `created_at = now` and `source = 'reflection'`. That decision is only as good as the row it is made against, so the pick is deliberate too: `takeReusableRow` prefers the candidate whose category matches the emission (a project can hold one text under several categories, since Upsert keeps the incoming category on a linked copy) and falls back to the first, and the candidate query is `ORDER BY created_at, id` so a duplicate keeps the oldest age deterministically rather than whichever row the planner happened to return
- Reflection operation contract: the LLM tier returns `{"learned_context", "ops"}` where each op is `keep <id>` / `merge <id>,<id> -> <text>` / `rewrite <id> -> <text>` / `drop <id> reason: obsolete|superseded by <id>` on the ids the prompt renders (`internal/reflection/ops.go`), because the retired free-text contract had no vocabulary for "this row is fine": only a byte-identical re-emission kept an identity, so retyping — including retyping to improve — reset the id, and with it the embedding, the link graph (both ON DELETE CASCADE) and the age (measured: 12 of 13 new ids in one project, 15 of 17 in another). A memory leaves the corpus only when its id is named as a merge source, named for a rewrite, or named in a drop with a reason — AND a surviving output accounts for it (the drop guard's 45% token containment, with a merge source measured against its OWN merge) or `--allow-drops` accepts the deletion. Naming an id is NECESSARY BUT NOT SUFFICIENT: a rewrite whose replacement does not carry the old row's substance leaves that row in the corpus verbatim, so a rewrite is not a free change of wording on the unattended path (#549). EVERY OTHER INPUT IS CARRIED THROUGH VERBATIM by `executeOps` — the pass-through that makes the tier zero-loss, and the reason the token guard is not load-bearing for omissions: an unnamed memory was emitted by nobody, so it survived only if the guard FAILED to recognise a survivor, which made a false positive in the "absorbed" direction a silent deletion with no warning and no `--allow-drops` (measured: "SSH to the bastion goes through port 2222, not 22" gone, because an unrelated survivor shared 45% of its tokens). A guard can only re-add what it flags, so an id the harness never mentioned needs no inference at all. A GROUNDING REFUSAL NAMES ITS IDS THROUGH THE TOKEN GUARD: the WARN `executeOps` writes when a merge or rewrite introduces an identifier absent from its sources renders every id it names (`refusalIDs`) through `memory.RefusalArg("memory_id", …)`, because a stored id is not token-guarded — `ghost import` writes an artifact's id verbatim, so an imported one can be token-shaped — and that line is a WARN, emitted without a debug switch, so on the detached lifecycle path it lands in `lifecycle.log`. Pinned by `TestGroundingRefusalWithholdsTokenShapedStoredIDs`. `keep` and the pass-through emit the stored row's own content, category, importance and tags so the exact-content reuse updates it in place; a merge or rewrite derives its category from the first id, its importance from the strongest source, its tags from the union, and its scope from `inferGlobalScope` — the model states none of them, and an unattended model choosing `global` moves knowledge into every project's injected context. An operation id may echo the prompt's own `id:` label, which is stripped rather than failing the pass. Everything else is strict, because a partially-understood operation list is a corpus rewritten as if the model had said something else: an unreadable line, an id Ghost did not feed the model (compared case-insensitively, never by prefix), an id claimed twice, a supersession whose target the same response does not carry forward, or a response with no `ops` array at all each fail the whole response so the tiered consolidator falls through to SQLite. A rejection costs ONE extra call, not the pass: the LLM tier re-reads the same prompt once with the reader's own complaint attached, and the second answer is read under the same strict rules — a re-read, not a repair, so there is still no fuzzy id matching, no prefix completion and no application of the answer just rejected (the pass-through above is what makes discarding it lossless). Two rejections fail the run with the SECOND complaint; a transport failure is not a rejection and is never retried here; the first complaint is logged at WARN and the turn is reported as `repair: 1` on the `Result:` line, which IS the append-only lifecycle.log on the unattended path, so a benchmark counts the token once — against the `Result:` lines, with the WARN line the record for a run that repaired and then failed outright (#689). The complaint has TWO renderings because the line it quotes is model prose over stored memory: `Error()` carries the clipped operation line for the person and the repair prompt, `opRefusal.Safe()` withholds it for the log and keeps the reason and the id — the same reason the three other lines the tier writes go through `previewContent`. TWO log sites use it: the tier's own WARN (`readerComplaintForLog`, fail-closed) and the tiered wrapper's `consolidator failed, trying next tier` (`safeTierError`, which redacts a refusal but passes a HARNESS failure through unchanged, because "signal: killed" is worth telling apart from a refused answer) — and on a manual `auto` run that wrapper's line lands in the log with no failure anywhere to explain it. TWO things are deliberately NOT redacted, and do not read the seam as a guarantee: a harness failure is not value-free either (internal/ai builds it from the child's own output, and opencode's stdout stream carries the model's `text` events — pre-existing and #540's deliberate trade, since the child's explanation is what made those failures diagnosable), and `runReflect` still prints the full refusal on its `error: consolidation failed:` line, which on the unattended path is lifecycle.log, the phase tail and the next session's maintenance alert (up to 300 bytes of its first line, the first whole line after the head cut once the tail starts inside it — withheld by the token guard on a match and framed as «...» data, see the lifecycle marker bullets). Every model-supplied fragment interpolated into a complaint is clipped at that point (`clipOpText`), or a `drop <id> reason: <a paragraph>` puts a paragraph into a prompt, a log and an error message — and THAT CLIP IS ALSO WHERE THE VALUE GATE RUNS: SIX reasons quote a model fragment (parseOpLine's unreadable drop tail and unknown verb; executeOps' unknown id, unknown superseded-by target, doubly-claimed id, and not-carried-forward supersession), all six routed through `clipOpText` at six call sites and eight invocations, enumerated in the source so the list can be audited against the code. That gate probes ALL THREE SPELLINGS (as written, lower-cased, upper-cased), and the two folds are what cover the case-SENSITIVE rules, which split by literal case — `gh[pousr]_` is lower, `AKIA|ASIA|ABIA|ACCA`+16 and `AGE-SECRET-KEY-1…` are upper. All three are reachable and each hides a token from the others: the parser upper-cases a supersession target (hiding a `ghp_…` from an as-written-only probe) while the free-form drop tail is probed in the model's own spelling (hiding a lower-cased `AKIA…` from a lower-fold-only probe, and folding that down is a no-op). The as-written probe is not redundant either — it is the ONLY one that reaches the mixed-case literals below, which is why `parseOpLine` keeps a raw copy of the verb instead of gating the folded one: lower-casing a token before the gate sees it turns a mixed-case literal into a shape no probe recognises. A stored id is safe here for two separate reasons, both from the detector's own constants: 32 hex characters is under the two bare-hex floors (`cardanoKeyMinRun` 68, `longHexFloor` 132) though above `assignedSecretFloor` (20), and it carries no provider prefix and no `key: value` assignment, which is what every remaining rule needs. `TestClipOpTextStillGatesARealId` pins that. KNOWN RESIDUAL, not closed: the MIXED-case literals (google-api-key `AIza…`, pypi `pypi-AgEIcHlwaS5vcmc…`, JWT `eyJ…`, PuTTY `PuTTY-User-Key-File-`) match ONLY the as-written probe, so a re-spelled one (`aizasyd-…`) is caught by no fold — closing THAT would mean lower-casing the rules in internal/secret, which changes what they match everywhere including the write boundary, so it is a change to that package, not to this probe set. Each of the three probes has a test that fails when it is removed, and `TestReaderComplaintGatesAMixedCaseLiteral` covers the as-written probe and the raw verb together rather than describing one, and a `drop <id> reason: <credential>` is refused BECAUSE the tail is free-form, so the value is in the reason. `clipOpText` therefore applies the same `secret.Detect` gate `previewContent` does, before the clip; a stored id is 32 chars (`hex(randomblob(16))`) so 60 never truncates a real one. A rewrite fixture cannot catch this class — its replacement text is inside the line that IS withheld. `ReflectionResult.RepairTurns` is per RUN, not per tier: `TieredConsolidator.Consolidate` carries a discarded tier's repair turns onto whichever result survives, so a run that repairs and then falls through to SQLite is still visible. The grounding check (`internal/reflection/grounding.go`) is the other half: a merge or rewrite that introduces a path, hash, version, hostname or number found in none of its source ids is REJECTED and its sources are emitted unchanged — the output is never repaired, because Ghost cannot know which side of a corrupted identifier (`2.BeXIAhbj.js` vs `2.BeXIAhbq.js`) is true, and a wrong specific is stored as fact forever. That is also why a `rewrite` fixes a CLAIM and never a specific: the corrected value would be an identifier the source does not contain, so the prompt asks for `keep` (or `drop`) instead and the check is never asked a question it must refuse. Ordinary prose is not an identifier: a merge that needs a new connective word is a legitimate merge, and a bare proper noun the model misspells is outside these classes
- A SUPERSESSION'S SUCCESSOR CLAIMS THE DROPPED ROW, AND THE CLAIM IS KEYED ON THE SUCCESSOR'S ID. `drop <id> reason: superseded by <id>` says the named row replaces the dropped one, so `cmd/ghost`'s `replacedIDsFor` keys the dropped id on that row's ID and `reflectMemoriesToMemory` hands it to the successor's `ReplacesIDs`: the dropped row is deleted INTO the successor, its delete history names it (`successorOf`), and the decision links it carried reach a live row instead of dying with it. A MERGE of the successor already inherited the link — the merge's own ids name the dropped row — so the KEEP is where it was lost: a keep names no ids of its own, and the dropped row was then an unaccounted candidate, deleted with no successor named, and a decision companion's link (the only thing `SupersedeDecisionReport` finds the companion by) gone with it, so a later supersede of that decision retired nothing while the row stating the reversed decision kept ranking. The claim cannot ride the TEXT join, because a restatement takes nothing from it (a row whose content equals a merge's text would otherwise inherit the merge's sources on its own keep, and the replace read that keep as a claim on rows the merge named); a stored id and a memory's text are different things that can look alike, so the join keys them apart rather than sharing one. Pinned by `TestReflectKeepsADecisionLinkWhenTheSuccessorIsKept`, `TestReplacedIDsReachTheKeptSuccessorOfASupersession` and `TestReplacedIDsLeaveAKeepNoDropNamesAlone`.
- Reflection input accounting: `ghost reflect` prints a section after the result list that accounts for EVERY input id (#684), identical for a dry run and for `--apply` and printed BEFORE the write so a dry run previews it exactly, with one exception named at the end of this sentence; a merge whose sources' scopes disagree is listed under its own refused bucket rather than under Merges, decided by `memory.FoldSourceScopes` (the write's own rule) over the input rows' scopes, and the one exception — the one scope refusal it does not predict — is the replace's reuse branch, a merge whose text is byte-identical to a stored row with a different scope (#1492). A merge whose sources fold more decision links than one source_ref can hold is likewise listed under its own refused bucket (`InputAccounting.LinkOverflows`, printed with the link count and the bound), decided by `memory.DecisionLinksOverflow` over the input rows' `source_ref` — the same function `decisionLinksOverflowTx` calls after it reads the rows, so the dry run and the replace share ONE rule — over the replace's own claim set, chosen by `memory.EmissionClaimsRow` — the predicate `keepEmissionClaims` uses, so the rows the line owns are exactly the rows the replace keeps: the named sources plus any input row whose content is the merge's text, the latter printed as `Carriers` and owned ahead of a drop, rewrite or keep line (a twin another merge or a grounding refusal already quotes stays there, so no id is quoted twice) — and checked BEFORE the scope fold because the replace drops an overflow before it folds scopes, so a merge failing both is an overflow only. The invariant is a partition: each input id appears in exactly one line or one count and EVERY count is a count of ids — a merge names any number of sources, so `Merges (3)` over one three-source merge is right and the count of operations is not — so an operator (or an independent judge) can add the numbers up to the input total, and the test asserts the quoted ids plus the two counts ARE the input set with no id twice AND that the section's own headers sum to the input total. The one place a line quotes an id it does not account for is a `superseded by` reason, which names the successor that replaced the row: that successor is accounted for by its own bucket, so the accounting is per input id and not per mention. It exists because the report used to list only what the corpus BECAME — an input a merge consumed vanished from it, so a merge and a loss printed identically, and the applied run recorded the answer in `memory_history` while the preview showed none of it. The truth lives in `reflection.AccountInputs` (`internal/reflection/accounting.go`), a PURE function of the result, because the records about a round belong beside the round; `reportInputAccounting` (`cmd/ghost/reflect_accounting.go`) only formats, and is a named function because `runReflect` calls `os.Exit`. A merge prints `new <- <ids> (<bytes> B from <bytes> B)` and NOT a successor id: the row does not exist until the apply writes it, and `memory_history`'s `related_id` is where the identity becomes visible, so the section cannot name an id that is not knowable yet and a dry run is still an exact preview. The buckets are the result's OWN records, which is why three of them had to be added: `Kept` (an explicit `keep`, otherwise indistinguishable from the pass-through), `Drops` (every explicit drop with its reason — an `obsolete` drop names no successor, so before this the result said NOTHING about the id it disposed of) and `Refusals` (a grounding-check rejection with the ids it kept and the identifiers that caused it, which used to be a log line inside the tier). Like `Replacements`, all three are RECORDS: nothing acts on them, and the drop guard still audits every disposed row on its own evidence. The sixth bucket, **deleted**, is the set of rows an apply REMOVES, one line each with its reason, and it is counted by the replace's own reuse pass rather than by whether the row's text is still in the result. `ReplaceNonManual` reuses stored rows by byte-identical content and claims ONE per emission — the row in the same category, else the oldest, which is why the claim is re-implemented in `claimedRows` over `(created_at, id)` — and deletes every candidate left in the bucket, so two inputs holding the same bytes both have their text in the result and only one is still there afterwards. Keying on the text called BOTH of them carried, which is this report's own defect pointed the other way (#684 review, a blocker). The two reasons are different outcomes: a row nothing carries is a loss, a row whose identical twin was reused is a deduplication whose knowledge is still in the project, and collapsing them is the confusion the section exists to remove. Every id a line prints is the STORED spelling, resolved through the input map rather than echoed from the record: `memIDKey` compares case-insensitively so a response may spell an id any way, and the parser normalises only a drop's successor target (a stored id is `hex(randomblob(16))`, which SQLite renders UPPER-case — measured on a real store, not read off the schema). A guard verdict annotates the line that named the id, by COUNT rather than by id (the line already names them, and a second mention would break the one-account contract), and says `re-added` or `--allow-drops accepts the deletion` so a drop line cannot be read as a deletion that did not happen. `--full` removes the 120-byte preview everywhere in the report through one `reflectTextLimit` seam, is display-only, and is deliberately not on the unattended lifecycle path whose stdout is an append-only log
- The `Applied: nothing` summary names every reason it has, in one line (#1555): of the refusal reasons a round can hit together — a scope disagreement, a concurrent edit or delete, a proposal held back because its memory was written in the same second the pass started, an overflowed decision-link fold — each single-reason line prints only when its reason is the round's SOLE one, and any two or more print the mixed line carrying every count (`refusalReasons`, `cmd/ghost/lifecycle.go`). The overflow used to reach that summary only through the concurrent-edit line unless a scope refusal was also present, which told the operator the next run would re-merge everything — false for an overflowed merge, which overflows again on every run until it is split. The same-second refusal is counted apart from the concurrent edit for the same reason the overflow is (#1745): nothing changed, the next pass can apply the proposal, and the concurrent-edit sentence tells the operator a memory moved when none did. A round with no same-second refusal prints the mixed line exactly as it always did, so the count is added only when there is one. `TestRunReflectCountsAnOverflowBesideAConcurrentEdit` pins the overflow-plus-concurrent-edit pair, both stderr notes, and that the skip fingerprint is still withheld.
- Hybrid search: 70% vector (cosine, Ollama) + 30% FTS5, RRF fusion — window selection is `Store.fuseAndRank` delegating to `selectWindow` (scope narrowing, keyword reservation, final cut), which every production search reaches including the FTS-only degenerate path; `FuseAndSelectWindow` is the exported entry point over the same seam, called only from the fusion tests. Explain mode does not RE-DERIVE any of it, and it is not a second search (#898, closing the deferral to #583 and #571): `ghost_memory_search` with `explain:true` sets `Explain` on the ONE `assemble.Request` the formatted answer uses and takes its payload from the SAME `assemble.Run` call (`Result.Explain`, `internal/assemble/explain.go`), so the budget, filters, clock and byte cap are the answer's own. The retriever is asked only to RECORD: `CandidateRequest.Explain` makes `Store.Candidates` create the ranking trace (`SearchParams.trace`, an unexported `*searchTrace`, nil on every request that did not ask) BEFORE the legs run and return the per-candidate `RankFact` map, the pool candidates the returned set does not carry (`Excluded`), the candidates only the vector floor removed (`FloorDropped`) and the fusion knobs the ranking ran with (`ExplainKnobs`). The flag changes what `Candidates` returns and nothing about how it ranks, scopes or windows — `TestCandidatesExplainChangesNothingAboutTheRetrieval` holds the rows, their order and scores, the legs and the edges equal with it on and off, and a flag that altered scope filtering, windowing or the verdict line would be a second ranking.
