The stages write the facts themselves: the leg ranks and the vector cosine in `fuseCandidatePool`, the scope verdict in `scopeEligiblePool` (for the DROPPED candidates too, the structural fix for #571), the fused base and the status factor in `demoteStatus` (the two sides of one multiplication; the product is not recorded), the reservation exchange in `selectWindow` (both the promoted row and the one whose slot it took), the decay factor and age in `decayRank`, the floor verdict through `stampVectorFloor` where the floor is applied, and the penalty counts plus their attributed counterpart ids in `supersedeVerdicts`/`nearDuplicateVerdicts` (one edge read each). Every stage already received `p SearchParams`, which is why the trace rides on it rather than as a new argument. Membership is the answer's: a row is `included` exactly when it is in `Result.Items`, so a row a category/retention/scope predicate, the item budget or the response-fit pass withheld is excluded WITH that stage's reason, and the included set equals the set the answer renders. A row the LEGS withheld for validity (#1053) never entered the candidate set, so the payload has no row for it — the projection of a ranking that never ranked it — while `validity_state` is reported for every row that did reach the set (and for the rare row the SQL superset admitted that stage 2 then dropped); both the keyword leg and the vector leg report how many they withheld (the keyword leg what its SQL predicate removed, the vector scan the above-floor matches it dropped), so a query whose matches all retired still answers `all_invalid` rather than reading as an absence. Every stored string in the payload (content snippet, ids, project, scope keys and values, counterpart ids, leg error text) is rendered by `assemble.Data` or `assemble.Token` exactly as the formatted answer renders it; a field rendered raw is a defect (`TestExplainRendersStoredStringsLikeTheAnswer`, and the tool-level `TestSearchExplainRendersStoredTextLikeTheAnswer`). An explain call writes NO retrieval record: the record counts answers delivered to a caller (the audit's denominator), an explanation is a diagnostic of one, and `Run` gates the sink on `!req.Explain` (`TestExplainWritesNoRetrievalRecord`, `TestSearchExplainWritesNoRetrievalRecord`). Explain is refused with `as_of` (a historical read ranks nothing; refused by the handler, `assemble.Run` and `Store.Candidates`) and without a query (a passive retrieval scores no candidate). A signal the ranking does NOT apply is reported as not applied and never given a contribution to fill the field: `confidence_contribution`, `provenance_contribution` and `validity_penalty` are 0, and `provenance_weight` is `"off"` (stage 4 pins an inert 1.0 that no score is multiplied by, and the payload does not publish it as a weight) — a multiplier that does not exist reported as 1.0 would read as "provenance was weighed and found neutral". Validity is a reported state read through `memory.ValidityState`, the ONE rule the assembler's validity stage also delegates to, and an expired or not-yet-valid row is excluded rather than ranked lower. The payload is bounded at `memory.ExplainMaxRows` = 150 CANDIDATE rows and `memory.ExplainSnippetRunes` = 120 runes of content, the tool's window ceiling being 100 and the answer never being part of the budget; the cut spends itself on excluded candidates and never on a returned row, and it is marked in a `truncation` FIELD rather than a note, because a note list is bounded from the end and a short list that looks complete is the failure the marker exists to prevent. `Store.ExplainSearch`/`ExplainSearchScoped` and their `provider.MemoryStore` methods are gone: nothing outside the tool path called them. Status demotion (`statusDemotionFactor` and the `resolvedDemotionFactor`/`globalDemotionFactor` constants in `internal/memory/demotion.go`) multiplies a resolved row's fused score — and a `_global` row's when a specific project is searched — by 0.5 inside `fuseCandidatePool`, before the cut, so it owns membership as well as order; it scales the score rather than dropping the row, so both stay searchable, and explain reports the same number as `status_factor`. With k=60 the factor effectively ranks a demoted row below every live candidate the legs fetched (a halved rank-1 two-leg score of ≈0.0082 sits under the 0.7/70≈0.01 floor of the deepest vector row in a default window), but it only reorders what the legs fetched. Each bucket now gets its own depth (limit*2 for the project's own rows and limit*2 for the shared `_global` rows), merged in the store-wide order, so project rows reach the candidate set regardless of how many shared rows match. The keyword reservation skips rows whose status factor is below 1 (raw rank and the demoted score would otherwise disagree), and the two passive surfaces still never reach FUSION — `loadSessionPassive` (`internal/mcpinit/session_passive.go`) assembles the session-start digest and `projectContextBudget` (`internal/mcpserver/project_context.go`) backs `ghost_project_context` with the `ghost://project/{id}/context` resource and the `recall_project` prompt, both filter `resolved_at IS NULL` — so no factor reaches either. The project-context read is the ONE passive bucket that admits `_global` into a project bucket (`SlicePolicy.IncludeGlobal`), because it is the one that has always read `project_id = ? OR project_id = '_global'` under a SINGLE cap; `ghost://memories/global` reads the SAME bucket over `projectContextGlobalBudget`, which is that policy with `IncludeGlobal` unset because the bucket is already the population, and the two browse surfaces still have their own reader. `Store.GetTopMemories` has NO call site left outside its own definition and its declaration on `provider.MemoryStore`, which is where it is staying for now: it ranked and trimmed in SQL, so every surface that could have wanted it moved onto the assembler and left the stages nothing to decide. It is left rather than deleted because the interface method is not this change's to remove, and a reader who greps for it will find a live exported method with no caller — which is the accurate description of it. It will not reach fusion when the digest moves onto the seam either, and that is deliberate: the status factors scale a FUSED score, and a passive retrieval has no fused score to scale. A passive bucket states its own order instead (`decay`, or `pinned_importance_updated` for `_global`), excludes `resolved_at IS NOT NULL` in SQL, and selects over a window rather than over a fusion — and that window is kept by a THRESHOLD SCAN, not by a SQL `ORDER BY ... LIMIT` (#1367). The policy's rank is a decay expression over a bound clock, so no index can serve it; an `ORDER BY` on it made the engine sort the whole bucket behind a `LIMIT` that then discarded almost all of it, which is O(project memories) of work at every session start for a window of tens of rows. The scan is exact because every factor of the decay — the category factor, the tier factor and the pin's — is at most 1, so a row's rank is never above its importance: `passiveKeyScanSQL` reads the rank columns in `importance DESC` order (the one order `idx_memories_project_imp` serves without a temp b-tree), and a policy that admits `_global` is read as two arms, `project_id = ?` and `project_id = '_global'`, joined by `UNION ALL` under one `ORDER BY` on the full key (`importance DESC`, then `created_at DESC` or `updated_at DESC`, then `id`, the order both composite indexes carry) that SQLite serves as a merge of the two index-ordered arms — never through the population's `project_id = ? OR project_id = '_global'`, which loses the index order and sorts both buckets in a temp b-tree before the scan sees a row (#1528; `TestPassiveKeyScanPlanDoesNotSortTheBucket`, `TestPassiveUnionKeyScanPlanStreamsBothBuckets`, and `TestPassiveUnionKeyScanReadsDoNotGrowWithTheGlobalBucket`, which counts the engine's page reads because the Go read count cannot see a sort) — the OR stays the population every other statement over the bucket shares, and a `_global` bucket that admits `_global` is one arm; `scanPassiveKeys` offers each row to a best-first window of `OverFetch` ordered by `passiveRankKeyLess` (the SQL ORDER BY's own key), and it STOPS once the window is full and the next row's importance is strictly below the window's last primary key — an unread row's rank is at most its importance, so none can enter. **THAT STOP IS THE SCAN'S ONLY END, SO IT SETS THE READ, AND THE READ IS O(WINDOW) ONLY WHILE THE WINDOW'S LAST KEY IS CLOSE TO ITS IMPORTANCE.** A bucket of rows that do not decay (fact, preference, convention), or rows that are fresh, holds a window whose last rank is its importance, the stop fires at the window's edge, and a bucket of any size costs the same read. A bucket whose best rows are AGED rows of a decaying category (gotcha, decision, dependency, pattern, architecture) is the other case: the `decay` order's last key is the window's k-th RANK and a rank decays with its row, so that rank sits far below those rows' importance, every later row is above it, and the scan reads the bucket to its end. The window is the SAME either way — the stop is exact, so a row the scan never reached cannot enter it — so what an aged window costs is the READ and not the block. The window's rows are therefore the policy's top rows over the population, and a test that ranks the whole bucket and compares the window against it (`TestPassiveThresholdScanMatchesTheLegacyWindow`, against a full `ORDER BY` over every row) is the oracle that says so; `TestPassiveThresholdScanRanksTheFreshRowsAboveTheAgedOnes` holds the case where the aged rows carry the higher importance and the fresh rows must still win, and `TestPassiveThresholdScanReadsWhatTheStopBounds` records the read count on both sides of the stop. A row whose `created_at` is LATER than the request's `Now` has its age clamped to 0 (`ageDays`), so its rank is at most its importance too — a future-dated row is scored as brand new rather than given the negative age that would lift its rank above its importance and break the bound the scan's stop condition rests on; this clamp is the ONE intended difference from the old SQL order, which used the raw `julianday('now') - julianday(created_at)` age and did not clamp, and a stored row cannot carry a future `created_at` over any shipped write path. Pinned rows are read as their own population and seed the window, because a pin outranks every unpinned row; the scan holds KEYS and hydrates the window's rows once, so the bucket's content is never read. The window's rank is recomputed against the clock `stampLayoutForSQL` truncates to, importance is read as the double the column stores (so the tie it breaks is the engine's, not float32's), and `PassiveEligibleCount` — header arithmetic the scan cannot answer, because the rows it skips are the count's population — stops at `passiveEligibleCountCap` and reports an unknown count past it. — so the resolved/global demotion has nothing to demote and adding it there would be a second ranking nobody measured. What the digest does share with the tool surface is the scope rule and its label: the block prints it with `assemble.ScopeLabel` — the same label a search line carries, so the two cannot spell one scope differently — and its fetch is narrowed by `injection.session_scope` inside the store's passive statement, using `memory.ScopeMatchesSQL`, the SQL statement of `memory.ScopeMatches` that the assembler applies in Go, held to the Go form by a test that runs both over the same rows. It has to be decided in SQL for the reason `scopesConflictSQL` gives: a statement that chooses its own candidate set cannot check scope after that choice, and a passive bucket's `OverFetch` IS its candidate set. Unset, the clause is absent and the block is byte-identical to the one that shipped. The formatted `ghost_memory_search` path does not read that window directly: it asks `Store.Candidates` for a widened untrimmed set and hands it to `assemble.Run`, so the caller's category and scope are applied before the window closes (the window itself is still chosen inside fusion, unchanged) — `internal/assemble` owns every decision after retrieval, and `internal/memory` never imports it. One decision the retriever does make is a row's visibility to a leg rather than its rank: `Store.Candidates` marks `Candidate.Unembedded` on a row that carries no embedding the vector leg could score — no vector at all, one stamped with a different identity, or one whose width differs from the query's (the two filters the scan applies, in its order) — read from the same snapshot only when the vector leg ran and answered, and the assembler's leg-consensus stage admits such a row outright because that leg could never have retrieved it (#1319). It also owns the RELEVANCE VERDICT (#580): every block is `answerable`, `weak` or `empty` with a reason from a closed set, and the search tool's last line is machine-readable (`[ghost:outcome=… reason=… floor_fts_rank=… abstain_cosine=… candidates=… admitted=… legs=… tokens_est=…]`) with a human abstention sentence for the two non-answerable cases. `weak` withholds nothing — it labels the rows so a caller treats them as leads; the ONLY thing that removes a row for a reason other than a stage is the `response_fit` post-pass, and that is bounded by `Budget.MaxBytes` against the COMPLETE response (`Slice.MaxBytes` is item content; one field cannot bound two units). Arm A (keyword rank 0-3) is on and arm B (a vector cosine) ships OFF, because the bench no-answer report shows the two cosine distributions overlap and no constant separates them — `context.abstain_cosine` is a user's decision, and `abstain_cosine=off` on the line is how a disabled threshold is told from a cosine of zero. An arm applies to a result when it holds a VALUE, not when its leg ran: a leg that answered can have retrieved nothing, leaving every row at the -1 sentinel. A leg that FAILED suppresses the floor outright (`retrieval_partial`); a result with no arm value at all is `no_floor_arm`; a leg that never ran changes neither — the keyword arm still judges keyword-only results, and the leg's state is on the line (`legs=`, `abstain_cosine=not_applied`), never in the reason. An outage is not evidence about the corpus. An empty result whose rows were FOUND and withheld says "no sufficiently trustworthy memory found" and names the cause; only `no_candidates` claims nothing matched, and only that reason is evidence about the store. A leg that never RAN and a leg that ran and FAILED are different facts with different next steps, and the empty branch keeps them apart the way the non-empty one does: the first is `vector_backend_unavailable` (the keyword leg was all that ran), the second is a tool error rather than a verdict (an incident — retry); `legNeverRan` covers both "no embedder" and "this query would not embed", because the tool swallows the embed error and the leg status cannot tell them apart
- **THE STATUS DEMOTION IS PART OF THE RANKING, AND A HISTORICAL READ RANKS WITH IT TOO (#1683).** `statusDemotionFactor` — the resolved factor and the project-scoped `_global` factor, both `× 0.5`, in `internal/memory/demotion.go` — scales a row's score rather than filtering it, so a demoted row stays searchable and a live project row takes the slot a demoted row was holding. The `as_of` leg applies the SAME factor (`internal/memory/asof_leg.go`), and it can, because both of the factor's inputs are VERSIONED: `AsOfRow.ResolvedAt` and `AsOfRow.ProjectID` come from the version row, so the demotion describes the row as it stood at T rather than as it stands now — which is the whole point of a historical read, and the reason the factor belongs on that path rather than being borrowed from the live row. It scales the BASE (`1/(RRFK+rank+1)`) rather than the historical composite, which is the same place the current path scales the fused score: the base is a rank score whose neighbours sit within a percent or two of each other, so halving it sinks the row below the rows around it, while a halved composite would leave a demoted row ahead of a live one whose importance is under half its own. It owns the CUT as well as the order — the historical window is cut by the demoted base, and the tail is the rows that window did not take — so a demoted row cannot hold the window slot a live project row would have had, and the two halves of a historical answer cannot disagree about which rows are in the window. Without it a historical read ranked a high-importance resolved row (or `_global` row) above a lower-importance live project row, the opposite of what the same query over the present returns from the same store: `TestCandidatesAsOfDemotesResolvedBelowLive`, `TestCandidatesAsOfDemotesGlobalBelowProject` and `TestCandidatesAsOfDemotionOwnsTheWindowCut`
- Vector scan: both legs are brute force over the project's embeddings, and they run in two phases (`internal/memory/vector_scan.go`, #556). `snapshotVectors` copies every candidate row out of SQLite and returns, holding the store's read lock and its single connection for the copy only; `vectorRows.search` scores the copy afterwards, so the O(corpus × dims) cosine pass — which `ghost supersede` runs once per memory — no longer blocks writers for the length of the corpus. The copy keeps the stored bytes rather than decoding them and the score loop reads the little-endian float32s back out in place (`cosineFromBytes`, bit-identical to `cosineSimilarity` on a decoded vector), and the scope column is parsed only for a row that wins a slot; a `scopeProbe` decides per-row eligibility by checking whether the stored text can mention a requested key at all, which can only save a `json.Unmarshal` and never decides membership, since `ScopeMatches` only excludes a row that names a requested key and disagrees. Ranking keeps a bounded window instead of the corpus — a min-heap whose root is the worst row held, ordered once at the end over at most `limit` rows — and the order is total (cosine descending, exact ties broken by the memory's rowid, older first; memories has a TEXT primary key, so an in-place VACUUM may renumber that implicit rowid, which Ghost never runs on the live database (backups use VACUUM INTO) and which could only reorder an exact tie; scan position is only the last resort for equal rowids) so the answer is a function of the stored data, not of the heap's internals or the scan order. `TestSearchVectorRankingMatchesTheCollectAndSortBaseline` pins the whole result against a transcription of the pre-#556 collect-and-sort search, and `TestVectorSearchCopiesTheCorpusUnderTheStoreReadLock` / `TestVectorSearchScoresWithTheStoreLockReleased` pin that the copy takes the read lock and the scoring pass does not. A writer is still kept out during the copy, and that is the connection rather than the mutex: OpenDB pins the pool at one, so no amount of lock work lets a write start while a query is streaming. The scratch is recycled through a `sync.Pool` on the store, and an oversized embedding buffer is dropped on return rather than retained; a corpus above that cap is then regrown from an empty buffer on every search, so the copy sizes it in one allocation from a row count before it starts (`vectorRows.reserve`; the count names only the rows the copy can use — the leg's project narrowing plus the loader's identity and width filters — so a small project on a large store, or a corpus mid-re-embed, does not reserve bytes for rows the copy skips) rather than by repeated append growth (#1036). What a query still allocates is the driver: `modernc.org/sqlite` copies every BLOB and TEXT column into a fresh allocation per row, which is why the remaining cost was a corpus cache (the next bullet) and, beyond it, an ANN index, not more work on the scoring loop
- Vector corpus cache (`internal/memory/vector_cache.go`, schema v24, #1031): ONE immutable copy of the corpus per store, valid while the database's `vector_epoch` is unchanged. The counter is in the database because several processes share the file; it is bumped by triggers in the writing transaction (`vector_epoch_emb_ai/au/ad` on `memory_embeddings`, `vector_epoch_mem_au` on `memories` for a changed `resolved_at`, `scope` or `project_id`, `vector_epoch_mem_validity_au` on `memories` for a changed `valid_from` or `valid_until` (schema v25), `vector_epoch_mem_ad` on a memory delete, `vector_epoch_mem_ai` on an insert that re-attaches an orphaned embedding) and NOT by anything the vector query does not read — the retrieval record every search writes, access counts, tags and links must not fire it, or every process's copy is invalidated on every query. A new column the vector query reads needs a trigger and a `TestVectorEpochInvalidatesOnEveryVectorAffectingWrite` case; the update triggers compare old and new values because `UpdateMemory` names `resolved_at` on every edit. The key is (epoch, configured identity). The epoch is read under the store's read lock on the handle the rows come from (a `Candidates` snapshot reads it inside its pinned transaction, so an older snapshot misses and scores its own rows); a copy is published only when exact — read in one transaction, or bracketed by two agreeing epoch reads — and never over a newer one; a mid-load write means the search uses its copy once and keeps nothing. The cache is read-only after publication and scoring on it runs with the store lock released (`TestCachedVectorSearchScoresWithTheStoreLockReleased`); its scratch rows and heap come from the pool, its column buffers are never handed to the pool (a pooled snapshot appends in place). Bounded by `vectorCacheMaxBytes` (the scratch pool's 32 MiB), checked from the row count before any load — the count names the rows the cache would keep, the configured identity's at the query's width, exactly as the uncached count does, so a corpus that fits is not refused because the table also holds another vector space's rows (#1257); above it, or without the counter, the search is the uncached path, whose pooled scratch the copy sizes from the row count before it starts (#1036). Per-search work (project/`_global` filter, width filter, scope probe, foreign and mismatched facts and warnings) runs over the copy, so facts and the once-per-identity warning match the uncached scan (`TestCachedVectorSearchMatchesTheUncachedPath`). Exact score ties order by creation order, the memory's rowid, older first (`compareCandidates`), so the cached scan, the project-index scan and the embeddings scan all return the same rows in the same order; ordering by the random memory id instead changed which of several identical-embedding memories survives near-duplicate demotion, and the backup-restore e2e caught it. The DDL is `vectorEpochSQL`, NOT `initSQL`: initSQL runs before a lagging database is migrated and the update trigger's `WHEN` names `memories.scope`, which a pre-v12 database lacks, so an early trigger breaks the legacy steps that alter `memories`; it is created after `initSQL` on a fresh database, by `migrateV24` on an upgrade, and by `ensurePostMigrationIndexes` on a current one; the validity arm rides that same DDL on a fresh or current store and reaches a pre-v25 one through `migrateV25`. The counter row is created by the first bump (an upsert), so opening a database writes nothing and a missing row reads as 0. `vector_epoch` must NEVER decrease, be deleted or be reset, because the cache matches on equality. A future migration that rebuilds `memories` or `memory_embeddings` drops these triggers with the table and must recreate them AND bump the epoch if it changes any vector-query input, because a rebuild's implicit delete fires no trigger. `vector_epoch_mem_ai` fires on a memory insert only when an embedding row for that id already exists (an orphan left by a delete with `foreign_keys` off). Worst-case resident memory is about 2x the bound (the cache plus the scratch pool), and racing misses can briefly hold two copies. Migration step v24 is frozen like the others and creates a trigger only on a table that exists
- Vector-space identity: a stored vector is only scored when `memory_embeddings.model` equals the identity this process embeds into (`Store.SetEmbeddingIdentity`, fed from `embedding.VectorIdentity`). A model, dimension or task-prefix change therefore retires the old vectors instead of comparing them with queries from the new space. The rule is read in four places, one per caller: `loadVectorRows` (may a vector enter a search), `GetEmbedding` returning nil (may a stored vector act as a QUERY vector — the link worker and `ghost supersede` both do), `UnscannedEmbeddedMemoryIDs` (may the linker compare this memory yet), `EmbeddingStats` (is it covered, which `ghost mcp status` and `ghost_health` report). A retired vector never hides its memory: the keyword leg still finds it. Two side effects of a rewrite ride along: `StoreEmbedding` clears the row's `link_scans` slot when the identity changes, in the same write transaction as the upsert (#1258) so a concurrent scan mark cannot re-arm it from the retired bytes (so the linker re-scans it in the new space — the re-scan ADDs current-space `related` edges alongside the ones the retired space produced; nothing invalidates those old rows, and `CreateLink`'s `MAX(strength, excluded.strength)` upsert means an edge whose new-space similarity is lower keeps its old strength), and the foreign-vector warning in `loadVectorRows` is emitted once per retired identity (a second reconfiguration in the same process warns again). `EmbeddingStats` reports stale and unembedded rows separately so `ghost mcp status` can show both, and counts an embedding row only while its memory exists (a delete with foreign keys off leaves an orphan the vector leg never returns), so embedded + stale never exceeds total. The identity is opaque (compared, never parsed) and empty means "no model configured", which keeps the bench harness and tests unfiltered
- Memory history: `memory_history` (schema v17, #578) is the append-only CHANGE LOG of how a memory reached its current state — one row per write. The name `memory_provenance` went to a different and later concept (evidence records, several per memory, answering "who or what supports this memory" — see "Evidence provenance" below), so never rename this table to it, and do not treat the two as the same thing; and every writer appends one row in the SAME transaction as the write — `Create`, `Upsert` (insert and the fold's `merge`), `UpdateMemory`, `ReplaceNonManual` (rewrite, reuse, bulk delete), `SetResolved`/`ClearResolved`/`MarkResolved` (the last recording the OPERATOR as performer, and clearing the row's KEEP cache in the same statement as the stamp — the only `resolve` row that can say a person decided rather than a classifier judged), `CreateLink` for a `supersedes` edge and `InvalidateLink` for its withdrawal (`unsupersede` — a memory can be superseded, withdrawn and superseded again, and only the sequence says which claim is live), `RestoreSnapshot`, `ImportMemory`, `Delete`, `PruneSessionMemories` (a `session` row removed by `ghost prune` — the one writer whose row is gone by design, and the reason the audit has to be a tombstone), the decision companion memory and the shipped seeds. Each row is a VERSION, not a diff: the state the memory held once that write landed, so "what did Ghost know at T" is the newest row at or before T (#647) and the prior content is the previous row. `agent`/`session_id` are the PERFORMER (the save/merge/import paths carry a Provenance; the lifecycle passes do not, and an empty field is an admission, not a claim) — which is what makes the issue's "a row changed by two different agents" answerable. `memory_id` deliberately has NO foreign key so a hard delete leaves a tombstone carrying the text the row held; `project_id` does cascade. Growth is bounded in the appending transaction: newest 50 versions per memory, newest 20 000 rows per store (rowid window, since an implicit rowid is max(rowid)+1 and never reused). A write that changes none of a memory's own recorded columns appends nothing (`Touch`, `TogglePin`, the KEEP cache), and a write that moves a memory BETWEEN projects must also move its history rows' project_id or the project-delete cascade takes the past of a memory that survived: `MergeProject` and `PromoteToGlobal` both do (the merge list exists twice on main). A verbatim consolidation re-emission is a NO-OP and appends nothing (#727: it measured at 80% of `memory_history` on a real store, and the per-memory cap was days from evicting real events) — when content, category, importance, tags and scope are all unchanged it writes no row and leaves `updated_at` alone, because `ghost supersede` orients each candidate pair by `updated_at` and `--skip-unchanged`'s fingerprint carries it as a change proxy; a change to any of those five IS a real update and is recorded (`reuseChangesNothing` compares each field by the value the side that produced it carries, an unreadable stored value counts as changed, and importance is compared at the EMISSION's float32 precision (`float32(stored) == emitted.Importance`) because Upsert's strengthen adds in the column's own float64 (with the increment itself bound as a float32) and leaves a value float32 cannot name, so a full-width comparison reported a change on every verbatim keep of a strengthened row and reinstated the flood #727 removed (#750)). TWO writers deliberately repeat the state instead, and neither is a licence to repeat: `ghost prune` appends one per removal and changes no other shape (the backup manifest counts the CORPUS, never the change log, so a tombstone cannot make a post-prune backup fail to verify), and a `supersedes` edge appends a `supersede` row when it BECOMES active, recording a claim about the target's standing rather than a state change. Both spend a slot under the cap, which is what bounds them. Two columns carry the event's other end: `related_id` (the id that replaced a deleted row, or the memory whose edge claims it) and `merged_content` (the text a FoldOnly fold dropped, which no row keeps). `ghost history purge <id>` and `ghost_memory_delete`'s `purge_history` delete a row AND its history in one transaction — the redaction path, because a plain delete keeps the text a memory used to hold — and `ghost_history_content` is the SQL seam where #656's `secret.Detect` will redact content on the way in. A purge reaches a memory that is ALREADY deleted through `Store.PurgeMemoryHistory` (history only, never the row), because the tombstone is the feature and a delete-time purge cannot cover it; the same operation is what frees an id for `ghost import`, which REFUSES an id that still has history rather than splicing two memories' records under it (ids are not reused by Ghost, but a portable artifact carries them verbatim). A purge redacts folded-in wording too, and that wording is collected SEPARATELY from the content list: the cross-row `merged_content` cells match on the purged memory's own content AND on its own `merged_content` — wording a fold brought INTO the purged memory is absent from every content column of it, so a set drawn from those columns alone could not reach the copy another row kept, and the purge redacted the cells it could see while the folded-in text stayed in the append-only table — but its own `merged_content` is deliberately NOT added to the content list, because that list also keys the snapshot delete and a snapshot is the rollback point for a whole project: adding it there would delete an unrelated memory's snapshot merely because its content equals the wording this one let go. `TestPurgeRedactsFoldedWordingCopiedOntoAnotherHistoryRow` pins the reach and `TestPurgeKeepsAnotherMemoriesSnapshotWhoseContentIsTheFoldedWording` pins the boundary (found by the write-paths-parity audit). `migrateV17` backfills nothing, so a pre-v17 memory's first edit files a `baseline` row before it overwrites the text. Read it with `Store.MemoryHistory` (oldest first, rowid order) or `ghost history <memory-id>` `as_of` (RFC 3339) on `ghost_memory_search` / `ghost_project_context` and `--as-of` on `ghost context` is the OTHER reader, and it is the reason the rows are versions rather than diffs: `Store.MemoriesAsOf` takes each memory's newest row at or before T, drops the ones whose newest row is a `delete` tombstone or whose first row is after T, and returns the version's own `content`/`category`/`importance`/`resolved_at`/`source`/`project_id` — one statement, driven by `memory_history` rather than by `memories` (a deleted memory's tombstone is the only surviving record of it, so a read that started from the live tables would report "Ghost never knew this" for a memory it held for months), ranked `ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY memory_id ORDER BY recorded_at DESC, rowid DESC)` with `rowid` as the second key because `recorded_at` is second-precision. A pre-v17 memory with no row at or before T is UNKNOWN, never guessed: it is left out of the set and counted in `AsOfSet.Unknown`, because a shorter set that says nothing about the gap reads as the whole truth — and that "no row at or before T" is TWO time-bounded tests, not one, because an unbounded `NOT EXISTS` asks "does this memory have history at all" and a pre-v17 memory the lifecycle touched after the upgrade does (only `UpdateMemory` files a baseline; every other first write appends a row read out of the memories row in the same transaction, so its text is recorded anyway, and that is why no writer needs a baseline there). The gap half also reaches the TOMBSTONES, because a pre-v17 memory deleted after T has no live row either and its gap is otherwise invisible — and that half must do TWO things, or a memory is counted twice: exclude ids that are LIVE again (because `RestoreSnapshot` reinstates a row under the id it recorded, so "tombstoned" does not imply "not live"), AND rank per memory keeping its newest tombstone (a delete → restore → delete memory holds two delete rows, and `UNION ALL` does not dedup). `CreatedAt` — the field the decay measures an age from — is the row's own when that column can answer and the version's `recorded_at` when it cannot: the row is gone (every column NULL, and an empty `created_at` reads as ~56,000 days, so a memory deleted yesterday sat at the decay floor in every listing covering the past year), or the column is LATER than T (no writer produces that, and it is refused because `ageDays` clamps a negative age at 0, making a future-dated column the most favourable value a row could carry into a ranking of the past). **`as_of` state survives `ghost history compact`, and two metadata fields do not.** For a memory that is still LIVE the answer is read from its `memories` row, so removing a redundant version changes nothing a historical read is *for* — the state columns, the supersede edges and `CreatedAt` all answer as before, and `TestCompactingRedundantVersionsLeavesTheAsOfStateAlone` compares them one at a time. Two fields do move, and only because they NAME which version answered: `VersionRecordedAt` and `VersionPhase` can describe an EARLIER version carrying the same state, so a memory that was resolved and unresolved — or folded — may read as merely saved for an instant whose reflect flood has since been compacted away. That is a documented consequence rather than an accident, and it is the price of removing the flood at all: those rows are exactly what made a past read name a reflect run as the last thing that happened to a memory. A DELETED memory is excluded from the repair instead (rule (6) above), so for one of those neither field moves and the age does not either — which is why that is a scope rule rather than a fourth retention rule. Everything else in `AsOfRow` is the current row, including the validity triple: `valid_from`/`valid_until` are the present's — [#575](https://github.com/wcatz/ghost/issues/575)'s writers ship, but the change log records no validity, so an `as_of` read takes the bounds from the current row, and **what a surface does with a borrowed bound is not one rule** ([#910](https://github.com/wcatz/ghost/issues/910)). A LISTING still judges them AT T through the one helper `memory.ValidityAt` (the assembler's stage 2 withholds on `memory.ValidityWithheld` too, but only for a CURRENT request): a row whose window had closed or not yet opened at T is withheld by `ghost_project_context` and `ghost context --as-of`, before the limit or cap, and `memory.AsOfValidityNote` says validity was judged at T. `ghost_memory_search` with `as_of` judges NOTHING and withholds NOTHING: the bounds are the present's, so a verdict drawn from them would be a claim about today rather than about T, and stage 2 records the empty state that renders the bounds with no verdict word while `qualifiersFor` appends `memory.AsOfBorrowedWindowNote` — which leads with the borrow the two notes share and then says no verdict was drawn, so it cannot claim the judgement the listing's own `memory.AsOfValidityNote` claims. `TestAsOfStageTwoKeepsWhatTheListingWithholdsAtT` pins that divergence in both directions — every row survives stage 2 on a historical request, and the listing's rule is still `memory.ValidityAt`'s — so the two surfaces cannot quietly converge on a second reading of it. Tags, scope, pin, access count and provenance are read from the current row as well, none of them versioned either — `Retention` is the ONE exception and is always empty, because it postdates the change log and a version has no tier to carry (see "Retention tiers"); the statement does not even name the column, since `ghost context --as-of` opens the store read-only and cannot migrate a store that predates it. A promotion REWRITES the history rows' `project_id` (it has to, or the project-delete cascade takes a memory's past with it), so a pre-promotion instant reports a promoted memory under `_global`; that imprecision is pinned by a test rather than worked around. Nothing in the as_of path reads `memory_provenance` (v18, the EVIDENCE table): an evidence record is one OBSERVATION of a memory, not a version of it, so nothing there can be placed at an instant — a count taken now would be a present-day claim about a past memory. The counts therefore stay zero, and since a zero renders as "no recorded evidence", the as_of DISCLOSURE says they were not read rather than leaving that phrase to stand as a claim. Every statement in the read names `memory_history` or `memories` and nothing else, so a pre-v18 store answers an as_of read in full (`TestCandidatesAsOfDoesNotDependOnMemoryProvenance` drops the table and reads on). In the assembler `as_of` is a BINDING, not a filter: `Run` moves `Now` to it, so a row's age and the trace that describes the block are all decided against T, and the store treats `AsOf` as authoritative over `Now` for the same reason. The one thing a borrowed bound is NOT decided against T is VALIDITY — see above: stage 2 records no verdict for an as_of row, so `Now` governs age and nothing else there. The retrieval is keyword-ONLY and says so in the answer, the trace and the leg status: `memories_fts` and every embedding describe CURRENT content, so an as_of search matches the query's terms against the versions' own text (through the same `ftsQueryTerms` extraction the FTS leg uses, phrase- and prefix-aware), ranks by matched terms then the same decay composite at T — not bm25, so not comparable with a current order — reports the vector leg as `not applicable` and REFUSES `vector_only` rather than downgrading it, and does not read `memory_links` at all (it records when an edge was invalidated, never the graph at T) so the supersede demotion comes from the recorded `supersede`/`unsupersede` sequence and the edge status is a fifth value, `not_applicable`, because "no edge joins two of these candidates" is a claim a retrieval that read no graph must not render. Those disclosures are `Result.Qualifiers`, not `Result.Notes`: a note list is bounded from the end and shown for the empty answer, where a qualifier is the first thing to go, and a block that is silently a past reading is read as a present one. `ghost context --as-of` runs NONE of that path's startup side effects (the Obsidian mirror, the session-count bump) — they exist because the command backs a session start, and counting a replay would move the present's session number to answer a question about the past. Restoring the store to a past state is not this; that is export/import (#586). **A HISTORICAL KEYWORD QUERY WITH NO EXTRACTABLE TERM MATCHES NOTHING (#1698).** `historicalTerms` prepares the terms `ftsQueryTerms` selected, and a query whose every word trims to empty — punctuation only, or a word holding nothing the edge trim keeps — yields none. A word in another script is NOT in that class: the trim keeps a letter, a digit and a mark of any script (see the `internal/memory/` bullet), so an empty list is a query with no words in it rather than a query in a script the trim did not know. `candidatesAsOf` then runs no keyword pass at all rather than appending every in-scope version: the empty list means no term matched, never "no filter", which is what the current FTS leg answers for the same query (no rows), and it is why the leg reports itself applicable, attempted and available with `Eligible` 0 instead of reporting the whole scope as hits. Scope, category and the other filters still apply on top; the defect was false relevance inside the scope (`TestCandidatesAsOfWithNoExtractableTermReturnsNoRows`, and the end-to-end `TestAsOfWithNoExtractableTermAnswersNothing` in `internal/assemble`).
- A version row is REMOVABLE only when it changed nothing, and "nothing" is every column a version records. `ghost history compact` (#730) is the repair for the rows a pre-#727 build left behind — #727 stopped every applied reflection from appending a byte-identical `reflect` version per kept memory and from moving that memory's `updated_at` to the run's own time, which measured at 80% of this table on one real store — and it removes a row ONLY when it records the same state as the row before it of the same memory, in rowid order (never `recorded_at`, which is second-precision and would make a pass's own writes a coin toss), compared over **every** column a version stores: `content`, `category`, `importance`, `resolved_at`, `source`. The three lists in `internal/memory/history_compact.go` partition the table's columns into that state, the event's own columns, and the row's identity and time, and `TestCompactHistoryColumnPartitionNamesEveryColumn` reads the partition off the live table — because a column added to `memory_history` and to none of the three lists is compared by none of them, which is exactly how a state change stops being one. The comparison is `IS` and not `=`, because every state column is nullable and `=` answers NULL for NULL, so two rows both holding no `resolved_at` would read as a change: the opposite of the damage being repaired. SIX things always stay, and each for its own reason. (1) A memory's FIRST version: the only statement of what it said, with nothing to duplicate. (2) A memory's NEWEST version: the statement of what it says NOW, and `pruneHistoryTx`'s per-memory trim already declines to take it for the same reason. (3) The phases that record a CLAIM the state does not — `delete` (the tombstone, the only record a deleted memory left), `supersede`/`unsupersede` (a claim about the memory's standing, and only the sequence says which claim is live), `resolve`/`unresolve` (who decided), `merge` (the fold), `import` and `restore` (a row arrived from outside) — and the test for that is an ALLOWLIST of the ONE phase whose only content is the state it records — `reflect` — rather than an exemption list, so a phase added to the schema later is not deletable until somebody classifies it. `save`, `update` and `baseline` record a state and nothing else and are deliberately NOT in it: `UpdateMemory` appends an `update` version on EVERY edit with no no-op guard, and a tags-only edit is a real change to what the memory says about itself that this table cannot see, because it has no column for tags. `TestCompactHistoryLeavesATagsOnlyEditAndItsStamp` drives two tags-only edits through the real writer and pins that both versions and the stamp they moved survive. (4) Any row carrying `related_id` or `merged_content`: such a row is a thread a reader follows from one memory's history into its successor's (#648), and it records a claim about ANOTHER memory. (5) Any row recorded at or after the BOUND on the repair, `reflectNoOpCutoff` — the instant #727 reached main (`d60aa301`, `2026-09-28 17:14:07` UTC), overridable by `--before <2006-01-02 | RFC 3339>` and resolved by the exported `ResolveCompactCutoff`. (6) EVERY version of a memory whose NEWEST version is a `delete` tombstone — a SCOPE rule, not a fourth retention rule, and it is a separate conjunct (`tombstonedMemorySQL`) beside the newest-version guard in `historyRemovableRowSQL` rather than a phase, because a tombstone restates the state the row held immediately before the DELETE and so is byte-identical to its predecessor exactly as a no-op `reflect` is. A deleted memory has no `memories` row, so `CreatedAt` falls back to the ANSWERING version's `recorded_at` and removing a version from it CHANGES THE AGE a past read computes — for a memory nobody can edit and nobody can restore. It is stable under the deletion, which is what the fixed-point argument needs: the conjunct reads the memory's newest version and no removable row is ever the newest, so removing rows cannot promote a different one into being newest and cannot un-retire a memory. The cost is nil by construction: a memory's history is frozen the moment it is deleted, so the flood is never written to again. The live memory beside it is still repaired, which is what `TestCompactHistoryLeavesADeletedMemorysHistoryAlone` runs — along with a count through `historyRemovableLikeSQL` showing the two rows are otherwise in reach, because a tombstone rule that is not load-bearing proves nothing. The comparison is a STRING comparison on `recorded_at`, which is exact for the layout every writer produces and for the whole-day form `date()` leaves behind (a whole-day value sorts before any stamp with a time on that day, which is right for a row that old), and and the bound's comparison is NOT a licence to skip an unreadable `recorded_at`: the comparison is a string comparison, and an unreadable value can land on either side of it — the epoch form a Unix writer left behind sorts BELOW the bound and would be read as damage, and any other malformed value sorts wherever its characters put it. So nothing in this file leans on the comparison for that. What is left alone is what the delete's own predicate never named, and `restorableStamp` reports a stamp it cannot read rather than guessing at one. The bound is LOAD-BEARING rather than belt-and-braces, because a current build still writes a byte-identical `reflect` version ON PURPOSE: a consolidation merge whose survivor is one of its own sources carries the union of that source's tags, `ReplaceNonManual`'s `reusePreservesAge` branch writes them, and the version it appends restates `content`, `category`, `importance`, `resolved_at` and `source` byte for byte. `TestAReflectReuseChangesTagsWithoutChangingAnyRecordedColumn` runs that path through a real sqlite consolidation tier and shows the version it files, so the claim is driven rather than argued. No state column can recognise that row, so the discriminator is a TIME: a row this build wrote is a current writer's business, and a row written before the fix shipped is this repair's. The bound reaches `--fix-updated-at`'s evidence gate for the same reason — a post-fix restatement is a deliberate change that moved `updated_at` on purpose, so it may not be read as proof that a reflection did. The cost is stated rather than hidden: a store whose clock is behind (a restored backup, a copied database) holds rows that look like damage and are not, and `--before` is how an operator says so after re-reading the dry run. The removable set is a **fixed point of the deletion** — a removable row has the same state as its predecessor, so removing it makes the row after it compare against that predecessor and reach the same answer, and it is never the newest, so removing it cannot promote another row into being newest — which is what lets the apply run in bounded `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` batches (each through `Store.beginWrite`, so #671's bounded BEGIN retry and its wait/hold measurement ride on them) and lets a dry run answer with one count and be exact rather than a sample. Guard (2) earns its place, and the case it is the ONLY answer for is a store whose clock is behind: it can hold a byte-identical `reflect` version older than the bound, and the bound alone would let the compaction take a memory's own latest statement about itself. One such row per memory, never the last. It is NOT what keeps a current build's restatement — the bound is, and a store running the lifecycle does not re-grow what was pruned because the rows it adds are all newer than any bound, so they are outside the repair entirely. `TestCompactHistoryLeavesTheRestatementACurrentWriterStillMakes` pins that with the harder half: TWO re-tag passes, so the first version is not the newest and the guard cannot be what spared it, and a full apply still removes nothing and rewinds no stamp. `--fix-updated-at` is the second repair and a separate flag, and it is gated on the SAME evidence for the same reason: a live memory's `updated_at` is restored to the `recorded_at` of the **ANCHOR**, but only where a REMOVABLE version sits **above** that anchor — that version is the only evidence a reflection run moved the stamp, and moving a stamp nothing accounts for would be the same invention #727 removed, one layer down.

**The anchor is the newest version this repair will NOT remove AND whose WRITER moved `updated_at` in the same statement that filed it** — that is, the newest KEPT row in `stampMovingPhases`. It is NOT the newest version that changed its recorded state, and the two are different questions: a `delete` or a state change records what the memory SAYS, and `stampMovingPhases` records what a writer did to the STAMP. The state-change half was this rule's first version and it was wrong in the permissive direction, which is the one that invents. `SetResolved` writes `resolved_at` and says in as many words that it leaves `updated_at` alone; `Upsert`'s unsaturated fold writes `importance` and moves nothing; `RestoreSnapshot` puts the snapshot's own text back. Any of them became the anchor, and the repair then set a memory's stamp to an instant the store had never held on that column — 49 of 288 restored stamps on one real store. Worse, a memory's FIRST version passes the state comparison trivially (a first version has no predecessor, the `max` is NULL, nothing matches, and it reads as a change), so a pre-v17 memory whose only version was a `ClearResolved` had its stamp "restored" to the moment that row was written, with seven no-op reflects above it. `TestCompactHistoryFixUpdatedAtAnchorsOnAStampMoveNotOnAStateChange` drives `SetResolved` and a real `FoldOnly` fold and pins the answer is the SAVE's instant; `TestCompactHistoryLeavesAStampWithNoRecordedStampWrite` pins the first-version case. There is nothing else to check the claim against, and that is the reason for the phase list rather than a cleverer predicate: `memory_history` records no `updated_at` at all, so a version row cannot be compared with the stamp it is supposed to account for. The only evidence a stamp write happened is the IDENTITY of the writer, and the writer is what the phase names. The deliberate-writer half is kept rather than dropped: `UpdateMemory` files a state-identical `update` version on a tags-only edit and `ReplaceNonManual`'s `reusePreservesAge` branch files a state-identical `reflect` version when a consolidation merge's survivor carries the union of its sources' tags — and both bump `updated_at` ON PURPOSE, so under a reading that could not see the writer they would be skipped and a pre-#727 no-op reflect row beneath them would become "evidence" of a rewind over a change the store had just made and recorded. `TestCompactHistoryDoesNotRewindADeliberateBumpUnderARemovableRow` runs the interleaving and pins it. The one clause of the removable predicate deliberately NOT part of the anchor is the NEWEST-VERSION guard, and the reason is arithmetic rather than judgement: a removable row is by definition not the newest, so treating the newest row as an anchor would put the anchor above every removable row, `removableLast > anchor` would never hold, and the whole flag would report 0 on a store holding nothing but the damage. A memory's newest version is the last row its last reflect run wrote, so it has to be allowed to be that run's evidence. A stamp-moving version's `recorded_at` is that same `updated_at`, read from the live row by the history INSERT (`appendHistoryGroupTx`) rather than taken from the column default: `datetime('now')` is evaluated per statement, so a second boundary between the writer's UPDATE and the INSERT would otherwise file the version one second after the row it records. The other phases keep the clock (`TestAStampMovingVersionCarriesTheUpdatedAtItsWriterLeft`).

The anchor is ALSO not every row the repair would not remove. A `supersede`/`unsupersede` version is non-removable, and `CreateLink` writes no `memories` row at all — so as an anchor it would close the gate for that memory permanently, on the very store where the supersede came after the damage. `stampMovingPhases` is what separates the two groups, and it is **three** phases long because three writers bump the column: `save` (`Store.Create`'s INSERT default), `update` (`UpdateMemoryWithOptions`) and `reflect` (`ReplaceNonManual`'s reuse branches and the provenance stamp it applies to an absorbing reuse). Everything else files a version without touching a live memory's stamp: `MarkResolved` changes `resolved_at` and says in as many words that it deliberately leaves `updated_at` alone, `CreateLink` writes no memory row, and `Upsert`'s importance fold files `phaseMerge` with the folded text and moves nothing. `TestCompactHistoryStillRepairsAMemoryThatWasSupersededAfterwards` runs every phase outside that set and pins that none of them closes the gate. The membership is audited PER WRITER, not per phase, because the rule is that EVERY writer of a listed phase moves the column: a phase with one writer that moves it and a second that does not would make the version's own phase an unreliable witness, and the anchor would land on a row whose writer left the stamp alone — the same invention the state-change reading produced. The audit: `save` has five INSERT writers (`insertMemory`, `seedMemoryTx`, both `UpsertWithOptions` insert branches, `RecordDecision`'s companion) and every one takes the column default `datetime('now')`; `update` has one (`UpdateMemoryWithOptions`, ending `tags = ?, updated_at = datetime('now')`); `reflect` has four in `ReplaceNonManual` (the `reusePreservesAge` UPDATE, the default reuse UPDATE, the fresh INSERT on the column default, and `markReusedSuccessorSourceTx`, the provenance stamp a reuse that absorbs a consumed source applies, whose UPDATE sets `source` and `updated_at = datetime('now')` together), and its `reuseChangesNothing` branch files no version of its own — when the stamp turns it into a write the pass appends it to the same reflected set, and that stamp moves the column, so the branch is either no writer of the phase or one of these four. Nothing was removed, and nothing needed to be. `allHistoryPhases()` exists so both classifiers are checkable against the schema's own phase set rather than a hand-written list, and `TestStampMovingPhasesNamesOnlyPhasesWithAWriter` asserts a three-way partition — every phase is either in `stampMovingPhases`, or staged by `inertPhaseRows` for the loop below, or `phaseDelete` — so a phase added to the schema has to be classified in one place or two. Getting this list wrong in the permissive direction is a **silent permanent** loss of the repair: the report then reads `0 updated_at restored` about a stamp that is still a reflect run's time, which is the same wrong orientation `ghost supersede` and `--skip-unchanged` read. The `related_id` and `merged_content` clauses are not what protects those rows — their **phase** is — and no shipped writer files a `reflect` version carrying either, so a fixture that staged one would be pinning the predicate against a shape nothing emits. `phaseDelete` is the one phase `TestCompactHistoryStillRepairsAMemoryThatWasSupersededAfterwards` does NOT run, and the reason is structural rather than an omission: `Delete` REMOVES the `memories` row, so a `delete` version that is a memory's newest version belongs to a memory the stamp repair cannot reach (it joins `memories`), and the one live shape in which a newest version is a tombstone is now outside the repair by name — rule (6) above is its test.

In SQL the anchor is `NOT removable-like AND phase IN stampMovingPhases`, and the parentheses around the first are load-bearing: `historyRemovableLikeSQL` opens with an `EXISTS`, so `NOT EXISTS (…) AND a.phase IN (…)` and `NOT EXISTS (…) a.phase IN (…)` are not the same query, and only one of them is the one anyone is reading. `TestCompactHistoryFixUpdatedAtRestoresTheLastStampWrite` catches it because every memory in it has both kinds of row. The two predicates are one function apart — `historyRemovableLikeSQL` and `historyRemovableRowSQL` — and the two retention guards (newest version, tombstoned memory) live in the second while the anchor takes the first, which is what lets a newest `reflect` version be the damage and a `resolve` version be an evidence-free row above the anchor. The pass therefore runs BEFORE the delete that removes its evidence, and both modes run in that same order, which is what keeps a dry run and the apply it previews in step. Then, and only then, the direction: **backward** only, since the damage moved a stamp forward, and a stamp already at or before the target is left alone. Both stamps are read through `ParseStamp` and the restored one is written in `StoredStampLayout`, because the two columns are written by different statements. `restorableStamp` returns THREE outcomes and they are three facts: restorable, unreadable (a stamp either layout cannot read — left alone, counted as `StampsUnreadable`), and **UNRECORDED** (no kept version of the memory was written by a stamp-moving writer at all, so `target` is the `COALESCE`ed zero the candidate statement substitutes). The last is checked BEFORE any parse, because an absent anchor and an unreadable value are different faults and merging them would send an operator looking for a timestamp shape that is not the problem. Its stamp is left exactly as it is and counted as `StampsUnrecorded`; the versions above it are still removed, since the delete never depended on there being a stamp to move. The `LEFT JOIN` on the anchor and the `COALESCE` around `recorded_at` exist so such a memory REACHES the caller rather than being dropped by an inner join: a row silently missing from a repair is a memory whose stamp reads as a reflect run's time with nothing to say so. The consequence the resolve writers force is now stated rather than left implicit: because `SetResolved` and `ClearResolved` do **not** bump `updated_at`, a memory whose last recorded change was a resolve has the resolve SKIPPED as the anchor — the stamp goes back to the last write that really moved it, and on a memory with nothing else that is the save. That is the difference between "the last change to the memory" and "the last change to the stamp", and only the second is what `updated_at` ever held.
- A purge erases the BYTES, not only the rows (#1043): `DeleteWithOptions(PurgeHistory)` and `Store.PurgeMemoryHistory` promise the redacted text is gone, and until #1043 that promise held for a row scan and not for a byte scan — a freed b-tree cell keeps its content when `secure_delete` is OFF, and a `DELETE` on an external-content FTS5 table fires a trigger that writes a 'delete' tombstone but leaves the deleted row's tokens in the segment blobs until a merge rewrites them, which no merge is obliged to run. Three things make the byte scan come back empty. `OpenDB`'s DSN carries `secure_delete(ON)`, so a freed page is zeroed as it is freed — `FAST` only overwrites the deleted cell, not the page's free space where a spilled content value lives and not the FTS5 segment blobs. The purge rebuilds the whole index inside its own transaction (`rebuildFTSAfterPurgeTx`, `INSERT INTO memories_fts(memories_fts) VALUES('rebuild')`) AFTER the row is deleted, so the index is re-derived from a content table that no longer holds it; it runs in the purge transaction so the row and every index of it move together, and the already-deleted direction runs it too, for the tokens a plain delete's tombstone left in a segment that direction never rewrites. Then a post-commit `PRAGMA wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)` (`checkpointAfterPurge`) folds the zeroed pages into the main file and drops the WAL frames the original writes left, which a `PASSIVE` checkpoint would not truncate. The checkpoint reads its own result row rather than discarding it: `wal_checkpoint` does not raise an error when another connection holds a read snapshot for the whole busy_timeout, it returns `(busy, log, checkpointed)`, and `busy != 0` or a log the run did not fold means the purge's frames are still in the -wal. Both purge entry points return a `PurgeOutcome` carrying `CheckpointComplete`, so a caller says the text stays in the log rather than that the erase is done — the delete has already committed, so this is a warning, not a rollback. The checkpoint cannot run inside a transaction (`wal_checkpoint` is a no-op there), so it runs after the commit on the same pinned connection (`SetMaxOpenConns(1)` is why it is not moved out of the store mutex: the busy wait occupies that one connection, so every other store operation queues for it either way), and a `PRAGMA` is a read to the write-seam scan and needs no exemption. Two reaches the store cannot measure are named so the caller does not over-claim: `secure_delete` only zeroes bytes freed from the moment the connection opened, so text an OLDER build freed before it was on stays in the file's free space until a compaction rewrites it — Ghost never VACUUMs the live database in place, because that renumbers rowids — and only a fresh `ghost backup` (VACUUM INTO) is a copy without it; a backup taken before the purge, or another machine's store, is untouched. The connection pragma itself measured within ~10 % on ordinary writes and ~30 % on a bulk delete; the rebuild is the fix's real cost and is O(corpus) rather than O(1) — measured at ~77 µs per ~4 KiB row to re-derive 20 000 rows — which a deliberate redaction can afford. Backups taken before the purge and another machine's store are still out of reach, and the caller-facing messages say so.
- History growth is ONE read with two renderings, and it reuses the compaction's own comparison rather than a second spelling of it. `Store.HistoryGrowth` (`internal/memory/history_growth.go`, #729) is the only implementation; `ghost mcp status` and the `ghost_health` MCP tool both print ITS result fields and ITS `Detail` strings, so a terminal and an agent reading one store cannot be told different things about it — a second threshold or a second sentence in either surface is a second answer to the same question. The numerator is `historyEqualPredecessorSQL`, the function `CompactHistory`'s delete, dry run and `updated_at` pass all use, because a second predicate would let the report disagree with the command that fixes what it reports. The report's share deliberately OVER-counts against that repair: it counts every restatement while `CompactHistory` removes a SUBSET of them, and the subset is LARGE — FIVE guards keep a restatement the share counts: it is the memory's newest version, it names another memory (a thread, not a state), its phase is anything but `reflect`, it was recorded at or after the repair's `--before` bound, or its memory has since been DELETED. So the share is how much noise the table carries, not how much is disposable, and `TestHistoryGrowthNoOpShareAgreesWithWhatCompactWouldRemove` reconciles the difference row by row — STATING the bound through `HistoryCompactOptions.Before` rather than inheriting the default, because on any clock past #727 the default excludes every row a 24-hour-window fixture writes, so the run would remove nothing and the test would pass while saying nothing about how the two commands relate. THE ADVICE MATCHES THAT SUBSET and is pinned in BOTH directions: the finding says the retention caps are what evict a restatement, because the bound means every row inside a 24-hour window is one the repair keeps by design — the measurement stays true against a moved bound while only the advice goes stale, which is why the sentence asserts both what it names and what it does NOT promise. THE TOMBSTONE GUARD MAKES THE PRESSURE WORSE rather than the advice weaker, and #709 is what made it routine: retention tiers give `expires_at` a day to arrive, so a store now deletes memories nobody asked it to, and each leaves a frozen history no repair will ever reach — those rows still count against the store cap, and its oldest-first trim is the only thing that reclaims them. The store-cap finding is therefore the actionable one on such a store, and the agreement test STAGES the retired memory so the fifth guard is OBSERVED rather than listed: its restatement is otherwise in reach, counted through `historyRemovableLikeSQL`, because a guard that cannot fail proves nothing. The scope is the WHOLE store, because `pruneHistoryTx` ranks over the whole table: both caps are store-level, and a per-project number would be a different question asked of a policy that does not answer per project. The counts are kept in TWO clocks and never blended — the share, the rate and both projections are about the 24-hour window, `MaxVersions` and `TotalRows` are about the table as it stands — and the window is spelled ONCE (`historyWindowModifier`) across both statements, because a share whose denominator is not the set its numerator was counted over is a report that looks right and is not. The per-memory projection measures each memory against its OWN 24-hour rate and takes the soonest, and a memory that wrote nothing in the window is left out entirely rather than counted as infinitely far from the cap: a report about what is moving, not about what is parked. THE DEEPEST MEMORY AND THE MEMORY THE COUNTDOWN IS ABOUT ARE NOT THE SAME MEMORY, and the result keeps them apart rather than blending them: `MaxVersions` is an AGGREGATE for the summary line, while `CapMemoryID`/`CapMemoryVersions`/`CapMemoryRecent` are ONE memory's identity and its OWN two figures, carried precisely so the finding can name it and quote numbers its countdown closes against — a sentence that spliced the store's widest history onto the fastest writer's rate would name no memory at all. The summary line therefore labels the aggregate "deepest memory" rather than "busiest memory", so the line cannot be read as the subject of the finding printed under it, and the id is printed WHOLE because `ghost history <id>` takes it — through `RefusalArg("memory_id", …)` rather than a bare `%s`, because a stored id is not token-guarded (`ghost import` writes an artifact's id verbatim): an ordinary id is rendered whole and quoted, still the operand, while a token-shaped or over-256-byte one is withheld behind a placeholder naming the field and the format. A memory AT its cap is a SEPARATE SENTENCE from a countdown, not a countdown of zero days, because `pruneHistoryTx` holds a memory at exactly the cap while it is still being written — that is the ordinary steady state of an active store, and `historyDaysText` is only ever handed a POSITIVE number of days so it cannot splice a state into a duration frame. A store that opened cleanly and then cannot be read REPORTS THE ERROR in place of the block, rather than printing nothing, on both surfaces — a check that cannot run must not look like a check that had nothing to say. A FINDING IS A `!` LINE AND NEVER A FAILED CHECK: the `mcp status` verdict is about WIRING, so a history finding changes neither the exit code nor the `ghost mcp init` footer — the repair it names is `ghost history compact`, and a footer pointing at init would name a repair for the wrong thing. The thresholds live beside the result rather than beside a surface, and each is `>` at a documented value: a restatement share above `HistoryNoOpShareWarn` (20%, and exactly 20% is silent, because a threshold that fires on the ordinary shape of a working store teaches an operator to ignore the line) and a cap inside `HistoryCapHorizonDays` (14, long enough to compact at leisure and short enough that the warning is not the first notice of a table that has been full for a month). There is NO `recorded_at` INDEX, deliberately and on a measurement: the window filter cannot use one because `recorded_at` is the second column of `idx_history_memory`, and the pass is bounded because `pruneHistoryTx` holds the table at the store cap on the write path of every save; a standalone index would cost a fifth of the cost of writing the history row at all, permanently, to save a couple of milliseconds on a status line (1.7 ms typical, 183 ms worst case at 20 000 rows), and `migrate_test.go` asserts that absence as an ABSENCE. The cost is bounded by pinning the other side instead: `TestHistoryGrowthQueryPlanIsOnePass` holds the plan to one scan and two index seeks with no temporary B-tree, and a linear-in-table-size canary holds the wall clock. The read takes the store's READ lock across its two statements (a consistent snapshot), and the lock is taken in `HistoryGrowth` rather than inside the per-memory helper so that the FIRST statement is inside it — a lifecycle pass or a compaction from this process appending or deleting between the two statements would leave `TotalRows` and `RowsInWindow` describing different instants, and `DaysToStoreCap` is one of those numbers divided by the other. A read leaves no trace, so a statement that has not run is indistinguishable from one that has: `TestHistoryGrowthIsExcludedByAWriter` reaches between the two statements through the `historyGrowthAfterWindow` seam, which is nil in production. It opens NO transaction, for the reason `compactHistoryPreview` opens none: nothing is written, so there is no write lock to take and a writer must not queue behind a status line for the length of two scans
- Evidence provenance: `memory_provenance` (schema v18, #673) is the EVIDENCE table — SEVERAL rows per memory, one per observation, answering "who or what supports this memory", which the change log above cannot: a fact claude-code reported in one session, codex in another, and a human verified is three rows here and one row of mutable columns on `memories`, which can only remember the last. Three concepts stay distinct and are not interchangeable: SOURCE is how Ghost received a memory (`memories.source`: mcp, reflection, import, manual), AGENT is which agent produced or observed it (`agent`), PROVENANCE is the evidence records themselves. `memory_id` CASCADES (unlike the change log's deliberately FK-free `memory_id`) because "3 observations support this" is a claim about a memory, and once the memory is gone the claim is about nothing; a purge deletes these rows explicitly as well, because `PurgeMemoryHistory` leaves the memory in place and no cascade fires for a row that stays — and it deletes the `memory_snapshot_evidence` rows too, since a snapshot is the rollback point for a whole PROJECT, so its evidence would otherwise outlive the purge by however many reflects prune that snapshot Every value except `kind` is NULL when the host did not report it — a guessed agent or session is a provenance claim nobody made, and `observed_at` is NULL for the migration's seed because the seed can say what the columns hold now and not when the fact was first seen. Writers, each in the SAME transaction as the write, each ONE statement: `Create` and `Upsert`'s insert append `observed`; a near-duplicate FOLD appends the second report to the SURVIVING memory (the point of the issue — the folding agent used to be discarded with the incoming wording, and `FoldOnly` stores no row of its own so without this the report would leave no trace); `RecordDecision` appends `observed` for its companion memory (an ordinary memory search returns and a later save folds against), empty of provenance because the decision tool reports none; `ReplaceNonManual`'s FRESH INSERT carries its sources' records onto the new id with `carried_from` naming each source — a rewrite or a merge mints an id whose evidence the cascade would otherwise have just deleted, which would make every consolidated memory read "no recorded evidence" forever, so the emissions loop runs BEFORE the delete and a test fails if the two swap; the REUSE branch appends nothing for the reused row itself, which never changed identity, but when the emission's `ReplacesIDs` names OTHER sources beside it (a merge whose text is byte-identical to one source) those rows are deleted into it exactly as a fresh insert's sources are, so it carries their records with `carried_from` too and fills the reused row's UNSET claims from them (`fillReusedClaimsTx`: the reused row is the first source and its own values win — except that the decision links the absorbed sources carried are APPENDED to its own `source_ref` (`boundedDecisionRefs`), because a link is a list and keeping the row's value whole left an absorbed decision with no companion (#1206) — the window is filled only when the row states neither boundary, and no `updated_at` or history row is written since `memory_history` records none of those columns) — before #1207 a reuse-branch merge dropped the absorbed rows' evidence and claims, a decision companion's link among them; `ImportMemory` appends `imported` plus whatever records the artifact carried (and NOT `carried_from`, which names a memory this store has never heard of); `RestoreSnapshot` restores a re-created row's records from `memory_snapshot_evidence` (a table, because a memory has several records, pruned with the snapshots — by `ReplaceNonManual`'s prune and by `DeleteProject`'s own, since a project delete also takes its snapshots through the projects cascade —) and touches NOTHING for a row it updates in place — the snapshot holds an older copy of that row's evidence, and restoring it would drop every corroboration recorded since — with ONE exception: the records a merge carried onto an in-place row from a row this snapshot brings back (`carried_from` naming a snapshot row: ReplaceNonManual's reuse-branch merge and FoldRows' survivor) are removed (`uncarryRestoredSourcesTx`), because the absorbed row returns with its own records and a left-behind copy would count each of them twice beside a restored `verified_at` that no longer states them. `AppendVerifiedEvidenceTx` is the exported seam the validity writers (#677) own the reserved `verified` kind through, and they now CALL it. State the contract as an OUTCOME, because the mechanisms differ on purpose and a reader filtering by `kind` sees three shapes for one rule: every writer that stores a `verified_at` leaves a record carrying a verification stamp in the same transaction, and `EvidenceCounts.Verified` counts any record carrying one whatever its kind. The live callers are `insertMemory` (behind `Create`) and all three of `UpsertWithOptions`' branches plus `UpdateMemoryWithOptions`; each goes through `appendVerificationIfStatedTx`, which gates on the caller stating a verification IN THIS CALL and never on the column, because a partial update COALESCEs a `verified_at` an earlier save wrote and appending for that would record a check nobody made. Their stamp is the STORE's clock, not the caller's (a verifier must not be able to date its own check), so the column carries what was claimed and the record carries when Ghost heard; the two are different facts and both are kept. `UpdateMemoryWithOptions` appends the `verified` leg but NOT an `observed` one — an edit is a change to a fact already reported, not a new report of it, and widening #673's design from inside #575 would inflate the report count on every content edit. THREE more writers store a `verified_at` and are deliberately not callers, each for its own reason. `ImportMemory` is IN the set and not among the callers: the artifact carries an OBSERVATION, so a check happened and is attested, and it rides the import's own `imported` record instead of adding a `verified` one — same store-clock stamp, same count, no second statement. That arrival stamp is CONDITIONAL, and the condition is the round trip: it is skipped whenever ANY carried record already holds a stamp, kind-agnostically, because a record with a stamp says the check happened and stamping again would make a corpus that crosses a machine boundary N times report N+1 verifications of one check. Kind-agnostic rather than `verified`-only because the second hop's artifact carries the FIRST hop's `imported` arrival, and that is the only stamped record it may hold — a kind-specific guard re-inflates on every hop after the first. The condition is a NON-EMPTY value, since a hand-edited artifact can carry `"verified_at": ""` and every reader treats that as no claim. `CreateFromCorpus` leaves NO record carrying a stamp — but it still records, because `insertMemory` appends the ingestion's `observed` row on that route unconditionally, and "no verified record" is the whole of it; a third-party dataset's `verified_at` is a value in a column with no observation behind it, so there is no event and the only stamp a record could carry would manufacture one. It opts out through `insertOptions.recordVerification` because it shares `insertMemory` with `Create`, which is why it needed a PARAMETER and not a comment — and the column keeps the dataset's value either way, since losing the dataset's own data would be the opposite defect. `RestoreSnapshot` writes it in pure SQL from the snapshot table, and a restore is a faithful copy of a corpus whose evidence rows travel with it, so appending per restored row would double-count every check the snapshot holds.
