That enumeration has been wrong three times — `CreateFromCorpus`, then `ImportMemory`, then by over-claiming what its own enforcement could see — every time because prose is not checkable, so it is ALSO two tests that answer different questions. `TestEveryVerifiedAtWriterIsEnumerated` asserts the OUTCOME each writer leaves: whether a record carries a stamp, and of what kind. `TestEveryVerifiedAtMentionIsClassified` walks `internal/memory` with `go/parser` and requires every function naming `verified_at` IN ITS OWN BODY to be in one of FOUR classifications (writes a memory row, touches a snapshot or evidence side, is a migration or reader, or genuinely SPANS two of those surfaces — `ReplaceNonManual` does, since its fresh-insert path now writes the memory row's validity and provenance as well as the snapshot tables), so a new one cannot be added without being placed; it found four mentions a hand-written list had missed on its first run, all scanners, and caught two of this PR's own new functions when they landed. The limit of that one is part of the claim: it does not resolve the column out of a package-level literal, so `migrateV10` — the migration that ADDS the column, naming it only through `phase1aProvenanceColumns` — is invisible to it, and `TestColumnListWritersAreClassified` closes that one indirection by requiring every function iterating that list to be classified. `AppendVerifiedEvidenceTx` is placed explicitly too, since the writers reach the column THROUGH it and its own body names no column. A function reaching the column by some other indirection is still invisible, and that is the honest edge of the guarantee. There is no growth prune, deliberately: it would be a second statement inside the write transaction, which is what took `TestMultiProcessSharedDatabase` to SQLITE_BUSY for the change log's prune, and the cascade keeps deleted memories from contributing. `migrateV18` REFUSES a database whose `memory_provenance` is not the EVIDENCE table (a pre-rename #664 dev build used the reserved name for the change log) rather than converting or dropping it, naming `sqlite3 <db> 'DROP TABLE memory_provenance'`, and `OpenDB` runs the same check BEFORE the pre-migration backup so a refusal costs no copy and its same-second retry reports the real error — the check tests for `id`/`memory_id`/`kind` ONLY, so it is an identity test and not a version test: a future column here must not make it refuse a legitimate store with a remedy that DROPs its evidence — initSQL's CREATE INDEX cannot catch that one, because the change log has a `memory_id` too, and a warning nobody sees leaves every writer failing on the same missing column. Otherwise it seeds one `legacy` row per memory that recorded at least one of agent/session_id/source_ref/confidence, and NONE for a memory that recorded nothing — an evidence row with nothing in it still says "this was observed", and a row per memory would make every store report one observation, the same fabrication as inventing a session id. Read it with `Store.MemoryProvenance` (oldest first, rowid) and `Store.MemoryEvidenceCounts`, whose `Label()` renders "supported by 2 observations, 1 verified"; the assembler records the counts in stage 4's trace (`Signals.Evidence`, carried on `memory.Candidate` because that is the only read the assembler has) and NOTHING RANKS ON THEM — the stage's multiplier stays 1.0 until a measured change justifies one. The portable artifact (v2) carries the records, nested under the memory they support because a bare evidence line cannot be attributed; the Obsidian mirror does not — it is a human-readable mirror, not a transfer format
- Memory links: `memory_links` edge table auto-populated by cosine similarity (internal/linking worker); links cascade-delete with memories and self-heal after reflection. A graph-expansion ranking bonus was evaluated and removed — dominated by a deeper vector-k (links and the vector leg are both cosine); the link graph is retained for Obsidian export and supersedes ranking (see `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-20-graph-expansion-stays-off-design.md`). No edge may join a scope-conflicting pair: every writer refuses one (`Upsert`'s dedup probes, the linker's `related` edge, `ghost supersede`'s candidates) and every reader ignores one already stored — the two ranking guards (`DemotionPenalties`, `SupersedePenalties`), `Store.SupersedesWithin`, `ghost resolve`'s supersedes piggyback and the repair pass's matching floor (`scopeCompatibleSupersedes`, which needed `GetByIDs` on `resolveStore`/`reassessStore`/`resolveCapableStore` for the link endpoints' scopes), and the two fold-target liveness checks (`foldTargetStillLive` + the cross-category probe). Scope exempts a pair from ranking and folding; it never deletes a `memory_links` row. Where the exemption is decided: inside the candidate query, always, because the same `LIMIT` picks the candidates — a scope check applied after the cut spends the budget on rows the caller may not use and misses a compatible one ranked just below (#665). So the two cosine writers narrow before their window (`SearchVectorScoped`, in Go over the scan, so its top-k is the limit and there is no statement to fold the rule into) so their neighbour budgets count only linkable rows, and `Upsert`'s two dedup probes carry the rule inside their own `LIMIT 15` for exactly that reason. `foldTargetStillLive` is the one statement that carries it without a window to protect (it names one row by id) and for a different reason: a scope-conflicting `supersedes` edge is not a verdict that row may be acted on. Both are one rule in two forms, `scopesConflictSQL` in `internal/memory/scope.go`, held to `ScopesConflict` by `TestScopesConflictSQLAgreesWithScopesConflict`
- Resolution classifier: `ghost resolve` runs a keyword prefilter, then a deterministic KEEP veto, then batches up to 8 notes per KEEP-biased CLI-harness classify call (`claude`/`opencode`/`codex`/`goose` subprocess, numbered `N: RESOLVED | closed-by: <fact>|KEEP` reply judged by a strict first-field line parser) to mark `resolved_at`, dropping resolved-evidence memories (changelogs, cost estimates, closed experiment notes) from ranked injection while keeping them searchable. KEEP-biased in code, not prose (#640: an independent judge found ~1 resolve in 3 on a real DB had buried durable knowledge): `resolve.VetoKeep` settles a candidate KEEP with no harness call when its text carries an imperative (`never|do not|don't|must|always|required`) or an open marker (`not yet|outstanding|still pending|still open|still stale|unresolved|todo`), all case-insensitive and word-bounded; the rubric asks the fresh-session question (would an agent starting fresh make a mistake, repeat work, or break a rule without this?) and says dates, PR numbers, commit hashes and "fixed in" do not resolve a note; a RESOLVED must carry a non-empty `closed-by:` or the parser reads it as KEEP. The veto guards the classifier only — the supersedes-edge piggyback and correction pairing still demote. Only explicit KEEP verdicts are cached in `memories.resolve_kept_hash`, under `resolve.KeepStamp` (schema v7; the v3 hash prefix invalidates the v1 implicit-KEEP and v2 explicit-KEEP caches, so #640's re-judged rules are re-asked) — the bare content hash when the verdict saw no negative audit evidence, that hash combined with the evidence when it did (#880), so un-doubted content keeps the byte-for-byte pre-#880 value and a contradiction recorded after a KEEP re-asks it exactly once — once meaning one re-ask per verdict that COMES BACK, because a stamp is written only for an explicit KEEP; the evidence read sits above the gate and is bounded to a pass with something at the gate, so the one new payer is a converged pass, which pays one bounded read and still no call. UNKNOWN parse failures remain uncached and are therefore retried — asked and billed again on EVERY later pass until the parser reads them — so a converged project makes no calls only after explicit KEEPs. Dry-run writes nothing, `--apply` writes resolved_at and explicit KEEP cache entries; transport or verdict-count failures fail fatally rather than applying incomplete results, while UNKNOWN lines remain eligible for a later pass. The stamp is CONTENT-AND-SCOPE-fresh (#1052): writes go through `memory.SetResolvedJudged`, whose SELECT and UPDATE both match the exact text the verdict was judged on, and the project the pass read the row under, so a confirmed row a concurrent save or edit rewrote during the classify call is NOT stamped and is counted separately as `Stale` — content rather than `updated_at`, because the classifier is handed content and the KEEP cache is content-keyed, so a metadata-only edit never invalidates a verdict and two writes in one clock second cannot confuse a rewrite with the save that stored the judged text. The CAS pairs are TRIPLES and the project is the second axis: `PromoteToGlobal` moves a row by changing `project_id` only — no content, no `updated_at` — so a content match alone would keep a verdict alive over a row that left the pass's project mid-classify, which is a project-scoped pass deciding for `_global` and exactly what MarkResolved refuses. The triple `(id, content, project_id)` matches what the pass READ, so the promoted row is eligible yet is no longer the row the verdict was about: NOT stamped, and REPORTED stale like a rewrite rather than buried in silence. A row declined for any OTHER reason (pinned, recategorized or already resolved, retention-exempt) stays silent exactly as before. `ghost resolve <project> --reassess` is the repair path for the resolutions the pass already got wrong: it re-runs the vetoes and the classifier (no keyword prefilter) over memories that are ALREADY resolved, prints the ones that now come back KEEP, and with `--apply` clears their `resolved_at` via `memory.ClearResolved` and caches the classifier's KEEP verdicts under the same `KeepStamp` over the same audit evidence the ordinary pass reads — it reads that evidence once for its pool, appends it to the ask and stamps what came back with it, so both passes write and read one key and a repaired note is not re-asked one lifecycle later (#880); SCOPE THAT REPAIR with `--only <id>[,<id>...]` (full ids or 8+ character hex prefixes) or `--only-file <path>` (one per line, `#` comments allowed; both may be given, the list is the union) — both are refused without `--reassess`, because the ordinary pass has no resolved pool to narrow, and a scoped report says how much of the project it read ("3 of 143 already resolved judged") instead of describing a pool it never looked at. Scoping narrows WHICH rows are judged and nothing else: every floor below still holds a judged row back. A selector that names nothing (mistyped, another project, or already cleared by an earlier repair) is reported and skipped, never an error for the run; an ambiguous prefix IS an error, because nothing can be said about which rows were meant. #698 measured why: an unscoped repair over a real store proposed un-hiding 143 memories and an independent judge sampling 40 found ~35% stale (completed changelogs, PR/host snapshots, notes a newer memory had superseded, a retired code path). `ghost supersede --reassess --apply` therefore prints its own follow-up — the exact `ghost resolve <project> --reassess --only <ids> --apply` over the withdrawn edges' targets — and writes the same id list under the data dir's scratch (`scratch.Root()`, `supersede-reassess-<project>-<ts>.ids`) for `--only-file`; the file is collected by scratch.Reap after 14 days (idFileStaleAge), so a repair nobody ran does not accumulate against the budget forever. A dry run prints neither, because it withdrew nothing and resolve would refuse the command while the edges are live. The repair path is also the only place `VetoKeep` does not settle KEEP by itself: when the same project holds a NEWER unresolved memory sharing >=2 of the note's key identifiers — a backticked span, an `#NNN` reference, a file name, a host name (`resolve.keyIdentifiers`, `subjectIndex.shadowing`; NOT a bare number or a version, which are what changes when a note goes stale) — the note goes to the classifier instead, because a veto protects a phrase and not a claim. The pool is `ResolveCandidates` for the correction floor's reason: a memory that is itself resolved is a row the next ordinary pass will not see. `Run`'s veto is unchanged, and the asymmetry is why: there a false veto keeps a note OUT of the resolution (one noisy memory, a later pass can still bury it), here it un-hides one permanently; a failed clear is fatal, a failed cache write only warns; `memory.ClearResolved` runs every batch in one transaction so "a failed clear repaired nothing" is literally true. It honours Run's two free demotions as a floor — a row a live `supersedes` edge still asserts, or an older row a NEWER *unresolved* correction pairs with (the same pool Run searches, via `correctionPairTargetsFrom`), is counted as `Demoted` and left resolved, because Run fires both before the veto and the KEEP cache, so clearing it would report a repair the next pass undoes. The pairing is re-checked after the re-KEEP set is known (`holdBack`), because a correction that is itself repaired leaves the resolved pool and would re-assert its pairing; both the floor and the hold-back pair only Run's keyword-prefiltered subset, since a keyword-free row is never a pairing target and holding it would be a permanent, mislabelled hold no later pass could undo. THE RE-CHECK READS THE POOL THE NEXT ORDINARY PASS WILL READ — the unresolved pool PLUS the rows this run clears — and uses it for BOTH the frequency count and the correction search, the two jobs `Run` gives its own `ResolveCandidates` pool (#712). Counted over the live pool alone, a row this run clears is missing from the count, so its subject tokens look rarer than the next pass will find them and a pairing the next pass will NOT make holds a row back for a second, identical pass to release. So the re-check ITERATES, bounded at `maxHoldBackRounds` (8): holding a row takes it back out of that pool, which lowers the frequency of the tokens it carries, which can turn a token rare that was not and reveal a pairing the previous round was blind to. WHAT TERMINATES THE LOOP IS NOT THE PAIRINGS, and the difference matters: a held-back row leaves the pool the re-check reads, and that pool is BOTH the frequency pool and the correction pool, so dropping a row does two OPPOSITE things — it lowers the document frequency of the tokens the row carries, which can reveal a pairing the round before was blind to, AND, when the row is correction-marked, it removes every pairing that row was asserting. A later round can therefore report FEWER pairings than the round before it (`TestHoldBackPairingsAreNotMonotoneAcrossRounds` is the case that does), and the ONLY termination invariant is that every round which finds anything removes at least one row from the repair set, which bounds the loop by `len(repair)` however the pairings move. So `maxHoldBackRounds` caps the WORK ONE RUN DOES, not safety: with it and without it the re-check ends, and what changes is whether one run reaches its fixed point or leaves work for the next. Dropping is ONE-WAY — a row held is never re-added, and a held-back correction therefore cannot free its row again — precisely because the pairings can retreat, and without that rule the answer would depend on how they moved between rounds and the LAST round would win rather than the union. A bound reached WITH a row still changing is a finding and not a truncation: `ReassessResult.Rounds`/`BoundHit` carry it and `reassessRoundsLine` prints its own sentence (“a further pass may clear more”), because a run that iterated is reporting an answer no single re-check would have given. Printed ONLY when there is something to say — more than one round, or the bound reached — since a line an operator learns to skip is a line that stops being read when it matters. No round re-asks the harness: the iteration is over the hold-back decision alone, on verdicts the run already holds, because a re-ask is billed work for an answer in hand. THE FLOOR DELIBERATELY STAYS THE CONSERVATIVE ONE, drawing its corrections only from the live pool, so it may over-hold a row the re-check releases — the right way round, because an over-held row is left resolved and REPORTED with a reason an operator can check, while an under-held one is cleared and immediately re-stamped by the pass that follows. EVERY HELD ROW IS NAMED, not counted: `ReassessResult.Held` (one entry per row, in pool order) is printed by `reassessHeldLines` as `held by supersedes <source-id>` or `held by correction <id>`, because the two reasons need different remedies — a live edge is undone by `ghost supersede --withdraw`, a correction pairing is undone by nothing and can only be READ — and `Demoted` is `len(Held)`, so the summary count and the list under it cannot disagree. EVERY HOLDER IS NAMED, never just one: a note two newer notes both supersede is held by both and withdrawing one of the two releases nothing, so naming one would send the operator to undo an edge whose undo changes nothing; `HeldMemory.Reason` sorts on a copy (supersedes first, then corrections, each by id) so two runs are comparable. A pairing therefore carries its correction now — `correctionPairingsFrom` returns one entry per (correction, target) pair and `correctionPairTargetsFrom` deduplicates the targets on top of it — so there is still ONE implementation of the rare-token rule and `Run`'s demoted-row order is byte-for-byte what it was. Those ids print WHOLE, unlike every other id on a Ghost report, because here they are OPERANDS rather than references: the source is what `--withdraw` takes, the held row's own id is what a scoped repair names, the correction's id is a note somebody has to go and read, and an eight-character abbreviation of any of them turns a repair already in hand into a second lookup. `Held` and `Rounds` are properties of the DECISION and not of what was written, so a dry run reports the same fixed point `--apply` reaches and writes nothing: a preview reporting a smaller repair than the run would reach is a preview an operator cannot plan from. A reasonless `closed-by` is not a reason: a bare mention of the key without its colon ("no closed-by available") and a placeholder value (`none`, `n/a`, `tbd`, …) are both KEEP — and so is a value whose FIRST WORD declines to name one ("not applicable", "no decision", "cannot name it", "unclear"), because a refusal is a phrase far more often than it is one of the nine placeholder keywords. That grammar is `resolve.ReasonedField`/`resolve.ReasonlessValue` and it is NOT resolve's alone: internal/supersede's `replaced:` field is the same contract for a supersedes verdict (#686), so one list decides both and a value that keeps a note here cannot bury it there. `VetoKeep` is likewise split — `VetoKeepImperative` is the imperative half, exported because internal/supersede's `VetoSupersede` reads the same words for the opposite decision (a rule is not a fact that goes out of date), while the open markers stay resolve's. The repair pass treats a live 'supersedes' edge as a floor, which is why `ghost supersede <project> --reassess --consensus 3 --apply` has to run FIRST: withdrawing the edge is what makes the `resolved_at` it justified clearable, `TestReassessResolvesAChainIntoResolveReassess` runs that chain against a real store, and the e2e suite runs it against the built binary end to end (`TestCLISupersedeReassessFeedsResolveReassess` — write the edge, resolve stamps the target, withdraw it, then the printed `--only-file` clears that one memory and leaves another resolved memory in the same project resolved). That floor is counted per EDGE, not per note: a note two newer notes both supersede is held by either one, so withdrawing one of the two does NOT release it (`Demoted`, nothing cleared, no harness call) and only the last withdrawal does. A withdrawal wider than the edge it names would otherwise hand the note back to injection, where the very next ordinary pass re-stamps it from the surviving edge and no edge ever changes again (`TestReassessHoldsTheNoteWhileAnySupersedesEdgeSurvives`). `resolve.Mark` (`mark.go`) is the OTHER repair, and the only one that goes the other way from a caller's judgement: `ghost resolve <project> --mark <ids>|--mark-file <path> [--apply]` stamps `resolved_at` on the memories a caller NAMES, dry-run by default. It exists because NO pass reaches the case it serves — the ordinary pass only proposes a memory carrying a resolution keyword, and even where it does, a KEEP-biased classifier asked about THAT NOTE is not asked whether another memory in the project supersedes it, so a note a newer note says was fixed is routinely invisible to every pass (`--reassess` cannot help either: it judges what is ALREADY resolved, and this row is not). Like `supersede.Withdraw` it judges NOTHING, so no harness is involved and nothing is billed, and a machine that cannot spawn one can still repair its corpus. The refs are internal/memref's, not a second copy, so a ref means here exactly what it means to `--withdraw`. A request is settled before anything is written and a refusal writes NOTHING, not even the refs that were fine (`TestMarkRefusesAnAmbiguousPrefix` marks neither of two refs when one is ambiguous), and the per-row outcomes are three SEPARATE states rather than one "not marked" — `AlreadyResolved` (the issue's no-op: a memory the previous pass already buried, on which nothing is written including NO history row, because a history row records a write), `Pinned` and `ExemptCategory` (the store's guard, whose refusals have different remedies) — each counted on `MarkResult` off the same rows the report prints, so a count and a list cannot disagree. Only rows IN THE PROJECT may be marked, and that is checked on the READ that can see `project_id`, before any write, because `MemoryIDsByIDPrefix` reaches `_global` for the supersede endpoints' sake and a row every project shares is not one a project asked to bury; the store binds the project AGAIN in the same statement as the stamp, so a caller reaching the store directly cannot cross it either, AND refuses the `_global` SENTINEL beside the empty-project refusal — the binding does not catch it (a promoted row's own project_id IS `_global`, so naming `_global` satisfies every predicate MarkResolved has), and it is refused at the STORE layer rather than re-derived per caller, like every other `_global` refusal here. `resolve.Mark` ALSO refuses `_global` as the named project before any ref is read, which both `ghost resolve --mark` and `ghost_resolve_mark` pass through, because the store's refusal is one a dry run never reaches and the preview would otherwise promise a mark `--apply` refuses; the store's refusal stays as the guard for every other caller. The report's per-row marker defaults to the NEGATIVE claim under `--apply`, never the success, because the eligibility guard is re-checked WHERE THE WRITE HAPPENS and a row pinned or recategorized between the read and the write is declined SILENTLY (another process's decision, not a failure) — a state the result says nothing about, so a default of `marked` would report a change that did not happen. `MarkedMemory.Declined` names it, `MarkResult.Declined` counts it, and both the CLI report and the MCP result fall through to `not marked` for any state they do not recognise, so a state added tomorrow reads as unwritten rather than written.  `Store.MarkResolved` is a separate method rather than a `SetResolved` wrapper for two reasons: it BINDS the project (the pass's ids are already project-scoped by the selection it made), and it RECORDS the performer, so the `resolve` history row it writes is the only resolve row that can say a PERSON decided rather than a classifier judged — which is the whole reason this is a command and not SQL, since the SQL route bypasses `memory_history` entirely and leaves the record showing a memory resolved with nothing saying who said so. It also drops `resolve_kept_hash` in the SAME statement as the stamp, or a later pass would skip the row as `N KEEP cached` and a memory buried on purpose would return the moment anything rewrote its text (#714's second effect). Unlike `supersede.Withdraw` there is NO partial outcome to report: the stamp, the cache clear and the history row are ONE transaction (`TestMarkHistoryAndStampLandInOneTransaction` drops `memory_history` and requires that `resolved_at` not survive), so an error returns NO ids — returning the ones it had collected would claim a stamp the rollback undid. Its inverse is `--reassess --only`, and every applied run prints that command through internal/followup, scoped to the ids it stamped and never to the project. The MCP `ghost_resolve_mark` calls the same core through a `markCapableStore` separate from `resolveCapableStore` — that one must never be able to reach the other direction — and its result names the inverse as a CLI COMMAND, because there is no MCP surface for CLEARING a resolved_at (`ghost_resolve` is the forward pass) and an agent with no shell should be told so rather than pointed at the tool that would bury more memories. Never invoked from the stop hook's synchronous path (that path forbids DB access); the stop hook's detached `ghost lifecycle` spawn (whose resolve phase runs `--apply`, never `--reassess`) and the standalone batch command are the only callers. See `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-26-resolution-classifier-design.md`. The deterministic demotions are re-checked too (#1389): a row demoted by a supersedes edge (mechanism 1) or by a correction memory (mechanism 2) is written through `memory.SetResolvedJustified`, which confirms INSIDE the stamping transaction that at least one of the signals the pass read still holds — the `supersedes`/`llm` edge still live (`invalidated_at IS NULL`), or the correction row still present with the exact content it was paired on (content only, so a correction another process resolved or promoted during the window, content unchanged, still justifies the stamp) — and otherwise refuses the stamp and counts the row in `Stale`, so a `ghost_link_withdraw` or a deleted or rewritten correction landing during the classify call cannot be undone by the in-flight pass. A row with no such signal (a classifier verdict) is written as before; `SetResolvedJudged` is `SetResolvedJustified` with none.
- Classifier backend: `resolve`/`supersede` classify via the calling session's own CLI harness — the backend is picked from the MCP client's reported identity (an opencode session uses the opencode binary, claude uses claude, etc.) via `ai.SourceProvider`, or — on the headless CLI path — from `--source`, else `ai.DetectSource()` (env markers plus process ancestors); if no calling harness can be determined it fails fast with an actionable error. There is no claude-first cascade: `NewSourceProviderForSource("")` is unavailable, and `ai.CLIProvider` is an availability probe only. MCP sampling was retired from this path per SEP-2577 — see `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-24-resolve-sampling-path-design.md`.
- Lifecycle spawn guards: the stop hook spawns the reflect→resolve→supersede chain behind TWO independent per-project guards. `AcquireLifecycleLock`'s PID file says "a run is not already in progress"; `lifecycle.min_interval` (default 30m, `0` to disable) says "a run did not start too recently". The second is the mtime of `lifecycle-<project>.last` in the data dir — a file, not a row, because the stop hook's synchronous path forbids DB access. Only the `ghost lifecycle` process writes it (`mcpinit.TouchLifecycleStart`). A config file that cannot be loaded is the one precondition that no longer dies silently: the coordinator records a failure marker for the phases the environment still enables and touches the start stamp before returning, so the next session start shows the alert and the cooldown still holds. Every other precondition that can exit non-zero still returns before the stamp, so a run that dies before doing any work does not burn a window. Most unknowns read as "run" (no stamp, unreadable, non-regular) so that a bookkeeping problem cannot silently stop maintenance; a stamp dated in the FUTURE is the deliberate exception, and the window is checked on both sides of it: less than one window ahead it reads as INSIDE the window, because a future date is not evidence the window expired, and more than one window ahead it reads as STALE, so a clock that was far ahead cannot hold consolidation off until wall time catches up. `config.LifecycleConfig.MinIntervalDuration` resolves an unreadable value to the default rather than `0`, because `0` means the cooldown is off. The PID file's claim has THREE answers and they stay apart: `claimPidFile` returns `(true, nil)` for a claim, `(false, nil)` only when a live process holds the slot, and `(false, err)` when the claim could not be evaluated (its `.lock` cannot be opened or locked, or the pid cannot be written); `AcquireLifecycleLock` maps the error to its fail-open outcome (no-op release, `true`, error), so `ghost lifecycle` warns and runs the chain rather than reporting a run that does not exist as "already running" (#1116). `safeProjectIDComponent` also refuses an id longer than `maxProjectIDComponentBytes` (NAME_MAX 255 minus the longest derived name's overhead, `lifecycle-<id>.last.tmp<random>`), so the hook never spawns a child for a project whose lock and stamp files cannot be created; its refusals render the id through `memory.RefusalArg` (`TestAcquireLifecycleLockReportsAnUnevaluatableClaimAsAnError`, `TestAcquireLifecycleLockRefusesAnOverlongProjectID`). Every line `runLifecycle` and `runAuditPhase` write themselves goes through `lifecycleLogf`, whose `lifecycleLine` writes `<RFC3339 UTC> lifecycle: project <memory.RefusalArg>: <message>` — the cooldown-skip line's shape — and the first is `run started`, written before the lock: `lifecycle.log` is shared by every project's chain, and a line naming neither its run nor its time cannot be matched to one when two runs interleave. The only direct stderr writes left are the argument error, before any project is known, and the audit report (`TestRunLifecycleWritesNoUnattributedLine`, `TestRunLifecycleEveryLineNamesItsRun`, `TestRunLifecycleOpensWithAStartLine`). EXIT STATUS FOLLOWS THE MARKER: `runLifecycle` returns `1` when the run wrote a failure record — a phase that failed, reflect skipped for want of an LLM backend, a signal that cut the chain short, a config that did not load — and `0` when every phase that ran succeeded or the run was a clean no-op (a lock held by another live run, nothing enabled, a signal that landed after the last phase had finished and so recorded nothing). It RETURNS the code rather than exiting, so the deferred lock release and the marker write run before the process ends, and the dispatch hands it to `os.Exit`; the detached stop-hook spawn reads no status, so only a foreground run and a supervisor see it. A phase disabled by config is not a failure. Pinned by `TestLifecycleExitStatus*` (built binary, since the phases are separate processes) and by the exit-status checks in the two interrupt tests.
- **A COORDINATOR KILLED AFTER THE START STAMP IS A FAILURE WITH NO WRITER, AND THE FILES IT LEFT ARE READ AS THAT FAILURE (#1656).** A SIGKILL, the OOM killer or a power loss cannot run `runLifecycle`'s outcome records, and every one of those writes is inside the process — a config load failure, `FinishLifecycleRun`, or a caught SIGINT/SIGTERM. So the only evidence of an unfinished run is the pair it left: `lifecycle-<project>.last`, written after the claim and the precondition gates, and `lifecycle-<project>.pid`, which a run removes only on the way out with an outcome recorded. `unfinishedLifecycleRun` (`internal/mcpinit/lifecyclecooldown.go`) reads both as one fact — both regular files, the claim naming no live process, the stamp's mtime as the run's start — and `readLifecycleFailure` returns it as a synthesized marker when no stored marker does, so the next session-start alert is raised by the same renderer and the same guards. A claim alone is not enough: a run killed between the claim and the stamp burned no cooldown window, so the next stop hook retries at once and nothing is paused. A live claim is a run in progress and is not a failure. **THE STAMP MUST BELONG TO THE CLAIM, WHICH IS A COMPARISON AND NOT A PRESENCE.** The stamp file is never removed, so it outlives the run that wrote it, and a claim NEWER than the stamp is a run that died before it stamped — one that burned no cooldown window, and one whose exit skipped the deferred release (an unlocatable binary) rather than being killed mid-phase. So the pair counts as an unfinished run only when the stamp is at least as new as the claim (`lifecycleStampCoversClaim`), and a claim newer than the stamp is silent on both paths. The next run reads the same evidence from the reclaim: `claimPidFileReclaim` reports the dead claim's OWN timestamp (the claim file is replaced by this process's own as part of taking the slot), and `AcquireLifecycleLock` writes the same cause through `WriteLifecycleFailure` before the phases start — but only when the stamp covers that claim, so the marker survives even if that run is killed the same way and a later successful `FinishLifecycleRun` clears it. `claimPidFileReclaim` stats the claim before the liveness read, so a FIFO planted where a claim belongs cannot block the claim the way `isAlive`'s own read would. Pinned by `TestUnfinishedLifecycleRunAlertsAtSessionStart`, `TestUnfinishedLifecycleRunNeedsTheCooldownStamp`, `TestUnfinishedLifecycleRunIgnoresAClaimNewerThanTheStamp`, `TestLiveLifecycleClaimDoesNotAlert`, `TestReclaimedLifecycleClaimRecordsTheUnfinishedRun` and `TestReclaimedClaimNewerThanTheStampRecordsNoMarker`.
- **An unwritable data directory must not silence lifecycle failures (#1580).** When the data directory refuses new entries, the stop hook refuses the spawn (a writability check creates and removes a temporary file) and reports the refusal on the hook's stderr — a surface that does not depend on writing to the data directory — instead of running a full chain every turn with no cooldown and no record of its outcome. The same refusal is reported by `recordSpawnFailure` and `recordReflectSkipMarker` when their marker write fails, instead of discarding that error. At session start, a marker that cannot be cleared (the directory refuses writes) is not presented as the current cause: `lifecycleFailureAlert` reports the directory as the blocker and states the recorded failure can be neither cleared nor confirmed as current. An expired marker whose self-clean removal the directory refuses is the same un-clearable marker, so it reads the same way rather than leaving the alert silent and repeating the failed removal at every session start. `lifecycleBookkeepingWritable` is the shared writability check.
- **AN EMPTY PHASE GROUP IS NEVER SIGNALLED, AND A GROUP IS NOT EMPTY JUST BECAUSE THE PHASE HAS EXITED.** A phase runs in its own process group (`setPhaseProcessGroup`) so the deadline’s Cancel can ask the WHOLE group to shut down. That group no longer contains the CLI harnesses the phase spawns: each harness runs in a group of its own (`boundHarnessProcess`, `internal/ai`) so the harness’s own deadline can sweep it, and the reach one level up is restored by the phase command rather than by group membership — `maintenanceContext` (`cmd/ghost/lifecycle.go`, used by `ghost reflect`, `ghost resolve`, `ghost supersede` and `ghost opencode cleanup-sessions`) catches SIGINT/SIGTERM, and on unix SIGHUP when it was not inherited ignored, and cancels the phase’s work, so each in-flight harness’s own Cancel signals **its** group before the phase exits, and the call the phase is blocked on is what delivers that signal on the way out. `phaseWatchdog` (`cmd/ghost/lifecycle.go`) is the escalation after the deadline, and the question it asks before signalling is about the GROUP (`phaseGroupInhabited`, a signal-0 probe of `-pid` whose one empty answer is ESRCH), NOT about the phase process. It has to be: `cmd.Wait` returns the instant the direct child is reaped, while another member of the phase’s group — anything the phase started without a group of its own, with its own stdio so it does not hold the phase’s pipes — can ignore or slowly handle the signal and survive as an orphan. That orphan is the reason `killPhaseProcess` exists at all, so “the phase has been waited for” is not a reason to stay the escalation; “the group is empty” is. The sweep covers the phase’s group and nothing else: it does NOT reach a CLI harness, which is in a group of its own, and a harness is ended by the phase’s own SIGTERM handling (`maintenanceContext`, then the harness’s Cancel, which signals its group and force-kills what the harness grace leaves) before the phase exits. Asking the group is also what keeps the id safe to signal: an empty group’s id belongs to no run here and, on a busy machine, can already have been reused. Two orders were wrong and both are now fixed in one place. The old inline goroutine slept out the grace period and killed unconditionally, so a phase that timed out and then exited on SIGTERM — the case the grace period exists for — always took the late kill. And Go’s `select` picks a ready case AT RANDOM, so a phase that ended before the goroutine parked reached the same kill about half the time. `phaseWatchdog` now waits on `phaseDone` or the deadline, escalates only when `ctx.Err()` is `DeadlineExceeded` or the coordinator’s root context is cancelled (the loop’s own `cancel` is neither: it is not a deadline, and it leaves the root untouched), and then signals only if `phaseGroupInhabited` says something is still in the group. The grace is a deadline on the KILL, not a sleep: the watchdog re-asks the group every `phaseGroupPollInterval` and returns the moment it is empty — the same shape the harness escalation already uses (`harnessGracePoll`, `internal/ai`) — so an escalation costs what the group costs and not the full thirty seconds. The grace, the poll interval, the group question and the signal are fields of `phaseEscalation` so a test sets the boundary where the grace is up and the group has emptied at once, rather than waiting for it. The phase loop does NOT wait for the watchdog before cancelling the context — that would stall every later phase by the grace period — but a run a SIGNAL stopped DOES wait for it before returning (`awaitPhaseSweep`), because that is the one path where the loop is about to release the per-project claim: `cmd.Wait` returns the moment the phase is reaped while a group member that ignored the SIGTERM is still running, so returning first would orphan that member and let the next chain start beside it. That wait is bounded by the group and not by the clock, for the reason just given, with a backstop against a clock that never fires. The watchdog goroutine is launched only AFTER `cmd.Start()`, because `killPhaseProcess` reads `cmd.Process` and a goroutine reading it before `Start` writes it races with `Start` on the one field the kill is decided by. THIS IS UNIX-ONLY: on Windows `setPhaseProcessGroup` is a no-op, `terminatePhaseProcess` kills the direct child outright, there is no group to signal, and `phaseGroupInhabited` is always false. Neither the watchdog’s decision nor its kill is observable from outside a process group, so `killPhaseProcess` is a parameter the tests assert through (`TestThePhaseWatchdogSkipsTheKillWhenThePhaseExitsInsideTheGracePeriod`, `TestThePhaseWatchdogSkipsTheKillWhenThePhaseEndedBeforeItRan`, `TestThePhaseWatchdogAsksTheGroupBeforeItSignals`, `TestThePhaseWatchdogKillsAPhaseThatOutlivesTheGracePeriod`, `TestThePhaseWatchdogSweepsAHarnessThePhaseLeftBehind`, `TestThePhaseWatchdogReturnsWhenTheGroupEmpties`). The sweep test keeps its name but models the production layout: its fake harness runs in a group of its own (`setsid`), the phase ends that harness itself on the deadline’s SIGTERM and waits for it, and the escalation sweeps the TERM-ignoring helper the phase left in its own group — the harness is gone before the escalation runs, and the phase’s group is empty after it. **A SIGNAL TO THE COORDINATOR IS A THIRD INTERRUPTION, AND IT IS NOT THE LOOP’S OWN CANCEL.** The phase loop derives every phase’s context from the coordinator’s own `maintenanceContext` root, so a SIGINT/SIGTERM — or, on unix, a SIGHUP from a closed terminal or a dropped SSH connection — aimed at the coordinator alone — `kill <pid>`, Ctrl-C on a foreground run, a supervisor stopping only that pid — cancels the phase in flight and delivers the phase group’s SIGTERM through the phase’s Cancel, instead of taking Go’s default action and killing the coordinator while the phase, in a group of its own, keeps running and writing. `phaseWatchdog` therefore takes that root context beside the phase context, because the phase context alone cannot tell the two cancels apart: the loop cancels it once the phase has returned (nothing to sweep), while a root cancellation is a signal that terminated the phase in flight (escalate after the grace period if the group is still inhabited). The loop then stops rather than starting the next phase under a cancelled context, and the interruption is recorded as its OWN outcome rather than as a phase’s failure. `interruptOutcome` decides it from two facts — the phase that was running, if any, and the enabled phase the chain never reached. The running phase is NAMED (`interrupted by SIGTERM while running <phase>`, with the output it had printed), because it started and did not finish; a phase that never started is named by NOBODY (the cause is `interrupted by SIGTERM before <next phase>` and the failure list is empty), because a record attributing a failure to it would claim work that never happened — the old shape recorded the next phase in line with the bare cause a cancellation produces, `context canceled`, blaming a phase for a process that was never meaningfully started. A chain that had already run every enabled phase records NOTHING: nothing was cut short, so there is nothing to pause. An interrupt that names no phase cannot go through `FinishLifecycleRun`, which reads an empty failure list as a successful run and would clear an earlier record, so it is written directly; when an ordinary phase failure is already recorded, the interrupt cause is appended to it so one marker carries both facts. The next session start surfaces an alert instead of the silence a coordinator killed mid-run left behind. `TestThePhaseWatchdogEscalatesWhenTheCoordinatorIsSignalled` pins the escalation, `TestTheInterruptRecordNamesOnlyAStartedPhase` and `TestTheInterruptIsAppendedToAnEarlierFailure` pin what the interrupt records (the phase caught in flight, the never-started phase named by nobody, the run that records nothing, and the append beside an earlier failure), `TestTheSweepIsAwaitedOnlyForAnInterruptedRun` pins that only a signalled run waits, and `TestLifecycleSignalStopsTheRunningPhase` and `TestLifecycleSIGHUPStopsTheRunningPhase` in the e2e suite drive the built coordinator with a phase blocked on its harness. SIGHUP is caught only when it is not already ignored (`signal.Ignored`): `signal.Notify` replaces an inherited ignore with a handler, so catching it unconditionally would turn the hangup a `nohup` run exists to absorb into an interruption — `TestLifecycleIgnoredSIGHUPKeepsTheRunGoing` pins that. The recorded sentence reads `interrupted by SIGTERM` whichever of the three signals reached the coordinator: it names the group SIGTERM the phase received, and the alert matches that fixed text. A signal that lands during the steps BEFORE the phase loop (config read, scratch reap, sidecar sweep, start stamp) ends the run the same way once the step in progress returns: the retrieval audit, which writes to the store, is skipped when the root context is already cancelled, and the loop's first check records `interrupted by SIGTERM before <first phase>` with the same marker. Those steps run outside every phase deadline, so none of them may block on an entry it did not create — the sidecar sweep's regular-file rule above is what keeps that signal reachable (`TestLifecycleRunsPastAFIFOSidecarInTheTempDir`, `TestLifecycleSignalBeforeThePhaseLoopIsAnInterruption`, built binary). Two tests in `cmd/ghost/lifecycle_signal_unix_test.go` and `cmd/ghost/lifecycle_signal_linux_test.go` drive the same wiring through a built binary inside `go test ./cmd/ghost/`, so a `runLifecycle` that dropped any of it fails the ordinary run: `TestLifecycleInterruptBetweenPhasesStartsNoPhase` (Linux only) holds the coordinator before its first phase on a write to a nearly full stderr pipe — a FIFO config no longer holds it, because a config layer that is not a regular file is skipped unread — and signals it there, and requires that no phase starts and the record names none (`interrupted by SIGTERM before resolve`); `TestLifecycleInterruptDuringAPhaseRecordsItAndSweepsItsGroups` signals it while resolve is blocked on its harness, and requires that the harness’s own group is swept, that the coordinator neither records the run nor exits while a member of the phase’s group is still winding down (`awaitPhaseSweep`), that resolve is recorded as interrupted rather than failed, and that supersede never starts.
- **A TEST THAT SPAWNS A PHASE ENDS ITS OWN PROCESS GROUP ON THE WAY OUT, WHETHER ITS ASSERTIONS PASSED OR FAILED.** The escalation cannot be that guarantee, because it waits the grace period first: a test that is over before that is up — an assertion that failed, a pid file the phase never wrote, a phase that took the deadline's SIGTERM while a member of its group trapped it away — leaves that member running after the test binary has gone, reparented to init, still burning CPU and holding every descriptor the test process handed down. `registerPhaseSweep` (`cmd/ghost/lifecycle_watchdog_test.go`) therefore registers two cleanups: the kill (`killPhaseProcess` — the whole group by its id, behind the same `phaseGroupInhabited` question the escalation asks first, plus the recorded harness by the pid it wrote, liveness-checked before it is signalled) and, registered first so the LIFO order runs it last, the assertion that the harness is gone and the group is empty — POLL, not a single probe, because a killed child is still a member of its group until the reap lands. The phase's own stdio and `ExtraFiles` stay unset, so a phase that outlives the test holds no pipe open behind it (#1336). `TestThePhaseGroupIsSweptWhenTheTestAbortsBeforeTheEscalation` runs the phase and the harness with NO watchdog at all, leaves the way a failed assertion leaves, and finds the group empty anyway. The sweep test's own assertion of the empty group stays in its BODY (`TestThePhaseWatchdogSweepsAHarnessThePhaseLeftBehind`), not in the cleanup, because a sweep running behind it would hide a regression in the kill that test exists to observe.
## Key pattern: no harness child gets a tool

A harness child is given a **minimal environment AND no tool surface**, because
its prompt is memory text and memory text routinely carries third-party content.
The environment half is `harnessEnv`'s explicit allowlist; this is the tool half.
The rule is per-harness because the mechanism is, and the mechanism is whatever
that harness's own CLI documents — never a guessed flag.

**Every harness argv is a WHOLE-SLICE GOLDEN**, not a per-flag grep. A grep passes
when the flags it checks are present and says nothing about a flag that was never
added, which is exactly how goose shipped with an entire tool surface unchecked.
Each argv is built by a named function (`claudeInvocationArgs`,
`codexInvocationArgs`, `gooseInvocationArgs`, `openCodeInvocationArgs`,
`openCodePolicyFor`) so the whole slice is assertable;
`TestHarnessInvocationArgsAreGoldens` covers all four harnesses including both
opencode major versions, because V1 is the only shape that takes `--pure` and the
only one eligible for the deny policy.

A golden cannot check a flag against its upstream, so the shell-fake
harness-policy tests re-check what actually reaches the child, and two of them
cannot be checked by a fake at all. A fake that reads `$GOOSE_MODE` out of its own
environment proves only that Ghost WRITES the string — not that the harness accepts
the value, ranks it above its own config file, or acts on it. So the two upstream
facts this policy rests on have `GHOST_LIVE_TESTS=1`-gated tests that run the real
binary, pick a command that makes no LLM call, and report rather than assert where
the honest answer is "cannot settle".

**claude** is the only harness whose CLI can remove tools outright.
`claudeInvocationArgs` requires five capabilities probed from the INSTALLED
binary's own `--help` (`--tools`, `--disallowedTools`, `--safe-mode`,
`--restricted`, `--strict-mcp-config`) and REFUSES to run without them — a claude
that lacks one gets an error naming the upgrade, never a silently weaker policy.
`--tools ""` is the restriction (claude's own "disable all tools" spelling, which
unlike a list of names covers a tool shipped later). The child test walks its own
argv to assert the value is EMPTY, because `"$*"` renders a missing value and an
empty one identically — and a tool name there is both a grep hit and an open tool.

**codex has NO "no tools" flag.** `--sandbox read-only` bounds what a tool may do,
not which tools exist, so the policy is a list. Both exec tools are named
(`features.shell_tool` AND `features.unified_exec`) because codex registers either
the unified PTY-backed tool or the one-shot exec, and disabling one leaves the
other. `view_image` is named because it is a plain local file read the sandbox's
write policy does not cover. The plugin and connector group (`apps`, `plugins`,
`tool_suggest`, `skill_mcp_dependency_install`) goes together because each
contributes a tool of its own and installing an MCP server on demand RUNS A
COMMAND. `agents.enabled` and the top-level `web_search="disabled"` are not
feature keys, so `features list` says nothing about them and they are never
filtered.

**The codex list is filtered against the installed binary and NEVER refuses.**
`codexFeaturesFor` probes with `codex features list`, keyed on path/size/mtime by
a `sync.Map` exactly as claude's capability probe is, and caches the result. The
negative verdict is cached too, for `codexFeatureRetry` (5 minutes), and that
asymmetry is the point rather than an oversight: an unanswering codex is the
OLD-codex install this design exists to support, so probing it per call would add
a process to each of a lifecycle's hundreds of calls — the doubling the cache
exists to prevent, for the whole run. Caching the negative for the life of the
process is the opposite failure, because a codex upgraded in place under a
long-lived MCP server would never be noticed. A positive answer does not expire at
all.

**Both capability probes are SINGLE-FLIGHTED on a cold cache.** `codexFeaturesFor`
and `claudeCapabilitiesFor` each pair their per-identity `sync.Map` with a
`singleflight.Group` keyed on the SAME identity (`codexBinaryID`/`claudeBinaryID`
`probeKey`, built from the same three fields the cache key compares), so N
concurrent first-callers run ONE child process and all receive that probe's answer.
The cache alone cannot do this — it is check-then-probe, so every caller arriving
before the first one stores ran its own (measured: 50 spawns for 50 callers). A
`singleflight.Group` rather than a per-key mutex map because the two differ on the
FAILURE path, which is the whole difference: a mutex map serializes the burst, and
since a failed claude probe is deliberately NOT cached, each follower would take
the lock, find the cache still empty, and probe again — N processes, the bug rather
than a fix for it. The group shares the concurrent call's outcome whether it
succeeded or not, and retains NOTHING of its own, so the caches above keep owning
retention exactly as documented: a positive is kept for the life of the process, a
negative expires at `codexFeatureRetry`, a failure is never stored, and the
warn-once latches are untouched. The freshness test for codex therefore lives in
`codexCachedSupport` rather than at the lookup, because it is now read from two
places and a check-then-probe pair with that test in only one of them is how a
long-lived parent stops noticing a codex upgraded in place. The mutex-map argument
is CLAUDE'S, and codex is the case that would survive without the group:
the flight caches a verdict the probe could not REACH, so a per-key lock would
leave each follower a fresh negative to return. (A verdict reached under a DEAD
context is the one that is not cached — a cancellation, not an answer — which does
not change this.) Both use the same mechanism, chosen once, rather than a claim each
probe needs a guarantee it lacks.

**A caller's own context still governs its own call.** `Do` blocks on the leader's
flight and never consults the follower, so both probes use `DoChan` and `select` on
`ctx.Done()`: a caller whose context is dead reports its own error instead of waiting
out a verdict it cannot use — and on claude that error REFUSES the whole harness
call. The other half is that a leader whose context dies must not fail the followers
whose contexts are fine, or hand them its negative: in the long-lived `ghost mcp`
server one disconnected client would fail every concurrent turn in the burst. The
follower cannot tell those cases apart, because `exec.CommandContext` kills the child
and `Wait` reports `signal: killed`, never the context error — so the leader MARKS its
own failure (`probeContextError`, `internal/ai/probe.go`) and a live follower goes round
AGAIN, re-entering the same `singleflight.Group` under its own context. Re-entering the
group is load-bearing on the retry: a direct re-probe would restore the #741 burst (one
child per follower) on the rare path out of a common one, and re-reading the cache would
not do it either, because the followers all arrive within microseconds of each other and
would all miss it. Each round consumes one more cancellation, so rounds are bounded by
the callers sharing the key, and a caller whose OWN context is dead leaves without looping
— `isSharedProbeCancellation` requires a live `ctx`, and both arms of the select check it.
The converse is not treated as shared — a leader sees the same marker, and a dead caller
must not start a fresh attempt under a dead context, which is why
`isSharedProbeCancellation` requires a LIVE `ctx` as well as the marker.

**A cancelled caller leaves NOTHING behind.** The negative a killed probe produces says
more about the caller's connection than about the codex, so the flight inside
`codexFeaturesFor` stores the verdict of `probeCodexFeatures` only while THAT caller's
context is alive, and marks the flight instead when it is not. The store lives in the
flight rather than in the probe because the decision needs both facts at once — is the
answer worth keeping, and was the caller that produced it still alive — and a
check-then-act split across two functions disagrees with itself in the window between
them. It is measured on the CALLER's context and not on `probeCtx`: a probe that hit
its own 10s cap also knows nothing, and that one IS cached, for `codexFeatureRetry`,
because an unanswering codex is the install this design supports. Nothing was cached by
the round that died, so the next round's re-read misses and a live caller really does
re-probe rather than inherit the negative. For the same reason a caller that stopped
waiting gets no `warnOnWeakerCodexPolicy` at all (`CodexClient.run` checks `ctx.Err()`
first): nothing was learned about the codex, so the placeholder it was handed is not a
probe result, and reporting it would fire the `unverified` WARN and CONSUME its
once-per-process latch — silencing the real diagnostic for the rest of a long-lived
server, over the one turn that lost the most information.

**Both arms of the probe's `select` check the caller's own context.** When a caller's
context is already done AND the flight channel already holds a result, both `select` cases
are ready and Go picks between them at random, so a dead caller leaves by the flight arm
about half the time. The flight arm therefore re-checks `ctx.Err()` before it treats a
result as this caller's answer, rather than leaving the outcome to a coin — otherwise a
caller whose context died while the probe was running would report the leader's success
(or its kill) as its own, which is the one outcome the `ctx.Done()` arm exists to prevent.

The filter matters because codex **silently IGNORES** a `-c` key it does not know
(the fail-OPEN direction): `-c` overrides are applied onto the config tree without
a `deny_unknown_fields` check, so a key renamed upstream leaves the tool on and
the call still succeeds. `--strict-config` is the strict reading, it is opt-in, and
Ghost does not pass it, so `TestLiveCodexDeclaresTheNoToolFeatureKeys` provokes an
unknown key and reports which way the installed codex actually goes.

Probe output is validated by SHAPE before it is believed: one line must parse as
`<key> <known codex stage> <bool>`. A positive verdict is cached for the life of
the process, so a header row, a footer count or a single stray token read as an
answer would strip all ten overrides permanently and the only signal would be one
WARN. The test is deliberately NOT an overlap check against the policy's own key
list: a real codex older than this policy answers with a full table naming
features Ghost has never heard of and none of the ten it disables, and that is a
genuine weaker policy rather than an unreadable answer, so the two need different
warnings and only one of them means a tool is on.

`warnOnWeakerCodexPolicy` fires ONCE PER PROCESS **PER VERDICT** — two latches,
not one, because the two messages are not interchangeable and the suppressed one
is the more informative. The reachable order is a long-lived MCP server whose
first probe fails while the binary is mid-upgrade (unverified), after which the
`codexFeatureRetry` re-ask returns a real answer naming the missing keys; a shared
latch would drop the second, and that is the one which says which surfaces are on.
A probe that cannot answer says "unverified" rather than accusing the codex of
lacking features it was never asked about. The refusal is the part deliberately
absent, and the reason is which failure is larger: codex is selected with **no
version floor anywhere** — `CLIProvider` picks it by `exec.LookPath` below claude
and opencode, and a session-routed client names it as its backend with nothing
checked at all — so refusing would fail that session's reflect, resolve and
supersede calls on a working install over a surface that may not exist. The
failure the policy prevents is a tool that is already absent; the failure a refusal
creates is a lifecycle that does not run at all. An older codex therefore gets the
keys it understands, a weaker policy than a current one, and a warning that says
so.

The cache key is `{path, size, modTime}`, not the path alone, and an independent
review deleting the other two components passed the whole suite — so
`TestCodexProbeIsRekeyedWhenTheBinaryChanges` now rewrites a real executable in
place and requires a second probe. The limitation that test does NOT solve: codex
is commonly an **npm shim**, and the identity observed is the shim's, not the
vendor binary's. A real binary replaced behind a byte-identical, mtime-stable shim
is invisible to this key, and to every one like it in this package.

**opencode has no disabling flag either** — `run`'s only permission flag is
`--auto`, which moves in the WRONG direction and is never passed. The policy is a
permission deny/ask in the isolated config Ghost already writes, and the WILDCARD
is what makes it total: opencode defaults every unset permission to `allow`, so a
name it adds later is covered by `*` on the day it ships rather than the day
someone remembers it. V2 must use `ask` (a non-interactive `run` auto-declines
each ask) because a deny rule or a disabled tool strips that tool from the request,
and the free tier answers a request without the shell tool with 403 `provider.auth`
(the shell is the only tool it insists on, bisected on 2.0.18); V1 keeps `deny` since its
non-interactive ask behaviour is unverified.

**goose has NO flag for this at all** (its extension options only ADD extensions;
`--no-profile` governs the profile, not plugin discovery), so the restriction is
the `GOOSE_MODE=chat` environment variable goose's own reference documents as
"chat only, no tool calls", against a default of `auto` (approves tool calls). It
is SET rather than inherited, and `GOOSE_MODE` is deliberately absent from the
goose allowlist, because an inherited value could only weaken it while dropping it
returns the child to goose's autonomous default.

**Residuals, named rather than papered over.** codex's `apply_patch` has no
disabling flag or key (its registry entry is gated on the model catalog, and
`apply_patch_freeform` is marked Removed), so the read-only sandbox is its boundary
and a write attempt is refused rather than the tool withheld; the same holds for
codex's skills-extension read/list tools, which are off unless a skill exists and
the child's working directory is a neutral scratch dir holding none.

On the goose side two questions are open, and they are different in kind. Whether
`chat` mode SUPPRESSES EXTENSIONS in a given build is a property of the binary
that no configuration-printing command reports, so it is documented as unverified
rather than claimed as tested. Whether `chat` mode is COMPATIBLE WITH A HEADLESS
`goose run` is the question that decides whether this policy breaks the goose
backend at all, and `TestLiveGooseRunsATurnInChatMode` runs the production argv
under the production environment to answer it — behind `GHOST_LIVE_TESTS=1`,
because a real turn costs a model call. So the mode is confirmed end-to-end only
where goose is installed and a provider is configured, and not in CI.

## Key pattern: a harness call is bounded by its whole deadline

The deadline bound a harness child gets is ONE function, `boundHarnessProcess`
(`internal/ai/scratch.go`), so a caller can share it and a caller that forgets
it is a missing call a reader can see. `harnessCommand` applies it to every
child the funnel builds. The one child that is not built by the funnel is the
opencode cleanup runner's (`openCodeCleanupRunner.run`): it builds its own
command because it must reach the user's own opencode store rather than the
isolated tree the LLM clients use, and it takes the same bound through the same
function — pinned by `TestOpenCodeCleanupRunnerBoundsAPipeHoldingHelper`, so the
bound is a property of a harness child rather than of having gone through the
funnel. `exec.CommandContext`'s default cancellation is NOT that bound: on the
deadline Go kills only the direct child, and a harness helper that inherited the
child's stdout keeps the copy pipe's write end open, so `os/exec` reads that
pipe until EOF and `Run` blocks until the orphan exits, however long that is —
measured as a 500 ms context returning after 6 s, which is exactly what
`defaultTimeout` (`internal/ai/call_bound.go`) claims to prevent. Three settings
close it, in the same shape the lifecycle phase spawn uses one level up
(`cmd/ghost/lifecycle.go`): the child starts in its own process group
(`Setpgid` on unix and `CREATE_NEW_PROCESS_GROUP` on Windows), `cmd.Cancel`
signals the WHOLE group and then force-kills whatever the grace period left
(`terminateHarnessProcess`), and `cmd.WaitDelay` (`harnessWaitDelay`, 2 s) is
the backstop for the PIPES: after it `Wait` closes them and returns, so the call
ends at the deadline plus the backstop rather than at the orphan's lifetime. The
group signal reaches a helper on unix, the grace-period force-kill ends the one
that ignores it, and on Windows there is no portable group signal, so the direct
child is killed and the backstop does the bounding. **The escalation is not a
second timer racing `Wait`.** `terminateHarnessProcess` sends the group signal,
polls the group until it empties or the grace is up, force-kills it if not, and
polls once more for the kill to land — all on the goroutine `os/exec` called
`Cancel` from — so the orphan is gone by the time `Wait` returns rather than
beside it. Both halves are pinned so neither is inferred from the other:
`TestHarnessDeadlineSweepsTheProcessGroup` requires a SIGTERM-respecting helper
to be gone once the call returns, and
`TestHarnessWaitDelayBoundsAPipeHoldingGrandchild` requires a SIGTERM-ignoring
helper to be gone once the call returns too, with the call still bounded by the
deadline plus the backstop. The process-group moves themselves live in one
place, `internal/procgroup` (`SetGroup`, `TerminateGroup`, `KillGroup`,
`GroupInhabited`), which both the harness spawn and the phase spawn use, so the
two cannot drift apart; the phase's `terminatePhaseProcess`,
`killPhaseProcess` and `phaseGroupInhabited` are thin named wrappers over it,
and its `setPhaseProcessGroup` wraps `SetGroup` on unix but stays a no-op on
Windows for the console Ctrl-C reason above.

**A context with no deadline gets the per-call default unless the caller
removed it on purpose.** Every client's `run` takes its bound from ONE helper,
`boundHarnessCall` (`internal/ai/call_bound.go`), in this order: a deadline on
the context is kept as it is; a context marked `ai.WithoutDefaultBound` gets no
timer; any other context gets `defaultTimeout` (5 minutes). The marker exists
because a client cannot tell "the caller wants no bound" from "the caller
forgot a deadline" by looking at `ctx.Deadline` alone, and treating both alike
cut an explicit `reflection.consolidation_timeout_minutes: 0` at five minutes
per call. The marker is set in one place, the zero branch of
`consolidationContext` (`cmd/ghost/lifecycle.go`), so no other caller carries
it: resolve, supersede, the `ghost_resolve` tool and the bench classifier keep
the default, and the storyline eval keeps its own per-call deadline; the
marker never lifts a deadline the context carries; and the 10 s
capability and version checks keep their own bounds under it. A call the
default cuts names the default in its error (`explainDefaultBound`, keyed on
the timer's cancellation cause), because the harness itself reports only a
bare SIGTERM with an empty tail; a cut at the caller's own deadline is not
blamed on it. Pinned by `TestHarnessCallWithoutDeadlineKeepsTheDefault`,
`TestHarnessCallWithoutDefaultBoundIsNotCut`,
`TestHarnessCallKeepsTheCallersDeadline` (all four clients, with a shortened
default) and `TestConsolidationContextZeroLiftsTheClientDefault`.

**A harness child in its own group means the command that spawns it owns the
reach.** The child no longer shares the spawner's process group, so a signal
aimed at that group (the lifecycle phase deadline, or a terminal Ctrl-C on a
foreground maintenance command) no longer reaches the harness. The reach is
restored by the spawner instead: `maintenanceContext` (`cmd/ghost/lifecycle.go`)
makes `ghost reflect`, `ghost resolve`, `ghost supersede` and `ghost opencode
cleanup-sessions` catch SIGINT/SIGTERM — and on unix SIGHUP, unless the
process started with it ignored (`nohup`), the signal list being
`maintenanceSignals` in `lifecycle_unix.go` / `lifecycle_windows.go` — and
cancel the context their harness calls derive from, so each in-flight harness's own Cancel signals its group
before the process exits — the call the process is blocked on is what delivers
the signal on the way out. **A cancelled run is fatal rather than a tier failure**:
the LLM tier reports the cancellation as its error, and reading that as a tier
failure would let the mechanical tier run and, with `--apply`, rewrite the
corpus and exit 0 for a command the operator stopped. So
`TieredConsolidator.Consolidate` (`internal/reflection/consolidator.go`)
returns the context error after EVERY tier rather than falling through, and
`runReflect` checks the context after the consolidation and before it applies,
which covers a bare mechanical selection that never consults the context at
all. Pinned by `TestTieredConsolidatorDoesNotFallThroughACancelledCaller` and
`TestTieredConsolidatorRefusesAMechanicalResultFromACancelledCaller`. The
lifecycle watchdog's phase-group sweep stays as the backstop for a phase that
does not wind down. This is why the phase-group invariant above now says the
phase's group does not contain the harnesses.
